SOLICITOR Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (The agreement of purchase and sale, which is the contract between buyer and seller.): The agreement of purchase and sale is the contract between the buyer and the seller, and it is the document the lawyers work from, because it sets the closing date, the requisition date, the chattels and fixtures, the conditions and the adjustments. A listing agreement is a different contract between the seller and the brokerage, so it governs marketing and commission rather than the terms of the sale. The brokerage's own form and the seller's instructions to the brokerage do not replace the contract of purchase and sale.
Case real_e_00

A lawyer acts for Priya and Dev Anand, who agreed on 6 May 2026 to buy a resale detached house in Whitby from Ronald Petrie. The agreement sets a requisition date of 6 June 2026 and a closing date of 30 July 2026. The seller provides a survey from 2004. The purchasers' lawyer orders a new survey and a title search on 20 May 2026, and both are delivered on 12 June 2026. The new survey shows that a neighbour's detached garage and a wooden shed encroach about 0.6 metres onto the rear of the lot, and that a rear deck crosses the lot line by 0.3 metres. The seller will not remove the deck or the garage. The purchasers have already waived the financing condition and the inspection condition.

  1. The correct answer is A (Deliver a written requisition to the seller's lawyer before the requisition date.): The requisition date is the contractual deadline by which the purchaser must deliver requisitions on title, and the purchaser's lawyer must complete the off title searches in time to meet it. A written requisition puts the title problem to the seller and requires the seller to fix it or respond before closing. A caution does not require the seller to respond to a title objection, and title insurance addresses risk after closing rather than preserving the right to object. The municipality has no role in a private boundary dispute between adjoining owners.
  2. The correct answer is C (The purchasers take title subject to matters they could have requisitioned but did not.): The title clause in the standard form is read together with the requisitions clause, so a purchaser who could have requisitioned a title matter and did not is taken to have accepted it. Once the requisition date passes without an objection, the encroachments become a risk the purchasers carry unless the seller agrees to fix them. A title objection that is never delivered does not give the purchasers a right to terminate, and marketability is tested by what the purchaser was entitled to require rather than by what a new survey later revealed. The deposit is not repaid where the purchasers simply failed to raise the matter in time.
  3. The correct answer is B (The 40 year title search period set by the Registry Act.): The Registry Act provides that a person dealing with registry land need not show a good and sufficient chain of title for more than the 40 years immediately preceding the dealing, and it calls that the title search period. The lawyer therefore searches the abstract index and builds the state of title from the recorded instruments. A parcel in the land titles system is different, because the parcel register shows the registered owner and the instruments that affect the property. The other periods do not appear in the Registry Act.
  4. The correct answer is D (Advise the purchasers which searches are not covered and review the policy exceptions.): Where title insurance is used, the lawyer must explain to the client which searches will not be performed and what those searches would have revealed, then review the policy exceptions and exclusions personally. That advice is what allows the purchasers to make an informed decision to carry the encroachment risk. The insurer does not take over the lawyer's search obligations, and title insurance does not eliminate the value of a survey. The seller's lawyer cannot speak for the purchaser's insurer or promise what the policy will cover.
  5. The correct answer is A (A consent to sever the lot and a minor variance for the rear yard.): A consent severs land so that it can be conveyed, mortgaged or otherwise dealt with separately, and it is the approval that a plan of subdivision is not needed. A minor variance changes how the zoning by-law applies to a property, such as a required yard, and it is granted by the committee of adjustment. They are separate applications made under different sections, so one does not substitute for the other. A plan of subdivision and a zoning amendment are larger approvals that are not required on these facts.
  6. The correct answer is B (The agreement does not create or convey any interest in land.): An agreement, conveyance, mortgage or charge made in contravention of the subdivision control provisions does not create or convey any interest in land. There is a narrow exception for an agreement that is expressly made effective only if the subdivision control provisions are complied with. The absence of a consent is not cured by registration or by the municipality's lack of knowledge. A licence is also an interest in land, and the section does not create one.
  7. The correct answer is B (The purchaser, because the agreement authorizes the purchaser to apply.): An owner, a chargee or a purchaser of land, or a duly authorized agent, may apply for a consent, and a purchaser is defined to include a person who has entered into an agreement of purchase and sale and is authorized in the agreement to make the application. The purchaser here holds that authorization, so the seller's refusal does not block the application. A consent is given by the upper tier council, a single tier council, a prescribed council or the Minister, depending on the location, but the municipality's role as decision maker does not mean that the municipality applies. Waiting until closing would be too late to satisfy a condition of the agreement.
  8. The correct answer is A (The transfer stands, because the buyer took for value and without notice.): A disposition of an interest in a matrimonial home without the required consent may be set aside, but the Act protects a person who acquires the interest for value, in good faith and without notice that the property was a matrimonial home. This buyer paid market value, had no notice of the marriage, and registered the transfer, so the protection applies. A missing consent does not automatically make the transfer void. The remedy of the disappointed spouse is against the transferring spouse rather than against a protected purchaser.
  9. The correct answer is B (Every property they own that they ordinarily occupy as their family residence.): The Act defines a matrimonial home as every property in which a person has an interest and that the person and the person's spouse ordinarily occupy as their family residence. A couple can therefore have more than one matrimonial home, so the cottage can also be one if they ordinarily occupy it as a family residence. A designation is a way to change which properties are treated as matrimonial homes rather than a necessary step to create one. Assessment value and the tax concept of a principal residence do not decide the question.
  10. The correct answer is A (A statement of spousal status under the Family Law Act made by the transferor.): Every electronic transfer must contain a statement of spousal status under the Family Law Act made by the transferor, unless the transferor is a corporation. A spouse's joinder or consent is required only where the property is a matrimonial home, so it is not needed on these facts. A certificate of status relates to a corporation rather than to an individual, and the transferor rather than the lawyer makes the spousal status statement. That is why the spousal item is a standard part of the closing agenda.
  11. The correct answer is C (Land titles title is supported by the parcel register and the Assurance Fund.): Ontario land is registered either under the Registry Act, where the lawyer searches the recorded chain of instruments, or under the Land Titles Act, where the parcel register shows the registered owner and the instruments that affect the property. Land titles title is backed by the Land Titles Assurance Fund, which is a statutory compensation scheme. Registry land is not guaranteed in that way, and the 40 year title search period applies to registry land rather than to a parcel search. Land in the land titles system is searched by parcel, not converted before a search can be done.
  12. The correct answer is A (The parcel register kept by the land registrar for the property.): The Land Titles Act requires the land registrar to keep an automated index called the parcel register and to enter every instrument that affects a property under the property identifier assigned to it. A land titles search is therefore a parcel search rather than a search of a 40 year chain of instruments. The abstract index belongs to the registry system, and the sheriff's index records writs of execution rather than title. The assessment roll is a tax record and does not show the instruments on title.
  13. The correct answer is D (In the sheriff's electronic index, which the lawyer searches by name.): Writs of execution are filed with the sheriff and entered into an electronic index that the sheriff maintains, and the purchaser's lawyer searches that index for the names of the parties who have owned the land. A writ against a prior owner is not enforceable against land titles land unless a note of the writ was entered on the title register. The parcel register shows charges on title, but the writ itself lives in the sheriff's index, and a tax lien is a separate municipal claim. The Assurance Fund record concerns compensation claims rather than writs.
  14. The correct answer is A (Updated the search close to closing so the writ would be revealed.): Registered land is not bound by a writ of execution until the sheriff has complied with the notice requirements in the Execution Act, which is why the executions search is run close to closing and updated to the closing date. A search taken three weeks early will not show a writ that binds after the search date, so the lawyer misdates the risk. A transfer registered before the sheriff complies can be protected in some circumstances, but the lawyer cannot rely on winning a race with the sheriff. A writ is not invalid because it was filed close to closing, and the seller's confirmation is not a substitute for the search.
  15. The correct answer is A (Unpaid realty taxes are a special lien on the land with priority.): The Municipal Act, 2001 provides that taxes are a special lien on the land in priority to every claim, privilege, lien or encumbrance of every person except the Crown. A tax certificate therefore tells the purchaser's lawyer the state of the account and whether anything must be adjusted or paid on closing. Water and utility accounts are separate off title matters, and there is no requirement to prepay a full year of taxes before a sale. A municipality does not control the issuance of surveys.
  16. The correct answer is B (A minor variance from the committee of adjustment.): The committee of adjustment may authorize a minor variance where the variance is desirable for the appropriate development of the land and the general intent and purpose of the by-law and official plan are maintained. A small setback shortfall with the rest of the proposal complying is the usual case for that application. A zoning amendment is a heavier step reserved for changes to the by-law itself, and a consent deals with severing land rather than with a yard requirement. Site plan approval controls site design matters under a different municipal process.
  17. The correct answer is A (No, because a compliance order can bind the owner and affect the property's use.): A building inspector who finds a contravention of the Building Code Act or the building code may make an order directing compliance, and such an order can bind the owner and affect how the property may be used. Registration of a transfer does not wipe out a compliance order that runs with the property. The order is not merely personal to the seller, and a municipal inspector rather than a court makes these orders. That is why the purchaser's lawyer searches permit records and checks for outstanding work orders before closing.
  18. The correct answer is C (The regulation under the Land Titles Act dealing with surveys and plans.): The Land Titles Act requires the legal description entered on a parcel to comply with its description requirements, and a regulation under that Act prescribes the requirements for surveys, plans and descriptions of land. The lawyer also decides early whether to advise that a survey be obtained, because a survey shows the buildings and any encroachments on the ground. The Planning Act deals with subdivision control and consents, and the registry 40 year rule governs chain of title searches rather than the form of a description. A zoning by-law controls permitted use and zone standards, not survey requirements.
  19. The correct answer is C (A written demand that the seller fix a title problem or provide a document.): A requisition on title is the purchaser's written demand that the seller fix a title problem or provide something needed to complete the transaction, and the Law Society's guidelines treat the letter of requisition as a document the lawyer personally reviews and signs. It is a step in the contract rather than a registration, so an oral request does not meet the contractual requirement and a caution is a different remedy. A requisition does not, by itself, convert the transaction into a claim for compensation, because its purpose is to clear the title objection before closing. That is why the lawyer must have the search results in time to raise the matter.
Case real_e_01

A lawyer acts for Northgate Holdings Ltd., which agreed on 4 May 2026 to buy a small strip plaza in Mississauga from 1245 Dundas West Inc. The purchase price is $2,450,000 plus HST, the due diligence condition expires on 30 June 2026, and the closing date is 15 September 2026. Two of the four units are leased to retail tenants on written leases, and the other two are vacant. The buyer is registered for HST purposes and will continue to rent out the plaza in its commercial business. The seller has not yet provided the leases, a rent roll or a corporate profile report, and the buyer's lender wants a report on the leases before it advances funds. The buyer's lawyer has ordered the title search and the zoning and tax searches.

  1. The correct answer is D (The sale is generally a taxable supply, so HST applies to the price.): A sale of commercial real property by a person who is not a builder is not within the residential exemption, so it is generally a taxable supply unless a specific exemption applies. A retail strip plaza used for commercial rental is not a residential complex, so the residential resale exemption does not help. The buyer's status as a registrant affects its ability to claim input tax credits rather than whether the sale is taxable. HST applies to the whole price rather than only to the leased units.
  2. The correct answer is A (Thirteen per cent, being the federal and provincial parts combined.): HST applies in Ontario at a combined rate of 13 per cent, made up of the 5 per cent federal part and the 8 per cent provincial part. The buyer may be able to claim input tax credits for the tax it pays in the course of its commercial rental business, but that does not change the rate stated in the agreement. Five per cent alone is the GST rate in a non participating province, and 8 per cent is only the provincial part. Fifteen per cent is not the Ontario rate.
  3. The correct answer is D (Advise the buyer promptly and obtain instructions on whether to waive or extend.): A due diligence condition is a deadline: the buyer's lawyer must have the search results and the lease review in time to advise the buyer whether to waive the condition, extend it by agreement or let the agreement come to an end. Leaving the results outstanding means the buyer loses the chance to make that decision. Waiting until after closing is too late, and a caution is a title remedy that does not extend a condition. Silence from the seller does not fulfil a condition that depends on the buyer's own review.
  4. The correct answer is D (The agreement ends and the deposit is returned, because the condition was not met.): A due diligence condition is a condition precedent to the buyer's obligation to complete, so if it is not fulfilled or waived by the date in the agreement, the agreement comes to an end and the deposit is returned. Completing a review is not the same as waiving the condition, and the buyer is entitled to decide that the results are not satisfactory. The seller's damages remedy applies where the buyer breaches after the conditions are satisfied or waived. There is no breach to litigate where the agreement ends in accordance with its own condition.
  5. The correct answer is D (Within six years from the time the loss was suffered.): The Land Titles Act requires an application for compensation to be made within six years from the time the loss was suffered, and the application goes to the Director of Titles. The applicant must show the due diligence the Director specifies, cannot recover just compensation otherwise, and must fall within one of the compensating situations, such as deprivation by a fraudulent instrument. The two year period belongs to the general limitation of actions rather than to this application, and there is no 90 day or 10 year period in the section. That is why the lawyer must consider the Fund early when a fraud has occurred.
  6. The correct answer is D (The Registry Act system, where the state of title is built from recorded instruments.): Ontario land is registered either under the Registry Act or under the Land Titles Act, and an abstract listing recorded instruments with no parcel identifier is the mark of registry land, where the state of title must be built up from the recorded chain of instruments. Land titles land instead has a parcel register that shows the registered owner and every instrument affecting one specific property identifier. Ontario runs these as two distinct statutory schemes rather than one merged index, and the Land Titles Act is Ontario's own legislation, not a separate Torrens statute.
Case title__00

A lawyer acts for a purchaser buying a semi detached house in Barrie from Elena and Marco Rossi, who bought the property from Devon Clarke in 2015. The property is registered under the Land Titles Act. The purchaser's lawyer orders a title search, an executions search against the Rossis and the prior owners named on the parcel abstract, a municipal tax certificate, and a zoning search. The parcel register shows a writ of execution recorded years ago against Devon Clarke. The tax certificate shows the current taxes are paid in full. The zoning search shows the detached garage was built without a permit and does not meet the required side yard setback.

  1. The correct answer is B (Check whether a note of the writ was entered while Clarke held title.): Under the Land Titles Act, a writ against a prior owner is not enforceable unless a note of the writ was entered on the title register while that prior owner held title, so the lawyer must check for that note. Assuming the writ binds the current owners without checking that record skips a step the Act actually requires. Dismissing the writ as irrelevant, or demanding the Rossis pay a stranger's debt, both jump to a conclusion before the register has been checked.
  2. The correct answer is A (The writ against Clarke does not bind this title, since no note was entered.): Because the writ against Clarke was never noted on the register while he held title, it is not enforceable against a later registered owner's title, so the purchaser can take title free of that writ. The writ remaining visible on the abstract as a historical entry does not change its unenforceable status against this chain of title. Neither a sheriff's release nor the creditor's consent is required where the writ was never properly noted in the first place.
  3. The correct answer is B (When an enforceable writ against that owner has been recorded on the title register.): The registered owner's title is subject to enforceable writs of execution against that owner that have been recorded on the register, which is why a title search must include a search of the sheriff's index for the relevant names. A same name match elsewhere in the province, without a proper recording against this owner, does not bind the title. Physical seizure and a separate confirming court order are not what makes a writ enforceable against registered land.
  4. The correct answer is D (Deliver a written requisition requiring the seller to fix the permit issue.): A requisition on title is the purchaser's written demand that the vendor fix a title problem before closing, so the lawyer must deliver a requisition about the permit and setback before the requisition date passes. Staying silent and relying only on title insurance denies the client the chance to make an informed decision about the risk. Waiting until after closing is too late once the requisition date has passed, and assuming the municipality will not act is not a safe basis for advice.
  5. The correct answer is A (The deed does not convey any interest in the land to the neighbour.): Section 50 of the Planning Act prohibits conveying land that is not described in accordance with a registered plan of subdivision, and an agreement, conveyance, mortgage or charge made in contravention of that section does not create or convey any interest in the land at all. A fine does not cure the underlying lack of any conveyed interest, and the deed is void from the outset rather than merely voidable at the neighbour's option. Nothing in the section allows the parties one year to obtain a consent retroactively.
  6. The correct answer is B (The land registrar maintains a parcel register indexed by property identifier for each parcel.): The land registrar keeps an automated index called the parcel register and enters every instrument affecting a property under the property identifier assigned to that property, which is why a land titles search is a parcel search rather than a chain of title exercise. The municipal assessment roll has nothing to do with how title itself is searched. There is no printed abstract automatically generated for a land titles parcel, and land titles searching does not use the registry style abstract index at all.
  7. The correct answer is C (A municipal tax certificate, which must show the deferred taxes and the interest owing.): A purchaser's lawyer obtains a municipal tax certificate to confirm the state of the realty tax account, and the Municipal Act, 2001 expressly requires that deferred taxes and the interest on them be shown on a tax certificate. An accountant's income summary does not confirm what is owing on title, and a seller's own declaration is not the reliable third party confirmation the closing agenda calls for here. Deferred taxes remain a charge on the property and are not automatically discharged simply because ownership changes.
  8. The correct answer is A (Requisition the seller to resolve or disclose the order before the requisition date passes.): A compliance order directing work under the Building Code Act is a title risk because it can affect the use of the property, so the purchaser's lawyer should requisition the seller to resolve or fully disclose it while there is still time before the requisition date. Such orders are not automatically extinguished by a change of ownership, so telling the purchaser it cannot bind them would be wrong. Waiting until after closing forfeits the chance to negotiate a fix, and no assumption about title insurance replaces actually reviewing the policy's exceptions for this order.
  9. The correct answer is B (The Land Titles Assurance Fund, which can compensate a wrongfully deprived owner.): The Land Titles Assurance Fund is continued under the Land Titles Act and exists to compensate a person who is wrongfully deprived of land in the circumstances the Act describes, which is the backstop that distinguishes land titles from an unguaranteed private search. There is no automatically attached government insurance policy separate from this statutory fund. No municipal guarantee of search accuracy exists, and mortgage insurance from a private lender is an entirely different product unrelated to the Assurance Fund.
  10. The correct answer is C (The builder, as a purchaser authorized in the agreement to make the application.): An owner, chargee or purchaser of land, or a duly authorized agent, may apply for a consent, and a purchaser is defined as a person who has entered into an agreement of purchase and sale and is authorized in the agreement to make the application, which describes the builder here. It is therefore incorrect to say only the client personally can apply. A surveyor may assist with the sketch but is not who applies, and the municipality processes the application rather than initiating it on the owner's behalf.
  11. The correct answer is B (An application may still be made, since claims allow six years from loss.): A person wrongfully deprived of land by the registration of a fraudulent instrument may apply for compensation from the Land Titles Assurance Fund, and the application must be made within six years from the time the loss was suffered, so an application at four and a half years is still within time. The one year figure is not the statutory limit that applies here. A police report within thirty days is not a precondition to a claim, and the claim is made to the Director of Titles against the Fund, not as a personal lawsuit against the registrar.
  12. The correct answer is A (No person may build or demolish a building without a chief building official's permit.): The Building Code Act, 1992 provides that no person may construct or demolish a building unless the chief building official has issued a permit, which applies to a detached garage regardless of its size or the owner's future plans. The requirement is not limited to structures over two storeys, and it is not limited to commercial buildings, since residential accessory structures are covered as well. Nothing in the Act ties the permit requirement to whether the owner intends to sell within a year.
  13. The correct answer is D (Ten per cent of the price of the services or materials actually supplied.): The Construction Act requires every payer to retain a holdback equal to ten per cent of the price of the services or materials as they are actually supplied. That holdback must be kept until every lien that may be claimed against it has expired, been satisfied, or been otherwise provided for. A different percentage such as five, fifteen, or twenty per cent has no basis in the statute and would leave the fund miscalculated. Getting this figure wrong exposes the owner to personal liability to lien claimants up to the shortfall.
Case mortga_00

A mortgagee holds a mortgage against a semi-detached house in London, Ontario. The mortgage contains no power of sale clause of its own, so the general statutory power of sale under the Mortgages Act governs this file. The mortgagor has missed five monthly payments and now owes a substantial arrears balance. Before any notice of sale went out, the mortgagor's lawyer wrote to the mortgagee under section 22 of the Mortgages Act, asking for a written statement showing the exact amount currently in default. Three weeks have now passed and the mortgagee has still not replied to that request. The mortgagor wants to know what can still be done to stop enforcement, and, separately, who the mortgagee will eventually have to notify once it does move ahead with a sale.

  1. The correct answer is D (Pay the arrears and the reasonable expenses owed to the mortgagee before any sale.): Section 22 of the Mortgages Act allows a mortgagor to perform the covenant or pay the amount actually due, together with any necessary expenses, at any time before a sale is completed or before an action to enforce the mortgage is started. Doing so relieves the mortgagor from the consequences of the default that has occurred. Asking a land registrar to cancel a validly registered mortgage has no legal basis, since only a discharge or a court order removes a registered charge. A receiver is a remedy a court may appoint over rents and profits in certain enforcement proceedings, not a step a mortgagor takes to cure its own default.
  2. The correct answer is C (November 15, forty five days after the date the notice was served.): The Mortgages Act requires that no sale take place under the statutory power of sale until at least 45 days have passed after the required written notice is given under Part III of the Act. Counting forward 45 days from October 1 gives November 15 as the earliest possible completion date for the sale. A shorter period such as 15, 30, or 60 days does not match the 45 day figure that Part III sets for this type of mortgage. This 45 day period replaced the former 35 day period for notices given under the current version of the section.
  3. The correct answer is C (The mortgagor and other registered parties such as subsequent encumbrancers and execution creditors.): The Mortgages Act requires the mortgagee to give the notice of exercising the power of sale to the mortgagor and to other listed persons with an interest in the property, which can include subsequent registered owners, encumbrancers, execution creditors, and construction lien claimants as specified in the Act. A municipal tax department collects taxes through its own certificate and lien process and is not among the parties who receive this particular notice. A real estate agent or an appraiser is retained to help with the sale itself and has no statutory right to the notice. Neighbouring landowners have no registered interest in the mortgaged property and are not entitled to notice under this section.
  4. The correct answer is C (The mortgagee cannot enforce its rights until it properly answers the request.): Section 22 lets a mortgagor require the mortgagee to give a written statement of the amount currently in default or the nature of the default, and the mortgagee must answer within 15 days. If the mortgagee fails to answer, or answers incompletely or incorrectly without a reasonable excuse, the mortgagee's rights to enforce the mortgage are suspended until it complies. The debt itself is not cancelled by a late reply, since the underlying obligation to repay the loan continues. A land registrar has no independent power to remove a validly registered mortgage simply because a statutory deadline under this section was missed.
  5. The correct answer is A (Yes, because $32,000 is less than the $46,000 statutory completion cost allowed.): The Construction Act treats a contract as substantially performed when the cost to complete or correct the improvement does not exceed 3 per cent of the first $1,000,000 of the contract price plus 2 per cent of the next $1,000,000. On a $1,800,000 contract that allowance is $30,000 plus 2 per cent of the remaining $800,000, or $16,000, for a total of $46,000. Since the actual remaining cost of $32,000 is below that $46,000 ceiling, the improvement is substantially performed. The formula applies to contracts of any size, it is simply calculated in tiers as the price grows.
  6. The correct answer is B (Sixty days after the certificate of substantial performance was published.): A lien expires at the end of the 60 day period after the earlier of the publication of a certificate or declaration of substantial performance and the completion, abandonment or termination of the contract, unless it is preserved before that date. Forty five days has never been the preservation period under the current Act. Ninety days is the separate period allowed to perfect a lien once it has already been preserved, not the period to preserve it. Thirty days is too short and does not match any deadline set out in the lien expiry provisions.
  7. The correct answer is C (No, the current Act allows 90 days after preservation to perfect the lien.): A preserved lien expires unless it is perfected before the end of the 90 day period following the last day on which it could have been preserved. The 45 day period the lawyer described was the rule under an earlier version of the legislation and is no longer correct for a lien preserved under the current Act. Thirty days is too short and does not appear anywhere in the perfection provisions. One hundred twenty days overstates the period the current Act actually allows.
  8. The correct answer is B (Give a copy of the claim for lien to the owner before it expires.): Where a lien does not attach to the premises, such as certain liens against public authority land, the lien is preserved by giving a copy of the claim for lien to the owner during the supply of services or materials, or at any time before the lien expires. Registering a claim for lien on title is the method used where the lien does attach to the premises, which is not the case here. Filing with a municipal clerk and publishing a newspaper notice are not preservation methods recognized anywhere in the Construction Act.
  9. The correct answer is B (A further ten per cent of the price of the remaining work.): Where a contract has been certified or declared substantially performed but services or materials remain to be supplied, the payer must also retain a further ten per cent of the price of those remaining services or materials, running from the date of substantial performance. Five and twenty per cent do not match the figure the Act sets for this second holdback. The obligation is separate from the original holdback on the substantially performed portion of the work, so it does not disappear once substantial performance is certified.
  10. The correct answer is C (The testator signs at the end before two witnesses present together.): Section 4(2) of the Succession Law Reform Act requires the testator to sign at the end of the will, to make or acknowledge that signature in the presence of two attesting witnesses who are present at the same time, and to have two of those witnesses subscribe in the testator's presence. One witness, or witnesses who sign at different times, does not satisfy the section. Ontario law does not require a notary or any particular certification for an ordinary will, and a will executed without valid witnesses is not saved by a notary's seal.
Case wills__00

Mrs. Adeyemi is eighty-four and lives in a retirement home in Scarborough. In 2024 her doctor recorded a diagnosis of moderate dementia and noted that she had trouble recalling the extent of her property. In March 2026 her nephew, Tunde, drove her to a solicitor he had chosen and sat in on the meeting, although he had never been involved in her affairs before. The solicitor met Mrs. Adeyemi once, took notes, and prepared a will that left her whole estate to Tunde and cut out her two daughters. The solicitor and a legal assistant witnessed the will. Mrs. Adeyemi died three weeks later, and the daughters now challenge the will. The circumstances of the preparation are the kind a court would treat as suspicious.

  1. The correct answer is B (To prove knowledge and approval of the contents on a balance of probabilities.): Where suspicious circumstances surround the preparation of a will, the propounder must prove that the testator knew and approved the contents of the will, and where the circumstances call capacity into question the propounder must also prove capacity. The ordinary presumption of capacity no longer decides the case. Estrangement of a child, the ability to manage bank accounts, or the quality of the solicitor's notes do not by themselves discharge that burden, although careful notes are valuable evidence.
  2. The correct answer is A (Testamentary capacity is a separate test about the will and its contents.): The statutory test in section 6 of the Substitute Decisions Act asks whether a person can understand information relevant to managing property and appreciate the reasonably foreseeable consequences of a decision. Testamentary capacity is a different question, described in Banks v. Goodfellow, about understanding the will and its effects, the extent of the property, and the claims of those who might expect to benefit. A person may be incapable of managing property and still be capable of making a valid will, so the file note does not by itself invalidate the will, and it creates no presumption of invalidity.
  3. The correct answer is D (A delusion unrelated to the will does not by itself defeat capacity.): The test in Banks v. Goodfellow requires the testator to understand the nature of making a will and its effects, the extent of the property being disposed of, and the claims to which effect ought to be given. No disorder of the mind may poison the testator's affections or prevent the exercise of the natural faculties, and no delusion may influence the will in a way that would not have happened with a sound mind. A delusion that has no connection to the contents of the will does not by itself make the testator incapable, so the belief about the husband matters only if it shaped the dispositions.
  4. The correct answer is C (That the testator's free will was overborne by pressure from Tunde.): Undue influence in a wills case means coercion that overbears the testator's independent will, so the resulting document expresses the pressure of another person rather than the testator's own intention. The burden of proving undue influence rests on the party who alleges it, and suspicious circumstances do not shift that particular burden, although they can shift the burden on knowledge and approval. A larger share, the arranging of a meeting, or practical dependence without more does not establish coercion.
  5. The correct answer is C (The handwritten letter, because a holograph will needs no witnesses.): Section 6 of the Succession Law Reform Act allows a testator to make a valid will wholly in his or her own handwriting and signature, without any formality and without the presence, attestation or signature of a witness. A typed will signed before only one witness does not meet section 4(2), which requires two attesting witnesses present at the same time, so that document cannot operate as a will on its own. A document that merely records intentions is not thereby a valid will, and handwriting carries no disadvantage compared with a printed form.
  6. The correct answer is D (Making a later will in accordance with the requirements of the Act.): Section 15 of the Succession Law Reform Act lists the ways in which a will may be revoked. They are a later will made in accordance with the Act, a written declaration of intention to revoke that is made with the same formalities as a will, and burning, tearing or otherwise destroying the will by the testator or by someone in the testator's presence and by direction with the intention of revoking it. Telling the executor or filing a notice does not revoke a will, and a written declaration only works if it is executed with the formalities required for a will.
  7. The correct answer is C (The gift and the appointment to the former spouse are revoked.): Under section 17(2) of the Succession Law Reform Act, where the testator's marriage is terminated by a judgment absolute of divorce or declared a nullity, a gift to the former spouse, the appointment of the former spouse as executor or trustee, and any power of appointment given to the former spouse are all revoked, and the will is read as if the former spouse had died first. The rest of the will remains in force, so the estate does not pass on intestacy. The rule applies unless the will shows a contrary intention.
  8. The correct answer is A (No, because marriage no longer revokes an existing will in Ontario.): Section 16 of the Succession Law Reform Act formerly provided that marriage revoked a will, subject to exceptions, but that section was repealed effective January 1, 2022. In Ontario today, marrying does not revoke an existing will, so the 2019 will stands and the brother takes under it. A surviving spouse may still have remedies, including an election under the Family Law Act or a dependant support claim, but those possibilities do not mean that the will was cancelled.
  9. The correct answer is D (The alteration has no effect unless it was made with will formalities.): Section 18 of the Succession Law Reform Act provides that an alteration made after a will is executed has no effect unless it is made with the formalities needed to make a will, or, for a holograph will or a forces will, with the testator's signature placed in the margin or near the alteration. A later conversation with the executor does not supply the missing formality. An alteration that is not validly made only invalidates the words that it makes unclear, and it does not revoke the whole will.
  10. The correct answer is C (The gift is void because the witness is the husband's spouse.): Section 12(1) of the Succession Law Reform Act voids a beneficial gift made to an attesting witness or to that witness's then spouse, so far as it concerns the witness, the spouse, or anyone claiming under either of them. The neighbour's husband is the witness's spouse, so the gift to him fails, although the neighbour remains a competent witness to prove the will. The absence of a gift to the witness herself does not save the gift to her spouse, and the fact that one witness is interested does not invalidate the whole will. Section 13 deals with a creditor whose debt is charged on estate property, which is a different situation.
  11. The correct answer is C (The court may treat the paper document as her will if it sets out her intentions.): Section 21.1 of the Succession Law Reform Act allows the Superior Court of Justice to order that a document or writing which was not properly executed or made under the Act is as valid and fully effective as a will if it sets out the testamentary intentions of the deceased. The power applies where the deceased died on or after January 1, 2022, which is the case here, and the missing witness does not take the power away. A friend's later opinion is not the test, because the court must be satisfied about the deceased's own intentions. The power is subject to section 31 of the Electronic Commerce Act, 2000, so it cannot validate an electronic will, but this document is on paper.
  12. The correct answer is A (A disposition added after the signature does not take effect.): Section 7(3) of the Succession Law Reform Act provides that a signature which complies with the Act does not give effect to a disposition or direction placed underneath or after the signature, or to a disposition inserted after the signature was made. The gift of the car therefore fails even though it is in the testator's handwriting and even if the witnesses watched him write it. Adding words after execution does not revoke the rest of the will, which remains valid.
  13. The correct answer is C (No, because only one witness subscribed in the testator's presence.): Section 4(2) of the Succession Law Reform Act requires two attesting witnesses to be present at the same time when the testator makes or acknowledges the signature, and it requires two of those witnesses to subscribe the will in the testator's presence. The friend signed four days later and outside the testator's presence, so the execution does not meet the statutory requirement. Ontario does not require three witnesses, and the signature of two witnesses is not effective if the required presence and contemporaneity are missing.
  14. The correct answer is A (Yes, because one witness was an LSO licensee and the signing was contemporaneous.): Section 4(3) of the Succession Law Reform Act allows the presence requirement to be met by audio-visual communication technology where at least one person acting as a witness is a licensee under the Law Society Act, the making or acknowledgment of the signature and the subscribing are contemporaneous, and any regulatory requirements are met. Counterpart copies may also be signed. These provisions are permanent law in Ontario and are not limited to a declared emergency, so the fact that the witnesses were in a different location does not defeat the execution. The presence of the daughter alone would not have been enough if no witness had been a licensee.
  15. The correct answer is A (No, because a member of the regular force may make a will.): Section 8(1) of the Succession Law Reform Act sets out the exceptions to the general rule that a person under eighteen cannot make a valid will. The exceptions cover a person who is or has been married, a person who is contemplating marriage to a named person and whose will says so and the marriage later takes place, a member of a listed component of the Canadian Forces, and a sailor at sea or on a voyage. A member of the regular force falls within the exception, so the mother's general statement is wrong. The testator may also revoke that will while still under eighteen.
  16. The correct answer is D (Meet alone, take detailed notes and record the instructions and the reasoning.): Where capacity may be in issue, the drafting solicitor should meet the client without the person who stands to benefit, take careful instructions, and keep a detailed contemporaneous record of what was asked, what was explained and why the client chose the particular dispositions. That record is the best evidence of knowledge and approval if the will is later challenged. Relying on the benefiting child for confirmation, insisting on a formal assessment before any instructions are taken, or preparing the will without addressing the concern all leave the file and the client exposed.
  17. The correct answer is A (Yes, because two disinterested witnesses also attested the will.): Section 12(1) of the Succession Law Reform Act voids a gift to an attesting witness or to that witness's spouse, but section 12(4) restores the gift where the will is attested by at least two persons who are not caught by the rule. The two neighbours are disinterested, so the exception applies and the gift to the niece is preserved. The husband's interest would have mattered if there had not been two other disinterested attesting witnesses. The niece's own failure to witness is not the deciding point, and an interested witness does not void every gift in the document.
  18. The correct answer is C (Yes, because no particular form of attestation is required.): Section 4(6) of the Succession Law Reform Act provides that where witnesses are required by section 4, no particular form of attestation is necessary. What matters is that the testator signed at the end and that two witnesses, present at the same time, subscribed in the testator's presence. The absence of a printed attestation wording does not make the will defective, and the witnesses do not have to record any view about capacity in the document. A later affidavit of execution helps prove the will in a probate proceeding but is not what gives it effect.
  19. The correct answer is A (The will remains valid and may be proved by other evidence.): Section 11 of the Succession Law Reform Act states that a will is not invalid because a person who attested it was incompetent at the time of execution or later became incompetent as a witness to prove its execution. The validity of the will does not depend on a witness being able to testify years later. Other evidence, such as the evidence of the other witness or an affidavit of execution, may be used to prove the will in the probate proceeding, and the surviving witness does not have to re-execute anything.
  20. The correct answer is D (A codicil, which is a will for the purposes of the Act.): The Succession Law Reform Act defines a will to include a codicil, so a document that adds to or varies an existing will must be executed with the same formalities as a will, which was done here. The codicil is admitted to probate with the will, and the two documents are read together. A memorandum that is not executed with those formalities would not change the will, a trust deed would require different arrangements for property, and a direction to the estate trustee does not create a testamentary gift.
  21. The correct answer is A ($0 on the first $50,000, then $15 for each $1,000 above it.): For an application made on or after January 1, 2020, the first $50,000 of value is exempt and the tax is $15 for each $1,000, or part thereof, by which the value exceeds $50,000. The older split rate of $5 and $15 applied to applications made before January 1, 2020 and does not apply to this application. A flat charge on the whole value would tax the first $50,000, which the current rule exempts.
Case estate_00

Doreen died in February. Her will named her daughter Maya as estate trustee and divided the residue equally between Maya and her brother Peter. At the date of death Doreen owned a house worth $620,000 that was subject to a mortgage of $180,000, and a bank account of $40,000 in her own name. She also held shares worth $90,000 in joint tenancy with her son Paul, who survived her. Doreen's funeral bill of $12,000 was unpaid at the date of death, and her final income tax return had not been filed. In May, before paying the funeral bill and before obtaining a clearance certificate, Maya paid the whole residue to herself and Peter. Maya has not yet filed the estate information return and gave no notice to creditors.

  1. The correct answer is A ($6,450, being $15 for each $1,000 above $50,000.): The value of the estate is the house at its date of death value less the mortgage that charges it, plus the bank account, which gives $480,000. Property held in joint tenancy with a surviving joint tenant passes by survivorship and is not included in the value of the estate. Funeral expenses are not deductible in arriving at the value, so the tax is $15 for each $1,000 above $50,000, or $6,450.
  2. The correct answer is C (She is personally liable for the unpaid tax to the extent of the value distributed.): A legal representative who distributes property without first obtaining the Minister's clearance certificate is personally liable for the amounts unpaid, to the extent of the value of the property distributed. That liability is personal to the representative and does not shift to the beneficiaries merely because they received the property. Notice from creditors does not displace the Income Tax Act rule, and estate administration tax is owed by the estate rather than by the representative personally.
  3. The correct answer is A (She is not liable for assets distributed where she had no notice of the claim.): A personal representative who gives the notices that the Superior Court of Justice would have directed in an administration suit may distribute, having regard to the claims then known, and is not liable for assets distributed to a person of whose claim there was no notice at the time of distribution. The protection does not extinguish a creditor's right to follow the assets into the hands of the persons who received them. It also does not depend on the estate being insolvent.
  4. The correct answer is A (Within 180 calendar days after the estate certificate is issued.): The estate information return must be received by the Ministry of Finance within 180 calendar days after the estate certificate is issued, and it must be filed even if the value of the estate is zero. A return based on an estimated value does not change that deadline, because the estimate is used in the return and an amended return follows once the actual value is known. The other periods named are not the filing deadline for this return.
  5. The correct answer is C (The fixed amount governs, because the will has already set the compensation.): The statutory allowance applies only where the instrument creating the trust does not fix the compensation. Because the will sets a sum for the trustee's work, the court has no power under that provision to increase it. The case law percentages are a guideline for estates where the instrument is silent, and they do not override an amount fixed in the will.
  6. The correct answer is A ($150,000, the amount prescribed by regulation under the Estates Act.): A small estate is an estate that does not exceed the amount prescribed by regulation under the Estates Act, and the prescribed amount is $150,000. Rule 74.1 then provides a simplified application for a small estate certificate. The $50,000 figure is the estate administration tax exemption, and the $100,000 figure relates to the sufficiency of one personal surety on a bond.
  7. The correct answer is D (No, because at least 30 days must pass after the documents are sent.): An application for a small estate certificate may not be filed until at least 30 days after the applicant has sent the application and the will to every person entitled to share in the distribution. The rule applies even though the estate is within the small estate limit. The waiting period is 30 days rather than 60, and the sending of the documents is the event that starts it.
  8. The correct answer is C (Apply for a certificate of appointment of estate trustee under Rule 74.): Where additional assets come to light and the estate is still within the small estate limit, the trustee applies for an amended small estate certificate. If the additional assets push the estate above that limit, the trustee applies for a certificate of appointment of estate trustee under Rule 74 instead, and the small estate certificate is deemed revoked when the new certificate is issued. A certificate of estate trustee during litigation is used where an administrator is needed while litigation is pending.
  9. The correct answer is C (Double the amount at which the property of the deceased was sworn.): The bond required on a grant of administration is in a penalty of double the amount at which the property of the deceased has been sworn, and the judge may direct that more than one bond be given so as to limit the liability of any surety. The judge may reduce the amount or dispense with the bond in special circumstances, but the starting amount is twice the sworn value. The other amounts are not the statutory penalty.
  10. The correct answer is A (One personal surety is sufficient where the assets do not exceed $100,000.): Unless the court orders otherwise, one personal surety is sufficient where the value of the assets of the estate does not exceed $100,000. A personal surety must be a resident of Ontario who is not a minor, and neither a registrar of the court nor a lawyer may act as a personal surety. The $50,000 figure belongs to the estate administration tax exemption rather than to the bond rule.
  11. The correct answer is A (The court may appoint the person it thinks fit where special circumstances make that necessary.): Where it appears necessary or convenient because of the insolvency of the estate or other special circumstances, the court is not obliged to grant administration to the person who would otherwise be entitled, and may appoint such person as it thinks fit. A trust corporation may also be appointed, either alone or jointly with another person. The consent of the person passed over is not required, and the court is not bound to follow the order of entitlement.
  12. The correct answer is C (His rights wholly cease, as if he had not been appointed.): Where a person renounces probate of a will in which he is appointed executor, his rights in respect of the executorship wholly cease. The representation to the testator and the administration of the property then devolve as if he had not been appointed executor. The renunciation is not a suspension, and the right does not pass to the renouncing executor's own estate.
  13. The correct answer is C (An affidavit of execution sworn by one of the witnesses to the will.): An application for a certificate of appointment of estate trustee with a will must be accompanied by the original will, with any codicils, and by an affidavit of execution of the will. The statement of value is also required, but it proves the value of the estate rather than the execution of the will. A certified copy without an affidavit of execution does not meet the requirement.
  14. The correct answer is C (The request has expired, because it lapses three years after filing.): A request for notice may be filed with the registrar before a certificate is issued, and it expires three years after it is filed. Filing it does not require any further step by the beneficiary during that period. The request is not renewed by the registrar, and it does not run until the estate is administered.
  15. The correct answer is A (By a renunciation in Form 74G filed with the application.): A person named in a will as estate trustee who has not joined in the application must give a renunciation in Form 74G. A notice of objection is a different document, filed by a person with a financial interest who opposes the issuance of a certificate. A letter from the applicant or an affidavit of execution does not take the place of the renunciation.
  16. The correct answer is D (Every person entitled to share in the distribution, including the charities.): The applicant must serve the application for a certificate of appointment of estate trustee, with its attachments, on every person entitled to share in the distribution of the estate, including charities and contingent beneficiaries. Serving only the residuary beneficiaries would omit the charity and the contingent beneficiaries. The obligation does not depend on a beneficiary asking for the will.
  17. The correct answer is C (Serve and file a notice of appearance within 20 days of service.): If the objector does not serve and file a notice of appearance within 20 days after service of the notice to objector, the application proceeds as if no notice of objection had been filed. It is the applicant who must move for directions, and that step is due within 30 days after service of the notice of appearance. Filing the notice of objection alone does not preserve the objection through the 20 day period.
  18. The correct answer is D (That the certificate was issued in error or as a result of fraud.): The court may revoke a certificate on the application of a person appearing to have a financial interest where the certificate was issued in error or as a result of fraud on the court, where the appointment is no longer effective, or where the certificate should be revoked for any other reason. Disagreement with the will is not a ground to revoke the appointment. An understated value is dealt with through the estate administration tax rules rather than as a ground of revocation in itself.
  19. The correct answer is A (Five years from the date the Registrar accepts the name for registration.): A business name registration under the Business Names Act is effective for five years from the date the Registrar accepts it. The one, three and ten year periods do not reflect the statutory term, and the clock runs from acceptance rather than from first use or from payment. A registrant may renew before the registration expires, which keeps the name in good standing without a gap.
Case busine_00

Fernbrook Holdings Inc. is an Ontario corporation with three shareholders and three directors. Marisol owns 51 per cent, Devon owns 29 per cent and Priya owns 20 per cent. Marisol is the president and manages the business day to day. In June, Marisol arranged for Fernbrook to buy marketing services from a company she controls, at prices well above the market rate. The board never reviewed the arrangement, and Marisol did not disclose her interest to Devon or Priya. Devon asked Marisol to have Fernbrook sue her company for the overpayment, and she refused. Fernbrook has also not held an annual meeting of shareholders since 2024. Devon wants to recover the overpayment for Fernbrook. Priya sold her shares six months ago.

  1. The correct answer is D (Give the directors at least 14 days' notice of the intention to apply.): A derivative action requires the complainant to give the directors of the corporation or its subsidiary at least 14 days' notice of the intention to apply for leave. Consent from a majority shareholder, a prefiled claim and a reference to the auditor are not statutory preconditions. The court must also be satisfied that the directors will not diligently prosecute the action, that the complainant acts in good faith and that the action is in the corporation's interests.
  2. The correct answer is B (The loss belonged to the corporation, so the claim belongs in its name.): Where the wrong reduces the corporation's assets, the loss is suffered by the corporation, and the remedy is an action brought in its name with leave. A shareholder cannot recover personally for a loss that is merely the reflected fall in share value. Holding a minority stake and the majority shareholder's control over spending do not convert a corporate loss into a personal one.
  3. The correct answer is D (Yes, because a former registered holder or beneficial owner is a complainant.): The definition of complainant includes a former registered holder or beneficial owner of a security of the corporation, so selling the shares does not end the right to apply. Current ownership is not required, and there is no need to hold a security of an affiliate. Consent from the current holders is not a condition of the application.
  4. The correct answer is A (An application for a compliance order directing the corporation to hold the meeting.): Where a corporation fails to comply with the Act, a complainant may apply for an order directing compliance or restraining a breach. A missed annual meeting is a compliance matter, and it does not require leave as a derivative action. An oppression order and a dissent remedy address different complaints and are not the route for enforcing the meeting obligation.
  5. The correct answer is C (Yes, because a partner's act in the usual way of business binds the firm.): Every partner is an agent of the firm and of the other partners for the purpose of the partnership business, and an act done in the usual way of business binds the firm unless the third party knows that the partner has no authority. An internal approval limit does not bind a supplier who is unaware of it, and the firm does not need to ratify the act. A supplier is not required to ask for proof of internal approval.
  6. The correct answer is D (The partnership is not a separate legal person from the partners.): A partnership is the relation between persons carrying on business in common with a view to profit, and it is not a separate legal person distinct from its partners. Registering the firm name and putting the agreement in writing do not change that status. The bankruptcy of a partner is not the test, and the partners carry the obligations of the firm personally, subject to the liability rules in the Act.
  7. The correct answer is A (Within 60 days after the expiry date, on payment of a late fee.): The Business Names Act permits a registrant to renew within 60 days after expiry on payment of the late renewal fee. The 15, 30 and 90 day periods do not appear in the provision, and no late renewal is free of the additional fee. A renewal filed inside the 60 day window preserves the registration without a new filing.
  8. The correct answer is A (The supervising partner is liable; the other partners are shielded by the partnership's status.): A partner in a limited liability partnership is not liable for a partnership obligation arising from another partner's or an employee's wrongful act, but the protection does not cover the partner's own negligence or the negligence of a person under the partner's direct supervision. The other partners are therefore not jointly and severally liable, and the firm is not the only party that is liable. Liability is not shared in equal portions among all the partners.
  9. The correct answer is A (A limited partner may contribute money or other property, but not services.): A limited partner may contribute money and other property to the limited partnership, but services are not a permitted contribution. The written agreement of the general partner, a minimum capital value and the consent of the Registrar do not create an exception. The client would have to contribute money or property instead of his work.
  10. The correct answer is D (All the general partners must sign the declaration before it is filed.): A limited partnership is formed when a declaration is accepted for filing, and the declaration must be signed by all of the general partners. Limited partners are not required to sign, and one general partner cannot sign for the others on the strength of an authority letter. The signing requirement follows the role of general partner rather than the contribution of capital.
  11. The correct answer is C (He may be liable as a general partner for taking part in control.): A limited partner is not liable as a general partner unless, in addition to exercising the rights and powers of a limited partner, he takes part in the control of the business. Paying the full contribution does not preserve the limit on liability, and the exposure is not confined to debts that the limited partner personally approved. The agreement of the general partner does not remove the statutory consequence of taking control.
  12. The correct answer is C (Five years from the date it is accepted for filing, unless cancelled or renewed.): Every declaration filed under the Act expires five years after the date it is accepted for filing, unless it is cancelled by a declaration of dissolution or replaced by a renewal filed before expiry. Expiry does not dissolve the partnership, but an additional fee is payable for a late renewal. The notice of the Registrar and a ten year term are not what the Act provides.
  13. The correct answer is C (The endorsement of the articles is the certificate of incorporation.): The endorsement of the articles by the Director constitutes the certificate of incorporation, which is conclusive proof that the corporation was incorporated on the date shown, except in a proceeding to cancel the certificate for cause. The endorsement is not a temporary receipt, a name approval or a business licence. The corporation is also assigned a corporation number that is specified in the certificate.
  14. The correct answer is D (The article prevails, so the higher approval threshold must be met.): Where the articles or a unanimous shareholder agreement require a greater number of votes of directors or shareholders than the Act requires, the articles or the agreement prevail, subject to the limit on removing directors. A provision that raises a voting threshold is not invalid merely because the Act would permit a lower one. The shareholders do not have to re-approve the provision, and the directors cannot rely on the Act to avoid it.
  15. The correct answer is D (The name must include Limited, Incorporated or Corporation, or an abbreviation.): Every corporation must have the word Limited, Limitee, Incorporated, Incorporee or Corporation, or the abbreviation Ltd., Ltee, Inc. or Corp., as part of its name. The corporation may be legally designated by either the full form or the abbreviation. The corporation number, the words Ontario Corporation and the registered office address are not required parts of the name.
  16. The correct answer is C (By a directors' resolution in writing signed by the sole director.): After incorporation, the directors may deal with the organizational matters listed in the Act at a meeting or by a resolution in writing under the written resolution provision. Appointing officers, authorizing the issue of shares and making banking arrangements are all within that list. A shareholders' meeting, a filed by-law and a court order are not the mechanism the Act provides.
  17. The correct answer is A (No, it is not effective until the shareholders confirm it.): A by-law made by the directors is effective from the date of the directors' resolution until it is confirmed, rejected or amended by the shareholders. Where the shareholders reject a by-law, a later by-law with substantially the same purpose or effect is not effective until the shareholders confirm it. The rejection of the earlier by-law therefore carries forward, and the March by-law ceases to be effective when it is rejected.
  18. The correct answer is D (A corporation is not required to have a corporate seal.): The Act states that a corporation is not required to have a corporate seal. Nothing in the Act makes a seal a condition of incorporation, a required by-law or a condition of issuing share certificates. A corporation that chooses to have a seal may adopt one, but the choice is not mandatory.
  19. The correct answer is B (Within 60 days after the date of incorporation of the corporation.): The Corporations Information Act requires a corporation to file an initial return within 60 days after the date of incorporation, amalgamation or continuation. The 15 day period applies to a notice of change, not to the initial return. The first annual meeting and the fiscal year end are not the triggers for the initial return, and the six month period is the published deadline for the annual return.
  20. The correct answer is D (Within six months after the fiscal year end, that is by 30 September.): The government's published rule is that a corporation files its annual return through the Ontario Business Registry within six months of its fiscal year end, which in this case is 30 September. The 60 day period applies to an initial return, and the 15 month period applies to calling the annual meeting of shareholders. Filing in December would be late for a fiscal year ending 31 March.
  21. The correct answer is B (The target keeps its own contracts, licences and employees, since its legal identity does not change.): A share purchase changes only the ownership of the shares, so the corporation continues as the same legal person and keeps its contracts, licences and employees. That is why the purchaser acquires the corporation together with its liabilities and must rely on due diligence and negotiated protection. An asset purchase is different, because there the buyer takes only the assets it buys, and each contract, licence and employment relationship must be assigned or novated. Consent requirements therefore matter far more in an asset transaction.
Case commer_00

A buyer has signed a letter of intent to purchase all of the issued shares of a corporation that operates three garden centres in Ontario. The price is $4 million, with $3.6 million payable at closing and a $400,000 holdback. During due diligence the buyer's lawyer learns that the Canada Revenue Agency has reassessed the corporation for an unpaid HST balance of $180,000 for a year before closing, and the seller's appeal of that reassessment is outstanding. The seller operates through a holding company that has no other assets, and the seller refuses to reduce the price. The transaction is scheduled to close in three weeks.

  1. The correct answer is A (Obtain a specific indemnity and fund a holdback or escrow out of the purchase price.): A share purchase leaves the corporation owing its own tax debts, so the buyer needs a contractual promise that the seller will cover the reassessed amount. An indemnity secured by a holdback or escrow gives the buyer money to draw on if the seller refuses to pay. A resignation does not reduce or transfer the corporation's tax liability. A financing statement records a security interest in personal property and does nothing to settle a tax assessment, and the tax authority will not withdraw a valid reassessment on request.
  2. The correct answer is A (The indemnity has little value, so the buyer should require security or restructure.): An indemnity is worth only as much as the covenantor's ability to pay, so a promise from a holding company with no assets does not provide real protection. The buyer should require security for the obligation, a reduction in the price, or a structure such as an asset purchase that leaves the tax liability behind. An indemnity is not a trust and does not need registration on title. It also does not shift the reassessment to the seller personally, because the corporation remains the taxpayer.
  3. The correct answer is C (The liability stays with the corporation and lowers the value of the shares.): The corporation is the taxpayer and it keeps its legal identity after a share purchase, so the reassessment remains its own debt. The price paid for the shares reflects that burden, because the buyer acquires the corporation together with its liabilities. The liabilities are not extinguished by the transfer of shares, and they do not become the personal debts of the buyer or of the seller simply because the share transfer was signed.
  4. The correct answer is A (A certificate of status issued through the Ontario Business Registry.): The certificate of status is the registry document that confirms the corporation is in good standing and has not been dissolved. A profile report gives current filing information such as the directors and the registered office, but it is not the standing certificate. A writ of execution search shows enforcement proceedings against a debtor and says nothing about corporate status. A federal clearance certificate under the Income Tax Act serves a different purpose and does not prove corporate standing in Ontario.
  5. The correct answer is C (Authorization by the shareholders, and the transaction is a dissent trigger for them.): Section 184(3) of the Business Corporations Act requires shareholder authorization for a sale, lease or exchange of all or substantially all the property of a corporation, and section 185(1)(e) makes that sale a dissent trigger. A lender's consent may be required by the loan documents, but it does not replace the shareholder authorization. The Director does not approve asset sales. The directors' general power to dispose of corporate property does not extend to the all or substantially all case without the required shareholder authorization.
  6. The correct answer is D (Her employment is continuous, and the purchaser is bound by the seller's agreement.): Section 9 of the Employment Standards Act, 2000 provides that where an employer sells, leases or transfers a business or part of a business, the purchaser is bound by the seller's collective agreement and the employees' employment is deemed continuous for the purposes of the notice, severance and related entitlements. Service therefore carries over, and the result does not depend on a new written contract. The continuity is not limited to wages. A share purchase reaches a similar practical result for a different reason, because the corporation remains the employer.
  7. The correct answer is D (Withhold and remit Canada Pension Plan contributions and employment insurance premiums from the first pay.): An asset purchase makes the purchaser a new employer of the staff it hires, so the ordinary withholding and remittance duties apply from the first pay under the Canada Pension Plan and the Employment Insurance Act. The seller's payroll account belongs to the seller and does not carry over to the purchaser. Treating the staff as independent contractors would mischaracterize the relationship and would not remove the employer's obligations. The statutes do not require a successor employer ruling before the purchaser pays wages and withholds.
  8. The correct answer is A (The share route preserves the arrangements but keeps the liabilities, so negotiate protection.): A share purchase keeps the corporation, and therefore its contracts, licences and employees, but the buyer also acquires the corporation with its liabilities, so due diligence, warranties, indemnities and holdbacks supply the protection. An asset purchase lets the buyer choose what it takes, but contracts, licences and employment relationships do not transfer on their own and each needs assignment, novation or consent. Unused income tax losses ordinarily remain with the corporation and do not move to a buyer of assets. The share route does not end the employment relationships, because the corporation remains the employer.
  9. The correct answer is A (The court reads the words of the document and the parties' intention to be bound.): Ontario has no statute that makes a letter of intent binding, so the court reads the document and decides what the parties intended. A letter that says it is non-binding, that it is subject to a definitive agreement, and that only specified items such as confidentiality bind will not create an obligation to complete the transaction. A letter that contains all essential terms and shows an intention to be bound can create one. Signature alone does not decide the question, and registration has nothing to do with it.
  10. The correct answer is B (State that the letter is non-binding and is subject to a definitive agreement.): A clear non-binding clause, coupled with a condition that a definitive agreement must be signed, tells a court that the parties did not intend to be bound. It is good practice to name the few provisions that do bind, such as confidentiality and the payment of each party's own expenses. Silence about binding effect invites a dispute, because the court will then read the whole document. A clause that recites all essential terms and an intention to be bound points the other way and may create an enforceable agreement.
  11. The correct answer is D (It may be enforceable, since it records all essential terms and an intention to be bound.): The parties' intention controls, and a document that records all essential terms and states an intention to be bound can amount to a binding contract even if it is headed as a letter of intent. The absence of a condition requiring a definitive agreement removes an argument that the parties intended to wait. A letter of intent is not automatically ineffective, so that statement is too broad. Court approval and a formal agreement are not required where the writing itself contains the terms and the intention to be bound.
  12. The correct answer is D (The statement is a warranty, and the promise to pay compensation is an indemnity.): A representation or warranty is a statement of fact that the seller asserts is true, and a breach gives the buyer a contractual claim. An indemnity is a separate promise to reimburse a specified loss, often without the buyer having to prove a breach of contract in the ordinary way. Calling the compensation promise a warranty confuses the two devices. The two provisions operate together in a purchase agreement, and neither is a condition or a representation in the sense used here.
  13.
