The correct answer is A (Obtain a signed officer's certificate that confirms the representations at closing.): A bring-down or officer's certificate is the usual closing document that confirms the representations and warranties are still accurate as of the closing date. It gives the buyer evidence of the state of affairs at the moment it pays the purchase price. Old search results speak only to an earlier date and may miss registrations made in the meantime. A lender's letter addresses the lender's own position, and a registered transfer document does not carry the transaction's representations.
  14. The correct answer is A (The buyer may claim under the indemnity, because the claim falls inside the survival period.): The contract sets the rules, so a claim brought during the stated survival period can be made under the indemnity that the parties named as the exclusive remedy. The survival clause keeps the representations alive for the agreed time rather than ending them at closing. A claim is not postponed until the period expires, because the buyer must bring it within the period. The exclusive remedy clause limits the buyer's ability to pursue a separate claim outside the agreement for the same breach.
  15. The correct answer is A ($8 for the profile report and $26 for the certificate of status.): The Ontario Business Registry publishes a fee of $8 for a profile report and $26 for a certificate of status. The profile report gives the corporation's current filing information, and the certificate confirms the corporation's standing. The two fees are not the same, and the higher fee attaches to the certificate of status rather than to the profile report. There is no combined $10 charge, and neither document costs $50, so the Ministry fees should be checked again before a later closing.
  16. The correct answer is C (A search of the Personal Property Security Act registry against the seller's legal name.): Security interests in personal property such as inventory and equipment are registered in the Personal Property Security Act registry, and the search is run against the debtor's exact legal name. Land registry searches cover interests in real property, not the goods themselves. A corporate profile search shows the corporation's filing information, and a bankruptcy records search addresses insolvency rather than the existence of a security interest. The asset purchase agreement should also require the seller to obtain releases or waivers from any secured party that the search discloses.
  17. The correct answer is C (The search speaks only as of its date, and the lender registered afterwards.): A registry search reveals only the financing statements on file at the moment the search is run, so a lender that registers after that date will not appear in the earlier result. The purchaser should bring the search down to the closing date or as close to it as the registry allows, and should negotiate contractual protection for anything registered in the gap. Paying value without notice is not a general answer. A later registration is not invalid merely because it postdates the search, and a security interest does not end because the goods are sold.
  18. The correct answer is C (Judgments and enforcement proceedings that could be executed against it.): A writ of execution search shows judgments and similar enforcement registrations that creditors have filed and may enforce against the corporation's property. It complements rather than replaces the corporate profile search and the Personal Property Security Act search. Tax returns and assessments come from the corporation's own records and from the tax authority. The names of secured lenders appear in the Personal Property Security Act registry, and the directors and registered office appear in the corporate filing records.
  19. The correct answer is C (Discharge all responsibilities to clients, tribunals and the public with integrity.): Rule 2.1-1 requires a solicitor to carry on the practice of law and to discharge all responsibilities to clients, tribunals, the public and other members of the profession honourably and with integrity, and rule 2.1-2 requires the solicitor to uphold the standards and reputation of the profession. Maximising fees, following instructions that conflict with professional duties, and quietly settling complaints all fall short of that standard. The duty runs to the profession and to the public, and not only to the paying client.
Case profes_00

A solicitor is asked to act for two brothers, Amir and Bashir, on the incorporation of a holding company and the preparation of a shareholders agreement in which each will hold half the shares. The solicitor has acted for Amir for six years on his dry cleaning businesses and has never acted for Bashir. At the first meeting she explains that she has been asked to act for both of them, that she cannot keep information from one brother confidential as against the other, and that she may have to withdraw from acting for both if a conflict develops that cannot be resolved. Both brothers sign a consent. Eight months later the brothers disagree about a cash call, and the disagreement becomes a formal dispute.

  1. The correct answer is A (That neither brother's information can be kept confidential from the other.): Rule 3.4-5 requires the solicitor to tell each joint client that she has been asked to act for both of them, that no information she receives from one of them can be treated as confidential as against the other, and that she may have to withdraw completely if an unresolvable conflict develops. The fee split, the amendment rules for the agreement, and any reporting duty to the Law Society are not part of that advice. The confidentiality point is the one that joint clients most often misunderstand, because each assumes the other's information stays separate.
  2. The correct answer is B (Decline to advise either brother and refer them to other lawyers.): Once a contentious issue arises between clients who consented to a joint retainer, rule 3.4-8 forbids the solicitor from advising either of them on that issue. She must refer them to other lawyers or, where no legal advice is needed and both clients are sophisticated, tell them that they may settle the issue by direct negotiation in which she takes no part. If the issue is not resolved she must withdraw from the joint representation. Staying on for both brothers or for one of them would breach the rule.
  3. The correct answer is D (Rule 3.4-9, and she must refer the other brother to a different lawyer.): Rule 3.4-9 provides that where clients consent to a joint retainer and also agree in advance that the solicitor may continue to advise one of them if a contentious issue arises, the solicitor may advise that client about the contentious matter and must refer the others to another lawyer. Rules 3.4-6 and 3.4-11 deal with continuing relationships and with firm screening, not with this situation. Rule 3.4-3 forbids acting for opposing parties in a dispute rather than permitting it, so consent does not assist under that rule.
  4. The correct answer is B (Advise him of the ongoing relationship and recommend independent advice.): Under rule 3.4-6, where the solicitor acts regularly for one client and is asked to accept joint employment with another client, she must tell the other client about the continuing relationship and recommend that the client obtain independent legal advice about the joint retainer. Rule 3.4-7 then requires her to obtain the clients' consent once that advice has been given. Refusing the retainer, notifying the Law Society, and demanding consent before any disclosure are not the steps the rule requires.
  5. The correct answer is A (Decline to act for either of them, because consent cannot cure it.): Rule 3.4-3 is an absolute prohibition: despite the consent rule in 3.4-2, a solicitor must not represent opposing parties in a dispute. Consent, priority of retainer, and separate paper or electronic files do not change that result. The solicitor must decline to act for either party in the dispute, and may need to help both of them find new counsel quickly.
  6. The correct answer is D (Rule 3.1-2, which requires the standard of a competent lawyer.): Rule 3.1-2 provides that a solicitor shall perform any legal services undertaken on a client's behalf to the standard of a competent lawyer. Rule 3.1-1 defines what a competent lawyer is, but it is rule 3.1-2 that imposes that standard on the retainer. Courteous service, candid advice, and reasonable fees are separate obligations, and none of them measures the competence of the work actually performed.
  7. The correct answer is D (Decline the retainer and explain that the work needs a competent lawyer.): A competent lawyer must recognise the limits of their own ability, and rule 3.1-2 requires the solicitor to perform legal services to the standard of a competent lawyer. Where the solicitor cannot reach that standard in the time available, and no colleague in the firm can supply the missing knowledge, the retainer must be declined. Relying on the client, carving out the difficult parts, or warning in writing that the advice may be wrong does not meet the standard the client is paying for.
  8. The correct answer is B (Advise the client candidly that the assertion cannot be supported.): Rule 3.2-2 requires a solicitor to be honest and candid when advising clients, and rule 3.2-7 forbids knowingly assisting in or encouraging dishonesty. Sending a demand that the solicitor knows misstates the position would breach both rules. A file note or a client indemnity does not cure the problem, because the defect is the solicitor's own participation in a false assertion. The solicitor should tell the client plainly what the file shows and discuss the real position.
  9. The correct answer is A (Keep the information in strict confidence, since the duty continues.): Rule 3.3-1 requires a solicitor at all times to hold in strict confidence all information about the client's business and affairs acquired in the course of the professional relationship. The words at all times mean that the duty is not limited to the life of the retainer and continues after it ends. The solicitor may not sell or give the information to a reporter, and the fact that some of it is already public does not release the rest of what was learned on the file.
  10. The correct answer is C (Disclose the required documents and withhold the unrelated material.): Rule 3.3-1.1 provides that where the solicitor is required by law or by an order of a tribunal of competent jurisdiction to disclose confidential information, the solicitor must disclose it but must not disclose more than is required. The solicitor therefore produces the documents the order actually covers and does not volunteer the unrelated personal material. Producing the entire file would go beyond the order, and refusing to produce anything the order covers would not comply with it at all.
  11. The correct answer is D (Disclose what is needed to prevent the harm, but nothing more.): Rule 3.3-3 permits a solicitor to disclose confidential information where the solicitor believes on reasonable grounds that there is an imminent risk of death or serious bodily harm and disclosure is necessary to prevent that harm, but the solicitor must not disclose more than is required. The solicitor may therefore warn the authorities about the threatened fire. Silence is not required, releasing the whole file goes beyond what the rule allows, and the client's consent is not a condition of the permitted disclosure.
  12. The correct answer is A (Rule 3.3-5, and only as much as is necessary to collect the fees.): Rule 3.3-5 allows a solicitor to disclose confidential information in order to establish or collect the lawyer's fees, but not more information than is required. Rule 3.3-4 covers defending against allegations that the lawyer committed an offence, is civilly liable, was negligent, or engaged in professional misconduct, rule 3.3-6 covers obtaining ethics advice from another lawyer, and rule 3.3-7 covers conflicts arising from a change of firm. Those three rules address different situations, and none of them authorises disclosure on a fee action.
Case profes_01

A solicitor is acting for a purchaser on a residential resale. The agreement price is $980,000, well above the value of comparable homes on the same street. The seller is a numbered company incorporated eight months ago, and the registered owner on title is a different individual who is said to be travelling overseas. The seller's agent presses the solicitor to close in four days and says the deposit must be paid in cash to a person named only in an email. The purchaser has never met the seller and does not know the registered owner. The solicitor's title search shows two transfers of the property in the past year, each at a higher price.

  1. The correct answer is B (Refuse to do anything that would facilitate the suspected fraud.): Rule 3.2-7 forbids a solicitor to knowingly assist in or encourage dishonesty, fraud, crime or illegal conduct, or to do or omit anything that the solicitor ought to know assists or facilitates such conduct by a client or any other person. Once the solicitor suspects title or mortgage fraud, the purchaser's instructions alone cannot justify carrying the transaction forward. Closing and leaving the question to the lender, or closing with only a note on the file, would still amount to facilitating the conduct.
  2. The correct answer is B (Immediately cease and, if unable to comply, withdraw from acting.): By-Law 7.1, sections 24 and 27 provide that where a licensee knows or ought to know that they are or would be assisting a client in fraud or other illegal conduct, the licensee must immediately cease and must not further engage in any activity that would assist the client, and must withdraw from providing professional services if unable to comply. A written risk acknowledgment from the purchaser does not permit the solicitor to proceed. Neither a police report nor holding the deposit in trust cures the problem, because the transaction itself is the facilitating step.
  3. The correct answer is C (Periodically monitor the relationship for signs of fraud or illegality.): By-Law 7.1, section 23.1 requires the licensee to monitor the professional business relationship periodically to determine whether the client's activities, the source of funds and the client's instructions are consistent with the purpose of the retainer and with the information already obtained, and to assess whether there is a risk of assisting in fraud or other illegal conduct. The licensee must also keep a record of the measures taken and the date. Re-verifying identity at the end of the retainer, reporting the retainer itself, and obtaining consent to further funds are not that duty.
  4. The correct answer is A (Report them to the purchaser and to the title insurer.): The Law Society's due diligence guidance states that the lawyer should keep notes of the title search on every real estate file and should report the results of the search and the due diligence process, including suspicious patterns of transfers or discharges and suspicious changes in value, to the purchaser or borrower if acting for them, to the lender if acting for the lender, and to the title insurer. Reporting to the Law Society is not what the guidance requires here. A file note alone is not enough, and the duty does not wait for the deal to fail.
  5. The correct answer is C (Decline the retainer because the solicitor's own interest conflicts with it.): A solicitor must not act where the solicitor's own interest conflicts with the client's interest, which is the duty of loyalty expressed in rule 3.4-1 and in the case law that section 3.4 codifies. A personal stake in the purchaser means the solicitor cannot give the vendor undivided loyalty, and disclosure of the interest does not remove the conflict. An appraisal and a separate lawyer for the buyer do not cure the solicitor's own adverse interest on the other side of the deal.
  6. The correct answer is A (Whether acting for both parties creates a conflict the rules forbid.): Rule 3.4-1 forbids a solicitor to act or continue to act for a client where there is a conflict of interest, except as section 3.4 permits. Because the two lawyers practise in the same firm, the landlord's matter and the tenant's matter are treated as the firm's matters, so the firm must examine whether it may act at all. Billing arrangements, the length of the tenant's consultation, and the identity of the client who retained the firm are not the question the rule poses.
  7. The correct answer is C (A designated trust account at a listed bank, credit union or trust corporation.): Money received in trust for a client must go into a designated trust account at an eligible financial institution, such as a listed bank, a credit union, or a registered trust corporation, and the account must be kept in the name of the licensee or the firm. Placing the deposit in the general operating account mixes client money with the lawyer's own funds, which the accounting rules forbid regardless of how soon the fee will be earned. A personal savings account or an account held jointly with the client does not meet the required designation and institution standard. Only a properly designated trust account at an eligible institution satisfies the obligation to safeguard client money.
  8. The correct answer is C (The payment is trust money and must be deposited into the firm's trust account.): Money paid to a lawyer before services are performed and before a bill is delivered belongs to the client and must be treated as trust money. A signed retainer letter does not change the character of the funds, since the lawyer has not yet earned any portion of the payment. Splitting the deposit between the trust account and the operating account before any work is billed would place unearned money in a general account where it does not belong. Only depositing the full amount into the trust account keeps the unearned funds properly segregated until the firm bills for the work performed.
  9. The correct answer is D (Deposit the whole cheque into trust, then transfer the earned fee portion to the general account.): Because the cheque mixes money already earned with money that is still trust money for the unclosed purchase, the safest and correct handling is to deposit the entire amount into the trust account first. Once the deposit clears, the earned fee portion can then be transferred out of trust to the general account through a proper billing entry. Depositing the combined cheque straight into the operating account would place client trust money for the unclosed purchase where it does not belong. Refusing the cheque or leaving part of it uncashed is unnecessary when the ledger can simply record and then transfer the earned portion correctly.
  10. The correct answer is A (Written permission from the Law Society allowing that specific withdrawal.): The by-laws list a limited set of purposes for which money may be withdrawn from a trust account, such as payment to the client, payment of properly billed fees and disbursements, and money paid in by mistake. A withdrawal for any other purpose is only permitted if the licensee first obtains written permission from the Law Society for that specific withdrawal. Verbal confirmation from a staff member and a one time general exemption do not satisfy the written permission requirement. Notifying the client is a separate obligation and does not itself authorize a withdrawal that falls outside the listed categories.
  11. The correct answer is A (The lawyer must obtain the Law Society's written permission for that withdrawal.): Because the proposed disbursement falls outside the categories the by-laws permit, the only route to a lawful withdrawal is written permission from the Law Society for that specific transaction. A client's verbal agreement over the telephone does not substitute for the Law Society's written authorization, since the requirement protects the integrity of trust money generally, not only the individual client's wishes. Internal documentation by the bookkeeper explains the firm's reasoning but does not satisfy the by-law requirement for outside written permission. There is no fixed waiting period that on its own converts an otherwise impermissible withdrawal into a lawful one.
  12. The correct answer is C (The licensee must maintain sufficient trust balances to meet every client obligation at all times.): Despite any other provision in the trust accounting rules, a licensee must at all times maintain sufficient balances on deposit in the trust accounts to meet every obligation with respect to money held in trust for clients. An unresolved fee dispute with one client does not excuse a shortfall affecting the trust money held for other clients on other files. Using a shortfall as an informal offset against a disputed invoice would misapply trust funds that belong to clients generally. The obligation to keep trust fully funded exists independently of when an assessment officer eventually resolves the separate billing dispute.
  13. The correct answer is D (An account that holds trust money for more than one client at the same time.): A mixed trust account is a trust account that holds money in trust for more than one client at the same time, as distinct from a separate interest bearing account maintained for a single client. It is not a blending of the firm's own operating funds with client money, since that kind of commingling is prohibited regardless of the account's label. It has nothing to do with combining deposits from multiple law firms or with mixing currencies for administrative convenience. The distinction matters because a mixed trust account carries its own interest handling rules that do not apply to a single client account.
  14. The correct answer is C (A direction to pay any interest the account earns directly to The Law Foundation of Ontario.): When opening a mixed trust account, the licensee must give the financial institution a written direction to pay any interest earned on the account directly to The Law Foundation of Ontario, rather than to the firm or to individual clients. Crediting interest proportionately to each client's ledger applies to a different structure, a separate interest bearing account maintained for one client rather than a shared mixed account. Holding interest in a suspense account pending reconciliation does not satisfy the specific written direction the by-laws require. Paying the interest to the firm as compensation would convert money that belongs to the Foundation into the firm's own funds.
  15. The correct answer is C (The interest belongs to The Law Foundation of Ontario, not to the client.): Interest earned on a mixed trust account is directed to The Law Foundation of Ontario under the Law Society Act, not paid out to any of the individual clients whose money happened to be in the account at the time. A client only becomes entitled to interest personally where the funds are instead held in a separate interest bearing account maintained for that one client, or where a written arrangement about the disposition of interest says otherwise. Sharing the mixed account's interest equally among the clients is not how the statute allocates it. A formal court application is unnecessary because the entitlement question is already settled by the governing statute.
  16. The correct answer is A (Within twenty five days after the last day of the month being reported.): The by-laws require the signed record comparing trust balances to unexpended client trust balances to be created within twenty five days after the last day of the month in respect of which it is being prepared. A thirty day period applies to a different requirement, the written authorization submitted when a new trust account is first opened, not to the monthly comparison. A fifteen day window is shorter than what the rule actually allows, and a sixty day window is far longer than the rule permits. Knowing the correct number matters because missing the true deadline can leave a firm out of compliance without the bookkeeper realizing it.
  17. The correct answer is B (By April 25, twenty five days after the last day of March.): Counting twenty five days from March 31, the last day of the reported month, brings the deadline to April 25, and the Law Society's own guidance uses exactly that example to illustrate the rule. Finishing the reconciliation on March 31 itself is not realistic, since the bank's own statement for the month is not yet available on the last day being reported. May 1 and April 15 are simply the wrong number of days from the end of March under the rule as written. Getting the count right avoids a firm mistakenly believing it has more time than the by-law actually gives it.
  18. The correct answer is B (Monthly reconciliation is required for every trust account type, even with no activity that month.): The Law Society's guidance is explicit that every firm trust account must be reconciled and compared every month, including a separate interest bearing account and an electronic registration bank account, even when no trust activity occurred during that month. Limiting reconciliation to months with deposits or withdrawals would leave dormant accounts unchecked for extended periods, which is exactly what the monthly requirement is meant to prevent. The size of the balance in a separate interest bearing account does not create an exception from the monthly obligation. An annual reconciliation cycle for an electronic registration account falls well short of what the guidance actually requires.
  19. The correct answer is A (All money received and disbursed for that client, and any unexpended balance.): A client's trust ledger must show separately, for that client, all money received in trust, all money disbursed from trust, and any unexpended balance remaining. Recording only a current total without the underlying history of receipts and disbursements would make it impossible to reconstruct how the balance was reached. A summary of billed fees is a general account matter and does not substitute for the trust ledger's own record of client money. Identification documents belong to a separate client identification file and have nothing to do with what the trust ledger itself must record.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Obtain and keep the client's written authorization before registering.): The Law Society's guidelines require the lawyer to obtain and keep the client's written authorization before registering electronic documents, and to retain the evidence that supports every compliance with law statement. The agreement of purchase and sale is a contract between the parties rather than the client's authorization to the lawyer to register. Registration cannot be treated as a step to be documented after the fact, and the lender's lawyer cannot authorize a registration on the buyer's behalf. Only a lawyer may sign for completeness a document that requires law statements.
  2. The correct answer is B (A statement of the consideration and the interest transferred.): The electronic registration regulation lists what a transfer must contain, including a statement of the consideration, a statement of the interest transferred, a statement that the transferor transfers the land, a statement of age and a statement of spousal status. These are the statements that lawyers call compliance with law statements. There is no required statement that the land is free of encumbrances, both because most land carries some charge and because the parcel register shows the charges. Financing terms and a tax clearance statement are not part of the prescribed list of statements.
  3. The correct answer is C (No, because a single lawyer may act for both sides only in limited situations.): A single lawyer is permitted to represent both the transferor and the transferee of title only in the limited situations set out in the Law Society's rules, such as a transfer between related persons or a remote location where no other lawyer could reasonably be retained. A signed agreement of purchase and sale is not itself an exception, and the clients' consent does not remove the prohibition. Two lawyers at the same firm may act for the two sides if they comply with the joint retainer rules and no conflict exists. The question is not whether the clients wish to save fees but whether the transaction falls within a listed exception.
  4. The correct answer is B (A compliance with law statement that the transfer is from an estate trustee.): The Law Society's list of permitted exceptions includes a transfer from an estate trustee to a person beneficially entitled, and for that case the lawyer registers a compliance with law statement describing the exception. The electronic registration regulation contains a matching solicitor statement for an estate transfer. An estate is not a corporation, so a certificate of status does not apply, and the beneficiaries' consent or a court order is not the registration requirement for this exception. The lawyer still must be alert to any conflict between the estate and the beneficiary.
  5. The correct answer is C (Information from one client cannot be kept confidential from the other.): Before accepting a permitted joint retainer, the lawyer must tell each client that the lawyer has been asked to act for both of them, that no information from one can be treated as confidential from the other, and that if a conflict develops that cannot be resolved the lawyer may have to withdraw from acting for both. The lawyer must also recommend independent legal advice and obtain each client's informed consent. Fee sharing and the lawyer's freedom to prefer one client are not part of the requirement, and a disagreement does not automatically end the retainer without the lawyer assessing the conflict.
  6. The correct answer is D (Review the pattern, investigate, and report it to the client and lender.): The Law Society's anti fraud guidance requires the lawyer to review the pattern of transfers and discharges on the parcel register, inquire about suspicious patterns, and check recent values for suspicious changes. The results must be reported to the client, to the lender if the lawyer acts for the lender, and to the title insurer where applicable. The buyer's lack of involvement does not remove the risk that the property or the parties are connected to fraud, and a police report does not replace the lawyer's own inquiry and reporting. Leaving the searches for the reporting letter is too late, because the transaction will already have closed.
  7. The correct answer is D (By the degree and purpose of the oven's attachment to the property.): Whether an item is a fixture or a chattel is decided by the degree and the purpose of its attachment: an item attached to the land for the better enjoyment of the land is part of the real property, while an item resting on the land for the better enjoyment of the item itself remains personal property. The built-in oven is screwed into the cabinetry and connected to the gas line, which points to a fixture. The price of the property, the listing of the item in the agreement and the age or value of the oven do not answer the legal question, although an express clause in the agreement would settle the parties' intentions. That is why the agreement should list included chattels and excluded fixtures.
  8. The correct answer is C (The first time purchaser refund, up to a maximum of $4,000.): A qualifying first time purchaser may claim a refund of all or part of the Ontario land transfer tax, and the maximum refund is $4,000 for a conveyance occurring on or after January 1, 2017. The purchaser must meet the eligibility conditions, including not having owned a home anywhere in the world and occupying the home as a principal residence within nine months after the conveyance. The application must be made within 18 months after the conveyance. The refund is capped rather than equal to the tax paid, and it is not limited to new homes.
  9. The correct answer is D (A power of attorney for property must be witnessed by two independent witnesses.): The red flags guidance notes that a titled spouse may try to sell a matrimonial home using a forged power of attorney to sign the non titled spouse's consent, and it points out that the power of attorney may not have been witnessed by two independent witnesses as the Substitute Decisions Act requires. The titled spouse is not independent, so the witnessing here does not meet that requirement, and the lawyer should inquire before relying on the document. There is no 30 day waiting period for a power of attorney, and a spouse is not prohibited from giving one. A neighbour can witness a legal document as long as the independence requirement is met.
  10. The correct answer is D (No more than $7,500 in cash may be received per client file.): By Law 9 sets a cash limit of $7,500 per client file, and a licensee must not receive cash above that amount in connection with a matter. If cash is received, the licensee must deposit it into a trust account promptly and must record the details of the cash. A client's waiver does not raise the limit, and holding the money in trust does not remove it. The limit is also not a general $10,000 transaction threshold.
  11. The correct answer is B (Within 25 days after the last day of the month.): By Law 9 requires the signed record comparing the total trust balances with the total of unexpended client balances, supported by a client listing and a bank reconciliation, to be created within 25 days after the last day of the month being reported. The March record is therefore due by April 25. The 30 day period in the same by-law governs something different, the filing of the authorization for a newly opened trust account, and it does not apply to the monthly reconciliation. Waiting for the next bank statement does not set the deadline.
  12. The correct answer is A (The interest belongs to the Law Foundation of Ontario and must be paid over.): Money held in trust for more than one client in a mixed trust account earns interest that is deemed to be held in trust for the Law Foundation of Ontario, and the lawyer must pay it over. A mixed trust account is opened with a written direction to pay interest to the Foundation for that reason. Dividing the interest among clients by balance is not the statutory scheme, and the firm cannot use it to offset bank charges. Treating the interest as the lawyer's income would breach the trust accounting rules.
  13. The correct answer is A (Pay it into a designated trust account promptly after receiving it.): By Law 9 requires a licensee who receives money in trust for a client to pay it into a trust account designated as a trust account, and the money must be kept separate from the firm's own funds. The deposit cheque is trust money because it is given for the purchase and held pending closing. Putting it in the general account, even with a liability record, commingles client money with the firm's money. Holding it outside a trust account, or forwarding it without recording the trust receipt, does not comply with the by-law.
  14. The correct answer is D (Provide the final report and duplicate registered mortgage within 60 days.): Where a lawyer acts for a lender and the loan is secured by a mortgage on real property, the rules require a final report on the transaction, with the duplicate registered mortgage, to be provided to the lender within 60 days of registration of the mortgage, or within any other time the lender instructs. The deadline applies even if the lawyer has paid funds to discharge prior encumbrances and holds an unregistered undertaking to register a discharge. The report is not optional, and the lawyer's fiscal year does not set the deadline. A 90 day period is not the rule.
Case real_e_02

A lawyer acts for Ardent Properties Ltd., which agreed on 12 May 2026 to buy a medical office unit in a commercial condominium from Kingsway Medical Centre Ltd. for $1,250,000. Ardent's lender, Gerald Whitfield, a private individual investor who is not in the business of lending money, has sent the lawyer a joint retainer request asking the lawyer to act for the lender as well as for Ardent on the mortgage. Provincial Trust also wants a corporate search of Ardent and a written opinion that Ardent is in good standing and authorized to complete the purchase. Ardent's sole director and officer is Maria Sousa, who owns the company with her spouse. The lender's loan is $1,250,000, secured by a first charge on the unit. The closing date is 31 August 2026.

  1. The correct answer is B (The lawyer must decline, because the loan exceeds the exception limit.): A lawyer, or two or more lawyers practising in partnership or association, must not act for both the lender and the borrower in a mortgage or loan transaction unless the transaction falls within a listed exception. One exception applies where the consideration for the mortgage does not exceed $50,000, which this $1,250,000 loan does not meet. A separate exception applies where the lender is a lending client, meaning a client that lends money as an ordinary part of its own business, and this lender is a private individual investor rather than a business that lends money in the ordinary course, so that exception is not available either. Where a lawyer does act for both under an exception, the rules require written disclosure to both clients before the advance of funds, but disclosure alone does not create the exception. Deferring the disclosure until after closing is not permitted.
  2. The correct answer is B (A certificate of status issued by the registry, for a government fee of $26.): The Ontario Business Registry's search products include a profile report at $8 and a certificate of status at $26, and the certificate of status is the product used to confirm the corporation's status. The profile report gives the corporation's current information but is not the certificate that confirms status. A certified copy of a document costs $3 per document and reproduces the document rather than confirming status, and a name search report concerns the availability of a name. The fees are set by the Ministry and are published on the government's fee page.
  3. The correct answer is D (An officers' certificate with the authorizing resolution and by-law attached.): A corporation acts through its directors and officers, so the buyer's lawyer confirms the chain of authority by obtaining an officers' certificate that identifies the signing officer and attaches the resolution or by-law that authorizes the sale and the execution of the transfer. A certificate of status confirms the corporation's existence and standing, but it does not show who may sign or whether the sale was authorized. A personal guarantee is a credit support document rather than proof of corporate authority, and the condominium corporation's board has no role in authorizing the unit owner's sale. The lawyer should also confirm the signer's identity and keep the evidence on file.
  4. The correct answer is D (No spousal status statement, because the transferor is a corporation.): The electronic registration regulation requires a statement of spousal status under the Family Law Act from the transferor, but the requirement does not apply where the transferor is a corporation. The spouse's Part II rights, if any, attach to the shares as an interest in the unit under the Family Law Act, and that is a matter to be considered in the corporate transaction rather than a statement in the transfer. The buyer's lawyer does not make the transferor's spousal statement, and the shareholders' consent is a corporate document rather than a family law statement. The president's signature on a spousal statement is not a substitute for the transferor's own statement.
  5. The correct answer is C (The buyer cannot rely on a condition that the buyer did not try to satisfy.): A financing condition is for the buyer's benefit, but a buyer who does nothing to satisfy it cannot rely on it as an escape from the agreement, because the buyer must act in good faith to obtain the financing. If the buyer applies and the lender refuses on the agreed terms, the buyer can normally terminate and recover the deposit. The seller has no obligation to arrange financing, and a condition is not waived automatically by the passage of the date. The buyer's lawyer should therefore diarize the condition date and obtain instructions in time.
  6. The correct answer is A (Deliver written notice waiving or terminating within the condition period.): A condition of this kind is time limited and is for the buyer's benefit, so the buyer's lawyer must deal with it in writing by the date set in the agreement, either waiving it and confirming the buyer's intention to close or giving notice that the buyer is not satisfied and the agreement is at an end. Relying on a hoped for price reduction leaves the buyer exposed if the date passes. An oral termination does not satisfy a requirement that notice be in writing, and the inspector's report is not a registrable interest in land. The buyer's remedy, if any, for the cracked foundation depends on whether the condition is properly exercised and on what the seller disclosed.
  7. The correct answer is C (The buyer must waive the condition within the set time or the agreement ends.): An offer conditional on the sale of the buyer's home is a condition precedent, and the standard practice is to include a term that lets the seller require the buyer to decide when another offer arrives. If the buyer does not waive the condition within the time set, the first agreement comes to an end and the deposit is returned. The seller is not required to wait for the buyer's home to sell, and the buyer does not forfeit the deposit merely because the home has not sold. A second offer can be accepted once the first agreement ends, so it is not void.
  8. The correct answer is D (The common expenses for the unit and any increase in them.): The Condominium Act requires the status certificate to contain listed items, including the common expenses for the unit, any increases in the common expenses, any assessment levied against the unit to increase the reserve fund contribution, applications to amend the declaration or by-laws, the most recent reserve fund study, the amount in the reserve fund, any legal action against the corporation and the agreements the person may examine. The market value of the units and the mortgage balance of the current owner are not items the corporation certifies. How individual owners voted is not part of the certificate either.
  9. The correct answer is C (The corporation is deemed to have given a favourable certificate.): The corporation must give the status certificate within 10 days after receiving the request and the fee. If it does not, it is deemed to have given a certificate stating, among other things, that there are no unpaid common expenses and that the board has not levied any reserve fund assessment against the unit since the current year's budget. The buyer's lawyer does not need to reapply or pay a second fee, and there is no further 30 day period. The deemed certificate is what protects the buyer when the corporation is late.
  10. The correct answer is B (The certificate binds the corporation, so the buyer is not liable for that assessment.): A status certificate binds the corporation, as of the date it is given or deemed to be given, with respect to the information it contains or is deemed to contain, as against a purchaser or mortgagee who relies on it. If the certificate omits material information it was required to contain, it is deemed to include a statement that there is no such information, so the omitted assessment does not bind the buyer. The fee for a status certificate is capped at $100 inclusive of all applicable taxes, which is why the buyer's lawyer can order one without a large expense. The buyer does not have to pay first and then chase the seller for the money.
  11. The correct answer is B (No, because a resale of a used home by a non builder is exempt.): A supply by way of sale of a residential complex, or an interest in it, made by a person who is not a builder of the complex is an exempt supply, so a typical resale home does not attract GST or HST on the price. The exemption can be lost in listed situations, such as where the seller claimed an input tax credit on the complex, so the seller's status and history matter. HST does not apply to every real property sale, and the buyer's registration status does not create tax on an exempt supply. There is no reduced residential rate in the legislation.
  12. The correct answer is C (O. Reg. 43/96, which prescribes requirements for surveys, plans and descriptions of land.): O. Reg. 43/96 under the Land Titles Act, Surveys, Plans and Descriptions of Land, prescribes the requirements that apply to legal descriptions used in the land titles system, including on an application for first registration. O. Reg. 19/99 instead governs electronic registration documents generally, not specifically the survey and description requirements. Building Code Act regulations address construction permits, an unrelated subject, and legal descriptions are not left purely to a surveyor's discretion under this scheme.
  13. The correct answer is D (A minor variance application to the committee of adjustment, not a consent application.): A minor variance changes the application of the zoning by-law to a property, such as a setback, while a consent severs land so it can be conveyed, mortgaged or dealt with separately, so a setback request with no change in ownership calls for a minor variance rather than a consent. Since no severance is involved here, a consent application does not fit these facts. The two applications are not always required together, and a setback rule for an accessory structure like a garage is exactly the kind of provision a minor variance can address.
  14. The correct answer is C (The council of the single tier municipality where the land is located.): A consent is given by the council of the upper tier municipality, the council of a single tier municipality, a prescribed single tier municipality in a territorial district, or the Minister, depending on where the land is, and a single tier municipality with no delegation in place means its own council decides. The provincial cabinet does not decide individual consent applications through an order in council. A school board and the neighbours have no formal decision making role under the Planning Act consent provisions at all.
  15. The correct answer is B (File a notice of appeal with the secretary treasurer within 20 days.): The applicant, the Minister, or a specified person or public body with an interest in the matter may appeal a committee of adjustment decision to the Tribunal within 20 days of the decision, by filing a notice of appeal with the committee's secretary treasurer and paying the Tribunal's fee. Writing to council does not create a valid appeal of a committee of adjustment decision. There is no 90 day waiting period before an appeal may be filed, and the appeal goes to the Tribunal rather than directly to the Divisional Court.
  16. The correct answer is D (The parcel register now shows title, so no forty year search is needed.): Once land is brought under the Land Titles Act, the parcel register replaces the need to prove a forty year chain of recorded instruments, and title is thereafter dealt with under the Land Titles Act with the Assurance Fund as the statutory backstop, so the parcel register itself shows the current state of title. The registry forty year rule remains relevant to registry land and to pre conversion history, not to searching the converted parcel going forward. Searching back to the original Crown grant is not what either system requires, and some search of the parcel register is still necessary rather than none at all.
  17. The correct answer is B (The certificate must show the reserve fund amount within the last 90 days.): The status certificate must contain the amount in the reserve fund no earlier than the end of a month within 90 days of the certificate, along with current plans to increase the reserve fund contribution. It is not limited to a historical balance from the corporation's original registration date. Individual owners' private bank statements have nothing to do with the corporation's own reserve fund disclosure, and the reserve fund balance is exactly the kind of information the certificate is designed to disclose, not withhold as confidential.
  18. The correct answer is B (The corporation is deemed to have given a certificate stating no unpaid common expenses.): The corporation must give the status certificate within 10 days after receiving the request and the fee, and if it does not, the corporation is deemed to have given a certificate stating, among other things, that there are no unpaid common expenses and that no reserve fund assessment has been levied against the unit since the current budget. The failure does not void the sale automatically, and it does not require the purchaser to pay a second fee to obtain protection. The ten day period is a binding statutory deadline, not merely a suggested guideline.
  19. The correct answer is C (The certificate is deemed to state no such action exists, binding the corporation.): The status certificate binds the corporation, as of the date it is given, with respect to the information it contains or is deemed to contain, as against a purchaser who relies on it, and if the certificate omits information it was required to contain, it is deemed to include a statement that there is no such information. That deeming provision is exactly what gives the certificate ongoing value even where it is wrong, rather than making it worthless. The purchaser is entitled to rely on the certificate without independently duplicating the corporation's own litigation disclosure, and the omission is not legally consequence free.
  20. The correct answer is C ($100, inclusive of all applicable taxes and all required material.): The fee a corporation may charge for a status certificate, including all material required to be included in it, must not exceed $100, inclusive of all applicable taxes, so a $175 charge does not comply with the regulation regardless of what documents are attached. There is no $250 tier available simply by itemizing the contents. The regulation does set a maximum, so corporations cannot simply charge whatever they consider reasonable, and no separate add-on charge for the financial statements is permitted beyond the $100 cap.
  21. The correct answer is A (The corporation must keep status certificate records for at least seven years.): Records of status certificates must be kept for at least seven years from the date the certificate is issued, so a certificate issued five years ago should still be part of the corporation's records and available for this kind of comparison. The regulation does not allow the corporation to discard a status certificate immediately after delivery, and it does not require destruction after only one year. Retention is fixed by the regulation itself rather than left purely to each corporation's own discretion.
  22. The correct answer is D (Review the pattern of transfers, inquire further, and report the concern to the client.): Because of the increasing risk of fraud, the guidelines require the lawyer to review the pattern of inactive or deleted instruments and rapid transfers on the parcel register, to inquire about suspicious patterns of transfers, and to check suspicious changes in value revealed by recent arm's length transfers, then report those matters to the client. This rapid, escalating pattern with no financing changes is exactly the kind of thing the guidance says merits inquiry rather than being dismissed as unremarkable. The guidance calls for investigation and client reporting, not an automatic refusal to act or an immediate police report before the lawyer has even gathered the facts.
  23. The correct answer is B (A power of attorney signing documents on behalf of the registered owner.): The Law Society's red flags list includes a power of attorney signing for the registered owner as one of its listed examples, since that arrangement has been used to disguise fraudulent transfers. A registered owner personally signing at the lawyer's own office is the opposite of a red flag, since it allows direct verification. A co-owner reachable by video call, or a request for a longer closing date, are not among the specific red flag examples the guidance lists as concerning on their own.
  24. The correct answer is C (Yes, two items from different sources meeting the listed categories can satisfy verification.): Verification for an individual may be done with a government issued photo identification document, or with a Canadian credit file of at least three years, or with any two of the listed categories from different sources, including information containing the individual's name and address and information confirming a loan with a financial institution, which is exactly what is offered here. It is therefore incorrect to say only a photo identification document will ever work. A bank loan record is expressly one of the categories the rule recognizes, and there is no thirty day freshness requirement stated for this alternative method.
  25. The correct answer is A (The termination date used for expiry becomes the date the notice was published.): No later than seven days after a contract is terminated, the owner or another responsible person must publish a notice of termination in the prescribed form and manner. Where that notice is published late, the termination date used for the expiry rules is treated as the date the notice was actually published, not the earlier true date of termination. This protects lien claimants who would otherwise have their time run from an event they had no way of knowing about. The rule changes the reference date rather than pausing or ignoring the calculation altogether.
  26. The correct answer is D (April 15, fourteen days after the proper invoice was received.): An owner who disputes a proper invoice may refuse to pay the disputed portion only if, no later than 14 days after receiving the invoice, the owner gives the contractor a notice of non payment in the prescribed form setting out the amount withheld and the reasons. Counting 14 days from April 1 gives April 15 as the deadline. The 28 day figure is the general deadline for paying an undisputed proper invoice, not the deadline for disputing one. Seven and 21 days do not match any timeline set out in the prompt payment provisions.
  27. The correct answer is C (Seven days after the contractor receives payment from the owner.): A contractor who receives full payment in time must pay each subcontractor within seven days after receiving that payment. Fourteen and 28 days do not match the timeline the Act sets for this particular obligation. Thirty five days is instead the outer deadline that applies when the owner has not paid the contractor at all, which is a different situation from the one described here. Distinguishing the paid and unpaid scenarios matters because each triggers a different clock.
  28. The correct answer is B (Give the notice within the specific time limits the Act sets for it.): A subcontractor pays its own subcontractors within seven days of receiving payment, or, if the contractor does not pay, no later than 42 days after the proper invoice was given to the owner, and a notice of non payment must be given within the specific times set out in the section for that chain of payment. Waiting until final payment defeats the purpose of the notice, since the deadline runs regardless of when the subcontractor is eventually paid. A court ruling is not a precondition to giving this statutory notice. An open ended deadline running to project completion does not match the fixed timeline the Act actually imposes.
  29. The correct answer is D (Interest begins to accrue on the amount from the date it was due.): Interest begins to accrue on an amount not paid when due under the prompt payment rules, calculated at the prejudgment interest rate under the Courts of Justice Act or the greater of that rate and a rate specified in the contract. The contractor does not simply lose the right to be paid because of a missed due date. Adjudication is a dispute resolution process a party may choose to start, it is not triggered automatically by a late payment. Interest accrues by operation of the statute itself, so no separate agreement between the parties is needed for it to apply.
  30. The correct answer is B (Ninety days after the contract was completed, unless the parties agree otherwise.): An adjudication about a contract may not be started more than 90 days after the contract is completed, abandoned, or terminated, unless the parties agree otherwise. Thirty and 60 days are shorter periods that appear elsewhere in the Act for different purposes, such as lien expiry, not for starting an adjudication. A full year is well beyond the outer limit the adjudication provisions actually allow. Missing the 90 day window without an agreement extending it generally closes off adjudication as a remedy for that dispute.
  31. The correct answer is C (Ask the Authority to appoint a registry adjudicator instead.): If the registry adjudicator the parties agreed on does not consent within four days after the notice of adjudication is given, the party who gave the notice must ask the Authority to appoint a registry adjudicator, and the Authority must appoint one, subject to consent, no later than seven days after receiving the request. Starting a court action instead abandons the adjudication process rather than moving it forward as required. There is no rule that lets the parties simply wait out an unresponsive named adjudicator. A party cannot unilaterally pick a replacement adjudicator outside the process the Authority administers.
  32. The correct answer is C (August 31, thirty days after the documents were received.): The adjudicator must make a determination no later than 30 days after receiving the notice, contract, and supporting documents, subject to a 14 day extension with the consent of the parties or a longer extension by written agreement. Counting 30 days from August 1 gives August 31 as the deadline in the absence of any agreed extension. Forty five and 60 days would only apply if the parties had agreed to extend the basic 30 day period, which the facts say did not happen here. Fourteen days is the length of the optional extension itself, not the base deadline.
  33. The correct answer is C (Until a court, an arbitration, or a written agreement changes it.): An adjudicator's determination binds the parties to the adjudication until a court determination, an arbitration determination, or a written agreement between the parties changes it. There is no fixed one year expiry built into the binding effect of a determination. The determination does not simply lapse at the next progress payment, since it addresses the specific dispute referred to adjudication rather than the ongoing payment cycle. Describing the determination as changeable only through those three routes is accurate, but treating it as fixed forever ignores that a court, an arbitration, or the parties themselves can still change it.
  34. The correct answer is B (Yes, the statutory power of sale in the Mortgages Act now applies.): Where a mortgage does not already contain a power of sale, and does not declare that Part II of the Mortgages Act is excluded, the statutory power of sale under section 24 becomes exercisable once three months have passed from a default in payment. Four months of default satisfies that three month threshold, so the statutory power is now available without any further step. Saying such a mortgage simply has no power of sale ignores the statutory power the Act supplies by default. A court order is needed for a judicial sale or foreclosure, not for exercising this out of court statutory power, and there is no one year threshold anywhere in the section.
Case wills__01

Mr. Whitfield is eighty-one and has early stage Alzheimer's disease. In January 2026 he signed a continuing power of attorney for property naming his son, Andrew, as his attorney. The document was properly executed before the family solicitor and a legal assistant, and it said nothing about compensation or charitable giving. In June 2026 a geriatric assessor found Mr. Whitfield incapable of managing property. In August 2026 Andrew transferred $120,000 from his father's account to himself and donated $40,000 to a local hospital foundation. The property that remains is more than sufficient for Mr. Whitfield's care. Mr. Whitfield's daughter, who was not named under the power of attorney, has asked the solicitor to review what Andrew has done.

  1. The correct answer is D (A child of the grantor who is not named as attorney.): Section 10(2) of the Substitute Decisions Act lists the persons who may not witness a continuing power of attorney for property. The list covers the attorney and the attorney's spouse or partner, the grantor's spouse or partner, a child of the grantor or a person the grantor has treated as a child, a person whose property is under guardianship or who has a guardian of the person, and a person under eighteen. The solicitor, a disinterested neighbour, and a person named as executor in the will are not on that list, so each of them may witness.
  2. The correct answer is A (Yes, if the earlier intentions are shown and the property remains sufficient.): Section 37 of the Substitute Decisions Act permits a guardian, and through section 38 an attorney, to make gifts or loans to the incapable person's friends and relatives only if the property is and will remain sufficient for the person's support and needs, and only if there is reason to believe, based on intentions the person expressed before becoming incapable, that he or she would make the gift if capable. Both conditions must be met, so the strength of the evidence about the father's intentions matters. Attorneys are not barred from gifting altogether, the authority is not unlimited, and gifts to friends and relatives do not require the consent of the Public Guardian and Trustee.
  3. The correct answer is A (The lesser of 20 per cent of the income and any cap in the document.): Section 37(4) of the Substitute Decisions Act allows charitable gifts where the incapable person authorized them in a power of attorney executed before incapacity or where there is evidence of similar expenditures when capable. The total charitable gifts may not exceed the lesser of 20 per cent of the income of the property in the year and the maximum provided for in the power of attorney. With income of $100,000 and no cap in this document, the limit is $20,000, so the $40,000 donation exceeds it. A larger gift requires court authorization under section 37(5).
  4. The correct answer is B (He must keep accounts and may be ordered to pass them.): Section 32(6) of the Substitute Decisions Act requires a guardian to keep accounts of all transactions involving the property, and section 38 applies that duty to an attorney under a continuing power of attorney once the grantor is incapable of managing property or the attorney has reasonable grounds to believe so. Section 42 allows the court to order that the attorney's accounts be passed, on the application of the attorney, the grantor or another listed person. The attorney does not have to report routine transactions to the Public Guardian and Trustee, obtain the daughter's consent, or file an estate tax return because of the power of attorney alone.
  5. The correct answer is B (She knows that the attorney must account for dealings with her property.): Section 8(1) of the Substitute Decisions Act sets out what a person must know to be capable of giving a continuing power of attorney for property. The person must know the kind of property he or she has and its approximate value, be aware of obligations owed to dependants, understand that the attorney may do anything with the property except make a will, know that the attorney must account, know that the power may be revoked if the person is capable, and appreciate both that the property may decline in value and that the attorney could misuse the authority. Exact share values, tax analysis and probate knowledge are not part of the test.
  6. The correct answer is A (The bank, because the new document terminates the earlier appointment.): Section 12(1) of the Substitute Decisions Act provides that a continuing power of attorney is terminated when the grantor executes a new continuing power of attorney, unless the grantor provides that there are to be multiple continuing powers of attorney. The grantor said nothing to preserve the earlier appointment, so the 2025 document replaces it and the bank is the attorney. An express revocation is one way to end the authority but not the only way, and the two documents do not operate side by side unless the grantor provided for that.
  7. The correct answer is A (It must be signed before two witnesses who each sign as witness.): Section 48(1) of the Substitute Decisions Act requires a power of attorney for personal care to be executed in the presence of two witnesses, each of whom signs it as a witness, and section 48(2) applies the prohibited witness list from section 10(2). The Act does not require a notary, a court filing, or the signature of the attorney, and section 46(8) provides that the document need not be in any particular form. A power of attorney for personal care that does not meet the execution rules is not effective unless the court declares it effective in the grantor's interests.
  8. The correct answer is D (No, because a compensated support worker may not act as attorney.): Section 46(3) of the Substitute Decisions Act provides that a person may not act as an attorney under a power of attorney for personal care, unless the person is the grantor's spouse, partner or relative, if the person provides health care to the grantor for compensation or provides residential, social, training or support services to the grantor for compensation. The worker is paid for those services and is not a relative, so she may not act. The client's freedom to choose is limited by that prohibition, the length of the care relationship does not matter, and the Act does not restrict the role to spouses and lawyers.
  9. The correct answer is B (Whether she understands the attorney's concern and may need decisions made.): Section 47(1) of the Substitute Decisions Act states that a person is capable of giving a power of attorney for personal care if she has the ability to understand whether the proposed attorney has a genuine concern for her welfare and appreciates that she may need to have that attorney make decisions for her. Managing property, understanding medical treatment options, and knowing the value of her property are different capacity questions. Section 47(2) adds that the power of attorney is valid if the grantor was capable of giving it even if she is incapable of personal care.
  10. The correct answer is A (Written consent from the Public Guardian and Trustee and the personal care attorney.): Section 40 of the Substitute Decisions Act allows an attorney under a continuing power of attorney to take annual compensation in accordance with the prescribed fee scale, and to take it monthly, quarterly or annually. The attorney may take more than the scale allows only with the written consent of the Public Guardian and Trustee and the incapable person's guardian of the person or attorney for personal care, or, where the Public Guardian and Trustee is the attorney, with the court's approval. These rules are subject to any compensation terms in the power of attorney itself, which is why the document must be checked first. Consent from the family or from beneficiaries is not the statutory route.
  11. The correct answer is A (The Public Guardian and Trustee becomes the statutory guardian.): Section 15 of the Substitute Decisions Act provides that where a certificate is issued under the Mental Health Act certifying that a patient of a psychiatric facility is incapable of managing property, the Public Guardian and Trustee is the person's statutory guardian of property. The same result follows when an assessor issues a certificate of incapacity under section 16 and the Public Guardian and Trustee receives a copy. The children do not take the role by seniority, the hospital does not become guardian, and no court application is needed for the statutory guardianship to arise.
  12. The correct answer is B (Apply to the court for appointment as guardian of property.): Section 22 of the Substitute Decisions Act allows the court to appoint a guardian of property for a person who is incapable of managing property and who, as a result, needs decisions to be made on his or her behalf by someone with authority. The court must not appoint a guardian if the need can be met by a less restrictive alternative that does not require a finding of incapacity, but a bank's refusal to deal with an informal manager creates a practical need for authority that the informal arrangement cannot satisfy. An attorney cannot execute a power of attorney on the grantor's behalf, and a doctor's letter does not give the son authority over his father's property.
  13. The correct answer is D (That no other suitable person is available and willing to be appointed.): Section 57(1) of the Substitute Decisions Act prohibits the appointment of a person who provides health care or residential, social, training or support services to an incapable person for compensation, unless the person falls within the listed exceptions. Section 57(2.1) creates a further exception where the court is satisfied that there is no other suitable person who is available and willing to be appointed. The length of the care relationship, the consent of relatives, and the insurance arrangements of the home are not the statutory test. The question is whether anyone else suitable is available and willing.
  14. The correct answer is A (Every adult is presumed capable unless there are reasonable grounds to believe otherwise.): Section 2(1) of the Substitute Decisions Act presumes that a person who is eighteen or older is capable of entering into a contract, and section 2(2) presumes that a person who is sixteen or older is capable of giving or refusing consent in connection with personal care. Section 2(3) allows a person to rely on the presumption unless he or she has reasonable grounds to believe that the other person is incapable, and the common law presumes every adult capable of making a will unless the contrary is proved. A family member's concern does not change the starting point, and there is no age at which an automatic assessment is required.
  15. The correct answer is C (The daughter may apply, with leave of the court as another person.): Section 42(2) of the Substitute Decisions Act allows an attorney, the grantor or any of the persons listed in section 42(4) to apply to pass the attorney's accounts. The listed persons include the grantor's guardian of the person or attorney for personal care, a dependant of the grantor, the Public Guardian and Trustee, the Children's Lawyer and a judgment creditor, and section 42(4) also allows any other person to apply with leave of the court. The daughter is not left without a remedy, because she may seek leave. The court may order that all or a specified part of the accounts be passed.
  16. The correct answer is B (Apply to the Consent and Capacity Board for a review of the finding.): Section 16 of the Substitute Decisions Act requires the Public Guardian and Trustee to inform the person that he or she may apply to the Consent and Capacity Board for a review of the assessor's finding that the person is incapable of managing property, and section 20.2(1) gives the same right to a person who has a statutory guardian of property. The Board may confirm the finding or determine that the person is capable, and it may substitute its opinion for that of the assessor. The Public Guardian and Trustee does not reverse the finding itself, the certificate is not set aside by the Superior Court on this route, and the statutory guardianship has no fixed one year term.
  17. The correct answer is D (The revocation must be in writing and executed like a continuing power of attorney.): Section 12(2) of the Substitute Decisions Act provides that a revocation of a continuing power of attorney must be in writing and must be executed in the same way as a continuing power of attorney, which means execution in the presence of two witnesses who each sign as witness. A verbal statement to the attorney is not enough, and neither court approval nor filing with the Public Guardian and Trustee is required. The same approach applies to a power of attorney for personal care under section 53(2).
  18. The correct answer is A (One witness must be an LSO licensee and the signatures must be contemporaneous.): Section 3.1 of the Substitute Decisions Act allows the requirement that a power of attorney be executed in the presence of witnesses to be satisfied by audio-visual communication technology if at least one person acting as a witness is a licensee under the Law Society Act, the required signatures are contemporaneously made, and any prescribed requirements are met. Identical copies may be signed in counterpart, and the rule also applies to a revocation. The grantor does not have to attend in person, two lawyers are not required, and a counterpart signing by the grantor alone would not satisfy the witness requirement.
  19. The correct answer is D (The trust company, because the court appointment terminated the power of attorney.): A court appointment of a guardian of property ends the authority given by a continuing power of attorney, because section 12(1) of the Substitute Decisions Act lists that appointment as a terminating event. The appointment of the trust company therefore ended the daughter's authority, and a written revocation was not needed. The two roles do not continue side by side, and the attorney's authority does not revive if the grantor later regains capacity, because the power of attorney has been terminated.
  20. The correct answer is A (To take under the will or to claim the equalization entitlement instead.): Section 6(1) of the Family Law Act provides that when a spouse dies leaving a will, the surviving spouse must elect either to take under the will or to receive the entitlement under section 5. Where the deceased died intestate, section 6(2) gives the choice between the intestate entitlement under Part II of the Succession Law Reform Act and the section 5 entitlement, and section 6(3) deals with a partial intestacy. Dependant support under Part V of the Succession Law Reform Act and an unjust enrichment claim are separate remedies, and a court does not simply vary the will on request.
  21. The correct answer is D (File the prescribed election with the Estate Registrar within six months.): Section 6(10) of the Family Law Act requires the surviving spouse's election to be in the prescribed form and to be filed in the office of the Estate Registrar for Ontario within six months after the first spouse's death. If the election is not filed within that time, section 6(11) deems the spouse to have elected to take under the will or to receive the intestate entitlement, unless the court orders otherwise. A written demand to the estate trustee is not the statutory election, and there is no twelve month objection period for this purpose.
  22. The correct answer is D (Direct that an estate trustee be appointed during litigation.): On an application or motion for directions, the court may direct that an estate trustee be appointed during litigation and file such security as the court directs. That appointment lets the estate be administered while the validity of the will is decided. Dismissing the action or appointing a permanent executor before the validity question is decided would not achieve that purpose.
  23. The correct answer is B (No, the Minister may reassess at any time where the information duty was not met.): The Minister may assess or reassess within four years after the day the tax became payable, and that day is the day the estate certificate is issued. The limit does not apply where the Minister establishes that a person failed to comply with the duty to give information, or that there was a misrepresentation attributable to neglect, carelessness or wilful default, or fraud. The four year period therefore does not protect an estate that did not file the required return.
  24. The correct answer is B (It is payable by the estate representative in a representative capacity only.): The tax is payable to the Crown in right of Ontario by the estate of the deceased immediately upon the issuance of an estate certificate, and it is payable by the estate representative in a representative capacity only. That means the representative is not personally liable for the tax itself, even though a failure to give the required information can attract a penalty. The tax is not deferred until the assets are distributed.
  25. The correct answer is B (It is refunded to the applicant, because no estate certificate was issued.): The deposit is refunded if no estate certificate is issued. The tax itself is payable only on the issuance of a certificate, so a refused application leaves nothing to pay. The deposit is not transferred to another tax account and is not held until the estate is administered.
  26. The correct answer is D (File a sworn statement of the actual value and pay any extra tax within six months.): Where the deposit is based on an estimate, the applicant must give the court a signed undertaking to file a sworn statement of the actual total value and to pay any additional tax within six months after giving the undertaking. The undertaking is separate from the estate information return, which is filed within 180 calendar days after the certificate is issued. A clearance certificate relates to income tax and is not what the undertaking promises.
  27. The correct answer is C (A fine of at least $1,000 and up to twice the tax payable.): A person who fails to comply with the information rules, or who makes or assists in making a false or misleading statement, is guilty of an offence. On conviction the penalty is a fine of at least $1,000 but not more than twice the tax payable by the estate if that amount is greater than $1,000, or imprisonment for not more than two years, or both. On these facts the maximum fine is twice $12,750, so the available sanctions are wider than a fixed civil penalty.
  28. The correct answer is C (Within 60 calendar days of the trustee becoming aware of the error.): If an estate representative becomes aware, within four years of the issuance of the estate certificate, that information on a return was incorrect or incomplete, an amended return must be received within 60 calendar days of becoming aware.
