The 180 day period is the deadline for the initial return rather than for an amendment. The 30 day period and the anniversary date are not the deadline for an amended return.
  29. The correct answer is B (Only the assets included in the specific will covered by the certificate.): Where the court issues a certificate of appointment of estate trustee with a will limited to the assets referred to in a will, only the assets included in that specific will are used to calculate the tax. That is what makes the multiple wills strategy effective, because the tax is based on the assets covered by the certificate that is applied for. The tax is not calculated on the combined value of every asset the deceased owned.
Case estate_01

Nadia died in January. Her will gave her cottage on Lake Simcoe to her son Ali and the residue of her estate to her daughter Fatima. Nadia owned the cottage alone, and she had rented it out for the last six years. The adjusted cost base of the cottage was $120,000 and its fair market value on the date of death was $410,000. Nadia had not designated the cottage as her principal residence for any year. She also held a registered retirement income fund worth $150,000 with Fatima named as the beneficiary, and a bank account of $60,000 in her own name. Nadia's adult grandson Ben lived with her for the two years before her death and she supported him during that time. Her will left Ben nothing.

  1. The correct answer is B (She is deemed to have disposed of it at fair market value immediately before death.): Where a taxpayer dies, the taxpayer is deemed to have disposed immediately before death of each capital property for proceeds equal to its fair market value immediately before death. The gain is therefore measured at the death rather than when Ali later sells the cottage, and only the gain, not the whole value, is brought into income. Ali takes the cottage at a cost equal to that fair market value.
  2. The correct answer is C (It is not included, because it passes to the named beneficiary.): A registered retirement income fund with a valid beneficiary designation passes outside the estate, and the value is not included in the value of the estate for this tax. The same treatment applies to a registered retirement savings plan or a tax free savings account with a beneficiary designation, and to insurance proceeds payable to a named beneficiary. The fund is not included merely because the estate trustee assists with the paperwork.
  3. The correct answer is A (An election to pay in up to ten equal consecutive annual instalments.): The legal representative may elect to pay the tax caused by the death deemed disposition rules in up to 10 equal consecutive annual instalments, with security and interest. The election spreads payment of the tax rather than deferring it until a later sale, and it is not limited to five instalments. The liability remains with the estate and is not transferred to the beneficiary who receives the property.
  4. The correct answer is C (He may apply for dependant support if he qualifies as a dependant.): A grandchild can be a dependant where the deceased demonstrated a settled intention to treat the grandchild as a child of the family and was providing support, or was under a legal obligation to provide support, immediately before the death. The application is made under Part V of the Succession Law Reform Act and does not depend on being named in the will. A person who is not an heir and was not a dependant has no intestate share on these facts.
  5. The correct answer is A (In the office of the Estate Registrar for Ontario, within six months of the death.): The election must be in the form prescribed by the regulations and filed in the office of the Estate Registrar for Ontario within six months after the death. If the election is not filed in time, the spouse is deemed to have elected to take under the will unless the court orders otherwise. The 60 day period and the two year period are not the deadlines for this election.
  6. The correct answer is D (No, because no distribution may be made within six months of the death.): No distribution may be made in the administration of the deceased spouse's estate within six months of the death unless the surviving spouse consents in writing or the court authorizes it. Reasonable advances to dependants for their support are permitted, but a payment of the residue to the children is not such an advance. The restriction runs for six months rather than a year, and a clearance certificate addresses tax rather than the spouse's election rights.
  7. The correct answer is D (The adult child's support order ranks ahead of the equalization claim.): The spouse's entitlement under the Family Law Act has priority over gifts in the will and over a share under the intestacy rules, and it also has priority over an order made against the estate under Part V of the Succession Law Reform Act, except an order in favour of a child of the deceased spouse. That exception puts the child's support order first on these facts. The claims do not share proportionately, and the will gifts rank behind the equalization entitlement.
  8. The correct answer is A (They are revoked, and the will is read as if she had died first.): Where the surviving spouse elects to receive the equalization entitlement, the gifts made to that spouse in the will are revoked and the will is interpreted as if the spouse had died before the other spouse, unless the will expressly provides that the gifts are in addition. The electing spouse is also deemed to have disclaimed the intestate entitlement. The gifts are not revived by the consent of the other beneficiaries.
  9. The correct answer is B (Within six months after he discovers the property.): When property belonging to the deceased at the time of death is discovered after the grant and was not included in the statement of total value, the executor or administrator must deliver a further verified statement of the value of the newly discovered property within six months after discovering it. The 180 day period belongs to the estate information return filed with the Ministry of Finance. The obligation is not tied to the end of a taxation year.
  10. The correct answer is D (All three debts are paid proportionately, with no priority among them.): In a deficiency of assets, debts due to the Crown, debts due to the personal representative, and debts to others, including judgment debts and simple contract debts, are paid proportionately and without any preference or priority of one rank over another. The rule preserves a lien that existed on the debtor's property during the debtor's lifetime. There is no preference for the Crown over the other creditors.
  11. The correct answer is D (Pay the claim on the evidence the trustee thinks sufficient.): A personal representative may pay or allow any debt or claim on any evidence that the representative thinks sufficient. A court order is not a precondition, and the trustee is not required to insist on a written contract. The trustee may also compromise or settle a claim, and is not responsible for a loss caused by an act done in good faith.
  12. The correct answer is A (A copy of any unanimous shareholder agreement known to the directors.): The corporation must keep its articles and by-laws, a copy of any unanimous shareholder agreement known to the directors, minutes and resolutions of shareholders, a register of directors, a securities register and the land and control registers. Supplier contracts, hourly employment contracts and invoices are not among the records the Act requires. Accounting records are dealt with separately under the accounting records provision.
  13. The correct answer is C (Yes, because the accounting records need only be kept for six years.): The Act requires accounting records to be retained for six years from the end of the last fiscal period to which they relate, provided the retention requirements of any applicable taxing authority have been met. Disposal after that period is permitted and does not require shareholder consent. A ten year retention requirement is not the rule stated for these records.
  14. The correct answer is B (The corporation must maintain the register from the day it was incorporated.): A corporation incorporated or continued on or after the coming into force of the Forfeited Corporate Property Act, 2015 must keep the register of ownership interests in land in Ontario from the day it is incorporated or continued. Waiting until the corporation acquires land is not what the Act requires. The obligation does not depend on the corporation's line of business or on an incorporation date before 2015.
  15. The correct answer is C (A person who is not an individual cannot be a director.): The Act disqualifies anyone who is less than 18 years of age, anyone found incapable of managing property, anyone who is not an individual and anyone who has the status of bankrupt. A corporation therefore cannot be a director because it is not an individual. Holding shares is not required unless the articles provide otherwise, Ontario has no residency requirement for directors, and the Act sets no upper age limit.
  16. The correct answer is B (On 30 June, the date specified in the written resignation of the director.): A resignation becomes effective when the written resignation is received by the corporation or at the time specified in the resignation, whichever is later. The specified date of 30 June is later than receipt on 15 May, so that is the effective date. Signing the letter does not end the office, and acceptance by the shareholders is not required.
  17. The correct answer is A (The shareholders may remove the director by ordinary resolution without cause.): Subject to the cumulative voting rules, the shareholders may remove a director by ordinary resolution at an annual or special meeting, and the Act does not require cause. A special resolution and a stated reason are not required. A court order and a prior resolution of the board are not the statutory route for removal by the shareholders.
  18. The correct answer is A (Honesty and good faith, plus the care of a reasonably prudent person.): The Act requires every director and officer to act honestly and in good faith with a view to the best interests of the corporation, and to exercise the care, diligence and skill that a reasonably prudent person would exercise in comparable circumstances. Loyalty runs to the corporation rather than to the majority shareholder, and the standard is prudence rather than profit maximization. Fairness to employees and obedience to shareholder directions are not the statutory formulation.
  19. The correct answer is A (Within seven days after becoming aware of the resolution, that is by 11 September.): A director who was not present at a meeting is deemed to consent unless, within seven days after becoming aware of the resolution, the director puts a dissent with the minutes or sends or delivers it to the registered office. The 14 and 30 day periods do not apply, and the clock runs from awareness of the resolution rather than from the date of the meeting. A dissent recorded in that way prevents the deemed consent.
  20. The correct answer is C (Disclose the nature and extent of her interest in the contract.): A director or officer who is a party to a material contract or transaction with the corporation, or who has a material interest in a party, must disclose in writing to the corporation, or request to have the nature and extent of the interest entered in the minutes. Resignation, shareholder approval and a trust arrangement are not the statutory requirements. The disclosure must be made at the meeting where the contract is first considered, or at the first meeting after the interest arises.
Case busine_01

Lian and Marcus are the only shareholders of Ace Ontario Inc., a corporation that operates a chain of repair shops. In 2024 they signed a unanimous shareholder agreement that transfers all of the powers of the directors to the shareholders, requires unanimous consent for any transfer of shares and sets out how the agreement may be amended. The corporation has one director, Ken, who is a nominee named in the agreement. In 2026 the corporation fell behind on wages owed to twelve employees and cannot pay them. The employees want to know who is responsible for the unpaid wages. Nadia, an investor who was not told about the agreement, has offered to buy Lian's shares.

  1. The correct answer is C (The shareholders have the directors' liabilities to the extent the agreement restricts them.): A shareholder who is a party to a unanimous shareholder agreement has the rights, powers, duties and liabilities of a director to the extent that the agreement restricts the discretion or powers of the directors, and the directors are relieved to the same extent. Serving as a director is not necessary for that result, and the corporate shield does not survive the statutory shift. Personal approval of the wages is not the test.
  2. The correct answer is C (She is deemed a party to the agreement once the shares transfer to her.): A transferee of shares that are subject to a unanimous shareholder agreement is deemed to be a party to the agreement. The absence of notice does not prevent that result, and no separate writing or shareholder vote is required. The Act instead gives a purchaser for value without notice a limited right to rescind the share acquisition contract.
  3. The correct answer is B (Rescind within 60 days after actually receiving a complete copy of it.): A purchaser for value without notice may rescind the share acquisition contract within 60 days after actually receiving a complete copy of the unanimous shareholder agreement. The period runs from receipt of the complete copy rather than from the transfer date or the date the purchaser first learned of the agreement. A summary does not start the statutory period.
  4. The correct answer is A (The shareholders, because the articles cannot raise the threshold for removal.): The articles or a unanimous shareholder agreement may require a greater number of votes than the Act requires, and that higher requirement prevails, but the articles may not require a greater number of votes to remove a director than the Act specifies. An ordinary resolution of the shareholders is therefore sufficient to remove the director. A prior resolution of the directors is not required, and the agreement cannot defeat the statutory removal rule.
  5. The correct answer is B (Six months of wages and up to twelve months of vacation pay.): Directors are jointly and severally liable to the employees for debts not exceeding six months of wages that become payable while they are directors, and for vacation pay accrued while they are directors for not more than twelve months. The reversed figures and the three month and six month variants do not match the Act. The periods in the Act cap the liability.
  6. The correct answer is C (The corporation must be sued and execution returned unsatisfied in whole or in part.): A director is liable for the unpaid wages only if the corporation is sued in the action against the director and execution against the corporation is returned unsatisfied in whole or in part, or the corporation goes into liquidation, is ordered wound up, makes an authorized assignment under the Bankruptcy and Insolvency Act or is subject to a receiving order, and the claim has been proved. A winding up order is one alternative route rather than a universal precondition. The amount recoverable is the amount remaining unsatisfied after execution.
  7. The correct answer is A (She may claim contribution from the other directors who were liable.): A director who has satisfied a claim under the wage liability provision is entitled to contribution from the other directors who were liable for the same debt. The liability is not hers alone, and the recovery is not limited to a claim against the corporation. Recovery from the former employees would defeat the purpose of the wage protection, so it is not the remedy.
  8. The correct answer is B (He acted honestly and in good faith with a view to the corporation's interests.): An indemnity is not permitted unless the individual acted honestly and in good faith with a view to the best interests of the corporation and, in a criminal or administrative proceeding enforced by a monetary penalty, had reasonable grounds for believing the conduct was lawful. An acquittal is not required, and shareholder approval is not the statutory condition. Repayment is relevant to an advance of costs that does not ultimately qualify.
  9. The correct answer is D (Repay the money to the corporation because the conditions were not met.): A corporation may advance money for the costs, charges and expenses of a proceeding, but the individual must repay it if the conditions in the Act are not met. The advance is not a separate entitlement that escapes those conditions. A court order and the eventual dissolution of the corporation are not what create the obligation to repay.
  10. The correct answer is A (The salary is not effective unless the directors fixed the remuneration.): Directors' remuneration, and the remuneration of officers and employees, may be fixed by the directors, subject to the articles, the by-laws or any unanimous shareholder agreement. A director cannot fix his own pay without a decision of the board, and an employment agreement with the corporation does not replace that requirement. Shareholder silence does not validate the amount, and the shareholders' meeting is not the body that fixes it.
  11. The correct answer is D (Shares must be in registered form and are without nominal or par value.): The Act provides that shares of an Ontario corporation must be in registered form and are without nominal or par value. Shares with a nominal or par value of a corporation incorporated before 29 July 1983 are deemed to be shares without nominal or par value. A par value in the articles, an amount equal to the issue price and a voting condition do not change that rule.
  12. The correct answer is D (The directors determine the time, the persons and the consideration.): Subject to the articles, the by-laws and any unanimous shareholder agreement, shares may be issued at such time and to such persons and for such consideration as the directors determine. A special resolution of the shareholders is not required for an ordinary issue, and the investor does not set the price. The auditor has no role in fixing the consideration.
  13. The correct answer is D (Review the property's environmental records and any outstanding orders.): Environmental exposure is managed in due diligence by reviewing the property's history and records, searching for outstanding orders and notices, and negotiating indemnities and holdbacks for the risks that are identified. The seller's own assurance is not a substitute for that review. A building code report addresses construction standards rather than contamination. A lender's confirmation addresses the lender's security position and does not assess the environmental condition of the property.
  14. The correct answer is B (Have the assignment recorded and obtain a warranty and indemnity from the seller.): An unrecorded assignment does not invalidate a trademark, but recording it puts the corporation's ownership on the public record and helps against later purchasers who deal with the same mark. The buyer should also protect itself with a representation, a warranty and an indemnity that address ownership and any competing claims. The transaction does not need to be abandoned for that reason. Continued use by the seller would work against the buyer, and a fresh application would not repair the gap in the chain of title.
  15. The correct answer is A (A non-competition covenant in an employment contract is void unless it meets the statutory test.): Section 67.2 of the Employment Standards Act, 2000 prohibits an employer from entering into an employment contract or other agreement with an employee that is, or that includes, a non-competition agreement, subject to the statutory exceptions. Non-solicitation covenants are treated separately and are not caught by that section. A short duration does not save a non-competition covenant, and paying the employee during a notice period does not change the analysis. The rule does not turn on whether the role is clerical.
  16. The correct answer is D (It is governed by the common law and is enforced if it is reasonable.): The Employment Standards Act, 2000 has a provision on non-competition agreements, but it has no non-solicitation provision, so a covenant of this kind is tested under the common law. Outside employment, the covenant is a restraint of trade and is enforced only if it is reasonable between the parties and in the public interest. A court does not pre-approve the terms, and the covenant is not free of limits on its duration or the territory it covers. The employment standards prohibition does not reach a non-solicitation covenant.
  17. The correct answer is D (The covenant is enforceable if it is reasonable, as a sale of a business justifies it.): A covenant given on the sale of a business is a restraint of trade, and such a restraint is enforceable if it is reasonable between the parties and consistent with the public interest. A three year period within a defined area may be found reasonable, although a court examines the geographic scope and the interest that the covenant protects. Restraints of trade are not void in every case. The seller's later departure from the business does not remove the covenant, and independent legal advice is not a legal requirement for enforceability, although it can help to meet other arguments.
  18. The correct answer is B (It sits between the non-share consideration value and the property value.): Under section 85, the parties jointly elect the amount that is treated as the seller's proceeds of disposition and the corporation's cost of the property. That elected amount is bounded below by the fair market value of any non-share consideration, such as the promissory note, and above by the fair market value of the property transferred. It is not automatically the property's fair market value, and it is not tied to the cost of the shares issued. The parties do not have unlimited freedom, because the statute sets both bounds.
  19. The correct answer is B (The cost of any non-share property received is its fair market value.): Under section 86, where a taxpayer disposes of all of the shares of a particular class in the course of a reorganization of capital and receives new shares plus other property, the taxpayer is deemed to dispose of the old shares for proceeds equal to the cost of the new shares and the other property received. The cost of any non-share property, such as the cash, is its fair market value. The rollover preserves the gain in the new shares rather than triggering it, so the transaction is not treated as an ordinary sale at fair market value, and the new shares do not have a nil cost.
  20. The correct answer is D (The new corporation continues each predecessor and inherits their tax attributes.): Section 87 treats the amalgamated corporation as the same corporation as, and a continuation of, each predecessor corporation for the purposes set out in the section. The practical result is that the amalgamated corporation inherits the predecessors' tax attributes, such as their cost bases and losses, subject to the rules that limit their use. The amalgamation does not trigger a disposition of the assets at fair market value. The continuing corporation is not a new taxpayer with no history, and the predecessors' attributes are not cancelled.
Case commer_01

A credit union advanced $600,000 to a manufacturer in March and took a general security agreement over all of the manufacturer's present and after-acquired personal property. The credit union's officer intended to register a financing statement immediately, but the registration was delayed. The financing statement was registered in July, four months after the advance was made. In September the manufacturer defaulted, and a competing lender, which had registered a financing statement covering the same collateral in June, demanded priority over the manufacturer's inventory and equipment. The manufacturer also owes $90,000 in unremitted source deductions.

  1. The correct answer is C (The order of registration, since the competing lender registered first.): Where no other priority rule applies, priority between perfected security interests registered under the Personal Property Security Act is decided by the order of registration rather than by the order of perfection. The competing lender registered in June and the credit union registered in July, so the competing lender ranks first on that rule. The order of advances, the dates of demand and the amounts owed do not decide priority between two registered interests. The result would differ if the credit union held a purchase money security interest or control, which are separate rules.
  2. The correct answer is C (Perfection occurs when attachment has taken place and the perfection steps are complete.): Section 19 provides that a security interest is perfected when it has attached and all the steps required for perfection have been completed, regardless of the order in which attachment and those steps occur. Registration before attachment is therefore effective once attachment takes place, and attachment before registration becomes perfection when the registration is made. Signing the security agreement alone perfects nothing, because registration or another perfection step is still needed. Advancing funds contributes to attachment by giving value, but it does not perfect the interest.
  3. The correct answer is B (Register the financing statement now and then assess the exposure created by the gap.): A registration cannot be backdated, so the credit union should register immediately to perfect its interest from that point forward and then examine what happened during the unperfected period. If a third party acquired rights in the collateral while the interest was unperfected, the credit union's priority may be affected even after registration. A court order does not backdate a financing statement, and the competing lender has no reason to agree to a change. A new security agreement alone does not perfect the interest, because registration is still required.
  4. The correct answer is C (The unperfected interest is subordinate to the trustee, who represents the creditors.): Section 20(1)(b) of the Personal Property Security Act makes an unperfected security interest subordinate to a person who represents the creditors of the debtor, including a trustee in bankruptcy. The trustee can therefore treat the collateral as available to the estate ahead of that interest. The date of the loan does not save an unperfected interest. An unperfected interest is ineffective against the trustee rather than surviving, and the trustee does not need to register a financing statement, because the trustee's position comes from the Act.
  5. The correct answer is D (The borrower must have rights in the collateral or the power to transfer them.): Attachment requires value, which the advance supplies, rights in the collateral or the power to transfer rights in it, and one of the acts listed in section 11(2), which the signed security agreement with a sufficient description satisfies. Registration is a perfection step and is not needed for attachment, although it is needed to protect the lender against other claimants. Physical possession is a way to perfect certain collateral but is not required for attachment. A certificate of status is a corporate search document with no role in attachment.
  6. The correct answer is C (Register a financing statement under the Personal Property Security Act.): Perfection of a security interest in personal property is normally achieved by registering a financing statement in the Personal Property Security Act registry. Registration on title covers real property and does not perfect an interest in equipment and accounts. Taking possession can perfect certain collateral, but it is impractical for accounts and for equipment that the borrower uses in the business. Filing with the sheriff is not a perfection step under the Act.
  7. The correct answer is C (Perfect before or within 15 days after the manufacturer takes possession of the press.): A purchase money security interest in collateral other than inventory takes priority over an earlier security interest if it is perfected before or within 15 days after the debtor obtains possession. For equipment, that rule applies instead of the inventory notice rule, which requires notice to earlier registered secured parties before the debtor receives possession. The earlier lender's consent is not required, and the timing of the advance is not the test. The key is the timing of perfection measured from possession of the press.
  8. The correct answer is B (Give written notice to the earlier lender before possession is delivered.): For inventory, the purchase money secured party must perfect its interest at the time the debtor obtains possession and must give written notice to every other secured party that registered a financing statement covering the same items or types of inventory before the debtor receives possession. The 15 day rule applies to collateral other than inventory, such as equipment. The debtor's consent does not give priority over the earlier lender, and a land registry filing is irrelevant to goods that remain personal property.
  9. The correct answer is D (Serve notice of intention to remove on each person shown in the registry.): A secured party with a right to remove a fixture must serve written notice of intention to remove on each person who appears by the records of the proper land registry office to have an interest in the real property, and the notice must contain the information required by section 34(5). A court order appointing a receiver is a separate remedy and is not the precondition. A financing statement change does not replace the notice, and the landlord's consent is not the statutory requirement, although the secured party must deal with the landlord's interest in the property.
  10. The correct answer is C (The lender, because the security interest attached before the machine became a fixture.): Section 34(1)(a) gives priority to a security interest in goods that attached before the goods became a fixture over the claim of any person who has an interest in the real property. The date the mortgage was registered does not defeat that priority. The second branch of the rule, which protects a person who had a registered interest at the time a later security interest attached, applies only where the security interest attached after the goods became fixtures. Fixture priority is set by the statute rather than by the mortgage terms alone.
  11. The correct answer is C (The debt becomes payable when the lender makes demand, rather than on a fixed date.): A demand debenture makes the debt payable on demand, so the lender may call for payment rather than waiting for a fixed maturity date. The instrument usually also contains covenants and events of default that support enforcement. It is not limited to insolvency or default, because the demand itself triggers the obligation to pay. An instalment loan has a scheduled repayment plan, and the borrower does not choose the repayment date under a demand instrument.
  12. The correct answer is D (Fully informed consent after disclosure and a belief in no adverse effect.): Rule 3.4-2 permits a solicitor to act in a conflict only where all affected clients give consent that is fully informed and voluntary after disclosure, and where the solicitor reasonably believes that they can represent each client without a material adverse effect on the representation of, or loyalty to, the other. Consent from only the paying client or the first client does not satisfy the rule, because every affected client must consent. An outside opinion is a useful precaution, but it is not a substitute for that consent plus the solicitor's own honest belief.
  13. The correct answer is D (The solicitor cannot act without the former client's consent to that.): Rule 3.4-10 provides that, unless the former client consents, a solicitor must not act against a former client in the same matter, in any related matter, or in any other matter where the solicitor has relevant confidential information arising from the former representation that may prejudice that client. The valuation information was obtained on the company file, and the new claim is related to that same company. The form of the new proceeding and the closing of the old file do not remove the restriction.
  14. The correct answer is A (That timely measures remove any risk of disclosure of the information.): Rule 3.4-11 allows another lawyer in the firm to act in a new matter against a former client if the former client consents, or if the firm establishes that it has taken adequate measures on a timely basis so that there will be no risk of disclosure of the former client's confidential information to the lawyer who has carriage of the new matter. The former client's consent is an alternative, not an additional requirement where the firm can demonstrate an effective screen. Having met the directors, or moving paper files, does not by itself establish one.
  15. The correct answer is B (The firm may act if different lawyers represent each side.): Rule 3.4-16.7 prohibits an individual lawyer from acting for both the transferor and the transferee in a transfer of title to real property. Rule 3.4-16.8 creates an exception for a law firm of two or more lawyers where the transferor and the transferee are represented by different lawyers in the firm and there is no violation of section 3.4. A single office address and the price paid are irrelevant to that exception, and there is no rule confining the retainer to the vendor's side of the transaction.
  16. The correct answer is A (Rule 3.4-16.9, which allows it for related persons.): Rule 3.4-16.9 allows an individual lawyer to act for both the transferor and the transferee if the Land Registration Reform Act permits the lawyer to sign the transfer for both, or the two are related persons as defined in section 251 of the Income Tax Act (Canada), or the lawyer practises in a remote location where no other lawyer is reasonably available. A mother and her adult daughter are related persons, so the retainer may proceed, subject to the joint retainer advice in rules 3.4-5 and 3.4-6 and the consent requirement in rule 3.4-7. Rule 3.4-16.8 applies to firms, and rule 3.4-16.7 states the general prohibition that rule 3.4-16.9 qualifies.
  17. The correct answer is C (Rule 3.4-16.3, which excuses the usual conflict search.): Rules 3.4-16.2 to 3.4-16.6 govern lawyers who provide short term legal services under the auspices of a pro bono or not for profit provider with no expectation of continuing representation. Rule 3.4-16.3 allows the solicitor to provide those services without taking steps to determine whether there is a conflict of interest arising from duties owed to clients of the solicitor's firm or of the provider. The solicitor must still take reasonable measures to keep the client's information from other lawyers in the firm, and must stop if a conflict actually comes to light.
Case profes_02

A solicitor is retained by a new client, Dana Whitfield, to act on the purchase of a small commercial unit. Dana will pay the deposit and the balance of the purchase price from her own bank account, and those funds will pass through the solicitor's trust account. A numbered company will be the registered purchaser. Dana gives the solicitor a driver's licence and says she works as a restaurant manager. She gives no home address because she says she has been staying with friends, and she cannot produce the company's records because, she says, it was set up informally by a cousin overseas. The solicitor opens a file and asks the client intake clerk to collect what By-Law 7.1 requires.

  1. The correct answer is D (Her full name, home address and telephone number, and occupation.): Section 23(1) of By-Law 7.1 requires the licensee to obtain an individual client's full name, home address and home telephone number, occupation, and if applicable the business address and business telephone number. A licence number, date of birth and social insurance number are not items on that list, and the company's incorporation number belongs to the separate corporate identification exercise. Marital status and the name of the employer are also not required. The source of funds is a further requirement that applies because funds are being received.
  2. The correct answer is A (Its incorporation number, place of issue and nature of business.): For an organisation that is not a financial institution, public body or reporting issuer, By-Law 7.1, section 23(1) requires the licensee to obtain the organisation's incorporation or business identification number and the place of issue, if applicable, and the general nature of its business or activities, if applicable. The licensee must also obtain the name, position and contact information for each individual authorised to give instructions on the matter. Bank account numbers, revenue figures, employee counts and the incorporation date are not items the section names.
  3. The correct answer is B (Identify the senior managing officer and assess the fraud risk.): By-Law 7.1, section 23(2.2) applies where the licensee cannot obtain the section 23(2.1) information about an organisation or cannot confirm its accuracy. The licensee must take reasonable measures to identify the organisation's most senior managing officer, determine whether the client's information, the source of funds and the client's instructions are consistent with the purpose of the retainer, record the results and the date, and assess and record whether there is a risk of assisting in fraud or other illegal conduct. Closing the file immediately, accepting the explanation, and relying on a telephone confirmation do not meet those requirements.
  4. The correct answer is C (The names and addresses of persons owning 25 per cent or more.): Where the client is an organisation and funds are involved, By-Law 7.1, section 23(2.1) requires the licensee to obtain the name of each director, other than for a securities dealer, to make reasonable efforts to obtain the names and addresses of persons owning directly or indirectly 25 per cent or more of the organisation or its shares, and to obtain the names and addresses of all trustees and all known beneficiaries and settlors of a trust, plus information establishing the ownership, control and structure of the organisation. Employee lists, lender names and customer lists are not required by that section.
  5. The correct answer is B (Immediately for the individual, within 30 days for the organisation.): By-Law 7.1, section 23(5) requires verification of an individual's identity to be done immediately after the licensee first engages in the activity described in section 22(1)(b). Section 23(6) requires verification of an organisation's identity to be done immediately and in all cases not later than 30 days after the licensee first engages in that activity. The reversed order and the two blanket answers do not match the by-law, and both periods run from the same triggering activity under section 22(1)(b).
  6. The correct answer is D (No, because the by-law exempts those two sources of funds.): By-Law 7.1, section 22(3) removes the identification and verification requirements for certain funds, including funds paid to or received from a financial institution, funds received from the trust account of another licensee or a lawyer, and funds paid, received or transferred by electronic funds transfer. A bank advance sent by electronic transfer and a cheque drawn on another lawyer's trust account both fall within those exemptions. The $7,500 figure is a separate limit on cash under By-Law 9 and has nothing to do with this question.
  7. The correct answer is C (The longer of six years after the work and the relationship.): By-Law 7.1, section 23(14) requires the licensee to retain a record of the information obtained under sections 23(1) and 23(2), and copies of all documents received under section 23(4), for the longer of the duration of the licensee and client relationship and as long as necessary to provide service to the client, and a period of at least six years following completion of the work. A fixed two year period, six years from opening the file, or a ten year period does not match the by-law. The records may be kept in electronic form if a paper copy can be readily produced.
  8. The correct answer is C (The solicitor cannot accept cash above $7,500 for this file.): By-Law 9, section 4(1) provides that a licensee shall not receive or accept from a person, in respect of any one client file, cash in an aggregate amount of more than 7,500 Canadian dollars, and section 4(2) supplies the conversion rule for foreign currency. The exceptions in section 6 cover only specified funds, such as a fine, penalty or bail, and certain fee and disbursement payments, not a purchase deposit. Recording the cash in trust or obtaining a receipt does not enlarge the limit, and the by-law does not impose a blanket ban on cash.
  9. The correct answer is D (The solicitor must not testify and act as advocate in that trial.): Rule 5.2-1 provides that a lawyer who appears as advocate shall not testify or submit their own affidavit evidence before the tribunal unless permitted by law, the tribunal, the rules of court or the tribunal's rules of procedure, or unless the matter is purely formal or uncontroverted. The solicitor may continue to act as advocate and call other evidence, so complete withdrawal is not the answer. The client's consent does not remove the prohibition, and the solicitor cannot both give evidence and conduct the examination of the other witnesses.
  10. The correct answer is A (Rule 5.1-1, which requires resolute and honourable advocacy before tribunals.): Rule 5.1-1 provides that when acting as an advocate, a lawyer shall represent the client resolutely and honourably within the limits of the law while treating the tribunal with candour, fairness, courtesy and respect. Solicitors are covered because they act as advocates whenever they appear before a court or tribunal. Attacking the arbitrator personally and withholding a document that damages the client's case would breach that standard. Rule 5.2-1 deals with the lawyer as witness, rule 3.4-3 with opposing parties, and rule 7.2-1 with courtesy between practitioners.
  11. The correct answer is D (Disclose the error and do what can reasonably be done to fix it.): Rule 5.1-4 provides that a lawyer who discovers an inadvertent error or omission in the course of advocacy shall disclose it, subject to the confidentiality rule in section 3.3, and do all that can reasonably be done to rectify it. Because the error was the solicitor's own and it affects the accuracy of the tribunal's record, silence is not an option. Putting the decision to the client and withdrawing from the matter before the hearing resumes are not the steps the rule requires.
  12. The correct answer is D (It is a trust condition that the receiving lawyer must honour.): An undertaking is a promise given by a lawyer to do something or to refrain from doing something, while a trust condition is a condition attached to the delivery of a document, money or other property that the receiving lawyer must honour before dealing with the item. Because the vendor's lawyer accepted delivery on the stated condition, the arrangement binds that lawyer as a trust condition. It is not an undertaking given by the sender, it is not a request that can be ignored, and it has no effect as a court order.
  13. The correct answer is D (Refuse to give the undertaking until the funds are confirmed.): Rule 7.2-11 provides that a lawyer shall not give an undertaking that cannot be fulfilled and shall fulfil every undertaking given and honour every trust condition once accepted. Giving an undertaking while the availability of the funds is unknown would itself breach the rule, because the solicitor may be unable to perform it. Hoping the funds arrive, limiting the undertaking, and asking the other side to wait do not make the promise one that can safely be given.
  14. The correct answer is D (Resolve the overdrawn client trust balance immediately once it is discovered.): The Law Society's guidance on managing trust accounts is clear that no client trust balance should be overdrawn, and that any overdrawn balance must be resolved immediately once it is identified. Deferring the correction to year end leaves client trust money short for an extended period, which the guidance does not permit. Covering the shortfall by moving another client's trust money creates a second problem, since that other client's funds would then be used for a purpose unrelated to their own matter. Waiting for the next scheduled reconciliation ignores the immediacy the guidance requires once the overdrawn balance has already come to light.
  15. The correct answer is D (The records must be entered and posted so as to be current at all times.): Subject only to the recognized period allowed for preparing the monthly comparison record, the by-laws require the financial records to be entered and posted so as to be current at all times, not merely brought up to date on some periodic schedule. A quarterly update cycle falls well short of the continuous currency the rule demands outside that limited exception. Waiting for an announced audit before updating records would leave the books unreliable for ordinary day to day supervision of client money. Similarly, updating only when a client happens to request an accounting ignores the firm's own independent obligation to keep the records current regardless of any particular request.
  16. The correct answer is B (Replenish the trust shortfall and report the misapplication through LSO Connects.): When a lawyer inadvertently transfers or withdraws the wrong amount from trust, the lawyer must promptly correct the error by replenishing the trust shortfall and must also report the misapplication of trust funds to the Law Society through the lawyer's LSO Connects account. Waiting until the next annual reconciliation to correct the error leaves client trust money short in the meantime, contrary to the obligation to act promptly. Asking the client to simply accept a smaller payout would let the shortfall stand rather than correcting it. Replenishing trust is a firm obligation that does not depend on whether the affected client happens to raise an objection first.
  17. The correct answer is C (Replace the shortfall from the lawyer's own money and report it to the Law Society.): Where a client's cheque is returned for non sufficient funds after funds have already been disbursed from trust in reliance on it, the lawyer must replace the funds immediately from the lawyer's own money, must never allow the trust account to go short or misuse other clients' funds, and must report the situation to the Law Society through LSO Connects. Waiting thirty days for the client to sort things out leaves the trust account short in the meantime, which the guidance does not permit. Drawing on other clients' trust ledgers to cover the gap would misapply money that belongs to those unrelated clients. Treating the shortfall as a bad debt ignores the requirement to replenish trust immediately and to report the misapplication.
  18. The correct answer is B (Rule 7.1-3 of the Rules of Professional Conduct, unless doing so would breach privilege.): Rule 7.1-3 of the Rules of Professional Conduct requires a lawyer to report to the Law Society the misappropriation or misapplication of trust monies, unless reporting would be unlawful or would breach solicitor client privilege. Rule 3.6-1 instead deals with whether a fee or disbursement is fair, reasonable and properly disclosed, which is a different subject entirely. Rule 5.1-1 concerns resolute and honourable advocacy before a tribunal and has nothing to do with reporting a trust misapplication. Rule 3.1-2 sets the general standard of competent performance of legal services, not the specific duty to report mishandled trust money.
  19. The correct answer is B (At least the six year period immediately preceding the most recent fiscal year end.): The by-laws require a licensee to keep the general financial records, including the books of original entry and the client trust ledgers, for at least the six year period immediately preceding the licensee's most recent fiscal year end. A three year period falls short of what the by-laws actually require for these records. A twenty year period vastly overstates the general requirement and would only be appropriate for certain longer guideline recommendations that are not fixed by-law minimums. A one year period is far too short to satisfy even the shortest retention obligation that applies to firm financial records.
  20. The correct answer is B (At least the ten year period immediately preceding the most recent fiscal year end.): For the specific categories of records listed in the by-law, including the monthly comparison record required under the paragraph on trust reconciliation, the retention period is at least the ten year period immediately preceding the licensee's most recent fiscal year end, longer than the general six year minimum. An eight year period is close but does not match the figure the by-law actually specifies for these listed records. A fifteen year period instead reflects a general guideline for closed client files rather than the specific books and records retention rule. A twelve year period likewise does not correspond to any figure fixed by the by-law for these categories.
  21. The correct answer is C (At least ten years following the discharge of the mortgage or charge.): For records required in connection with a mortgage or other charge held in trust, the by-laws require retention for at least ten years following either the discharge of the mortgage or charge, or the day the licensee ceases to be trustee, whichever applies. A two year retention period is far shorter than the ten year minimum the by-law fixes for these mortgage related records. A five year period likewise falls well short of the required minimum. A twenty five year period overstates what the by-law itself requires, even though some firms may choose to retain records longer for their own reasons.
  22. The correct answer is B (Within thirty days of opening the new trust account.): For a newly opened trust account, the by-laws require the irrevocable written authorization directing the financial institution to disclose account information to the Law Society on request to be submitted within thirty days of opening the account. A fourteen day window is shorter than the period the by-law actually allows for submitting this specific authorization. A ninety day window and a sixty day window both overstate the time the by-law gives, and relying on either could leave the firm technically late. Getting the correct thirty day figure matters because missed deadlines under this rule can affect a licensee's standing with the Law Society.
  23. The correct answer is D (By March 31, 2027, according to the Law Society's transitional guidance.): The Law Society's guide to managing trust accounts states that existing trust accounts must have the required authorization submitted by March 31, 2027, a separate deadline from the thirty day rule that applies to newly opened accounts. December 31, 2025 is not the date the guidance actually sets for existing accounts. March 31, 2026 is close in form but is the wrong year for this particular transitional deadline. December 31, 2027 pushes the deadline later than what the guidance actually states, which could lead a firm to miss the real cutoff.
  24. The correct answer is C (The new account's own thirty day deadline runs separately from the existing account's deadline.): The thirty day authorization deadline runs separately for the newly opened trust account, counted from the date that account was opened, and is a different obligation from the March 31, 2027 transitional deadline that applies to accounts that were already open before the requirement began. It is not correct to say both situations share one identical deadline running from the same date, since they are governed by different provisions. The idea that a new account has no deadline simply because the firm already operates other accounts ignores the specific thirty day rule for newly opened accounts. Authorization obligations attach to trust accounts that are open and active, not to one that has already been closed.
  25. The correct answer is D (It must be filed by March 31, and a late licensee may be administratively suspended.): All licensees must file their Annual Report through LSO Connects by March 31 of each year, and a licensee who fails to file by that date may be administratively suspended. A December 31 deadline paired with a mere warning letter understates both the true date and the real consequence that can follow. A June 30 deadline is well past the actual filing date the Law Society sets each year. Suggesting that a late filer must retake the licensing examination invents a consequence that has nothing to do with the actual administrative suspension risk that applies.
  26. The correct answer is B (The financial institution and branch, and the transit and account number, for each account.): The Annual Report requires a licensee who operates mixed trust accounts to report, for each mixed trust account including a special electronic registration bank account, the name of the financial institution and branch along with the transit and account number, and this information is then shared with The Law Foundation of Ontario. Reporting only a single aggregate balance across all accounts would not identify the specific institution and account details the filing calls for. Listing every client whose money passed through the account during the year goes well beyond what the Annual Report actually asks for in this section. A general narrative about bookkeeping software says nothing about the specific institution and account identifiers the report requires.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (The sale is taxable, and the buyer may claim the new housing rebate.): A builder who sells a new or substantially renovated home makes a taxable supply, so the buyer pays GST or the federal part of HST on the price. An individual who buys the home for use as the individual's or a relation's primary place of residence is then eligible for the new housing rebate for the federal part of the tax if the conditions are met. The buyer's intention to occupy the home supports the rebate rather than making the sale exempt. The tax applies to the sale rather than at the later date of possession, and a rebate is available.
  2. The correct answer is A (There is no statutory 10 day rescission right on a resale unit.): The 10 day rescission right in the Condominium Act belongs to a purchaser who receives a disclosure statement and the condominium guide on a purchase of a new or proposed unit from a declarant, and a similar right applies within 10 days of a material change. The Act gives no equivalent statutory rescission right to a buyer of an existing unit from an owner, so the status certificate is not a cooling off notice. A resale buyer's protection is the contractual condition, the status certificate itself and the buyer's own due diligence. Notice to the owner or the residential character of the unit does not create a statutory right to rescind.
  3. The correct answer is D (The rebate is reduced because the value is between $1 million and $1.5 million.): The first time home buyers' GST/HST rebate gives a rebate of 100 per cent of the GST or the federal part of HST on a new home valued up to $1 million, and it reduces the tax on a new home valued between $1 million and $1.5 million. At $1,300,000 the buyer is in the phase out range, so a reduced rebate is available rather than no rebate. The 100 per cent rebate does not continue above $1 million, and the program is not limited to homes below $500,000. Ontario also offers a provincial new housing rebate of the provincial part of the HST, capped at $24,000 and available to a qualifying buyer of new construction used as a primary residence, without a first time buyer requirement.
  4. The correct answer is C (It is declared and paid when the conveyance is tendered for registration.): For a registered conveyance, the land transfer tax is paid when, or before, the conveyance is tendered for registration, and in practice it is declared and paid in the electronic land transfer tax affidavit through the registration system. The person who tenders the conveyance is liable, and the person who holds the beneficial interest after registration is also liable for tax payable under the provision. There is a 30 day payment rule, but it applies to an unregistered disposition of a beneficial interest rather than to a registered transfer. The tax is provincial rather than municipal, and it is not paid annually with property taxes.
  5. The correct answer is B (The condition is fulfilled, and the agreement becomes unconditional.): A condition precedent must be satisfied or waived by the date in the agreement, and once it is fulfilled the agreement becomes unconditional and both parties are bound to complete. A written confirmation records that the buyer accepts the agreement as binding. The condition does not stay open until closing, and the buyer cannot withdraw a waiver or confirmation at will. The agreement becomes binding when the condition is fulfilled or waived, not later on the closing date.
  6. The correct answer is C (The buyer is in breach, and the deposit is at risk of forfeiture.): Once a condition in the buyer's favour is waived in writing, the agreement becomes binding and the buyer must close on the closing date. A job loss after the waiver does not revive the condition or excuse performance, so the buyer is in breach and the deposit is exposed. The seller is not obliged to extend the closing date, although the parties are free to agree to an extension. The buyer's proper course is to negotiate with the seller rather than to demand the deposit back on the basis of the lost financing.
  7. The correct answer is B (The seller, who was ready to deliver the transfer and the keys.): Time is of the essence in a real estate agreement, so the closing must happen on the closing date unless the parties extend it or a court relieves against the strictness. A party that wants to complain about the other side's failure must be ready, willing and able to close, and the seller here had the transfer and the keys ready. The buyer did not have the funds in trust, so the buyer was not in a position to tender performance. A mortgage approval is not the same as having the money in place, and the payment of a deposit does not make the buyer ready to close.
  8. The correct answer is A (The closing date is binding unless the seller agrees to extend it.): A real estate agreement normally makes time of the essence, so the closing date is binding unless the parties agree to extend it or a court relieves against the strictness. A delay in the lender's funds does not give the buyer a right to a unilateral extension, and the buyer should not assume that a reasonable request will be granted. Registering a transfer without the funds in place would expose the buyer and the lawyer to serious risk. The buyer's proper course is to press the lender, ask the seller for an extension in writing, and be ready to tender if the extension is refused.
Case real_e_03

A lawyer acts for Mercedes Okafor, who agreed on 3 June 2026 to buy a legal duplex in Hamilton from Peter Nowak for $875,000. Both units are rented to tenants under monthly tenancy agreements, and the agreement of purchase and sale requires the seller to deliver vacant possession of both units on closing. The closing date is 4 September 2026. One tenant, Alan Reid, has lived in the upper unit for three years and tells the buyer's lawyer that he has no intention of moving. The seller has served no notice to end either tenancy. The buyer plans to move into the upper unit and to keep renting the lower unit after closing.

  1. The correct answer is A (The tenancies continue, and the buyer's claim is against the seller.): A residential tenancy is an interest that continues when the property is sold, so the buyer becomes the landlord under the existing tenancy agreements unless the tenancies are lawfully ended. The seller's failure to deliver vacant possession is a breach of the agreement of purchase and sale, and the buyer's remedy is against the seller rather than against the tenants. Title does not automatically end a tenancy, and the buyer cannot evict before taking title. Whether the buyer should close and claim damages or refuse to close depends on the advice the buyer receives and the strength of the buyer's position.
  2. The correct answer is A (At least 60 days, ending on the last day of a rental period.): A landlord may give notice on behalf of a purchaser who in good faith requires possession of the unit for residential occupation for at least one year by the purchaser or a listed family member, and the termination date must be at least 60 days after the notice and must be the last day of a rental period or the end of the term. The notice is available where the landlord has entered into an agreement of purchase and sale and the residential complex contains no more than three residential units. The 120 day period applies to a landlord's own use notice given under a different section, which also avoids the compensation requirement. The landlord who gives the notice on the purchaser's behalf must compensate the tenant, and that obligation does not shift to the buyer.
  3. The correct answer is C (The buyer must wait 12 months from the last increase and give 90 days' notice.): A landlord who is lawfully entitled to increase rent may do so only if at least 12 months have elapsed since the last rent increase for that tenant in that unit, or since the unit was first rented to that tenant. The landlord must also give at least 90 days' written notice of the increase on the form approved by the Board. Eight months have passed here, so the buyer must wait until the 12 month point and then serve the 90 day notice, which means the increase will take effect later than the anniversary. A tenancy does not have to end before rent can be increased, and 60 days is not the notice period.
  4. The correct answer is C (Seven days, because the amended period applies to all tenancies.): For non payment of rent the landlord may give a notice of termination effective not earlier than the 14th day after the notice, or the 7th day for a daily or weekly tenancy, but a 2025 amendment substitutes a flat 7 day period for all tenancies and comes into force on September 21, 2026. A notice given after that date therefore uses the 7 day period. The notice is void if the tenant pays the arrears and additional rent before the landlord applies to the Landlord and Tenant Board. The 20 day period applies to damage or interference notices, and 60 days belongs to other termination grounds.
  5. The correct answer is C (Seven years from the date the buyer takes possession of the home.): If a home was enrolled after December 31, 1990, a claim for damages because of a major structural defect may be made at any time during the major structural defect claim period, which ends on the seventh anniversary of the date of possession. The one year warranties cover workmanship, materials and code compliance, and the two year warranties cover matters such as water penetration, the building envelope and the electrical, plumbing and heating systems. The clock runs from possession rather than from the agreement, the permit or enrolment. Five years is not a warranty period under the plan.
  6. The correct answer is A ($20,000 plus accrued interest on the net principal amount.): For a condominium dwelling unit, the maximum payable out of the guarantee fund for a deposit claim is $20,000, plus accrued interest on the net principal amount, and the net principal amount is the lesser of $20,000 and the deposit paid less permitted deductions. The larger freehold limits do not apply to a condominium unit, even though this deposit was larger. The $60,000 and $100,000 figures belong to the freehold formula, and the $40,000 figure is the older freehold limit for agreements entered into between February 1, 2003 and December 31, 2017. The buyer's recovery is capped at the condominium amount regardless of the size of the deposit.
  7. The correct answer is B (Ask Tarion to transfer the warranty into the buyer's name.): Warranty coverage begins when the agreement of purchase and sale is signed and remains in effect for up to seven years even if the home is sold, so the coverage travels with the home. A later purchaser who wants Tarion assistance must ask Tarion to transfer the warranty into that purchaser's name. The buyer does not need to be the original purchaser, but the buyer does need to take that step, so doing nothing would leave the claim in the original owner's name. The remedy is under the warranty plan rather than against the original owner, and the builder does not issue new certificates for resales.
  8. The correct answer is D (It is a patent defect, and the buyer is expected to find it.): A patent defect is one that a reasonable inspection of the property would reveal, and the buyer is expected to discover it, so the seller's silence does not normally give a remedy. A latent defect is one that is not discoverable by reasonable inspection, and a seller who knows of a latent defect that makes the premises dangerous or unfit for habitation must disclose it. The distinction turns on discoverability rather than on whether the buyer asked questions. A defect that a reasonable inspection would reveal does not become a latent defect because the buyer did not look, and the other descriptions do not match the legal categories.
  9. The correct answer is B (A claim for misrepresentation, which may lead to rescission or damages.): A misrepresentation of a material fact that induces the buyer to enter the agreement can support a claim, and the remedy may be rescission of the agreement or damages, depending on the nature of the misrepresentation. A statement by the seller's agent about the property is the kind of representation that can found such a claim, because the buyer relied on it in making the offer. The claim does not depend on the statement being repeated in the agreement, and it does not depend on the statement appearing in a Seller Property Information Statement. A negligent or innocent misrepresentation can also give a remedy, so proof that the speaker knew the statement was false is not the only route.
  10. The correct answer is C (No, because a seller is not required to volunteer every fact about the property.): A sale of land is governed by the principle of buyer beware, so the seller is not required to volunteer every fact about the property, and the buyer is expected to make the inquiries that matter to the buyer. The exceptions are narrow: a misrepresentation by the seller, and a known latent defect that makes the premises dangerous or unfit for habitation, must be dealt with. Bee keeping in a barn does not fall within those exceptions on these facts, so silence gives no right to rescind. A fact that merely affects the buyer's enjoyment, without more, does not create a duty to disclose.
  11. The correct answer is B (It is not part of the contract, but it can support a misrepresentation claim.): A Seller Property Information Statement is a disclosure document, and unless the agreement of purchase and sale incorporates it, it is not one of the contract terms and does not give the buyer a contractual warranty. Statements in it can still support a claim in misrepresentation if they are inaccurate and the buyer relies on them. The statement does not replace the seller's duty to disclose a known latent defect that makes the premises dangerous or unfit. It is not required by the Planning Act or by any other statute that makes it a mandatory disclosure form.
  12. The correct answer is A (The buyer can proceed but should arrange inspections and rely on the agreement's conditions.): A Seller Property Information Statement is not a required form, so a seller who declines to complete one has not breached the agreement, and the buyer can still proceed. The buyer's protection then comes from the agreement's conditions, a careful inspection, and the seller's duty not to misrepresent the property. The refusal does not make the agreement unenforceable and is not, by itself, a misrepresentation. Requiring the statement as a condition of closing would have to be negotiated and written into the agreement, and a buyer who simply refuses to close risks being in breach.
  13. The correct answer is A (Specific performance, because each parcel of land is treated as unique.): A contract for the sale of land engages the remedy of specific performance because each parcel is treated as unique and damages are not an adequate substitute for the property the buyer bargained for. The buyer must be ready, willing and able to close, and this buyer has the funds. The return of the deposit would leave the buyer without the property, and damages for disappointment are not the ordinary measure in a breach of a real estate contract. An order for moving costs is a form of damages rather than a remedy that transfers the land.
  14. The correct answer is A (The buyer may apply for relief against forfeiture of the deposit.): A deposit paid under an agreement of purchase and sale is at risk if the buyer breaches, but a court may grant relief against penalties and forfeitures on the terms it considers just under the Courts of Justice Act. Where the seller resells for the same price, the seller has suffered little or no loss, and the buyer may ask the court to order the return of the deposit or the part that exceeds the seller's actual loss. The deposit is not automatically a penalty that belongs to the seller, and it is not automatically returned because the seller resold. The seller may apply the deposit against any loss it did suffer.
  15. The correct answer is B (Damages for the shortfall and the carrying costs, less the deposit.): Where a buyer breaches an agreement to buy land, the seller may keep the property, resell it and claim damages for the loss caused by the breach, which here includes the $40,000 shortfall and the $6,000 in extra carrying costs. The deposit is applied against that loss, so the seller cannot recover both the deposit and the full amount of the loss. The seller cannot claim the whole contract price while keeping the property, and a resale does not end the right to damages. Reselling is the seller's way of mitigating the loss, and it does not require the seller to give up the claim.
  16. The correct answer is A (Remit 25 per cent of the purchase price to the Receiver General within 30 days.): Where a purchaser acquires taxable Canadian property from a non resident who has not obtained a clearance certificate, and the purchaser did not conclude after reasonable inquiry that the vendor was resident in Canada, the purchaser is liable to pay 25 per cent of the excess of the purchase cost over the certificate limit. That amount must be remitted to the Receiver General within 30 days after the end of the month in which the property was acquired, and the purchaser may withhold it from the vendor. A 50 per cent rate applies to certain non capital real property and depreciable property rather than to a capital commercial building. Paying the vendor in full leaves the buyer personally liable, and the land transfer tax is a different tax.
  17. The correct answer is B (An assignment of leases and rents from the seller to the buyer.): The landlord's interest in the leases is transferred on closing by an assignment of leases and rents, which is why that document appears on a commercial closing agenda. The buyer's lawyer also confirms the state of the leases through a rent roll and copies of the leases, and may require estoppel certificates from significant tenants. A notice of rent increase does not transfer the landlord's interest, and there is no consent to sever leases from the freehold. A tenant's certificate of status is not part of the transfer of the landlord's interest.
  18. The correct answer is C (A concern the power of attorney is forged, lacking two independent witnesses.): The Law Society's red flags list notes that a titled spouse may try to sell a matrimonial home using a forged power of attorney, and flags the concern that the document may not have been witnessed by two independent witnesses as section 10 of the Substitute Decisions Act, 1992 requires. A single witness, and one connected to the titled spouse's own business, falls short of that independent two witness requirement rather than raising no concern at all. Reachability by telephone that day does not cure a witnessing defect, and a business partner's signature is not treated as equivalent to notarization under this guidance.
  19. The correct answer is B (Utility status and other off title inquiries, given the arrears or disconnection risk.): The Law Society's residential real estate practice guidelines expressly refer to utility status and other off title inquiries alongside zoning, building searches and the tax account, because a purchaser can be exposed to utility arrears or a service disconnected at closing. The seller's personal credit history and bankruptcy filings are not the categories these guidelines identify as belonging on this particular checklist. Zoning and building searches do not cover every off title risk on their own, which is exactly why utility status is listed as a separate item.
  20. The correct answer is C (A zoning search, confirming the permitted use and by-law compliance.): Zoning is imposed by municipal by-law under the Planning Act, and a zoning search or zoning memorandum confirms the permitted use, the zone standards and whether the building complies, which directly answers whether the planned daycare use is allowed. A tax certificate addresses the state of the tax account, not permitted use, and an executions search addresses judgment creditors rather than zoning. A survey shows physical boundaries and encroachments, not whether a particular business use is permitted under the by-law.
  21. The correct answer is C (Tell the client which searches will not be done and review the policy's exceptions.): Where title insurance is used, the lawyer must tell the client which searches will not be done, check what off title matters are not covered by the policy, obtain waivers where appropriate, and personally review the search reports and the draft policy exclusions and exceptions. Title insurance does not automatically cover every possible off title matter, which is exactly why this review obligation exists. Using title insurance is meant to change what searches are ordered, and the client's own retained lawyer, not the insurer's in-house counsel, must give this advice.
  22. The correct answer is B (The sale binds the purchaser, since the sheriff had not yet given notice.): Registered land is not bound by a writ of execution until the sheriff has complied with the statutory notice requirements, and a sale or transfer under such circumstances is not valid against a good faith purchaser who buys before the sheriff has complied, which is exactly the situation described. It is therefore incorrect to say a recorded writ always defeats a purchaser regardless of timing. The outcome does not depend on whether the price exceeded market value, and the purchaser is not required to personally pay off the judgment creditor to obtain this protection.
Case title__01

A lawyer acts for a purchaser buying a condominium unit in Mississauga from Grace Oduya, who acquired the unit eight weeks ago in an all cash purchase and has now listed it at a price $210,000 higher. No real estate brokerage is named on the listing, and the seller's lawyer says the deposit was paid by e-transfer directly to Grace Oduya rather than held in trust by a lawyer or brokerage. Grace Oduya's identification on file is a photocopy of a provincial driver's licence with a redacted photograph, and the retainer instructions arrive only by email from an address that does not match the name on the identification. Closing is scheduled for three weeks from now.

  1. The correct answer is B (The presence of one or more red flags does not necessarily mean fraud is occurring.): The Law Society's guidance states that the presence of one or more red flag indicators does not necessarily mean fraud is occurring, but that the pattern may merit further investigation by the lawyer. It does not treat a single indicator as automatic proof of fraud, and it does not limit the guidance to commercial deals. The guidance applies whether or not a brokerage happens to be involved in the transaction.
  2. The correct answer is B (Record it as a red flag, ask the client to clarify, and document the findings.): A deposit paid directly to the vendor rather than held in trust by a lawyer or the brokerage is one of the Law Society's listed red flags, so the lawyer should record it, ask the client to clarify the circumstances, and document what is learned before closing. Treating it as outside the lawyer's concern, or waiting until after closing, defeats the purpose of raising red flags while there is still time to act. Assuming it is simply a preference skips the inquiry the guidance calls for.
  3. The correct answer is D (Obtain proper government issued photo identification and examine it before proceeding further.): By-Law 7.1 requires a licensee to verify the identity of the client, and a redacted photocopy examined only by email correspondence does not meet that standard, so the lawyer should obtain and personally examine proper government issued photo identification. Relying on matching email correspondence does not verify identity, and accepting a redacted photocopy simply because it arrived quickly does not satisfy the rule either. The purchaser's own lawyer cannot verify the identity of a client that lawyer does not represent.
  4. The correct answer is B (Information about the source of the funds involved in the transaction.): When a licensee engages in or gives instructions about receiving, paying or transferring funds, By-Law 7.1 requires the licensee to also obtain information about the source of those funds. An employer's name and a business number are the kind of information collected for an organizational client, not what the source of funds rule requires here. A certified cheque and a brokerage letter are not what the source of funds obligation calls for, and no brokerage is even involved in this transaction.
  5.
