The correct answer is D (The clerk must first obtain an authorization from the Director of Land Registration.): A person may not submit a document for electronic registration, or search the electronic land registration system, without an authorization from the Director of Land Registration, so the clerk needs that specific authorization before doing either task. A supervising lawyer's verbal approval alone does not satisfy this statutory authorization requirement. Teraview access is not simply open to any firm employee, and there is no one year articling requirement tied to this particular authorization.
  6. The correct answer is D (A statement that this transfer is from an estate trustee to a beneficiary.): The regulation lists a statement that the transfer is from an estate trustee to a person beneficially entitled as one of the alternative solicitor statements available where the ordinary two lawyer statement does not fit, and that is exactly the situation here. A statement claiming separate lawyers represent unrelated parties would be inaccurate on these facts. There is no statement about waiving independent legal advice among the listed alternatives, and a solicitor statement is required for a transfer of this kind, not dispensed with entirely.
  7. The correct answer is A (A statement in the electronic document attesting to a fact the regulation requires be confirmed.): Every electronic document must contain the information the regulation requires, including statements attesting to matters such as the parties' authority, spousal status and the interest being transferred, and these are what lawyers call compliance with law statements or law statements, built directly into the electronic document itself. They are not a separate sworn paper affidavit, and they are not an informal email exchanged once registration is already finished. A lawyer, not a court registrar, is the one who makes and signs for completeness on these statements.
  8. The correct answer is C (Explain no statutory right exists on a resale, and negotiate a condition.): The ten day rescission right in the Condominium Act applies to a purchaser who receives a disclosure statement from a declarant selling a new or proposed unit, and the Act gives no equivalent statutory right to a buyer purchasing an existing unit from an owner, so the lawyer should explain that gap and instead negotiate a contractual condition to protect the buyer. It is therefore wrong to say the same right applies to every condominium purchase. The right does not depend on the seller's later consent, and it is not tied to whether a brokerage happens to be involved in the resale.
  9. The correct answer is A (One needs 35 days notice after 15 days default, the other needs 45.): Where a mortgage contains its own power of sale clause triggered by a stated default, the notice of exercising the power may not be given until the default has continued for at least 15 days, and the sale may not be made until at least 35 days after that notice is given. Where the statutory power of sale under section 24 applies instead, no sale may be made until 45 days' written notice has been given under Part III of the Act. Treating both mortgages as needing the identical 45 day period ignores the shorter contractual timeline available where the mortgage has its own clause. Sixty days appears in neither provision, and both mortgages plainly do require notice before a sale.
  10. The correct answer is C (Expenses, then interest and costs, then principal, then later claims, then the mortgagor.): The Mortgages Act directs that money received from a sale under the power of sale is applied first to expenses, then to interest and costs, then to principal, then to subsequent encumbrancers, then to a tenant's rent deposit, with any surplus finally going to the mortgagor. Paying the mortgagor before other claimants reverses the statutory priority entirely. Paying subsequent encumbrancers ahead of the selling mortgagee's own principal and interest also gets the order backward. The tenant's deposit is addressed later in the sequence, not ahead of the mortgagee's own recovery.
  11. The correct answer is C (No, because the mortgage has excluded the statutory power of sale entirely.): The statutory power of sale in section 24 applies only where the mortgage does not already contain a power of sale, or where the mortgage declares that Part II does not apply. Where the mortgage expressly excludes Part II and has no clause of its own, the mortgagee has no power of sale at all under the Act and must instead pursue a court remedy such as foreclosure or a judicial sale. The length of the default does not overcome an express exclusion written into the mortgage. Farm properties are not carved out of the power of sale provisions by a separate licensing regime, the outcome here turns entirely on what this particular mortgage says.
Case mortga_01

A mortgagee has decided to bring a foreclosure action against a mortgagor in default on a property near Peterborough. Two subsequent encumbrancers are registered against the property after the mortgagee's own charge. The mortgagee's lawyer has been instructed to draft the statement of claim and to advise on the procedural choices available once the action is started, both for the mortgagor and for each subsequent encumbrancer. So far nothing has been filed in response by anyone, and every deadline connected with responding to the claim is still running. The lawyer wants to walk through what happens at each stage of the action, from naming the correct defendants through to a possible final order.

  1. The correct answer is C (All persons interested in the equity of redemption in the property.): In a foreclosure action, all persons interested in the equity of redemption must be named as defendants, which is why the statement of claim in this kind of action, Form 14B, is drafted to capture the mortgagor and every subsequent encumbrancer with a registered interest. Naming only the mortgagor and a brokerage would leave the subsequent encumbrancers free to assert their interests later, defeating the purpose of the foreclosure. Naming only the subsequent encumbrancers and leaving out the mortgagor omits the very party whose equity of redemption is being foreclosed. Neighbouring landowners have no interest in the equity of redemption in this property and have no place in the action.
  2. The correct answer is A (Within the time for delivering a defence, or before being noted in default.): A defendant who wants to redeem in a foreclosure action must serve and file a request to redeem, Form 64A, within the time for delivering a statement of defence or before being noted in default, whichever comes first. Waiting until trial is far too late, since a defendant who has already been noted in default has lost the opportunity described in the rule. Thirty days after a final order of foreclosure is granted comes after the foreclosure has already been completed, which defeats the purpose of redeeming. The encumbrancer's own deadline does not depend on whether the mortgagor has filed a defence.
  3. The correct answer is A (Judgment for foreclosure signed by the registrar, subject to a reference if needed.): Where a defendant has been noted in default and has not filed a request to redeem, the plaintiff may require the registrar to sign judgment for foreclosure with a reference, or judgment for immediate foreclosure, depending on what the circumstances call for. This judgment is not the same as a final order of foreclosure, which comes later on default of payment under that judgment or the report on a reference. There is no requirement to restart the action with a new statement of claim once defendants have simply failed to respond. Foreclosure actions of this kind are not decided by a jury.
  4. The correct answer is A (A motion to the court without notice to the defaulting parties.): A final order of foreclosure may be obtained on default of payment under the judgment or the report on a reference, on a motion to the court without notice. There is no need for a fresh trial once judgment for foreclosure has already been signed and the reference has been completed. A new statement of claim is unnecessary, since the same action continues through to the final order. The Superintendent role described in that distractor is not part of the foreclosure process under the Rules of Civil Procedure.
  5. The correct answer is D (Serve and file a request for sale in the time the Rules allow.): A defendant who wants a sale instead of foreclosure must serve and file a request for sale, Form 64F, within the time for delivering a statement of defence or before being noted in default, the same period that applies to a request to redeem. Refusing to respond risks being noted in default, which forecloses the option described in the rule rather than preserving it. A land registrar has no authority to convert a court action from foreclosure into a sale. There is no need for an entirely separate action, since the request for sale is made within the existing foreclosure proceeding.
  6. The correct answer is B (The mortgagee is deemed to be the landlord under the existing tenancy.): A person who obtains title to a mortgaged residential complex by foreclosure or power of sale, including a mortgagee in possession, is deemed to be the landlord under the existing tenancy agreement. The lease is not automatically terminated by the change in ownership, so the tenant's rights continue under the Residential Tenancies Act. No new lease is required for the mortgagee to step into the landlord role and collect rent going forward. There is no thirty day vacate rule triggered simply by a change in who owns the building.
  7. The correct answer is D (The principal amount and the yearly or half yearly interest rate.): Where principal or interest is payable on a plan involving blended payments, no interest is chargeable, payable, or recoverable at all unless the mortgage contains a statement showing the principal amount and the rate of interest chargeable on it, calculated yearly or half yearly and not in advance. A single lump sum interest figure without the stated rate does not satisfy this requirement. A licence number belongs to separate provincial mortgage brokering legislation, not to this federal disclosure rule. An independent lawyer's acknowledgment is good practice in many transactions but is not what this particular section of the Interest Act demands.
Case wills__02

In 2016 Rosa, a widow, added her son, Dan, to the title of her Muskoka cottage. The transfer was prepared by a lawyer and created a joint tenancy between Rosa and Dan, and Dan paid nothing for his interest. Rosa told a friend at the time that she had added Dan to help with the bills and the upkeep. In 2021 the two argued, and Rosa wrote to Dan saying that she wanted the cottage to go to all three of her children when she died. Dan did not reply and the title was never changed. Rosa died in 2026. Her other two children say the cottage belongs to the estate, and Dan says it passed to him by survivorship.

  1. The correct answer is C (On Dan, because a gratuitous transfer to an adult child is presumed to be a trust.): Pecore v. Pecore holds that a gratuitous transfer from a parent to an adult child is presumed to be a resulting trust, so the child holds the property for the parent's estate unless the child proves that a gift was intended. The presumption of advancement is limited to transfers from a parent to a minor child and does not apply to independent adult children. Dan must therefore prove the intention to give, and the fact that the transfer created a joint tenancy on title does not by itself answer the question of beneficial ownership. The burden does not fall on the estate or on the challenging children.
  2. The correct answer is A (As a joint tenancy, because that intention appears on the face of the document.): Section 13(1) of the Conveyancing and Law of Property Act provides that where land is granted or conveyed to two or more persons other than executors or trustees, those persons take as tenants in common and not as joint tenants, unless an intention that they take as joint tenants sufficiently appears on the face of the document. Here the intention appears on the face of the transfer, so the legal title is held as a joint tenancy. The payment of consideration affects the separate question of beneficial ownership under Pecore, and the Act does not require the intention to appear in the register rather than the transfer.
  3. The correct answer is B (That the parties mutually treated their interests as a tenancy in common.): Hansen Estate v. Hansen confirms that a joint tenancy may be severed by a joint tenant acting on his or her own share, by mutual agreement, or by a course of dealing sufficient to show that the interests of all joint tenants were mutually treated as a tenancy in common. A unilateral letter that the other joint tenant ignores, without more, does not show the mutual treatment that a course of dealing requires. The register and the existence of a family disagreement do not by themselves sever the joint tenancy.
  4. The correct answer is D (The cottage is not included in the value of the estate for the tax.): The value of the estate for estate administration tax includes Ontario real property that belonged to the deceased at the time of death, less any encumbrance on real property included in the estate. Assets that pass by joint ownership to the other owner, and insurance proceeds payable to a named beneficiary, are not included. If Dan takes the cottage by survivorship, it did not form part of Rosa's estate and is not counted in the tax base, however valuable it is. The result would be different if the joint tenancy had been severed, because the interest would then fall into the estate.
  5. The correct answer is B (The gift is revoked and the will is read as if she had predeceased him.): Section 6(8) of the Family Law Act provides that when a surviving spouse elects to receive the entitlement under section 5, the gifts made to the spouse in the deceased spouse's will are revoked and the will is construed as if the surviving spouse had died before the deceased, unless the will expressly provides that the gifts are in addition to the section 5 entitlement. Section 6(9) also deems the electing spouse to have disclaimed the intestate entitlement under Part II of the Succession Law Reform Act. The gift is not preserved and offset, and an election does not create a support order.
  6. The correct answer is B (It is credited against her entitlement unless the deceased said otherwise.): Sections 6(6) and 6(7) of the Family Law Act provide that where a surviving spouse elects the section 5 entitlement and is the beneficiary of a life insurance policy on the deceased spouse's life that the deceased owned, the amount of the payment, less any contingent tax liability, is credited against the entitlement. The credit does not apply if the deceased provided in a written designation, will or other written instrument that the spouse is to receive the payment in addition to the entitlement. A valid designation is paid directly to the named beneficiary rather than into the estate.
  7. The correct answer is A (The joint tenancy is deemed to have been severed immediately before death.): Section 26(1) of the Family Law Act provides that if a spouse dies owning an interest in a matrimonial home as a joint tenant with a third person and not with the other spouse, the joint tenancy is deemed to have been severed immediately before the time of death. The deceased spouse's interest therefore falls into the estate instead of passing to the third person by survivorship. A matrimonial home is not excluded property, so its value is included in the deceased spouse's net family property, and a pre-marriage interest in a matrimonial home is not deducted.
  8. The correct answer is B (No distribution within six months of the death without the spouse's consent.): Section 6(14) of the Family Law Act provides that no distribution may be made in the administration of a deceased spouse's estate within six months of the death unless the surviving spouse consents in writing or the court authorizes it. Section 6(15) adds that no distribution may be made after the personal representative receives notice of an application under Part I unless the applicant consents or the court authorizes it. The rule does not prohibit reasonable advances to dependants for their support. Probate, the filing of the estate information return, or the payment of debts does not remove the six month restriction.
  9. The correct answer is C (The day immediately before the date of the first spouse's death.): Section 4(1) of the Family Law Act defines the valuation date as the earliest of several events, including the date the spouses separate with no reasonable prospect of resuming cohabitation, the date a divorce is granted, the date a marriage is declared a nullity, and the date before the date on which one of the spouses dies leaving the other spouse surviving. On a death, the valuation date is therefore the day before the death, and the assets and debts are valued as of that moment. Neither the date of the estate certificate nor the date of the election affects the valuation date.
  10. The correct answer is C (No, because a matrimonial home is not excluded property under the Act.): Section 4(2) of the Family Law Act excludes property acquired by gift or inheritance from a third person after the marriage, but it expressly excepts a matrimonial home, and property into which excluded property has been traced is also not excluded if the result is a matrimonial home. Using the inheritance to pay down the mortgage increased the equity in the matrimonial home, which remains part of net family property. A matrimonial home is not excluded property, and a pre-marriage interest in one is not deducted. The husband's lack of contribution does not decide the treatment of the home.
  11. The correct answer is A (Yes, because a child includes a person treated as a child of the family.): Section 57 of the Succession Law Reform Act defines a dependant to include the spouse of the deceased, a parent, a child, and a brother or sister, in each case where the deceased was providing support or was under a legal obligation to provide support immediately before death. The definition of child includes a grandchild and a person the deceased demonstrated a settled intention to treat as a child of the family, so a grandchild raised from childhood is not excluded. The fact that the gift in the will went to charity is no answer, because Part V allows the court to make provision out of the estate where adequate support was not made.
  12. The correct answer is B (No, because the adequacy of the provision is judged at the hearing date.): Section 58 of the Succession Law Reform Act allows the court to order that adequate provision be made out of the estate where the deceased did not make adequate provision for the proper support of a dependant, and section 58(4) states that the adequacy of the provision is judged as of the date of the hearing. Rising care costs are therefore relevant, and the fact that the gift seemed sufficient in 2018 does not freeze the question. Part V is the route for a dependant's support claim, and it is not limited to spouses.
  13. The correct answer is B (Allow the claim as to the undistributed portion if it considers that proper.): Section 61(1) of the Succession Law Reform Act provides that no application for a support order may be made more than six months after the grant of probate or letters of administration. Section 61(2) allows the court, if it considers it proper, to permit an application at any time as to any portion of the estate that is still undistributed at the date of the application. The $200,000 fund is undistributed, so the court has that discretion, but the assets already distributed are outside the scope of a late application. The limitation applies to any dependant, not only to spouses.
  14. The correct answer is B (A lump sum of money to be paid or held in trust for the dependant.): Section 63(2) of the Succession Law Reform Act sets out the kinds of provision the court may make. They include an amount payable annually or otherwise, a lump sum paid or held in trust, specified property transferred or assigned to or in trust for the dependant, possession or use of specified property, security for payment, and reimbursement of an agency that supported the dependant. The court does not revoke the will or distribute the estate on intestacy, and it does not award a fixed share of the residue as of right, because the provision is tailored to the dependant's needs.
  15. The correct answer is A (An order suspending the administration of the estate, wholly or partly.): Section 59(1) of the Succession Law Reform Act provides that on an application by or on behalf of the dependants, the court may suspend the administration of the estate, wholly or partly, for the time and to the extent the court decides. That suspensory order is the protective remedy when a claim may be defeated by a distribution before the hearing. It does not appoint the dependant as estate trustee, freeze the dependant's own assets, or require the repayment of distributions that have already been made.
  16. The correct answer is A (Some pre-death dispositions are treated as testamentary dispositions.): Section 72 of the Succession Law Reform Act provides that the capital value of certain dispositions made by the deceased before death, including listed dispositions of property and certain deposits and joint holdings, is included as a testamentary disposition as of the date of death and treated as part of the net estate available to satisfy a support order. Section 72(3) places the burden on the dependants to establish that the funds or property, or part of them, belonged to the deceased. There is no general two year power to set aside transfers, and the reach of Part V is not limited to assets owned at the moment of death.
  17. The correct answer is D (Yes, a listed public agency that has provided support may apply to the court.): Section 58(2) of the Succession Law Reform Act allows the application to be made by the dependant or by the dependant's parent, and section 58(3) allows a listed public agency, such as the Ministry, a municipality, a district social services administration board or a delivery agent under the Ontario Works Act, 1997, to apply where it is providing or has provided benefits, assistance or income support for the dependant. The agency may also apply where an application for that support has been made. The dependant's own failure to apply therefore does not close the door.
  18. The correct answer is D (Her husband takes the entire estate, to the exclusion of all others.): Section 44 of the Succession Law Reform Act provides that where a person dies intestate and is survived by a spouse and no issue, the spouse is entitled to the property absolutely. There is no preferential share to apply in this situation because there are no issue, and the rest of the intestacy ladder is not reached. The Crown takes only where there is no spouse, issue, parent, brother, sister, nephew, niece or next of kin.
  19. The correct answer is D (The preferential share of $350,000, and then one third of the residue.): Section 45 of the Succession Law Reform Act provides that where a person dies intestate leaving a spouse and issue, the spouse takes the whole estate if its net value is not more than the preferential share and takes the preferential share outright if the net value is greater. The prescribed preferential share is $350,000 for deaths on or after March 1, 2021. Section 46(2) then gives the spouse one third of the residue where there is more than one child, and the children share the other two thirds.
  20. The correct answer is A (The legacy plus the shortfall needed to reach the preferential share.): Section 45(3) of the Succession Law Reform Act deals with partial intestacy. Where the deceased left a will for some property and died intestate for other property, and is survived by a spouse and issue, the spouse takes the shortfall out of the intestate property if the spouse receives less than the preferential share under the will. The husband has $100,000 and the preferential share is $350,000, so he takes an additional $250,000 from the intestate bank account. If he had received more than the preferential share under the will, the preferential share rules would not apply to top him up.
  21. The correct answer is C (The wife and the child each take one half of the residue that remains.): Section 46(1) of the Succession Law Reform Act provides that where a person dies intestate leaving a spouse and one child, the spouse takes one half of the residue after the preferential share is paid and the child takes the other half. Section 46(2) applies only where there is more than one child, in which case the spouse takes one third of the residue and the children share the other two thirds. The spouse does not take the whole estate once the net value exceeds the preferential share.
  22. The correct answer is B (Apply to the Superior Court of Justice for its opinion and direction.): A trustee or personal representative may, without starting an action, apply to the Superior Court of Justice for the opinion, advice or direction of the court on any question about the management or administration of the trust property or the assets of a testator. A trustee who acts on the direction given is treated as having discharged the duty in that matter, unless there was fraud, wilful concealment or misrepresentation in obtaining it. Passing accounts and obtaining beneficiary consents serve different purposes.
  23. The correct answer is A (Income from property needed to pay those expenses is treated as income of the residue.): Unless a contrary intention appears from the will, the personal representative is not to apply income of the estate towards the capital of debts, funeral and testamentary expenses, taxes and legacies, and income from the property required for those payments is treated and applied as income of the residuary estate. If the assets are not sufficient to pay those disbursements in full, the income is applied to make up the deficiency. Treating the whole income as capital, or accumulating it, is not what the section requires.
  24. The correct answer is A (A fair and reasonable allowance, increased for the necessary professional services.): A trustee, guardian or personal representative is entitled to the fair and reasonable allowance that a judge of the Superior Court of Justice allows for the care, pains and trouble and the time spent. Where a lawyer is the trustee and has rendered necessary professional services to the estate, the allowance is increased by a fair and reasonable amount for those services. The 2.5 per cent figures are a case law guideline rather than a fixed fee.
  25. The correct answer is D (At least 60 days before the hearing date specified in the notice.): The notice of application to pass accounts, with a copy of the draft judgment sought, must be served at least 60 days before the hearing date where the person is served in Ontario. Where the person is served outside Ontario, the period is 75 days. The 35 day figure is the deadline for serving a notice of objection to the accounts, and 30 days is not the period for this service.
  26. The correct answer is C (Serve and file a notice of objection at least 35 days before the hearing.): A person served with the notice of application who wishes to object to the accounts must serve and file a notice of objection to accounts at least 35 days before the hearing date. The 60 day period is the minimum notice of the application itself rather than the deadline for objecting. Filing the objection in time preserves the beneficiary's right to be heard on the accounts.
  27. The correct answer is A (The rules for passing an estate trustee's accounts apply with necessary changes.): The rules that govern the passing of an estate trustee's accounts apply, with necessary modifications, to the accounts of trustees other than estate trustees, persons acting under a power of attorney, guardians of the property of incapable persons, guardians of the property of a minor, and persons with similar duties directed by the court to prepare accounts. The court may order that all or part of an attorney's accounts be passed on the application of the attorney, the grantor or a listed person. There is no summary audit by the Public Guardian and Trustee in place of that procedure.
  28. The correct answer is C (At the instance of a person interested in the property or a creditor.): An executor or administrator is not required by any court to render an account of the property of the deceased, otherwise than by an inventory, unless at the instance of a person interested in the property or of a creditor of the deceased. A written request from a beneficiary does not by itself compel an accounting, and the rule is not tied to the payment of estate administration tax. The section applies despite any contrary provision in a bond or security given by the executor or administrator.
  29. The correct answer is D (Order the trustee to pay damages for the loss caused by the default.): On passing accounts, the judge has power to inquire into any complaint or claim by a person interested in the taking of the accounts of misconduct, neglect or default by the executor, administrator or trustee that caused a financial loss to the estate or trust fund, and on proof of the claim may order the personal representative to pay such sum by way of damages or otherwise as the judge considers proper and just. The judge may instead order the trial of an issue of the complaint. Referring a complaint to the Law Society or setting aside a completed sale is not the remedy the section provides.
  30. The correct answer is D ($3,000, the amount for receipts of $300,000 or more but less than $500,000.): Where the court grants judgment on passing accounts without a hearing, costs are assessed in accordance with Tariff C. For an estate trustee, the tariff allows $2,500 where the receipts are less than $300,000, $3,000 where they are $300,000 or more but less than $500,000, and $3,500 where they are $500,000 or more but less than $1,000,000. Receipts of $420,000 therefore fall in the $3,000 band.
  31. The correct answer is C (One half of the amount payable to the estate trustee for the tariff.): Under Tariff C, a person with a financial interest in an estate who retains a lawyer to review the accounts, makes no objection or withdraws one, and serves and files a request for costs is entitled to one-half of the amount payable to the estate trustee. The Children's Lawyer and the Public Guardian and Trustee are entitled to three-quarters in the same circumstances. A person represented by the same lawyer as another beneficiary receives only one person's costs.
  32. The correct answer is B (Yes, because success does not protect a party from an award of costs.): The court exercises its discretion over costs under the Courts of Justice Act and the factors in the rules, and the fact that a party is successful in a proceeding or a step in a proceeding does not prevent the court from awarding costs against that party in a proper case. A passing of accounts heard without a hearing is assessed under Tariff C, but that does not remove the court's general discretion where conduct has caused unnecessary expense. Costs may also be awarded to a party acting in person.
  33. The correct answer is A (The care, skill, diligence and judgment of a prudent investor.): In investing trust property, a trustee must exercise the care, skill, diligence and judgment that a prudent investor would exercise in making investments, and may invest in any form of property in which a prudent investor might invest. The standard is not the care an ordinary person would take with personal money, and it is not a promise that no loss will occur. The same standard applies to a trustee who holds for a life tenant and a remainderman.
  34. The correct answer is C (The expected tax consequences of investment decisions or strategies.): The listed criteria include general economic conditions, the possible effect of inflation or deflation, the expected tax consequences of investment decisions or strategies, the role each investment plays in the overall portfolio, the expected total return from income and appreciation, needs for liquidity and preservation or appreciation of capital, and any special relationship or value of an asset to the purposes of the trust or to a beneficiary. A trustee must consider those criteria in planning the investment of trust property. The personal preferences of the trustee and the fees charged are not among them.
  35. The correct answer is D (The trustee may be liable, because a written plan and agreement were required.): A trustee may authorize an agent to exercise the trustee's functions relating to the investment of trust property only to the extent that a prudent investor acting in accordance with ordinary investment practice would authorize an agent, and only if the trustee has prepared a written plan or strategy and a written agreement with the agent is in effect. Reliance on advice is a different matter, because it is not a breach of trust for a trustee to rely on advice if a prudent investor would rely on it in comparable circumstances. Delegation without the required documents leaves the trustee exposed for the loss.
  36. The correct answer is A (No, reliance on the advice is not a breach in those circumstances.): A trustee may obtain advice in relation to the investment of trust property, and it is not a breach of trust for a trustee to rely on that advice if a prudent investor would rely on the advice under comparable circumstances. The protection depends on the standard of a prudent investor rather than on a meeting with the beneficiaries or a court order. A trustee is not answerable for every decline in the value of the trust property.
Case estate_02

Rosa granted a continuing power of attorney for property to her nephew Ivan in 2019. In 2021 she suffered a stroke and became incapable of managing her property. Ivan has paid her bills from her account since then, and he has also transferred $40,000 of her money to himself in three payments. Ivan says the transfers were compensation for his work, although the power of attorney says nothing about compensation and no fee scale was agreed. He has kept no records of the payments out of Rosa's account and cannot explain the balance. Rosa's daughter Lina has asked the court to require Ivan to pass the accounts of his dealings with her mother's property.

  1. The correct answer is A (The duty to keep accounts of all transactions involving the property.): An attorney acting under a continuing power of attorney must keep accounts of all transactions involving the grantor's property when the grantor is incapable of managing property, and that duty mirrors the one imposed on a guardian of property. The accounts may be ordered passed by the court. Annual filing with the Ministry of Finance and approval of each payment by the Public Guardian and Trustee are not requirements of the Act.
  2. The correct answer is B (The procedure for passing executors' accounts, applied with necessary changes.): The court may order that all or part of an attorney's accounts be passed, and the procedure is the same as in the passing of the accounts of executors and administrators. The estates rules apply to attorneys, guardians of property and others with similar duties when the court directs them to prepare accounts. The Consent and Capacity Board reviews findings of incapacity rather than the accounts of an attorney.
  3. The correct answer is A (He may take compensation under the prescribed fee scale, but the excess is not justified.): An attorney under a continuing power of attorney for property may take annual compensation in accordance with the prescribed fee scale, and may take more only with the written consent of the Public Guardian and Trustee and the incapable person's guardian of the person or attorney for personal care, or with the court's approval. The power of attorney in this case is silent on compensation, so the fee scale is the measure, and good faith does not create an entitlement to more. A transfer to the attorney who is also unable to account for it is not a gift that the Act permits.
  4. The correct answer is D (He may take annual compensation in accordance with the prescribed fee scale.): An attorney under a continuing power of attorney for property may take annual compensation from the property in accordance with the prescribed fee scale, and may take it monthly, quarterly or annually. The right is subject to any compensation terms in the power of attorney itself, but a silent power of attorney does not remove it. An estate trustee's compensation is fixed by the court under the Trustee Act, which is a different regime.
  5. The correct answer is B (The note is not property, so the shares cannot be issued for it.): A share must not be issued until the consideration for it is fully paid in money or in property or past service that is not less in value than the fair equivalent of the money the corporation would have received. A promissory note or other document evidencing the indebtedness of the person to whom the shares are to be issued is not property for that purpose. Valuation by the board, a pledge of other shares and an interest charge do not convert the note into permitted consideration.
  6. The correct answer is C (Shares issued by the corporation are non-assessable and the holders are not liable.): Shares issued by a corporation are non-assessable, and the holders are not liable to the corporation or to its creditors in respect of them. Insolvency, a resolution of the directors calling for funds and the corporation's outstanding debts do not create an assessment right. The protection applies once the shares are validly issued for fully paid consideration.
  7. The correct answer is D (Add the full amount of the consideration to the stated capital account.): The corporation must maintain a separate stated capital account for each class and series of shares it issues, and must add to the appropriate account the full amount of the consideration it receives, as determined by the directors. The amount is not a liability to the shareholder, and because Ontario shares have no par value there is no par value to separate out. The consideration is allocated to the class of shares that was issued.
  8. The correct answer is A (It is permitted, because each basic right need only attach to a class.): Where the articles provide for more than one class of shares, the rights, privileges, restrictions and conditions of each class must be set out in the articles, and the voting right and the right to receive the remaining property on dissolution must each be attached to at least one class. They are not required to be attached to the same class. A unanimous shareholder agreement is not needed for the structure itself.
  9. The correct answer is B (In money or property, or by issuing fully paid shares.): Subject to its articles and any unanimous shareholder agreement, the directors may declare and the corporation may pay a dividend by issuing fully paid shares, or options or rights to acquire fully paid shares, and, subject to the solvency test, may pay a dividend in money or property. A dividend is not restricted to one of those forms. Shareholder approval is not the statutory mechanism for an ordinary dividend.
  10. The correct answer is C (Whether the corporation can pay its liabilities as they become due.): The directors must not declare, and the corporation must not pay, a dividend if there are reasonable grounds for believing that the corporation is or, after the payment, would be unable to pay its liabilities as they become due. A profit in the current year, shareholder approval and the form of earlier dividends are not substitutes for that test. The second branch of the test addresses the realizable value of the corporation's assets.
  11. The correct answer is D (No, because realizable assets would fall below liabilities plus stated capital.): Under the second branch of the test, the directors must not declare a dividend if there are reasonable grounds for believing that the realizable value of the corporation's assets would thereby be less than the aggregate of its liabilities and its stated capital. After the proposed $60,000 payment, realizable assets would be $740,000, which is below the $750,000 aggregate. The comparison is not simply assets against liabilities, and there is no limit based on one quarter of stated capital.
  12. The correct answer is B (Not later than 18 months after the corporation comes into existence.): The directors must call an annual meeting of shareholders not later than 18 months after the corporation comes into existence, and afterwards not later than 15 months after holding the last preceding annual meeting. The 12 month and 15 month periods do not apply to the first meeting, and the fiscal year rule belongs to the Canada Business Corporations Act. The directors may also call a special meeting at any time.
  13. The correct answer is B (The meeting may be held electronically if all persons can reasonably participate.): Subject to the articles or by-laws and to the reasonable participation requirement, a shareholders' meeting may be held entirely by telephonic or electronic means, and a person who attends or votes by those means is deemed present in person. The registered office rule, the approval of the Director and the agreement of every shareholder are not statutory requirements. The articles or by-laws may instead limit the permitted manners of holding a meeting.
  14. The correct answer is D (Yes, a written resolution signed by all the voting shareholders satisfies it.): A written resolution dealing with all matters required by the Act to be dealt with at a shareholders' meeting, signed by all the shareholders entitled to vote at that meeting, satisfies all the requirements of the Act relating to that meeting. There is no rule requiring a physical meeting, and a separate resolution of the directors is not the mechanism. A written resolution signed by all the shareholders is as valid as one passed at a meeting.
  15. The correct answer is D (The shareholders must authorize the sale as the Act requires.): The directors may sell, lease or exchange all or substantially all the property of the corporation only if the sale is authorized by the shareholders as the Act requires. The articles are deemed to state that the directors may sell or dispose of property, but that general power is subject to the requirement for a sale of all or substantially all the property. Creditor approval and a court order are not the statutory conditions.
  16. The correct answer is A (Assignments or novations, with any consent required by the contracts.): In an asset purchase, contracts, licences and employment relationships do not transfer automatically, so each must be assigned or novated and any consent required by the contract must be obtained. A court order and a new corporation are not the mechanism. Automatic transfer is not the rule in Ontario for an asset sale.
  17. The correct answer is B (An asset purchase, where only the identified assets and assumed liabilities transfer.): In a share purchase the corporation keeps its legal personality, its contracts and its liabilities, so the purchaser acquires them indirectly along with the shares. In an asset purchase, only the identified assets and any liabilities the purchaser agrees to assume transfer. A warranty or indemnity may give the purchaser a remedy against the seller, but it does not leave the liability behind, and an amalgamation is not the structure used for that purpose.
  18. The correct answer is C (The Ontario Securities Commission may apply for an oppression order.): A complainant, and in the case of an offering corporation the Ontario Securities Commission, may apply for an oppression order. The auditors do not have that standing, and the court does not start the application on its own motion. There is no 10 per cent shareholding threshold for bringing an oppression application.
  19. The correct answer is C (An order directing the corporation or another person to buy her shares.): On an oppression application the court may make any interim or final order it thinks fit, including an order directing the corporation or any other person to purchase the securities of a security holder, or to pay the security holder part of the money paid for securities. A free transfer of the majority shareholder's shares is not among the listed orders. The court is not limited to dismissing the application because valuation is difficult, and a return of the original price is not the only remedy available.
  20. The correct answer is D (No, because the corporation cannot pay its liabilities as they become due.): The corporation must not make a payment to a shareholder under the relevant oppression orders if there are reasonable grounds for believing that the corporation is or, after the payment, would be unable to pay its liabilities as they become due, or that the realizable value of its assets would be less than the aggregate of its liabilities. A court order does not override that restriction. Comparing the price only with total assets ignores the liabilities, and the court may in principle order a purchase of shares.
  21. The correct answer is D (No, a shareholder has no dissent right when the articles are amended under that remedy.): The Act provides that a shareholder is not entitled to dissent if an amendment to the articles is effected under the oppression remedy. The dissent right in the fundamental changes provisions is therefore not available for that amendment. The loss of the right does not depend on whether the shareholder objected, and the court order does not remove the shareholder's other rights.
Case busine_02

Northline Distribution Inc. and Ridgeway Logistics Ltd. are Ontario corporations with unrelated shareholders. Their boards have negotiated a merger under which the two businesses would continue as one corporation called Northline Ridgeway Inc. The parties have signed a written amalgamation agreement that sets out the terms of the merger, the shares that the shareholders of each corporation will receive and the proposed by-laws of the new corporation. Ridgeway is not a subsidiary of Northline. Ridgeway has a class of preferred shares whose holders are entitled to vote separately on the amalgamation. The boards want to know what approvals and filings the Act requires before the amalgamation can be completed.

  1. The correct answer is C (A special resolution of the holders of each class entitled to vote.): An amalgamation agreement is adopted when the shareholders of each amalgamating corporation approve the amalgamation by a special resolution of the holders of the shares of each class or series entitled to vote on it. An ordinary resolution and a resolution of the directors are not sufficient for an ordinary amalgamation. The Act does not require the written consent of every shareholder, although a dissenting shareholder who follows the statutory procedure is entitled to be paid fair value.
  2. The correct answer is A (Articles of amalgamation and any other required documents.): After an amalgamation has been adopted or approved, articles of amalgamation and any other required documents and information must be sent to the Director. The amalgamation agreement itself is not the document that is filed; the articles of amalgamation are. A court order is part of the arrangement procedure rather than an ordinary amalgamation, and creditor consents are not required.
  3. The correct answer is B (Yes, a holding corporation and its subsidiary may amalgamate that way.): A holding corporation and one or more of its subsidiary corporations may amalgamate and continue as one corporation without complying with the amalgamation agreement and shareholder approval provisions if the amalgamation is approved by a resolution of the directors of each amalgamating corporation and all of the issued shares of each amalgamating subsidiary are held by one or more of the other amalgamating corporations. The ordinary shareholder approval rule therefore does not apply on those facts. A subsidiary may amalgamate with its parent, and the separate consent of preferred shareholders is not part of this short-form route.
  4. The correct answer is C (That a dissenting shareholder is entitled to be paid fair value.): The notice of meeting must include or be accompanied by a copy or summary of the amalgamation agreement and a statement that a dissenting shareholder is entitled to be paid fair value under the dissent provisions. Failure to make that statement does not invalidate the amalgamation, so it is a required disclosure rather than a condition of validity. Shareholders remain free to vote against the agreement, and the other statements are not required.
  5. The correct answer is A (The guarantee must be in writing and signed by the founder.): Section 4 of the Statute of Frauds provides that no action may be brought on a promise to answer for the debt, default or miscarriage of another person unless the agreement, or a memorandum or note of it, is in writing and signed by the party to be charged or by someone lawfully authorized. Signing by the founder or an authorized agent therefore satisfies the requirement. A guarantee does not need to be registered under the Personal Property Security Act unless it is supported by security. Witnessing by a lawyer is prudent but is not a statutory condition, and board approval concerns the corporation rather than the guarantor's obligation.
  6. The correct answer is B (The election is not available, because the supplier is a registrant and the purchaser is not.): Section 167(1)(b) makes the joint election unavailable where the supplier is a registrant and the recipient is not a registrant, so the parties cannot use it on these facts. Even where the election is available, tax still applies to a taxable supply of a service to be rendered by the supplier, to a supply by way of lease or licence, and to a taxable sale of real property to a recipient who is not a registrant. Agreement by the purchaser to account for the tax later does not restore the election. The all or substantially all test is one condition, but it does not overcome the registrant requirement, and paying tax and claiming a rebate is a different mechanism.
  7. The correct answer is D (The accredited investor exemption in Ontario is in section 73.3(2) of the Securities Act.): The accredited investor exemption in Ontario is the exemption in section 73.3(2) of the Securities Act. Section 2.3(8) of National Instrument 45-106 states that subsection 2.3(1) does not apply in Ontario, so the national instrument provision is not the source here. Section 53(1) contains the prospectus requirement rather than the exemption. Section 2.4(1) of the instrument defines a private issuer and supports a different exemption.
  8. The correct answer is C (A signed risk acknowledgement obtained before the purchase agreement is signed.): Where an individual relies on the income, net assets or financial assets tests, the person distributing the security must obtain a signed risk acknowledgement at the same time as, or before, the investor signs the purchase agreement, and must retain it for eight years after the distribution. The investor must indicate how the accredited investor criteria are met, and any salesperson who met with the investor must be identified. A source of funds declaration is not the required document. A prospectus receipt is not needed for an exempt distribution, and a waiver of investor rights is not part of the requirement.
  9. The correct answer is A (It does not qualify, because a private issuer cannot have more than 50 holders.): Under National Instrument 45-106, a private issuer must not be a reporting issuer or an investment fund, its securities other than non-convertible debt must be subject to transfer restrictions, and its securities must be beneficially owned by not more than 50 persons, not counting employees and former employees. Family members are counted, so a corporation with 60 beneficial holders falls outside the definition. Transfer restrictions are one condition of the test but do not cure the holder count, and a private issuer is by definition not a reporting issuer, so the absence of reporting issuer status supports rather than defeats the exemption.
  10. The correct answer is A ($10,000, $30,000 and $100,000, depending on the purchaser's status and advice.): For the offering memorandum exemption in Ontario, the acquisition cost of all securities acquired under that subsection in the preceding 12 months must not exceed $10,000 for a purchaser who is not an eligible investor, $30,000 for an eligible investor, or $100,000 for an eligible investor who received suitable advice from a portfolio manager, investment dealer or exempt market dealer. There is no limit for an accredited investor, for a purchaser under the family, friends and business associates exemption, or for a purchaser that is not an individual. The other figures stated do not match the instrument.
  11. The correct answer is A (An offering memorandum in the required form and a signed risk acknowledgement.): The issuer must deliver an offering memorandum in the required form, Form 45-106F2, and obtain a signed risk acknowledgement in Form 45-106F4 at the same time as, or before, the purchaser signs the agreement to purchase. The memorandum must contain enough information for a reasonable purchaser to make an informed investment decision, and it must not contain a misrepresentation. A prospectus and receipts are not used for an exempt distribution. A rights offering circular or an annual information form belongs to a different kind of transaction.
  12. The correct answer is D (An investment fund cannot use it, and audited statements are due within 120 days.): The offering memorandum exemption is not available in Ontario to an issuer that is an investment fund, and the security distributed must not be a specified derivative or a structured finance product. Separately, an issuer that is not a reporting issuer and relies on the exemption must deliver annual financial statements to the securities regulatory authority within 120 days after the end of each financial year and make them reasonably available to each holder, and those statements must be audited, with a limited exception for comparative information that was not previously audited. Disclosure in the memorandum does not cure the investment fund exclusion, and the audited statement requirement is not limited to issuers whose statements are already audited.
  13. The correct answer is C (A report of exempt distribution in Form 45-106F1, filed within 10 days.): An issuer that distributes its own securities under a listed exemption, including the accredited investor exemption, must file a report of exempt distribution in Form 45-106F1 in the jurisdiction where the distribution takes place no later than 10 days after the distribution, and the form is filed electronically through SEDAR+. Where distributions are made under exemptions with different deadlines, the earliest deadline governs, and a single form may cover distributions within a 10 day period. A prospectus amendment and a material change report belong to the prospectus and reporting issuer regimes, not to this requirement.
  14. The correct answer is C (A close personal friend of one of the corporation's directors.): The private issuer exemption permits sales to directors, officers, employees, founders and control persons of the issuer, to directors, officers and employees of an affiliate, to specified close family members of those persons, and to close personal friends and close business associates of a director, executive officer, founder or control person. A friend of a supplier, an employee of a competitor and a shareholder of an unrelated company do not fall within those categories. A subscriber who falls outside the categories means the distribution must rely on another exemption.
  15. The correct answer is C (It is the long-stop rescission period where no disclosure was provided.): The two year figure in the Arthur Wishart Act is the long-stop rescission right in section 6(2): a franchisee may rescind no later than two years after entering into the agreement if the franchisor never provided a disclosure document. The damages action for a misrepresentation or for a failure to comply with the disclosure rules is in section 7 and runs on the general limitation period, not on a two year figure fixed by the Act. A material change statement must be delivered as soon as practicable after the change and before the earlier of signing and payment. The Act does not set a two year termination notice period.
  16. The correct answer is D (Control of the account through an agreement with the securities intermediary.): A secured party has control of a securities account in the circumstances set out in the Personal Property Security Act, including an agreement with the securities intermediary, and a security interest of a secured party that has control has priority over one that does not. Perfection of a security interest in the securities account also perfects the interest in the security entitlements carried in it. Registration alone does not give control. Physical possession of certificates does not give control of an account held with an intermediary, and a waiver from other lenders is not the mechanism the Act provides.
  17. The correct answer is D (Register a financing statement covering the machine in the registry.): A lease of goods for a term of more than one year falls within the Personal Property Security Act, and the lessor's interest is included in the definition of a security interest. The lessor should register a financing statement so that its interest is perfected against a trustee in bankruptcy and other creditors. Relying on the lease document alone leaves the interest unperfected. A fixture filing is not needed where the machine remains personal property, and a shareholder guarantee addresses a different risk.
  18. The correct answer is C (It confirms the registry priority and binds the two lenders by contract.): Priority between the two lenders can be arranged by contract, and a subordination clause records that the junior lender accepts the first lender's position. The clause binds the parties who agreed to it, and the registry already produces the same ranking on these facts. An agreement between two lenders does not by itself bind strangers who did not consent, so the clause does not rewrite priority against everyone. The Act does not prohibit lenders from agreeing to rank their interests, and the clause does not reverse the ranking.
  19. The correct answer is D (A distribution requires a prospectus unless an exemption applies to it.): Section 53(1) of the Securities Act prohibits a trade in a security that would be a distribution unless a preliminary prospectus and a prospectus have been filed and receipts issued. The obligation therefore starts with the prospectus requirement, and the next step is to see whether an exemption applies. Reporting issuer status is not the trigger, because the requirement applies to distributions by issuers that are not reporting issuers as well. The dealer used and the place of incorporation do not remove the requirement.
Case commer_02

A woman signed a five year franchise agreement for a coffee shop in June. The franchisor gave her a disclosure document six days before she signed the agreement and had not given her any earlier document. The disclosure document did not include the financial statements required by the statute, and it omitted a pending lawsuit against the franchisor. The franchise has lost money since it opened, and the franchisor has refused to allow the franchisee to join a franchisee association. She consults a lawyer in September of the same year.

  1. The correct answer is D (The document must be received at least 14 days before signing or payment.): Section 5(1) requires the franchisor to give the prospective franchisee the disclosure document not less than 14 days before the earlier of the signing of the franchise agreement or any related agreement and the payment of any consideration relating to the franchise, with a limited exception for a confidentiality or site designation agreement and a refundable deposit. Six days is too short, so the disclosure was late. Seven days does not meet the requirement. Delivery at signing or a delivery after signing does not satisfy the waiting period.
  2. The correct answer is C (Give written notice of rescission to the franchisor within 60 days.): Section 6(1) allows a franchisee to rescind without penalty or obligation no later than 60 days after receiving the disclosure document if the franchisor failed to provide it within the required time or if its contents did not meet the statutory requirements. The franchisee exercises the right by giving written notice of rescission to the franchisor. A court action and the franchisor's consent are not conditions of the right. The securities regulator has no role in a franchise disclosure dispute.
  3. The correct answer is A (She may rescind, and the two year long-stop period has not expired.): Where the franchisor never provided a disclosure document, section 6(2) gives the franchisee a rescission right that may be exercised no later than two years after entering into the franchise agreement. Three months after signing is well within that period, so the right is available. The franchisee does not lose the right by signing without objection, and she is not required to prove losses. The franchisor's agreement is not a condition of the statutory right.
  4. The correct answer is A (Every franchise agreement imposes a duty of fair dealing on both parties.): Section 3(1) provides that every franchise agreement imposes on each party a duty of fair dealing in its performance and enforcement, and section 3(2) gives a party a right of action for damages against another party who breaches that duty. The duty includes acting in good faith and in accordance with reasonable commercial standards. It applies to both parties and does not depend on an express clause, because the Act imports it into every agreement. Section 4(1) separately allows franchisees to form or join an organization of franchisees.
Case profes_03

Priya Sandhu practises alone and operates one mixed trust account and a separate interest bearing account for a single estate. On April 18 she reviews the March records, which a bookkeeper prepared. The bank statement for the mixed account shows $214,600 at month end, while the total of the unexpended balances in the clients' trust ledger is $219,100. One client's ledger shows a balance of $12,400, but only $7,900 was received on that file, because the bookkeeper posted a $4,500 cheque to the wrong client's ledger account. The bank also charged $32 in service fees directly against the trust account. Priya confirms the $4,500 was paid to the correct payee and that no client is owed the $32.

  1. The correct answer is B (April 25, which is within 25 days after the end of March.): By-Law 9, section 22(2) requires the reconciliation and comparison record under paragraph 8 of section 18, and the record under paragraph 3 of section 20, to be created within 25 days after the last day of the month being reported. For March that means April 25, and the Law Society's trust account guide gives the same example. Month end, the end of the following calendar month, and a 45 day window are not the deadlines the by-law sets.
  2. The correct answer is D (Replace the funds at once and report the misapplication through LSO Connects.): The Law Society's trust account guidance requires a lawyer who misapplies trust funds to correct the error promptly by replenishing the shortfall and to report the misapplication to the Law Society by submitting a complaint through LSO Connects. Rule 7.1-3 of the Rules of Professional Conduct also requires a lawyer to report the misappropriation or misapplication of trust money. Replacing the money without reporting leaves a reportable event undisclosed, and taking money from another client's account would create a second misapplication.
  3. The correct answer is C (All money received and disbursed, and any unexpended balance.): By-Law 9, section 18 requires, among its minimum records, a clients' trust ledger showing separately for each client all money received and disbursed and any unexpended balance. Section 18 also requires a book of original entry for money received in trust, a book of original entry for disbursements out of trust, and a record showing all transfers of money between clients' trust ledger accounts. A firm wide total, a bank statement, and an hourly rate entry do not satisfy the clients' trust ledger requirement.
  4. The correct answer is C (Only the withdrawals that section 9(1) lists, which exclude office rent.): By-Law 9, section 9(1) lists the only money a licensee may withdraw from a trust account, such as money properly required for payment to a client or on a client's behalf, money properly required for the payment of fees and disbursements, and money paid into the account by mistake. Office rent and courier charges are the firm's operating expenses. Section 14 requires the licensee at all times to maintain sufficient trust balances to meet all obligations with respect to money held in trust, and the Law Society's guidance states that trust funds must never be commingled with the firm's own money.
  5. The correct answer is B (The interest belongs to The Law Foundation of Ontario.): Section 57 of the Law Society Act provides that money held in trust for more than one client in one fund must be held in an interest bearing account and that the interest accruing on that money is deemed to be held in trust for The Law Foundation of Ontario. The licensee must file reports with the Foundation and remit the interest as the regulations prescribe, and a lawyer who opens a mixed trust account must give the financial institution a written direction to pay the interest directly to the Foundation. Interest on an account that holds funds for only one client belongs to that client.
  6. The correct answer is C (Within 30 days of opening the new trust account.): By-Law 9, section 7(8)(a) requires the irrevocable written authorization directing the financial institution to disclose account information to the Law Society to be submitted within 30 days of opening a new trust account. The 25 day figure belongs to the monthly reconciliation under section 22(2) and is a different deadline entirely. Submitting the authorization before the first deposit clears is not what the by-law requires, and the authorization cannot wait for the next Annual Report.
  7. The correct answer is B (Give prompt notice to the insurer so the client's protection is preserved.): Rule 7.8-2 requires a lawyer to give prompt notice of any circumstance that may give rise to a claim to an insurer or other indemnitor so that the client's protection from that source is not prejudiced. LAWPRO tells lawyers to provide notice of real or possible mistakes immediately, because late notice can jeopardise coverage. Waiting for a proceeding, waiting for a regulatory investigation, and seeking a release first all put the client's indemnity at risk and breach the rule.
  8. The correct answer is A (Apply to a tribunal for direction about who should receive it.): Rule 3.5-7 provides that if a lawyer is unsure of the proper person to receive a client's property, the lawyer shall apply to a tribunal of competent jurisdiction for direction. Rule 3.5-6 also requires the lawyer to account promptly for client property and to deliver it to the order of the client or, if appropriate, at the conclusion of the retainer. Paying on a first in time basis, holding the fund indefinitely, and splitting it between the claimants would protect neither the solicitor nor the rightful owner.
  9. The correct answer is A (Inform the estate trustee promptly and recommend independent legal advice.): Rule 7.8-1 requires a lawyer who discovers an error or omission that is or may be damaging to the client and that cannot be readily rectified to inform the client promptly, carefully avoiding prejudice to any rights of indemnity, and to recommend that independent legal advice be obtained. Where the client has died, the information goes to the person who now stands in the client's place, such as the estate trustee. Waiting, quietly preparing a codicil, and reporting to the Law Society first do not satisfy the rule.
  10. The correct answer is C (Refuse the arrangement, because fees cannot be shared with a non-licensee.): Rule 3.6-7 prohibits a lawyer from directly or indirectly sharing, splitting or dividing fees with any person who is not a lawyer or paralegal, and from giving any financial or other reward to a non-licensee for the referral of clients or client matters. Rule 3.6-7.1 forbids doing indirectly what the rules prohibit directly. Written documentation, a total fee that does not increase, and an exclusivity promise do not create an exception, because the limited exceptions in rule 3.6-8 deal with multi-discipline practices and interprovincial law firms.
  11. The correct answer is B (The fee must not increase the total amount payable by the client.): Rule 3.6-6.1 permits a lawyer to pay and to accept a referral fee only where the fee is fair and reasonable, does not increase the total amount of the fee payable by the client, is covered by a referral agreement entered into at the time of the referral or as soon as practicable afterwards, the receiving licensee has the expertise and ability to handle the matter, and the referral was not made because of a conflict of interest or by a suspended licensee. The referral agreement is a signed written agreement between the referring licensee, the receiving licensee and the client, so the client is not left out. Law Society pre-approval and a trust hold are not requirements.
  12. The correct answer is A (The client must consent and the split must reflect the work done.): Rule 3.6-5 provides that, with the client's consent, fees for a matter may be divided between lawyers, or between lawyers and paralegals, who are not in the same firm, if the fees are divided in proportion to the work done and the responsibilities assumed. A split that tracked the effort is therefore permitted once the client consents. An equal split would fail the proportionality requirement, the firm's approval is not the test, and the client's consent is required even where the total fee stays the same.
Case profes_04

A solicitor is retained by a chartered bank to act on a $520,000 first mortgage secured on a Toronto duplex. The bank is a lending client as defined in rule 3.4-13. The borrower, who is also the purchaser, asks the same solicitor to act for him on the mortgage as well, because the lawyer who was handling his purchase has gone on leave. The solicitor has no other relationship with either party. The borrower is a sophisticated investor who has completed several similar transactions. The bank's written instructions direct the solicitor to act for the bank, and the borrower says he understands that the solicitor will also be acting for the bank. The solicitor must decide whether she may act for both.

  1. The correct answer is D (Rule 3.4-14(a), because the lender is a lending client.): Rule 3.4-14 sets out the situations in which a lawyer may act for both lender and borrower, and the first of them is where the lender is a lending client as defined in rule 3.4-13. A chartered bank falls within that definition, and its written instructions to act are the basis for the deemed consent in rule 3.4-16. The $75,000 exception has no application to a $520,000 mortgage, rule 3.4-16.8 concerns transfers of title rather than mortgages, and rule 3.4-3 addresses opposing parties in a dispute.
  2. The correct answer is B (Disclose all material information in writing to both borrower and lender.): Rule 3.4-15 provides that when a lawyer acts for both the borrower and the lender in a mortgage or loan transaction, the lawyer must disclose to the borrower and the lender, in writing, before the advance or release of the mortgage or loan funds, all material information that is relevant to the transaction. The appraisal shortfall and the tax arrears notice both meet that test. Splitting the disclosure between the two parties, or waiting until someone asks, would breach the rule.
  3. The correct answer is B (Giving the joint retainer advice and obtaining the bank's consent.): Rule 3.4-16 provides that where a lawyer is jointly retained by a client and a lending client in respect of a mortgage or loan from the lending client to the other client, the lending client's consent is deemed to exist on the lawyer's receipt of written instructions from the lending client to act. The solicitor is therefore not required to give the lending client the joint retainer advice in rules 3.4-5 and 3.4-6 or to obtain consent under rule 3.4-7, unless the lending client requires that its consent be in writing. The disclosure duty under rule 3.4-15 still applies to both parties.
  4. The correct answer is C (The solicitor must not act for both lender and borrower.): Rule 3.4-12 provides that, subject to rule 3.4-14, a lawyer, or two or more lawyers practising in partnership or association, must not act for or otherwise represent both lender and borrower in a mortgage or loan transaction. The exceptions in rule 3.4-14 include a lending client, a sale where the mortgage forms part of the purchase price, a remote location, consideration of $75,000 or less, and non-arm's length parties. Client consent does not create an exception of its own, and the rule does not direct the solicitor to choose the borrower.
  5. The correct answer is C (The solicitor shall not borrow from the client in these circumstances.): Rule 3.4-28.1 provides that, except for borrowing from a regulated lender or from a related person, a lawyer shall not borrow from a client. Because this client is neither a regulated lender nor a related person, the prohibition applies and the solicitor may not take the loan. Independent legal advice and proper documentation are protections required in certain permitted transactions with clients, but they do not create an exception to the ban on borrowing from a client.
  6. The correct answer is D (Disclose the conflict and require independent representation for the client.): Rule 3.4-29 requires the lawyer, in sequence, to disclose the nature of any conflicting interest that exists or could develop later, and then to deal with independent legal advice and independent legal representation. For a loan to a client who is not a related person, the lawyer must require that the client receive independent legal representation. Independent legal advice is the lower requirement that applies to a loan to a related person, and a second opinion or a written confirmation is not what the rule demands.
  7. The correct answer is D (Fees and disbursements must be clearly and separately detailed.): Rule 3.6-3 provides that in a statement of account delivered to a client, a lawyer shall clearly and separately detail the amounts charged as fees and as disbursements. Professional time is a fee, while the land transfer tax and the courier charge are disbursements, so the bill must keep the two categories apart. Showing only a total, listing hours without separating the categories, and obtaining the client's signature before payment are not what the rule requires.
  8. The correct answer is A (The licensee may be administratively suspended for failing to file the Annual Report on time.): Licensees who fail to file the Annual Report by the March 31 deadline may be administratively suspended, which is a real and significant consequence rather than a purely technical one. Filing late within the same calendar year does not automatically erase that risk, since the suspension is tied to missing the specific deadline itself. The bank does not automatically close a trust account simply because an Annual Report filing deadline has passed, since account closure is a separate process the licensee controls. Framing the consequence as merely a small late fee understates the administrative suspension risk that can actually follow.
  9. The correct answer is D (A completed Form 9C Investment Authority signed and dated by each private lender.): Where a lawyer acts for a private lender and holds a mortgage or charge in trust, the by-laws require the file to contain a completed Form 9C Investment Authority signed and dated by each private lender before the first advance of money is made. Form 9D Report on Investment is a different document that reports on the investment after it is made, and it is not the pre-advance authorization the file must contain. A notarized identification document belongs to the separate client identification and verification process, not to the mortgage file's Form 9C requirement. A borrower's consent letter is not part of what the by-laws require in the private lender's mortgage file.
  10. The correct answer is D (An original declaration of trust, since the charge is not held in all the lenders' names.): When the charge is not held in the name of all the private lenders, the by-laws require the file to also contain an original declaration of trust, in addition to the completed Form 9C Investment Authority and the copy of Form 9D Report on Investment. A second Form 9C signed by the borrower is not what the by-law calls for, since Form 9C is meant to be signed by the private lender, not the borrower. A sworn affidavit about the lenders' financial sophistication is not among the documents the by-law lists for this file. A separate insurance policy naming each lender as loss payee is likewise not a requirement the by-law imposes for this trust file.
  11. The correct answer is A (Add a fresh Form 9C signed by each lender and a fresh Form 9D before release.): Each time the licensee releases collateral, changes the priority of the charge, or otherwise reduces the security in a way the by-laws describe, the licensee must add to the file a fresh Form 9C Investment Authority signed by each lender before the act is done, together with a fresh Form 9D Report on Investment delivered to each lender. Relying on the original Form 9C alone ignores the by-law's requirement that a fresh authorization be obtained before this kind of change is made. Notifying only the borrower does nothing to satisfy the private lenders' own right to a fresh authorization and report. An internal file note is not a substitute for the documents the by-law specifically requires to be added to the file.
  12.
