The correct answer is C (A lender is not permitted to execute an electronic funds transfer to withdraw trust funds.): The by-laws state plainly that a lender is not permitted to execute an electronic funds transfer to withdraw trust funds for any reason, regardless of how the request is documented. Requiring written confirmation from the lender does not change this outcome, because the prohibition is not about how the request is recorded but about the method of withdrawal itself. There is no dollar threshold in the rule that would make a smaller electronic transfer permissible where a larger one is not. Registration of the mortgage on title has no bearing on this particular restriction, which applies regardless of the stage the transaction has reached.
  13. The correct answer is C (Apply to transfer the remaining balance to the Law Society's Unclaimed Trust Fund.): When a client cannot be located after reasonable efforts and two years have passed, the correct step is for the licensee to apply to transfer the remaining balance to the Law Society's Unclaimed Trust Fund. Moving the money into the firm's own operating account would convert client trust money into the firm's revenue, which is exactly what the trust rules are meant to prevent. Holding the balance indefinitely without any formal step leaves client money in limbo rather than following the established process for genuinely unclaimed funds. Donating the balance to a charity of the firm's choosing bypasses the client's own ownership of the money and the Law Society's designated process for it.
  14. The correct answer is A (That all cheques have cleared and the final reconciliation shows a zero balance.): Before closing a trust account, the licensee must disburse or transfer all client funds, confirm that all cheques have cleared, and confirm that the final reconciliation shows a zero balance, only then instructing the financial institution to close the account and reporting the closure to the Law Society. There is no rule that ties closure to a specific dollar threshold such as an account balance under five hundred dollars. Recontacting every client who ever had funds in the account is not part of the closure process once those funds have already been properly disbursed or transferred. There is likewise no minimum length of time an account must have been open before it can be closed.
  15. The correct answer is C (No separate notice is needed, since the Law Society shares the closure information with the Foundation.): According to the Law Society's guidance, there is no need to notify The Law Foundation of Ontario separately when a trust account is closed, because the Law Society shares that information with the Foundation once the closure has been reported. Requiring a separate written notice within thirty days invents an obligation the guidance does not impose on the firm. Limiting a supposed separate notice requirement to special interest bearing accounts still gets the underlying answer wrong, since no separate notice to the Foundation is required for any type of trust account. Saying no notice is ever required to anyone overlooks the firm's real obligation to report the closure to the Law Society itself.
  16. The correct answer is A (No more than $7,500 in cash, in aggregate, in respect of any one client file.): The by-laws state that a licensee shall not receive or accept cash in an aggregate amount of more than $7,500 in Canadian dollars from a person in respect of any one client file. A $10,000 figure is a threshold used in some other contexts but is not the cash limit the by-law actually sets for a client file. A $5,000 figure understates the true limit the by-law establishes. A $15,000 figure overstates the limit and could lead a firm to accept more cash than the by-law actually permits.
  17. The correct answer is C (Refuse the cash amount that pushes the total past the $7,500 limit on this file.): Because the by-law caps the aggregate cash a licensee may accept from a person on any one client file at $7,500, the lawyer must refuse to accept the portion of the proposed $9,000 that would push the file over that limit. Issuing a receipt immediately does not remove the cap, since the limit is about what may be accepted at all, not about documenting what was received. There is no waiver mechanism in the by-law that lets a client authorize the firm to exceed the cash limit for the file. Accepting the excess and reporting it later does not cure the problem, because the rule limits what may be received in the first place, not merely what must be disclosed afterward.
  18. The correct answer is D (No, because the limit is an aggregate cap per client file, not a per transaction cap.): The cash limit set out in the by-law is an aggregate cap of $7,500 in respect of any one client file, so splitting the payments across two different days does not change the result, since the two amounts together still exceed the limit for that same file. Treating each day's cash payment as a wholly separate and independent transaction ignores that the by-law measures the total received on the file, not each individual payment in isolation. There is no provision that resets the cash limit at the start of each calendar day. The outcome does not depend on whether the two payments happen to fall within the same calendar week, since the aggregate file based limit applies regardless of the calendar period involved.
  19. The correct answer is B (The fee or disbursement must be fair and reasonable and disclosed in a timely fashion.): Rule 3.6-1 of the Rules of Professional Conduct provides that a lawyer shall not charge or accept any amount for a fee or disbursement unless it is fair and reasonable and has been disclosed to the client in a timely fashion. Simply matching what other firms in the area charge does not by itself satisfy the rule's own fairness and disclosure requirements. There is no requirement that the Law Society pre-approve a fee before work begins, since fee setting and disclosure remain the lawyer's own responsibility subject to later assessment. The rule does not require every fee to be fixed for the whole matter, since fees may properly be structured in other ways as long as they remain fair, reasonable and properly disclosed.
  20. The correct answer is B (The fee was not disclosed in a timely fashion, contrary to the rule on fees.): Waiting until the final bill to reveal how fees were calculated fails the timely disclosure requirement in the rule on fees, which calls for disclosure in a timely fashion rather than only at the conclusion of the matter. It is not correct that disclosure obligations only arise once the file closes, since the rule is meant to let the client understand the fee basis as the matter proceeds. The Law Society does not set fee rates for types of matters, so that is not a reason the lawyer's silence would be acceptable. The timely disclosure requirement is a separate obligation from whether the ultimate total is fair and reasonable, so the disclosure problem exists even apart from any dispute about the final amount.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (45 days' written notice in the prescribed form.): Where the mortgage does not contain its own power of sale, the statutory power of sale applies, and no sale may be made until 45 days' written notice in the prescribed form has been given to the persons and in the manner required by the Act. The notice may be given at any time after 15 days of default. The 15 day and 35 day figures belong to the different regime where the mortgage itself confers a power of sale, under which notice may not be given until the default has continued for 15 days and the sale may not be made until at least 35 days after the notice. Three months is the period after which the statutory power becomes exercisable, not the notice period.
  2. The correct answer is D (30 May 2026, within 90 days after the last preservation day.): A preserved lien expires unless it is perfected before the end of the 90 day period following the last day on which it could have been preserved, so the contractor has until 30 May 2026 on these facts. The old 45 day perfection period is no longer the rule, and a question that uses it is applying a repealed provision. The 60 day period in the Act governs the expiry of an unpreserved lien, not the perfection of a preserved one. There is no 120 day perfection period.
Case real_e_04

A lawyer acts for the purchaser of a detached home in Ottawa registered under the Land Titles Act. The agreement closes in six weeks. The purchaser's lawyer has ordered a parcel search, an executions search against the vendor's name, and a municipal tax certificate. The vendor is married, but title is registered in the vendor's name alone, and the vendor's spouse has never lived at the property, which the vendor has always rented to tenants. The vendor's real estate agent confirms the vendor bought the home eleven years ago and has occupied a different home with the spouse throughout the marriage. The purchaser's lawyer must resolve several items before the requisition date so the transaction closes without a title defect or a missed statutory requirement.

  1. The correct answer is D (No consent is required because the property was never the couple's ordinary family residence.): A matrimonial home is property ordinarily occupied by the spouses as their family residence, and the couple here has lived in a different home throughout the marriage. Because this rental property was never the family residence, the statutory consent requirement does not attach to it, regardless of the vendor's marital status at the time of purchase. Marriage alone does not trigger the consent requirement, and no court designation is needed to reach that conclusion here. A transferor's spousal status statement in the electronic transfer will still confirm these facts for the registration system.
  2. The correct answer is A (Arrange to have the outstanding taxes paid or credited through the statement of adjustments at closing.): Municipal taxes are a special lien on the land that ranks ahead of almost every other claim, so an unpaid balance on the tax account is a real risk that must be cleared before or at closing rather than left for later. The standard practice is to credit or hold back the outstanding amount through the statement of adjustments so the purchaser does not take title subject to that lien. Registration of the transfer does not extinguish an unpaid tax lien, and there is no rule that prevents a municipality from certifying an account that shows arrears; the certificate simply reports the account as it stands.
  3. The correct answer is D (The parcel register, because it lists the registered owner and every instrument affecting the property directly.): Under the land titles system the registrar keeps an automated parcel register and enters every instrument affecting a property under its own property identifier, so a lawyer can rely on that parcel search rather than building up a chain of title. This is different from registry land, where a lawyer must instead search the abstract index and prove a good chain of title going back through the recorded instruments. The sheriff's execution index is a separate search for writs and is not the primary source for the state of title, and a declaration and by-laws only apply where the property is a condominium unit.
  4. The correct answer is C (Confirm whether the debtor is truly the vendor and the sheriff met the notice steps.): Under the Land Titles Act, a writ of execution binds land only from the date the sheriff receives it, and a writ filed under a name other than the name the owner is actually registered under has no effect against that land. Before treating the search result as a real problem, the lawyer needs to verify that the debtor identified is actually the vendor rather than someone who happens to share the same name, since a mismatch is common on a name based search. Treating a coincidental name match as automatically binding, or as something that can be disregarded outright, both skip the verification step the lawyer actually needs to take.
  5. The correct answer is B (It must be paid on or before the day the transfer is tendered for registration.): Every person who tenders a conveyance of land for registration must pay the land transfer tax at that time, so the obligation is tied to the moment of registration rather than to possession, filing a tax return, or the anniversary of the agreement. A lawyer closing a purchase builds this payment into the closing itself because the electronic system calculates and collects the tax as part of tendering the document for registration. Waiting for possession or for a later filing would leave the transfer unregistrable and the client exposed to an unpaid tax obligation.
  6. The correct answer is C (A refund of up to $4,000 against the Ontario land transfer tax otherwise payable.): For a conveyance on or after January 1, 2017, a qualifying first time purchaser can receive a refund of up to $4,000, which is enough to eliminate the tax on roughly the first $368,000 of value. The older $2,000 figure applied only to conveyances before that date, so it no longer matches the current rule. The refund is capped at a fixed dollar figure rather than the full tax owed or a flat percentage of the price, so a purchaser buying above the threshold still pays tax on the balance.
  7. The correct answer is B (No, because the purchaser's spouse owned a home anywhere in the world during the marriage.): The eligibility rule looks not only at the purchaser's own history but also at whether the purchaser has a spouse who owned a home anywhere in the world while married to the purchaser, so the spouse's condominium ownership during the marriage defeats the claim even though the purchaser has never personally owned a home. The rule is not limited to Ontario property, so an out of province home still counts. The disqualification also does not turn on ownership before the marriage began, since what matters is ownership while the parties were married to each other.
  8. The correct answer is A (The tax is generally payable on the thirtieth day after the disposition of the beneficial interest.): A disposition of a beneficial interest in land, such as an unregistered assignment or sale of an interest, triggers a tax obligation on the thirtieth day after the disposition rather than being tied to a calendar date or to a later registration event. This rule exists precisely because these dispositions do not pass through the registration system, so there is no tendering moment to anchor the payment date. A different quarterly schedule applies only to certain large trusts and partnerships with fifty or more holders, which is not the situation described here.
  9. The correct answer is D (The Non Resident Speculation Tax, currently applied at a rate of twenty five per cent.): A conveyance of designated land in the specified region to a foreign entity attracts the Non Resident Speculation Tax in addition to the general land transfer tax, and the current rate under the regulation is twenty five per cent for conveyances tendered after October 24, 2022. The lower fifteen per cent figure describes an earlier version of the rate before it was increased and is no longer accurate. There is no separate annual vacancy levy created by the Land Transfer Tax Act, and this tax is a provincial land transfer tax rather than a federal sales tax add on.
  10. The correct answer is A (No, because the unexpired term including every renewal cannot go beyond fifty years.): No land transfer tax is payable on a lease, or a transfer or notice of a lease, whose unexpired term, counting every renewal and extension, cannot exceed fifty years, and this lease's combined term tops out at forty five years. There is no separate ten year rule that automatically taxes longer commercial leases, and registering a notice of a lease does not by itself create a tax obligation where the fifty year threshold is not crossed. The correct explanation is the specific fifty year exemption, not a blanket claim that leases are outside land transfer tax entirely, since a lease that could exceed fifty years is deemed to be sold at fair market value instead.
  11. The correct answer is C (No, because only a person with authorization from the Director may submit or search electronically.): A person may not submit a document for electronic registration, or search the electronic land registration system, without an authorization from the Director of Land Registration, and firm membership alone does not substitute for that individual authorization. Electronic registration is not open generally to the public, and it is not limited to real estate agents either, since agents are not the persons who submit registrable documents at all. The assistant would need to obtain a proper authorization before submitting anything through the system on the firm's behalf.
  12. The correct answer is B (It compensates certain title losses and is topped up once its balance falls below one million dollars.): The Land Titles Assurance Fund is continued under the Land Titles Act to compensate people who suffer certain kinds of title loss, and it is replenished from the Consolidated Revenue Fund whenever its balance drops below one million dollars. It is not financed by municipal property tax revenue, and it is not restricted to mortgage lenders, since an individual owner can also be entitled to compensation in the right circumstances. It is also a statutory fund rather than a private insurance product that a purchaser must separately buy.
  13. The correct answer is D (Whether the application can still be made in time, since the limit is six years from the loss.): An application to the Fund must generally be made within six years from the time the loss was suffered, so an owner who is five years and eight months past the loss still has time left, though not much. A five year cutoff would be wrong here and would wrongly tell the client the window has already closed. Fraud based claims are not exempt from any time limit, and a four year cutoff has no basis in the Act, so both of those statements would give the client incorrect advice about urgency.
  14. The correct answer is B (The committee of adjustment can grant a minor variance if the general intent of the by-law is kept.): A council that has adopted a zoning by-law may constitute a committee of adjustment, and that committee can authorize a minor variance where it is satisfied the variance is desirable for the appropriate development of the land and the general intent of the by-law and the official plan are maintained. This gives a route to a small exception that does not require the council to pass a full by-law amendment. The provincial cabinet has no everyday role in approving individual setback exceptions, and the Landlord and Tenant Board deals with residential tenancy disputes, not zoning matters.
  15. The correct answer is C (Raise a requisition asking the vendor to resolve the unpermitted addition before closing.): Because construction generally requires a permit from the chief building official, an addition with no permit on record is a real risk the purchaser needs addressed, so the lawyer should raise it as a requisition asking the vendor to resolve it, whether by obtaining a retroactive permit or otherwise, before closing. There is no rule limiting the permit requirement to work done within the past year, and an unpermitted addition does not automatically void the agreement on its own. Leaving the issue for the purchaser to discover only after taking possession defeats the whole purpose of ordering the search before closing.
  16. The correct answer is C (The order can bind the owner of the property and can restrict how the property is used.): A compliance order made under the building code can bind the owner of the property and can restrict its permitted use, which is exactly why an outstanding order is treated as a title risk rather than a private administrative matter that a purchaser can safely ignore. Signing an agreement of purchase and sale has no effect on whether a municipal order remains outstanding, so the order does not simply disappear at that point. The risk exists regardless of whether the purchaser has any plan to demolish the building, since the order can affect ordinary use of the existing structure too.
  17. The correct answer is B (A consent, sometimes called a severance, from the appropriate approval authority.): The subdivision control provisions of the Planning Act prohibit conveying land unless it is described in accordance with a registered plan of subdivision, unless a consent, commonly called a severance, has first been obtained from the appropriate council or the Minister. A landowner cannot simply convey a portion of an unregistered parcel without going through that process, and federal approval has no role in this purely provincial land use control. A survey alone does not satisfy the requirement, since the Act specifically calls for a consent from the designated approval authority.
  18. The correct answer is B (Yes, because a purchaser authorized in the agreement may apply for the required consent.): An owner, a chargee, or a purchaser of land, or a duly authorized agent, may apply for a consent, and a purchaser who has entered into an agreement of purchase and sale authorizing the application fits squarely within that definition. The application is not restricted to the registered owner alone, and there is no requirement that the application wait until after closing, since the whole point is usually to resolve the severance before the transaction completes. Being a licensed real estate agent has nothing to do with eligibility to apply for a Planning Act consent.
  19. The correct answer is A (The conveyance does not create or convey any interest in the land at all.): A conveyance made in contravention of the subdivision control provisions of the Planning Act does not create or convey any interest in the land, so the buyer receives nothing despite having paid for the deed. This is a stronger result than a mere penalty for late registration or a temporary suspension, since the transaction simply never transfers title. It is also not merely voidable, meaning valid until challenged, because the statute treats a contravening conveyance as ineffective from the outset rather than valid until a court intervenes.
Case real_e_05

A lawyer acts for a purchaser of a resale condominium unit in a mid rise building in Mississauga. The agreement of purchase and sale is conditional on the purchaser's lawyer reviewing a status certificate satisfactory to the purchaser, with ten days after the lawyer receives the certificate to satisfy or waive that condition. The purchaser's lawyer requests the certificate and pays the corporation's fee. The certificate arrives showing the current common expenses, a recent reserve fund study, and a note that the board recently approved a special assessment against all owners to top up the reserve fund. The purchaser, who has never bought a condominium unit before, asks the lawyer several questions about the certificate and the closing before deciding whether to proceed.

  1. The correct answer is B (The fee cannot exceed one hundred dollars, including all applicable taxes.): The regulation caps the fee a condominium corporation can charge for a status certificate, including every piece of material required to be in it, at one hundred dollars inclusive of all applicable taxes. A lower fifty dollar figure understates the actual cap, and a higher two hundred dollar figure overstates it. The corporation does not have open discretion to charge whatever it wants, since the fee is a fixed statutory maximum rather than a number the board sets on its own.
  2. The correct answer is A (The corporation is deemed to have given a certificate stating there are no unpaid common expenses.): The corporation must give a status certificate within ten days of receiving the request and the fee, and a failure to do so results in the corporation being deemed to have given a certificate stating, among other things, that there are no unpaid common expenses and that no reserve fund assessment has been levied since the current budget began. There is no fixed daily penalty imposed on the corporation for the delay, and the agreement of purchase and sale is not automatically defeated simply because the certificate was late. The deeming provision protects the purchaser by supplying favourable deemed content rather than leaving the purchaser with no certificate at all.
  3. The correct answer is D (The statutory ten day rescission right applies only to a new unit purchase from a declarant.): The ten day statutory rescission right in the Condominium Act belongs to a purchaser who receives a disclosure statement and the condominium guide on a purchase of a new or proposed unit from a declarant, not to a buyer purchasing an existing unit from its current owner. A resale purchaser's real protection comes from the contractual condition built into the agreement and from reviewing the status certificate itself, not from a statutory cooling off period. The right therefore does not automatically apply to every resale, and it has nothing to do with whether a particular special assessment has been paid.
  4. The correct answer is B (Any assessment levied against the unit to increase the reserve fund contribution, and the reason for it.): A status certificate must set out any assessment levied against the unit to increase the reserve fund contribution, along with the reason for the assessment, alongside items like the common expenses and any increases to them. It is not limited to reporting the fund's total balance without explaining a specific assessment, and it does not stop at a bare yes or no answer with no figures or reasoning attached. Reserve fund assessments are squarely within the required content of the certificate, not outside it, which is exactly why this purchaser's certificate flagged the special assessment by name.
  5. The correct answer is A (No later than twenty eight days after the owner receives the proper invoice from the contractor.): Under the prompt payment rules, an owner must pay the amount payable under a proper invoice no later than twenty eight days after receiving it, unless the owner gives a notice of non payment within the allowed time. A fourteen day figure understates that period, while sixty and ninety day figures both overstate it and would leave a contractor waiting far longer than the statute permits. The twenty eight day clock runs from receipt of the proper invoice itself, not from some later milestone in the project.
  6. The correct answer is B (Yes, since no other lawyer practises within 200 kilometres and remote meeting is genuinely difficult.): The remote location exception applies where no other real estate lawyer practises within 200 kilometres and the clients cannot meet remotely, and 240 kilometres exceeds that 200 kilometre threshold while the clients describe a genuine difficulty meeting by video, so the exception is available here. There is no 300 kilometre threshold in the rule, so that distractor overstates what is required. The exception is not defeated simply because video calling is technically possible somewhere, since the test asks whether the clients can genuinely meet remotely, and there is no requirement that clients pay for another lawyer's travel costs to use this exception.
  7. The correct answer is C (The forty year period immediately preceding the date of the current dealing with the land.): The Registry Act defines the title search period as the forty years immediately preceding a dealing with registry land, so a purchaser's lawyer need not trace the chain of title further back than that. A ten year search would leave real gaps in the record, and there is no rule limiting the search to sixty years or tying it to the original Crown grant. Land titles searches work differently because they rely on a parcel register rather than on a recorded chain of instruments.
  8. The correct answer is C (Search the parcel register maintained under the Land Titles Act rather than a recorded chain of deeds.): Once land is brought under the Land Titles Act, dealings after conversion are searched and registered through the automated parcel register for that property identifier, which lists every instrument affecting the land rather than requiring proof of a recorded chain. Continuing to search a forty year chain after conversion misunderstands how the parcel register replaces that method. The Assurance Fund is a compensation backstop for loss, not a substitute for actually reviewing the parcel register, and conversion does not automatically cancel interests that were properly noted on the register.
  9. The correct answer is B (Review the pre-conversion registry history separately, since the parcel register mainly governs dealings after conversion.): The forty year registry rule remains relevant to land that was formerly under the Registry Act, particularly for understanding the pre-conversion history, while the parcel register becomes the operative record for dealings from the date of conversion onward. It would be a mistake to assume the parcel register automatically captured and validated every detail of the pre-conversion abstract without the lawyer reviewing it, and it is equally wrong to apply the forty year rule to instruments registered under the Land Titles Act after conversion. A careful lawyer treats the two periods as governed by different rules and checks both.
  10. The correct answer is D (The registrar keeps an automated parcel register listing every instrument affecting the property identifier assigned to it.): Under the Land Titles Act the registrar maintains an automated parcel register and enters every instrument that affects the land under the property identifier assigned to that parcel, which is why the search is described as a parcel search rather than a chain of title search. There is no rule under which the registrar destroys superseded instruments, since the historical entries remain part of the parcel record. Ownership is not proven by private affidavits, and the parcel register covers far more than mortgages, since it records transfers, charges, easements and other interests affecting the parcel.
  11. The correct answer is D (It shows the registered owner and every instrument currently entered against that property identifier.): The parcel register lists the registered owner and every instrument entered against that property identifier, including charges, easements and notices, so it gives the lawyer the current state of the title rather than a market opinion. Older instruments that remain in force are not automatically stripped from the register when a new instrument is added, and the register does not state an outstanding loan balance, since that figure is obtained directly from the lender. Treating the parcel register as a substitute for an appraisal misunderstands its purpose, which is to record legal interests rather than value.
  12. The correct answer is A (The writ likely does not bind the title because it was never entered on the register after conversion.): Under the Land Titles Act a registered owner's title is subject to enforceable writs against that owner, but a writ against a prior owner is not enforceable unless a note of the writ was entered on the title register. Since the writ was never noted, the current registered owner's title is not bound by it in the way it would be if the writ had been properly recorded. It is wrong to say Land Titles land can never be subject to execution, and the correct method of checking is a proper search of the register and the sheriff's index rather than an informal inquiry to the creditor.
  13. The correct answer is A (An electronic document has the same legal effect as a written document signed by the parties.): Part III of the Land Registration Reform Act provides that an electronic document is not required to be in writing or signed by the parties, and that such a document has the same effect as a written document that was signed. There is no requirement for a later paper original with a wet ink signature, and the electronic document binds both parties to the transaction, not just the transferor. Understanding this rule is essential because electronic registration is now the standard method of completing real estate transactions in Ontario.
  14. The correct answer is D (The lawyer needs an authorization from the Director of Land Registration before submitting or searching electronically.): A person may not submit a document for electronic registration, or search the electronic land registration system, without an authorization from the Director of Land Registration, so being licensed to practise law is not by itself enough to gain access. There is no rule limiting electronic submission to law clerks with municipal certification, and being called to the bar does not automatically grant electronic access. The lawyer must apply for and receive the proper authorization before either submitting documents or conducting searches through the system.
  15. The correct answer is B (The submission is a serious problem because only an authorized person may submit documents electronically.): The regulation requires that only an authorized person may submit a document for electronic registration, and using someone else's login credentials does not create the personal authorization the rule demands. Informing the lawyer after the fact does not cure the underlying problem, because the authorization requirement exists to make sure the person actually making the compliance with law statements has the authority to do so. Whether the transfer happens to be accurate is a separate issue from whether it was submitted by a properly authorized person in the first place.
  16. The correct answer is B (The completion date, the parties, the legal description, an address for service and preparer contact details.): Every electronic document must contain the date it was completed, a statement that each completing party has authority to complete it, the legal description, the parties, an address for service, and the preparer and submitter contact information required by the regulation. The legal description alone is not sufficient, since the other elements are mandatory rather than optional for the registrar. Neither a notarized agreement of purchase and sale nor a mortgage payout statement is part of the baseline content the regulation requires in every electronic document.
  17. The correct answer is D (A statement that the transferor's solicitor and the transferee's solicitor are not the same person.): Where separate lawyers act for the transferor and the transferee, the transfer must include the statement that the two solicitors are not the same person, which is the standard solicitor statement for an ordinary arm's length sale. The alternative statements about a change in legal tenure, an estate trustee transfer, or a government body exemption are only used when those specific circumstances actually apply. Using the wrong alternative statement would misdescribe the transaction and could expose the lawyer to a compliance problem later.
  18. The correct answer is D (Yes, principal plus interest and three months further interest instead of notice.): Where principal or interest secured by a mortgage of Ontario freehold or leasehold property is not payable until more than five years after the date of the mortgage, after those five years have passed the person entitled to redeem may pay the principal and interest then due, together with three months further interest in place of notice, and no further interest is chargeable after that. Since six years have already passed on an eight year term, this right is now available without waiting for the full term. No consent from subsequent encumbrancers is required to exercise this particular statutory right. The rule specifically does not apply to mortgages given by corporations, so the availability described here depends on the mortgagor not being a corporation.
  19. The correct answer is B (The lender may recover no interest at all until it adds the required statement.): It is true that any agreed rate of interest may generally be charged under the Interest Act, since Canadian law imposes no general statutory cap on mortgage interest. That general freedom does not excuse compliance with the separate disclosure rule for blended payment plans, which makes no interest chargeable, payable, or recoverable at all unless the mortgage states the principal amount and the interest rate calculated yearly or half yearly. There is no separate legal maximum rate that caps the lender's recovery here, the problem is the missing statement, not the size of the rate. A court application is not what restores the lender's right to interest, adding the missing statement is what the section actually requires.
  20. The correct answer is C (A mortgage broker's or agent's licence, acting on behalf of a brokerage.): No individual may deal in or trade in mortgages for remuneration in Ontario unless the individual holds a mortgage broker's or agent's licence and is acting on behalf of a licensed mortgage brokerage, subject to any exemption in the Act. A municipal business licence has nothing to do with this specific provincial licensing scheme for mortgage brokering. Membership in a real estate association does not satisfy the licensing requirement under this Act. Non lawyers regularly arrange mortgages for a fee under this licensing scheme, so a law degree is neither required nor sufficient on its own.
  21. The correct answer is A (The cost of borrowing expressed as a rate per annum.): A mortgage brokerage must disclose to each borrower the cost of borrowing and other prescribed information, calculated on the basis that all of the borrower's obligations are fulfilled, expressed as a rate per annum and, where the regulations require, also in dollars and cents. An internal commission arrangement between the brokerage and a referring agent is not what this disclosure requirement is about. Audited financial statements of the lender are not part of the cost of borrowing disclosure owed to this particular borrower. A list of other borrowers has no place in this disclosure and would raise its own confidentiality concerns.
  22. The correct answer is D (Whether prepayment is allowed and, if so, any charge or penalty.): For a mortgage repayable on a fixed date or by instalments, the brokerage must disclose whether the borrower may prepay before maturity, the terms and conditions of that right, and any rebate, charge, or penalty and how it is calculated. The maturity date alone does not address the separate question of what happens if the borrower wants to pay out early. Prior ownership history of the property belongs to a title search, not to this cost of borrowing disclosure. An internal hiring policy has nothing to do with the disclosure a borrower is owed under this section.
  23. The correct answer is D (Exercise extreme caution, since acting for both raises a real risk of harm.): A single lawyer, or lawyers practising together, may represent both a borrower and a lender only in very limited situations, and the Law Society directs lawyers to exercise extreme caution when asked to act for both a borrower and a private lender because of the increased risk of fraud and exploitation in that kind of transaction. Treating this as freely permitted in every case ignores the caution the Law Society specifically attaches to private lending. Describing it as impermissible outright overstates the rule, since limited joint retainers remain possible when the requirements are met. A signed waiver from the lender does not by itself satisfy the duty of candour and disclosure a lawyer owes to both clients.
  24. The correct answer is B (It is not enforceable against the current registered owner's title at all.): Under the Land Titles Act, the title of the registered owner is subject to enforceable writs of execution against that owner that have been recorded on the register, and a writ against a prior owner is not enforceable unless a note of the writ was entered on the title register. Since the writ here was never noted against any owner in the chain, it has no effect on the current registered owner's title. Nothing in the Act causes an unnoted writ to spring back to life on a later sale. The rule described concerns real property on title, not the current owner's personal property generally.
  25. The correct answer is A (With the sheriff, in an electronic index the sheriff maintains.): Writs of execution are filed with the sheriff and entered into an electronic index maintained by the sheriff, and a writ against real and personal property binds personal property from the time it is filed and entered in that index. The parcel register maintained through the land registration system is a separate record used for a Land Titles parcel search, not for execution filings. The Law Society has no role in maintaining a public execution index. A municipal tax roll tracks realty taxes, not writs of execution against a person's name.
  26. The correct answer is A (The sale stands, since the writ was not yet enforceable against the land.): Registered land is not bound by a writ of execution until the sheriff has complied with the notice requirements set out in the Execution Act, and a sale or transfer under such a writ is not valid against a good faith purchaser who buys before the sheriff has complied with those requirements. Since the purchaser here closed in good faith before compliance, the writ was not yet enforceable against the land and the sale is protected. Saying a filed writ instantly binds all of the debtor's land ignores the notice requirement the Act specifically builds in. The purchaser's protection does not depend on the sale being merely voidable at its own option, and it does not turn on the purchaser's personal knowledge in this scenario.
  27. The correct answer is C (The same redemption and notice rights the mortgagor has under the Mortgages Act.): When a person proceeds to realize on an encumbrance against property that is a matrimonial home, the spouse with a right of possession has the same right of redemption or relief against forfeiture as the other spouse, and is entitled to the same notice about the claim and its enforcement, with sections 33 and 34 of the Mortgages Act applying to the notice. This right exists precisely because the non owning spouse is not on title, so the absence of her name does not defeat it. She does not have an unconditional right to simply block the sale without addressing the underlying debt. Her rights are tied to redemption and notice, not to a fixed thirty day post sale occupancy period.
Case wills__03

Mr. Bassey died in Toronto in 2026. He left two wills. His primary will deals with his house in Scarborough, his bank accounts and his personal effects. His secondary will deals with shares in the private company he ran, which are not being submitted for probate, and with a family trust. The house and the accounts are worth $600,000, and the company shares are worth $600,000. The primary will names his daughter, Ngozi, as estate trustee. Ngozi has asked the solicitor what application she should make for a court certificate and how the estate administration tax is calculated.

  1. The correct answer is D (A certificate of appointment of estate trustee with a will limited to those assets.): Where a deceased has multiple wills and the court issues a Certificate of Appointment of Estate Trustee with a Will Limited to the Assets Referred to in the Will, only the assets included in that specific will are included in the value of the estate for estate administration tax. That is the certificate that matches Ngozi's plan to deal with the primary will assets. A certificate without a will would be wrong because there is a will, the estate is far above the small estate limit of $150,000, and a confirmation by resealing applies to a foreign grant rather than an Ontario estate.
  2. The correct answer is C ($8,250, being $15 for each of the 550 thousands of value above $50,000.): Estate administration tax is payable on the value of the estate covered by the estate certificate. An application made on or after January 1, 2020 is exempt from tax on the first $50,000, and the tax is $15 for each $1,000, or part thereof, by which the value exceeds $50,000. A value of $600,000 is $550,000 above the exemption, so the tax is $15 multiplied by 550, which is $8,250. The $2,850 figure is the tax on a value of $240,000, and charging tax on the whole $600,000 or on both wills would overstate the tax.
  3. The correct answer is B (The tax applies only to the assets covered by the certificate.): Ontario permits the multiple wills strategy, under which a primary will deals with assets that require probate and a secondary will deals with assets that do not. Estate administration tax is payable on the issuance of an estate certificate, and the Ministry of Finance confirms that where the court issues a certificate limited to the assets referred to in a specific will, only the assets in that will are included in the value of the estate. A secondary will must still be executed with all the formalities of a will, the tax is not limited to real property, and the strategy is a drafting technique rather than a rule that excludes other estates.
  4. The correct answer is D (File the estate information return within 180 days after the certificate.): Section 4.1 of the Estate Administration Tax Act, 1998 requires an estate representative who applies for an estate certificate to give the Minister of Revenue prescribed information, and the Ministry of Finance requires the initial estate information return to be received within 180 calendar days after the certificate is issued. A return must be filed even if the calculated value of the estate is zero. The deposit paid when the application is made does not replace the return, and the 180 day period runs from the certificate, not from the death.
  5. The correct answer is C (Ada and Ben take one third each, and Charles's children share his third.): Section 47(1) of the Succession Law Reform Act provides that where the deceased leaves issue but no spouse, the property is distributed equally among the issue of the nearest degree in which there are issue surviving, and section 47(2) provides that the share of a predeceased issue goes to that person's issue in the same way. Ada and Ben are of the nearest degree, and Charles's children take the share that Charles would have taken, so the estate is divided in three parts. Equal shares for all five descendants, or excluding the grandchildren, would not follow the per stirpes rule.
  6. The correct answer is B (The parents take the estate equally, and the brother takes nothing.): Section 47(3) of the Succession Law Reform Act provides that where a person dies intestate with no spouse or issue, the property is distributed between the parents of the deceased equally, or to the surviving parent absolutely if only one parent survives. Siblings take only under section 47(4), which applies where there is no surviving spouse, issue or parent. The brother therefore takes nothing while both parents are alive, and the family cannot rewrite the statutory order by agreement.
  7. The correct answer is A (The nephews share the estate equally, and the cousins receive nothing.): Section 47(5) of the Succession Law Reform Act provides that where there is no surviving spouse, issue, parent, brother or sister, the property is distributed among the nephews and nieces equally and without representation. The two nephews therefore take the whole estate in equal shares, and the next of kin rule in section 47(6) is not reached while nephews or nieces survive. The cousins take nothing, and the nephews take in their own right rather than representing their father.
  8. The correct answer is B (No, because a spouse separated at death is excluded from those rules.): Section 43.1 of the Succession Law Reform Act provides that any provision in Part II that gives property to a person's spouse does not apply to the spouse if the spouses were separated at the time of the person's death, using the separation test in section 17(4). A valid separation agreement under Part IV of the Family Law Act is one of the events that establishes separation, so the wife does not take under the intestacy rules. Her rights under the separation agreement and any support claim are separate questions.
  9. The correct answer is C (Each of them is deemed to have held as tenant in common with the other.): Section 55(2) of the Succession Law Reform Act provides that if two or more people hold legal or equitable title to property as joint tenants, or hold a joint account, and all of them die at the same time or in circumstances where it is uncertain who survived, each is deemed for the survivorship rule to have held as tenant in common with the others. The property therefore falls into both estates instead of passing automatically to the survivor. Age is not the deciding factor, and the property does not go to the Crown on that basis alone.
  10. The correct answer is D (A small estate certificate, at least 30 days after notice to those entitled.): A small estate is an estate that does not exceed the amount prescribed under the Estates Act, which is $150,000, and the simplified procedure is in Rule 74.1. Rule 74.1.03(2) provides that the application may not be filed until at least 30 days after the applicant has sent the application and the will to every person entitled to share in the distribution. Rule 75 governs contentious proceedings, and a confirmation by resealing applies to a foreign grant.
  11. The correct answer is D (Personal liability for unpaid income tax to the extent of the distribution.): Section 159 of the Income Tax Act makes a legal representative jointly and severally liable with the taxpayer to the extent of the property in the representative's possession or control, and it requires the representative to obtain a certificate confirming that all amounts for which the representative is or may become liable have been paid or secured before distributing property. A representative who distributes without that certificate is personally liable for the unpaid amounts to the extent of the value of the property distributed. Filing the return is not the same as obtaining the certificate, and estate administration tax is payable by the estate representative in a representative capacity only.
  12. The correct answer is A (A fair and reasonable allowance fixed by the court under the Trustee Act.): Section 61(1) of the Trustee Act entitles a trustee, guardian or personal representative to such fair and reasonable allowance for care, pains and trouble and time expended as a judge of the Superior Court of Justice allows, and the amount may be settled when the accounts are passed. Where a lawyer is the trustee and has provided necessary professional services to the estate, the allowance is increased by a fair and reasonable amount for those services. The commonly cited percentages of 2.5 per cent of receipts and disbursements are a guideline developed by the courts rather than a statutory rate, and the section does not apply where the will fixes the allowance.
  13. The correct answer is A (At least 60 days before the hearing date for a person in Ontario.): Rule 74.18 of the Rules of Civil Procedure requires the notice of application to pass accounts (Form 74.44) and a draft of the judgment sought to be served on each person with a contingent or vested interest in the estate at least 60 days before the hearing for a person served in Ontario and at least 75 days for a person served outside Ontario. A person who wants to object to the accounts must serve and file a notice of objection to accounts (Form 74.45) at least 35 days before the hearing. The 75 day period applies only to service outside Ontario.
  14. The correct answer is A (The trustee is protected, but the creditor may follow the assets to the recipients.): Section 53 of the Trustee Act protects a personal representative who has given the notices that the Superior Court of Justice would have directed in an administration suit, asking creditors to send in their claims by a stated time. The representative may then distribute, having regard to the claims of which the representative has notice, and is not liable for the assets distributed to a person of whose claim the representative had no notice. That protection does not take away the creditor's right to follow the assets into the hands of the people who received them.
  15. The correct answer is D (A fully discretionary trust for the son, often called a Henson trust.): A Henson trust is a fully discretionary trust for a beneficiary with a disability. Because the trustee has complete discretion and the beneficiary has no enforceable right to the capital or income, the beneficiary does not have a beneficial interest in assets available to be used for maintenance within the meaning of the ODSP regulation, so the trust capital is not counted against the asset limit. An outright inheritance is counted as income in the month received and then as an asset, which can affect both income support and the drug and dental benefits attached to it. The other arrangements would give the son an interest that the program would count.
  16. The correct answer is A (The 2024 will designation, because a later designation revokes the earlier one.): Section 51 of the Succession Law Reform Act allows a participant to designate a beneficiary by an instrument signed by the participant or by will, and a designation in a will is effective only if it relates expressly to a plan, either generally or specifically. Section 52 provides that a later designation revokes an earlier designation to the extent of any inconsistency. The 2024 will designation is later and expressly relates to the plan, so it governs unless the earlier designation was made irrevocable. A plan form does not automatically prevail, and conflicting designations do not cancel each other out.
  17. The correct answer is B (The spousal rollover applies if the shares vest indefeasibly in the spouse.): Section 70(5) of the Income Tax Act deems a taxpayer to have disposed of capital property immediately before death at fair market value, which would trigger the gain on the shares. Section 70(6) provides a spousal rollover where the property is transferred or distributed because of the death to the deceased's spouse or common-law partner, so that the deemed disposition does not apply and the property passes at its cost. The rollover requires the property to become vested indefeasibly in the spouse within 36 months after the death, or a longer period the Minister considers reasonable. The death rollovers to a child are limited to farm and fishing property.
  18. The correct answer is C (No, a will is not valid unless it is in writing.): The Succession Law Reform Act requires a will to be in writing before any other formality is even considered, so a spoken or recorded statement cannot take effect as a will however clearly it expresses the client's wishes. Transcribing the call afterward does not cure the missing writing, because the will itself must exist in written form when the testator dies. Without a signed written document, the client's estate will pass under the intestacy rules instead.
  19. The correct answer is A (It is not valid, since both witnesses must watch her sign together.): Execution is only valid where the testator signs, or acknowledges her signature, in the presence of two attesting witnesses who are both present at the same time, and where those witnesses then sign in the testator's presence. Having the second neighbour sign later, after being told what happened rather than watching it happen, breaks the same time requirement. Relatives are not barred from witnessing, so that is not the problem here.
  20. The correct answer is C (This works if the signing and witnessing happen together and a witness is a licensee.): Ontario permits the presence requirement for witnessing a will to be met through audio visual communication technology, provided at least one witness is a licensee under the Law Society Act and the signing and witnessing happen contemporaneously. Complete identical copies may then be signed in counterpart, so the nurse and the lawyer do not need to sign the same physical page as the testator. Only one witness needs to be a licensee, and no prior judicial approval is required.
  21. The correct answer is C (The relative is wrong, since no particular form of attestation clause is required.): Where witnesses are required for a will, the statute expressly says that no particular form of attestation clause is necessary, so plain language confirming that the named people watched the signing is enough. The relative's argument about traditional wording therefore has no basis in the current law. Attestation clauses were not abolished, they are simply optional rather than required in any set form.
  22. The correct answer is D (36 months after the death, if the other conditions are met.): A graduated rate estate means the estate that arose on and as a consequence of the individual's death if, among other conditions, that time is no more than 36 months after the death, the estate is then a testamentary trust, the individual's Social Insurance Number is provided in the estate's returns, and the estate designates itself as the graduated rate estate in its return for its first taxation year ending after 2015. Only one estate may make that designation for the individual. The 21 year period relates to the deemed disposition rule for trusts rather than to graduated rate estate status.
  23. The correct answer is A (Within 36 months after the death, unless the Minister allows longer.): The rollover applies only if it can be shown, within 36 months after the death, that the property has become vested indefeasibly in the spouse, common-law partner or qualifying trust. The Minister may allow a longer period if the legal representative applies in writing within those 36 months, and the legal representative may elect out of the rollover. The 21 year period is the deemed disposition rule for trusts, and the date of the estate certificate is not the measuring point.
  24. The correct answer is D (No, the death rollovers to a child cover farm and fishing property.): The death rollovers to a child in the Income Tax Act are for farm and fishing property, for property of a trust transferred to the settlor's children, and for shares of a family farm or fishing corporation or an interest in a family farm or fishing partnership. There is no general rollover on death for ordinary small business corporation shares, and the ordinary deemed disposition rule applies. The lifetime capital gains exemption may reduce the tax in the right circumstances, but it is not a rollover.
  25. The correct answer is A (Yes, because a gift within 60 months of the death can qualify.): A gift made by an individual's graduated rate estate can qualify for the estate donation rules if it is made no more than 60 months after the individual's death, the death occurs after 2015, and the property is covered by the rules. A gift made three years after the death falls within that period. The 60 month period for the gift is longer than the 36 month period for graduated rate estate status, so the two rules measure different things.
  26. The correct answer is D (The formula adds one extra year to the years of principal residence.): The principal residence exemption reduces the gain by a formula in which one element is one plus the number of taxation years ending after the acquisition date for which the property was the taxpayer's principal residence and during which the taxpayer was resident in Canada. The extra year can cover a year in which the property was not occupied, such as a year in which the owner moved to a care home. The formula does not count every year of ownership, and it does not deduct a year for the year of the move.
  27. The correct answer is C (The later designation revokes the earlier one to the extent of any inconsistency.): A participant may designate a beneficiary by an instrument or by will, and a later designation revokes an earlier designation to the extent of any inconsistency. The designation in the will was made two years after the instrument, so it governs. A designation in a will is of no effect against a designation made later than the making of the will, which is not the position here.
  28. The correct answer is C (No, an irrevocable designation cannot be changed without the daughter's consent.): Where an insured files a declaration designating a beneficiary irrevocably during the lifetime of the person whose life is insured, the insured cannot alter or revoke the designation while the beneficiary is living without the beneficiary's consent. The insurance money is not subject to the insured's control, is not subject to the claims of the insured's creditors, and does not form part of the insured's estate. A court order is not the route to change an irrevocable designation.
  29. The correct answer is C (The beneficiaries may require the trustee to transfer the property to them.): Where a beneficiary is absolutely entitled to the trust property, is of full legal capacity and is the only person with an interest in the property, or where all the beneficiaries agree, the beneficiaries may require the trustees to transfer the property to them and so bring the trust to an end. The rule is known by the name of the case that established it, Saunders v. Vautier. The beneficiaries do not need the court's approval for that step.
  30. The correct answer is A (The trust is deemed to dispose of the property every 21 years.): Every trust is deemed to dispose of its capital property, other than exempt property, at fair market value on the day that is 21 years after the latest of the listed days, which include January 1, 1972, the day the trust was created and, where applicable, the day of the settlor's death, and again 21 years after each such day. The trust is treated as having reacquired the property at that fair market value. The rule is separate from the deemed disposition on death, and it is not tied to a beneficiary's age.
  31. The correct answer is C (The court may approve for them if the arrangement is for their benefit.): The Superior Court of Justice may approve an arrangement varying or revoking all or any of the trusts on behalf of a person who is incapable of assenting because of infancy or other incapacity, a person who may become entitled in future, an unborn person, or a person whose interest may arise under a discretionary power. The court shall not approve an arrangement on behalf of a person within those first three categories unless it appears to be for that person's benefit. Other beneficiaries cannot supply the consent that the statute requires the court to give.
  32. The correct answer is D (The capital is not counted as his asset, because he has no enforceable right.): A trust drafted with full discretion leaves the beneficiary with no enforceable right to the capital or income, which means he does not have a beneficial interest in trust assets available to be used for maintenance and the capital is not counted against the asset limit. Payments actually made out of the trust are counted as income, subject to the prescribed exemption of $10,000 for any 12 month period. A direct inheritance would be counted and could affect both income support and the benefits attached to it.
  33. The correct answer is C (As tenants in common, unless a joint tenancy intention appears.): Where land is granted, conveyed or devised to two or more persons, other than executors or trustees, those persons take as tenants in common and not as joint tenants, unless an intention that they take as joint tenants sufficiently appears on the face of the document. The default is therefore a tenancy in common, and no particular form of words is needed for it. A family relationship such as sisterhood does not change the default.
  34. The correct answer is B (The joint tenancy has been severed, and they hold as tenants in common.): A joint tenancy may be severed by a joint tenant acting on his or her own share, by mutual agreement, or by any course of dealing sufficient to show that the interests of all the joint tenants were mutually treated as constituting a tenancy in common. The Court of Appeal for Ontario confirmed those three routes in Hansen Estate v. Hansen. Registration of a transfer is not required for severance by agreement or by a course of dealing.
  35. The correct answer is C (The joint tenancy is deemed severed, so the interest falls into his estate.): Where a spouse dies owning an interest in a matrimonial home as a joint tenant with a third person and not with the other spouse, the joint tenancy is deemed to have been severed immediately before the time of death. His interest therefore falls into his estate rather than passing to his brother by survivorship, and the wife's rights are worked out through the estate and the equalization rules. The deeming provision applies to a matrimonial home, which is the property the spouses used as their home.
  36. The correct answer is A (Understanding the will's nature and effect, the property and the claims on it.): To have a sound disposing mind, the testator must understand the nature and effect of a will, recollect the nature and extent of the property, understand the extent of what is being given, remember the people who might be expected to benefit, and, where applicable, understand the claims of persons being excluded. A delusion that has no connection to the contents of the will does not by itself make the testator incapable. Knowledge of tax, probate practice or the calendar is not the test.
  37. The correct answer is A (The person propounding the will must prove knowledge and approval.): A testator is presumed to have testamentary capacity, and the party attacking the will bears the burden of proving incapacity. Where suspicious circumstances are present, the burden shifts, and the propounder of the will must prove knowledge and approval of the contents, and where the circumstances call capacity into question must prove testamentary capacity on a balance of probabilities. The leading authority is Vout v. Hay.
  38. The correct answer is D (The date of the hearing, when the adequacy of the provision is judged.): Where a deceased person has not made adequate provision for the proper support of a dependant, the court may order that adequate provision be made out of the estate, and the adequacy of the provision is judged as of the date of the hearing. A change in the dependant's circumstances after the death is therefore relevant to the application. The date of the will and the date of death are not the measuring points for adequacy.
  39. The correct answer is C (He is a dependant, because the deceased was providing support to him.): A dependant is the spouse, a parent, a child, or a brother or sister of the deceased, but a parent, child or sibling qualifies only if the deceased was providing support to that person, or was under a legal obligation to provide support, immediately before the death. A parent who was receiving monthly support therefore fits the definition. The size of the payment goes to the amount of any order rather than to whether the applicant is a dependant.
  40. The correct answer is C (Yes, but only as to the portion of the estate still undistributed.): No application for a support order may be made more than six months after the grant of letters probate or letters of administration, but the court may, if it considers it proper, allow an application to be made at any time as to any portion of the estate that is still undistributed at the date of the application. The undistributed fund therefore keeps the claim alive to that extent. The court's discretion is express in the statute, so the six month period is not an absolute bar.
  41. The correct answer is C (The corporation may be ordered to pay her reasonable legal fees.): In connection with a derivative action, the court may make any order it thinks fit, including an order requiring the corporation or its subsidiary to pay reasonable legal fees and other costs reasonably incurred by the complainant. Personal payment with no recovery and a bar on any costs order are not the rules. Costs in a derivative action are not limited to a punitive award against the defendants.
  42. The correct answer is D (The notice is not required because all the directors are defendants.): The requirement to give 14 days' notice to the directors does not apply if all of the directors of the corporation or its subsidiary are defendants in the action. The objection by the corporation therefore fails on these facts. A shareholder may bring a derivative action against directors with leave, and an adjournment to serve the same directors is not required.
  43. The correct answer is C (As of the close of business on the day before the resolution was adopted.): A shareholder who complies with the dissent provisions is entitled, when the action approved by the resolution becomes effective, to be paid the fair value of the shares held by the shareholder, determined as of the close of business on the day before the resolution was adopted. The date of the objection, the date of a court application and the date the transaction is completed are not the valuation date. The fixed valuation date protects the dissenting shareholder from a fall in value after the vote.
  44. The correct answer is D (The corporation within 10 days; the shareholder within 20 days of the notice.): The corporation must send notice that the resolution was adopted within 10 days after adoption to each shareholder who filed an objection. The shareholder must send a written demand for payment of fair value within 20 days after receiving that notice, or within 20 days after learning that the resolution was adopted if no notice is received. The reversed and extended periods do not match the Act.
  45. The correct answer is C (No, because its words show no intention to be bound.): Whether a letter of intent binds is decided by its words. A letter that states that it is non-binding, that it is subject to a definitive agreement and that no legal obligations arise except for specified items will not bind. A price term, signatures and an alleged presumption do not override that language, and Ontario has no statutory rule that makes a letter of intent binding.
  46. The correct answer is C (A non-competition covenant in an employment contract is void unless it meets the test.): Ontario restricts non-competition covenants in employment. A covenant that is a non-competition covenant is void as against the employee unless it meets the statutory requirements, and that test is strict. Non-solicitation covenants are treated separately and are not caught by the same statutory prohibition. The length of the restriction, the employee's salary and any training provided by the employer do not decide the statutory question.
  47. The correct answer is D (A copy of the instrument of continuance within 60 days after issuance.): A corporation that continues out of Ontario under the Business Corporations Act agrees, as a condition of the electronic filing, to file with the Ministry a copy of the instrument of continuance issued by the other jurisdiction within 60 days after the date it is issued. The shorter periods do not apply to that filing. A certificate of status and a certified copy of the shareholders' resolution are different documents.
  48. The correct answer is D (That the security interest attached by value, rights and a signed agreement.): An escrow or holdback that is security for a seller's indemnity is a security interest in personal property for the purposes of the Personal Property Security Act. A security interest is not enforceable against a third party unless it has attached, and attachment requires value given, the debtor having rights in the collateral and one of the listed acts, typically a signed security agreement containing a description of the collateral. A trust account, a corporate seal and notice to a bank do not by themselves satisfy the attachment requirement.
  49. The correct answer is A (Yes, a transfer of substantially all the property for securities is listed.): The arrangement provision includes a transfer of all or substantially all the property of the corporation to another body corporate in exchange for securities, money or other property, an exchange of securities for other securities, money or other property, a liquidation or dissolution, and any other reorganization or scheme that is at law an arrangement. The provision is not limited to a liquidation, and the court does not propose the arrangement. The corporation does not have to be an offering corporation.
  50. The correct answer is B (A statement of the arrangement, then special resolutions and court approval.): A corporation proposing an arrangement must prepare, for shareholder approval, a statement of the arrangement that sets out in detail what is proposed and how it is to be done. Where the arrangement has been approved by the shareholders, and by the holders of each class or series entitled to vote separately, in each case by special resolution, the arrangement has been adopted and the corporation may apply to the court for an order approving it. A circular, a prospectus and a by-law are not the required documents, and a simple majority is not the required approval.
  51. The correct answer is A (Require a greater majority or an additional separate meeting or vote.): On an arrangement application the court may make any order it considers appropriate, including requiring a greater specified majority than two thirds of the votes cast, requiring an additional meeting or separate vote, or determining or dispensing with notice. The court is not limited to approving or refusing the plan as voted. Unanimous written consent is not the standard, and the court does not substitute its own amendment of the articles for the approval process.
  52. The correct answer is C (An order under the oppression remedy, the bankruptcy Act or the CCAA.): The reorganization definition covers a court order made under the oppression remedy, an order made under the Bankruptcy and Insolvency Act (Canada) and an order made under the Companies' Creditors Arrangement Act (Canada) approving a proposal. It is not limited to orders made under the Business Corporations Act. A prospectus receipt and an order reviving a dissolved corporation are not reorganization orders within that definition.
  53. The correct answer is C (Notice of the resolution within 10 days after it is passed.): The shareholders may require the corporation to be wound up voluntarily by special resolution, and at that meeting they must appoint one or more persons as liquidator. The corporation must file notice of the resolution with the Director within 10 days after it is passed. The other documents and periods do not reflect the Act.
  54. The correct answer is C (Yes, the court may order a winding up under the oppression remedy.): The oppression remedy expressly allows the court to order that the corporation be wound up. The powers of the court are not limited to ordering a purchase of the applicant's shares, and a buyout is only one of the listed orders. Insolvency is not a condition of the winding up order under that provision, and the consent of the other shareholders is not required.
  55. The correct answer is B (The application must not be made more than 20 years after dissolution.): The Act provides that the application for revival must not be made more than 20 years after the date of dissolution. The 10, 15 and 25 year periods are not the statutory limits. Within the 20 year window the Director may, in the Director's discretion, revive the corporation on the application of an interested person, which includes a director, officer or shareholder.
  56. The correct answer is A (Articles of revival, endorsed with a certificate of revival.): The application for revival must be in the form of articles of revival. On receipt of the articles of revival and any other required documents and information, the Director endorses the articles with a certificate that constitutes the certificate of revival. A petition, a notice of change and a statement of dissolution are not the documents the Act uses for this process.
  57. The correct answer is B (The corporation is deemed never dissolved, subject to rights acquired during the gap.): On revival the corporation is deemed for all purposes never to have been dissolved, subject to any terms and conditions the Director imposes and to the rights acquired by any person during the period of dissolution. The revival does not create a clean slate and does not cancel the corporation's earlier debts. Property that forfeited to and vested in the Crown on dissolution is dealt with under the Forfeited Corporate Property Act, 2015 and the Escheats Act, 2015, and may remain forfeited.
  58. The correct answer is A (The name must be registered, and she is personally liable for the business debts.): Where an individual carries on business under a name other than their own, the Business Names Act requires that name to be registered by the individual before it is used. Because a sole proprietorship is not a separate legal person, the owner remains personally liable for the debts the business incurs, and registration does nothing to change that exposure. There is no revenue threshold that triggers the registration duty, so waiting until sales grow would not excuse the omission. A sole proprietor cannot rely on the business structure itself to separate personal assets from business creditors.
  59. The correct answer is C (It expires after five years, and a late renewal is available within 60 days after that.): A business name registration under the Business Names Act is effective for five years from the date the Registrar accepts it, so a filing made three years ago still has time left before it lapses. The Act allows a registrant to renew before expiry, or within 60 days after expiry on payment of a late renewal fee, so a short delay is not necessarily fatal. There is no ten year term available for a standard registration, and treating the registration as permanently lost after three years misstates the actual expiry period entirely. Understanding this window lets counsel advise a client accurately about how much time remains to act.
  60. The correct answer is A (The estate is severally liable for the debt once the surviving partner cannot pay in full.): For ordinary firm debts, partners are liable jointly with each other rather than jointly and severally, but the estate of a deceased partner becomes severally liable in due course of administration for debts that remain unsatisfied by the surviving partners, subject to payment of the deceased partner's own separate debts first.
