This means the estate is not automatically off the hook simply because the partner has died, but it also is not liable in the same joint and several way a living partner would be from day one. There is no requirement that judgment be obtained before death for the estate's liability to arise. A general partnership is not a corporation, so there is no separate corporate entity to absorb the debt instead.
  61. The correct answer is C (No, because shared ownership and shared receipts alone do not by themselves create a partnership.): The Partnerships Act specifically provides that joint tenancy, tenancy in common, joint property or part ownership does not by itself create a partnership, and sharing gross returns does not by itself create a partnership either. A partnership requires carrying on business in common with a view to profit, and merely co-owning an income producing property with a shared income split does not establish that relationship. Registering a name is not what determines whether a partnership legally exists in the first place, since that question turns on the substance of the relationship. Splitting gross receipts is therefore not conclusive of anything on its own.
  62. The correct answer is B (The waiver is void, because a franchisee cannot give up rights under the Act.): Section 11 provides that any purported waiver or release by a franchisee of a right given under the Act, or of an obligation or requirement imposed on a franchisor or a franchisor's associate, is void. The franchisee therefore keeps the statutory rescission right. Voluntary signature and independent legal advice do not make the waiver effective, because the Act forbids the waiver itself. Rescission under section 6 is a statutory right rather than merely a contractual remedy, which is why the anti-waiver provision applies.
  63. The correct answer is D (She may sue the franchisor and the persons who signed the document.): Section 7(1) gives a franchisee who suffers a loss because of a misrepresentation in a disclosure document, or because of a failure to comply with the disclosure requirements, a right of action for damages against the franchisor, the franchisor's associate, every person who signed the document and the other persons listed in the section. Section 7(2) deems a franchisee who acquired the franchise to have relied on the misrepresentation, so proving actual reliance is not required. Insolvency of the franchisor is not a condition of the claim, and competitors are not within the listed defendants.
  64. The correct answer is B (Three months before the initial bankruptcy event for an arm's length creditor.): Section 95(1)(a) of the Bankruptcy and Insolvency Act voids a transfer, payment or other transaction made in favour of an arm's length creditor with a view to giving that creditor a preference, if it occurred during the three months before the date of the initial bankruptcy event. Section 95(1)(b) uses a 12 month period where the creditor is not dealing at arm's length with the insolvent person. A six month period is used for the wage priority in section 81.3, not for preferences. The one year period belongs to the transfer at undervalue rules.
  65. The correct answer is C (Twelve months before the date of the initial bankruptcy event.): Section 95(1)(b) extends the preference period to 12 months before the date of the initial bankruptcy event where the creditor is not dealing at arm's length with the insolvent person. The three month period in section 95(1)(a) applies to arm's length creditors. A six month period appears in the wage priority provisions rather than in the preference rule. There is no 24 month preference period, and where an arm's length transaction has the effect of giving a preference, section 95(2) presumes the required intent absent evidence to the contrary.
  66. The correct answer is D (A transfer to a related party is caught within a five year window.): Section 96 catches a transfer at undervalue by an arm's length party if it occurred in the one year before the date of the initial bankruptcy event, the debtor was insolvent at the time or became insolvent because of the transfer, and the debtor intended to defraud, defeat or delay a creditor. For a non-arm's length party, the transfer is caught if it occurred in the one year before that date or in the five year period before that, subject to the insolvency or intention test. The transfer to the nephew therefore falls within the longer window. The other time periods stated do not match the section.
  67. The correct answer is C (The receiver takes its powers from the security agreement and the court.): Part XI of the Bankruptcy and Insolvency Act regulates the notice and reporting obligations of secured creditors and receivers, but it does not create the receiver's authority. A receiver appointed under a security agreement takes its powers from that agreement, and where the appointment is made by the court, from the court order. The borrower's articles do not confer powers on a receiver. The Personal Property Security Act registry records security interests and does not grant receivership powers.
  68. The correct answer is D (A conveyance made with intent to defeat creditors is void against them.): Section 2 of the Fraudulent Conveyances Act makes a conveyance of real or personal property void as against creditors if it was made with intent to defeat, hinder, delay or defraud them, and section 4 confirms that this applies even where the conveyance was made on valuable consideration. Section 3 protects a transferee who took on good consideration and in good faith without notice of the intent. Bankruptcy is not required, because the Act applies to creditors generally. The timing of land registry registration is not the test, and consideration is not the dividing line.
  69. The correct answer is B (That it was made while insolvent with intent to give an unjust preference.): Section 4(2) of the Assignments and Preferences Act voids a transfer made by a person in insolvent circumstances, unable to pay debts in full, or on the eve of insolvency, to or for a creditor with intent to give that creditor an unjust preference over other creditors. The claim is made against the creditor injured, delayed, prejudiced or postponed. Inadequacy of consideration and a family relationship are not the test, although they may be evidence. Registration after judgment is not required, and the transferee's knowledge of unpaid suppliers is not the statutory element.
  70. The correct answer is B (Necessary clothing of the debtor and the debtor's dependants.): Section 2(1) of the Execution Act exempts, at the debtor's option, necessary clothing of the debtor and dependants, household furnishings and appliances up to the prescribed amount, tools used to earn income up to the prescribed amount, one motor vehicle up to the prescribed amount, and other prescribed personal property up to the prescribed amount. Only one motor vehicle is within the exemption, so a second vehicle is not covered by that paragraph. An investment portfolio and rental income are not within the listed exemption categories.
  71. The correct answer is D ($8,578 for the vehicle and $12,997 for the residence equity.): The current prescribed amounts under the Execution Act and its regulation are $8,578 for one motor vehicle and $12,997 for the debtor's equity in a principal residence. The household furnishings and appliances amount is $17,091, and the tools amount is $17,362. Where the value of an exempted item exceeds the prescribed amount, the property may be seized and sold, and the debtor is entitled to be paid the prescribed amount out of the proceeds. The figures in the other options do not match the regulation.
  72. The correct answer is B (No tax applies to the assets, but tax applies to the services.): Once the election applies, no tax is payable on a supply of property or service made under the agreement for the supply of the business, except for a taxable supply of a service to be rendered by the supplier, a taxable supply of property by way of lease, licence or similar arrangement, and, where the recipient is not a registrant, a taxable supply by way of sale of real property. The management services are a taxable supply of a service rendered by the seller, so tax applies to them. The election therefore removes tax on the business assets but not on the services.
  73. The correct answer is D ($17,362, and $37,820 for a debtor engaged solely in farming.): The prescribed amount for tools and other personal property used by a debtor to earn income from the debtor's occupation is $17,362, and the amount is $37,820 for a debtor engaged solely in farming. Where the property is worth more than the prescribed amount, it may be seized and sold, and the debtor is entitled to be paid the prescribed amount out of the proceeds. The other figures belong to different exemption categories: $17,091 covers household furnishings and appliances, $8,578 covers one motor vehicle, and $12,997 covers equity in a principal residence.
  74. The correct answer is D (The landlord may re-enter if the rent remains unpaid for 15 days.): Section 18(1) of the Commercial Tenancies Act deems every demise to include an agreement that if the rent or any part of it remains unpaid for 15 days after the day it ought to have been paid, the landlord may re-enter and repossess the premises at any time afterwards without a formal demand. There is no 30 day or 60 day threshold in the Act for non payment of rent. Immediate re-entry on the day after the due date is not available under the section. The tenant may apply for relief against forfeiture.
  75. The correct answer is C (Serve notice of the breach and require the tenant to remedy it.): Section 19(2) provides that a right of re-entry or forfeiture for a breach of a covenant or condition other than payment of rent is not enforceable unless the landlord serves a notice specifying the particular breach, requiring the tenant to remedy it if it is capable of remedy and to make compensation in money, and the tenant then fails to do so within a reasonable time. The 15 day rule in section 18(1) concerns unpaid rent and does not apply here. A court order is not a precondition to serving the notice, and registering a notice on title is not the statutory requirement.
  76. The correct answer is C (The right of distress does not depend on an express agreement.): Section 3 of the Commercial Tenancies Act provides that the relation of landlord and tenant does not depend on tenure and that a reversion in the lessor is not necessary to create the relation or to make its incidents apply. The section also states that an agreement for that purpose is not necessary to give the landlord the right of distress. A written clause is therefore not required, and the absence of a reversion does not defeat the right. Distress is a landlord's remedy for arrears of rent, not a remedy belonging to the tenant.
  77. The correct answer is C (Apply to the Superior Court of Justice for relief against forfeiture.): Section 20(1) of the Commercial Tenancies Act allows a tenant facing enforcement of a right of re-entry or forfeiture to apply to the Superior Court of Justice for relief, and the court may grant relief on terms as to payment of rent, costs, expenses, damages, compensation or other matters, including an injunction against a like breach in the future. The Landlord and Tenant Board does not hear this commercial application. A property standards complaint addresses the condition of the premises rather than forfeiture, and a counter notice does not provide the statutory relief.
  78. The correct answer is D (The landlord may not distrain on goods belonging to someone else.): Section 31(2) of the Commercial Tenancies Act provides that a landlord must not distrain for rent on the goods of any person except the tenant or the person liable for the rent, even if those goods are found on the premises, subject to the listed exceptions. The customer's stored goods therefore fall outside the landlord's distress. A landlord may not distrain merely because goods are on the premises. The tenant's agreement does not make the customer's goods distrainable, and a court order is not the route the section provides.
  79. The correct answer is B (Tax is payable, since the renewals can push the term past 50 years.): Section 2(6) of the Land Transfer Tax Act exempts a lease from tax where the unexpired term at the time of registration cannot exceed 50 years, including renewals and extensions provided for in the lease or in a related document. A 30 year term with two 20 year renewal options can run for 70 years, so the exemption does not cover it. Where the exemption does not apply, the value of the consideration for the conveyance is the fair market value of the land rather than the rent. The length of an individual renewal option and the total rent are not the tests.
  80. The correct answer is D (Both returns are due within six months after the taxation year end.): Section 149.1(14) of the Income Tax Act requires every registered charity to file, within six months after the end of each taxation year, both an information return and a public information return in the prescribed form, without notice or demand. The registered charity information return is Form T3010. A three month or 12 month deadline does not match the section. Charities file with the Canada Revenue Agency rather than the Ontario Business Registry, although the information in the public return is made available to the public by the Minister.
  81. The correct answer is D (Obtain an order on requisition from a local registrar within one month.): Under section 3 of the Solicitors Act, where the retainer is not disputed and there are no special circumstances, an order for assessment may be obtained on requisition from a local registrar of the Superior Court of Justice. A client may seek the assessment of a bill already delivered within one month from its delivery. The Law Society does not set fees and cannot reduce a bill, so it may refer the person to the Assessment Office instead, and waiting to be sued is not the statutory route.
  82. The correct answer is B (No reference after judgment or 12 months, absent special circumstances.): Section 4(1) of the Solicitors Act provides that no reference for assessment shall be directed on an application by the party chargeable with the bill after a verdict or judgment has been obtained, or after twelve months from the time the bill was delivered, sent or left, except under special circumstances proved to the satisfaction of the court or judge. Both limits apply on these facts. A two year window, payment of the judgment, and the solicitor's consent are not substitutes for proof of special circumstances.
  83. The correct answer is C (Deliver the bill within 14 days from service of the order.): Section 6(1) of the Solicitors Act provides that where a client or other person obtains an order for the delivery and assessment of a solicitor's bill, the bill must be delivered within fourteen days from service of the order. The bill then stands referred to an assessment officer, and on the reference the solicitor must give credit for, and an account must be taken of, all sums received from or on account of the client, with any overpayment refunded. The 25 day period belongs to trust reconciliation, and the other two answers have no basis in the section.
  84. The correct answer is D (A contingency fee agreement is prohibited in criminal proceedings.): Section 28.1 of the Solicitors Act permits a solicitor to enter into a contingency fee agreement, but prohibits one where the solicitor is retained in respect of a proceeding under the Criminal Code (Canada), any other criminal or quasi-criminal proceeding, or a family law matter. Rule 3.6-2 of the Rules of Professional Conduct states the same prohibition. The size of the percentage, court approval, and a written agreement cannot save an agreement that the Act forbids in a criminal matter.
  85. The correct answer is C (Apply jointly to the Superior Court for approval within 90 days.): Section 28.1 of the Solicitors Act caps the amount payable under a contingency fee agreement that involves a percentage of the amount or value of property recovered. A solicitor may agree to more than the maximum percentage if the solicitor and client make a joint application to the Superior Court of Justice within 90 days after the agreement is executed and the court approves it. An agreement that is subject to that approval is not enforceable unless it is approved, and registration with the Law Society and a client acknowledgment are not the statutory route.
Case profes_05

A solicitor has acted for a construction company for three years on a series of lien claims. The company's new controller now instructs the solicitor to stop producing documents in a pending action, to backdate a letter that was never sent, and to tell the court that a required notice was served on time when it was not. The solicitor explains that she cannot do any of those things and that she will have to withdraw from the file. The controller says the company will not pay the outstanding account if the solicitor withdraws before the trial. The trial is six weeks away and the company has not yet retained another lawyer, but the solicitor has confirmed that the trial date can be preserved.

  1. The correct answer is A (Rule 3.7-1, which requires good cause and reasonable notice.): Rule 3.7-1 provides that a lawyer shall not withdraw from representation of a client except for good cause and on reasonable notice to the client. The more specific rules in section 3.7 identify grounds such as a serious loss of confidence and non-payment, and rule 3.7-8 governs what the lawyer must do after deciding to withdraw. Rules 3.7-2 and 3.7-8 do not state the general test, and rule 3.7-3 concerns non-payment of fees rather than final accounts.
  2. The correct answer is A (She must withdraw, because the instructions require breaching the rules.): Rule 3.7-7 provides that, subject to the rules about criminal proceedings and the direction of the tribunal, a lawyer shall withdraw if discharged by the client, if the client's instructions require the lawyer to act contrary to the rules or by-laws under the Law Society Act, or if the lawyer is not competent to continue to handle the matter. The controller's instructions fall within the second branch, so withdrawal is mandatory rather than a matter of choice. An indemnity, a file note, and a delayed withdrawal do not make the instructions permissible.
  3. The correct answer is C (Deliver the client's papers and property, subject to the solicitor's lien.): Rule 3.7-9 requires a lawyer on discharge or withdrawal to notify the client in writing stating the fact of withdrawal and the reasons if any, and in litigation to state that the trial will proceed on the scheduled date and that the client should retain new counsel promptly. Subject to the lawyer's lien, the lawyer must deliver to the client all papers and property to which the client is entitled, must account for all funds of the client held or previously dealt with, including refunding any remuneration not earned, and must render promptly an account for outstanding fees and co-operate with the successor. Holding every document for a regulator's approval, keeping the original documents for the trial, and sending the trust balance to the Law Society are not steps the rule permits.
  4. The correct answer is C (That the former solicitor approves, has withdrawn, or was discharged.): Rule 3.7-10 provides that before agreeing to represent a client, a successor licensee shall be satisfied that the former licensee approves, has withdrawn, or has been discharged by the client. In this file the first solicitor has withdrawn, so that condition is met. Payment of the former solicitor's account, a fresh retainer and deposit, and the former solicitor's consent to the transfer of the file are not what the rule requires.
  5. The correct answer is A (Direct supervision and complete professional responsibility for the work.): Rule 6.1-1 requires a lawyer, in accordance with the by-laws, to assume complete professional responsibility for the practice of law and to directly supervise non-lawyers to whom particular tasks and functions are assigned. The rule and By-Law 7.1, Part I require the lawyer to review the non-lawyer's work at frequent intervals and to maintain a direct relationship with the client. A lawyer may assign routine drafting to a competent non-lawyer under supervision, so personal performance of every task is not required, and the rule imposes no Law Society permission and no annual confidentiality agreement.
  6. The correct answer is B (The solicitor must not permit the clerk to use her personal package.): Rule 6.1-5 provides that when a lawyer has a Personalized Security Package, the lawyer shall not permit others, including a non-lawyer employee, to use it. Rule 6.1-6 imposes the matching duty where a non-lawyer employee holds a package, requiring the lawyer to make sure that the employee does not permit others to use it. Because only the appropriately qualified person may make the prescribed statements, the package is personal and cannot be lent, whatever signing authority the clerk may have.
  7. The correct answer is D (Meaningful training and exposure to the practical aspects of law.): Rule 6.2-2 provides that a lawyer acting as a principal to a student shall provide the student with meaningful training and exposure to and involvement in work that will give the student knowledge and experience of the practical aspects of the law, together with an appreciation of the traditions and ethics of the profession. The commentary adds that the principal or supervising lawyer is responsible for the actions of students acting under their direction. Payment of fees, monthly reports, and a set number of instruction hours are not what the rule requires.
  8. The correct answer is C (That no one is denied services on a prohibited ground.): Rule 6.3.1-2 prohibits a lawyer from allowing anyone to be denied services, or to receive inferior service, on the basis of the grounds set out in rule 6.3.1, which include creed, ancestry, place of origin and ethnic origin. Rule 6.3.1-1 gives a lawyer a special responsibility to respect the requirements of human rights laws in force in Ontario and not to discriminate in professional dealings. Referring the client elsewhere, charging a reduced fee, and reporting the refusal would not make the policy lawful.
  9. The correct answer is D (Accommodate the employee unless doing so would cause undue hardship.): Rule 6.3.1-3 requires a lawyer's employment practices not to offend rules 6.3.1-1 and 6.3.1-2 or rule 6.3-3. The commentary states that a rule applied to everyone can still have a discriminatory effect, and that a requirement with such an effect breaches rule 6.3.1-3 unless changing or eliminating it would cause undue hardship. The Human Rights Code requires accommodation to the point of undue hardship. A neutral schedule, the client's consent, and paid vacation time are not answers to an adverse effect.
  10. The correct answer is A (Agree to the reasonable request, since the client is not prejudiced.): Rule 7.2-1 requires a lawyer to be courteous, civil and to act in good faith with all persons with whom the lawyer deals in the course of practice, and rule 7.2-1.1 requires the lawyer to agree to reasonable requests concerning trial dates, adjournments, the waiver of procedural formalities and similar matters that do not prejudice the client's rights. Because the adjournment causes no prejudice here, the request is reasonable and should be granted. Refusal, a costs motion, and a trade of favours all fall short of the rule.
  11. The correct answer is D (The marketing must be demonstrably true, accurate and verifiable.): Rule 4.2-1 permits a lawyer to market legal services only if the marketing is demonstrably true, accurate and verifiable, is neither misleading, confusing or deceptive nor likely to mislead, confuse or deceive, and is in the best interests of the public and consistent with a high standard of professionalism. The commentary lists statements about amounts recovered unless accompanied by a warning that past results do not indicate future results, suggestions of qualitative superiority, and suggestions that a lawyer is aggressive as examples that may contravene the rule. Law Society pre-approval and a published fee schedule are not required.
  12. The correct answer is B (The advertisement must list the extra charges and honour the stated fee.): Rule 4.2-2 permits a lawyer to advertise fees only if the advertising is reasonably precise as to the services offered for each fee quoted, states whether other amounts such as disbursements, third party charges and taxes will be charged in addition to the fee, and requires the lawyer to adhere strictly to the advertised fee in every applicable case. Rule 4.2-2.1 adds detailed requirements where a price is advertised for a residential real estate transaction. Nothing in either rule exempts market priced fees, or requires the firm's highest fee or its file count.
  13. The correct answer is B (It has a Hearing Division and an Appeal Division.): Section 49.20.1 of the Law Society Act establishes the Law Society Tribunal, made up of a chair, the members of the Hearing Division, the members of the Appeal Division, and any temporary members. The Hearing Division and the Appeal Division hear licensing and discipline matters under the Act and the by-laws, and the Tribunal has its own Rules of Practice and Procedure. The Complaints Resolution Commissioner is a separate office under sections 49.14 to 49.19 and is not a division of the Tribunal.
  14. The correct answer is A (The complaint is filed in LSO Connects and generally acknowledged within 48 hours.): The Law Society publishes that a complaint is made through the online complaint form in LSO Connects, that the Law Society generally acknowledges receipt within 48 hours, and that up to 15 supporting documents may be uploaded. Complaints and investigations are otherwise confidential, subject to section 49.12 of the Law Society Act, unless a Tribunal proceeding follows, which is open to the public and the media. A sworn filing with the Tribunal, a 30 day deadline, and a first stop at the Commissioner's office do not match the published process.
  15. The correct answer is C (Disclose the changed fee basis to the client in a timely fashion as it develops.): Because rule 3.6-1 requires timely disclosure as well as fairness, a lawyer whose fee basis changes as the scope of a matter expands must disclose that changed basis to the client in a timely fashion as the change develops, not only once the file is complete. It is not enough that the original estimate was fair and reasonable at the time it was given, since the timely disclosure obligation continues as circumstances change during the retainer. Allowing the client to discover the higher cost only at the final bill is exactly the kind of delayed disclosure the rule is meant to prevent. The rule does not require the lawyer to cap the bill at the original estimate, since fees may properly increase with genuinely expanded scope as long as the increase is fair, reasonable and disclosed in time.
  16. The correct answer is C (Interest may run from a date one month after the bill was delivered.): Under the Solicitors Act, a solicitor may charge interest on unpaid fees, charges or disbursements calculated from a date that is one month after the bill was delivered, which in this case would be July 1, not the date of delivery itself. Rule 3.6-1.1 confirms that a lawyer shall not charge interest on an overdue account except as the Solicitors Act or other law permits, so an earlier starting point tied to the retainer date is not available. Calculating interest from when the underlying work was performed ignores the statute's own trigger, which is tied to delivery of the bill, not performance of the services. A missed payment date chosen informally by the lawyer has no basis in the statute's fixed one month rule.
  17. The correct answer is D (The client is entitled to interest on the overpayment, calculated from the date it was made.): The Solicitors Act provides that where the client has overpaid, the client is entitled to interest on the overpayment calculated from the date the overpayment was made, mirroring the rule that lets a solicitor charge interest on an unpaid account from a fixed date. Treating the interest as something the lawyer may simply choose to pay voluntarily ignores that the statute creates an actual entitlement rather than a discretionary courtesy. Denying any interest on an overpayment misstates the statute, which expressly addresses this situation. Calculating from the date the assessment concludes rather than the date of the original overpayment would shortchange the client for the period the lawyer already held the excess money.
  18. The correct answer is D (No, because interest may only run from one month after the bill was delivered.): The statute fixes the starting point for interest at one month after the bill was delivered, not at the start of the retainer when the work first began, so the lawyer's proposed earlier date is not permitted. Suggesting the retainer letter can simply specify any starting date the lawyer prefers ignores that rule 3.6-1.1 only allows interest as permitted by the Solicitors Act or other law, not by private agreement alone. The client's general agreement to pay for services as rendered does not itself create a separate contractual right to charge interest from an earlier date. It is also incorrect to say interest can never be charged, since the Solicitors Act does permit it, just from the later, statutory date.
  19. The correct answer is C (The fees and the disbursements must be clearly and separately detailed on the statement.): Rule 3.6-3 requires that in a statement of account delivered to a client, the lawyer must clearly and separately detail the amounts charged as fees and as disbursements, rather than presenting one blended figure. Showing everything as a single combined amount does not satisfy the rule's requirement that the two categories be kept clearly distinct. Itemizing only the disbursements while leaving the fee as an unexplained lump sum still fails to give the client the separate fee detail the rule requires. The reverse, itemizing only the fee while leaving disbursements unspecified, equally fails to meet the rule's requirement for the disbursement side of the account.
  20. The correct answer is A (The statement fails rule 3.6-3, which requires fees and disbursements to be shown separately.): A statement showing only one undivided total, with no breakdown of fees versus disbursements, does not satisfy rule 3.6-3, which requires the two categories to be detailed clearly and separately regardless of whether the client agreed to an overall price in advance. A prior agreement to a flat price does not excuse the lawyer from the separate itemization the rule requires once the account is delivered. Fairness and reasonableness of the total amount, governed by a different rule, does not cure a statement's failure to separate fees from disbursements. The itemization requirement applies automatically and does not depend on the client first asking for a breakdown.
  21. The correct answer is B (The lawyer must divide the fees and disbursements equitably between the clients.): Rule 3.6-4 provides that where a lawyer acts for two or more clients in the same matter, the lawyer must divide the fees and disbursements equitably between them unless the clients themselves have agreed otherwise. An equitable division is not the same as an automatically equal split in every case, since an equitable outcome depends on the circumstances of the particular matter. Billing whichever client happens to be easiest to reach for the entire amount ignores the equitable division the rule requires among all of the jointly retained clients. Nothing in the rule requires a third party arbitrator to decide the split, since the lawyer is expected to divide the account equitably in the ordinary course.
  22. The correct answer is D (A referral agreement confirming the client was told acceptance of the referral is not required.): Rule 3.6-6.0 defines a referral agreement as a signed written agreement between the referring licensee, the receiving licensee and the client, in the Law Society's own form, confirming that the client was advised they have no obligation to accept the referral. A general retainer agreement describes the scope of the new lawyer's work but is not itself the specific referral document the rule requires. A conflicts waiver signed only by the two lawyers leaves out the client entirely, when the rule requires all three parties to sign. An assignment purporting to transfer the file outright is not how a referral fee arrangement under this rule is meant to be documented.
  23. The correct answer is B (The referral fee must not increase the total fee the client ultimately has to pay.): Rule 3.6-6.1 permits paying and accepting a referral fee only where, among other conditions, the fee is fair and reasonable and does not increase the total fee payable by the client, which matches the referring lawyer's plan to keep the client's total cost unchanged. Adding the referral fee on top of the receiving firm's normal charge would increase what the client pays, contrary to the rule's own condition. There is no fixed percentage such as ten percent that the rule specifies for a referral fee. Saying the referral fee has no bearing on the client's ultimate cost overlooks the very condition that ties the referral fee directly to what the client is charged.
  24. The correct answer is D (A referral fee is not permitted where the referral resulted from a conflict of interest.): Rule 3.6-6.1 sets out several conditions for a permitted referral fee, one of which is that the referral was not made because of a conflict of interest or by a suspended licensee, so a referral fee is not available in this situation regardless of the client's consent. Written client consent does not cure a referral that fails this specific condition of the rule. Disclosing the fee to the Law Society afterward does not convert an otherwise impermissible referral fee into a permitted one. The fact that the client ultimately receives competent representation elsewhere does not change the rule's separate prohibition on charging a referral fee when the referral itself was prompted by a conflict.
  25. The correct answer is D (A lawyer shall not directly or indirectly share or split fees with a non-licensee.): Rule 3.6-7 provides that a lawyer shall not directly or indirectly share, split or divide fees with anyone who is not a lawyer or paralegal, which squarely prohibits the proposed arrangement with the marketing consultant. There is no exception in the rule for a modest dollar amount, since the prohibition applies regardless of the size of the payment. Disclosing the arrangement in the retainer letter does not make an otherwise prohibited fee split with a non-licensee permissible. The rule draws no distinction for general business advice as opposed to other kinds of non-licensee involvement, so that framing does not create an exception either.
  26. The correct answer is D (It shares legal fees with a non-licensee, which rule 3.6-7 prohibits.): Paying a marketing company a percentage of fees earned from the clients it sends amounts to sharing legal fees with a non-licensee, which rule 3.6-7 prohibits regardless of how the arrangement is labelled. Online marketing is not treated as a special category exempt from the fee sharing prohibition simply because the referrals arrive through a website rather than by phone or in person. Disclosure of the arrangement to the clients who are referred does not cure the underlying prohibited fee split between the firm and the non-licensee company. The marketing company never giving legal advice is beside the point, since the rule targets the sharing of fees itself, not whether the non-licensee also practises law.
  27. The correct answer is B (Rule 3.6-8 creates a limited exception to the fee sharing prohibition for such practices.): Rule 3.6-8 creates limited exceptions to the general fee sharing prohibition for multi-discipline practices, as well as for interprovincial or international law firms, recognizing that these particular structures involve fee arrangements the general rule was not designed to prevent. It is therefore not accurate to say the general prohibition applies identically in every practice structure without exception. The exception is not limited to firms with offices in more than one province, since it separately covers genuine multi-discipline practices within Ontario. There is no requirement in the rule that a non-lawyer partner be a close relative of a lawyer in the firm for the exception to apply.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Give a notice of non payment, no later than fourteen days after receiving the proper invoice.): An owner who wants to withhold part of a proper invoice must give the contractor a notice of non payment, in the prescribed form and manner, no later than fourteen days after receiving the invoice, setting out the amount not being paid and all of the reasons. The twenty eight day figure describes the payment deadline itself, not the shorter window for the notice of non payment. Simply refusing to pay with no written notice does not satisfy the Act, and the owner cannot wait for an adjudicator's determination before ever giving notice, since the notice comes first and is what can lead to an adjudication in the first place.
  2. The correct answer is B (It must be at most three percent of the first million dollars, plus two percent further.): Substantial performance is reached when the improvement, or a substantial part of it, is ready for its intended use and the remaining cost to complete or correct it does not exceed three per cent of the first one million dollars of the contract price plus two per cent of the next million and one per cent of the balance, rather than a flat percentage of the whole contract. A flat five per cent or one per cent test does not match this tiered structure, and the standard does not require the remaining deficiency to be zero, since some remaining cost is expected under the test itself.
  3. The correct answer is D (No later than seven days after the contractor receives payment from the owner.): Once a contractor receives full payment from the owner under the prompt payment scheme, the contractor must pay each subcontractor within seven days of receiving that payment, not fourteen, thirty, or forty five days. A different, longer timeline of thirty five days after the contractor's own invoice to the owner applies only where the owner has not paid the contractor, which is a different situation than the one described here. The short seven day window when payment has actually been received is meant to move money down the payment chain quickly.
  4. The correct answer is A (No more than ninety days after the contract is completed, abandoned or terminated.): An adjudication about a contract may not be started more than ninety days after the contract is completed, abandoned or terminated, unless the parties agree otherwise, so the ninety day figure is the outer limit rather than thirty days or a full year. A thirty day window would be far too short given how these disputes typically develop, while a full year overstates how long a party actually has. The rule is also not open ended, since a limit does exist, it is simply subject to being extended by agreement between the parties.
  5. The correct answer is A (Pay $100 into court, then apply once the amount due, including costs, has been paid.): Section 23 lets the mortgagor pay $100 into court as security for costs and apply to the court, and if judgment has not yet been recovered, the court must dismiss the action once the mortgagor performs the covenant or pays what is due together with the costs of the action. A simple notice to the mortgagee's lawyer, with no payment into court, does not satisfy this provision, and there is no reason to wait for a final order of foreclosure before acting, since that would come much later and after other rights had already been lost. The section also does not require the consent of subsequent encumbrancers before the mortgagor can apply.
  6. The correct answer is C (A request to redeem, filed within the time to deliver a defence or before default.): A defendant who wants to redeem must serve and file a request to redeem, in the form the rule prescribes, within the time allowed for delivering a statement of defence or before being noted in default, whichever comes first. A request for sale is the different form used by a defendant who wants a sale instead of foreclosure, and filing it only after judgment has already been signed would be too late for either purpose. A motion for summary judgment or a cross claim against co defendants are not the mechanisms this rule uses for a defendant seeking to redeem.
  7. The correct answer is A (The lender is deemed to be the landlord under the existing tenancy agreement.): A person who obtains title to a mortgaged residential complex by foreclosure or power of sale, where the complex is subject to a tenancy agreement, is deemed to be the landlord under that tenancy agreement, so the lender steps into the former owner's shoes rather than starting with no relationship to the tenant. The tenancy does not automatically end simply because the power of sale transfer is registered, and the lender cannot treat an existing lawful tenant as a trespasser, since the deeming provision preserves the tenancy rather than extinguishing it.
  8. The correct answer is D (No, unless a judge grants an order permitting the lender to proceed earlier.): Where a mortgage contains a demand or notice provision of this kind, no further proceeding and no action to enforce the mortgage may be started until the time named in the demand or notice has passed, unless a judge grants an order permitting it. The lender cannot simply proceed whenever it chooses, and there is no requirement to serve a second identical notice before an order can be sought. A judge does retain the authority to permit earlier action in an appropriate case, so it is inaccurate to say no court can ever shorten the period.
  9. The correct answer is D (At least ninety days' written notice, in the Board's approved form, stating the new rent.): A landlord must give the tenant at least ninety days' written notice of an intention to increase rent, in the form approved by the Board, setting out the new rent, and this rule applies whether or not the increase exceeds the guideline. A thirty or sixty day figure would both understate the actual notice period the Act requires. Staying within the guideline changes whether Board approval is needed, but it does not remove the separate requirement to give the tenant proper written notice before the increase can take effect.
  10. The correct answer is C (No, because at least twelve months must pass since the last increase for that tenant.): A landlord may increase rent only where at least twelve months have elapsed since the tenant's last rent increase in that unit, so an increase attempted at the eight month mark comes too soon, regardless of the size of the increase. There is no six month minimum interval that would permit an increase now, and the rule is not simply about calendar years, since it turns on a full twelve months measured from the date of the previous increase rather than from January to December.
  11. The correct answer is D (At least one hundred twenty days' notice, ending on the last day of a rental period.): A landlord who instead gives at least one hundred twenty days' notice, ending on the last day of a rental period or the end of the term, is not required to pay the tenant one month's rent or offer another unit, unlike the ordinary sixty day landlord's own use notice which does carry that compensation obligation. A thirty day period is not the figure tied to the compensation exemption, and giving no notice at all is never an option, since a notice in the proper form is always required to end a tenancy this way.
  12. The correct answer is D (At least one hundred twenty days, ending on the last day of a rental period or the term.): Where a landlord requires possession to demolish, convert to a non residential use, or carry out repairs or renovations needing a building permit and vacant possession, the termination date in the notice must be at least one hundred twenty days after the notice is given, ending on the last day of a rental period or the end of the term. Sixty, thirty, and fourteen day periods all belong to other kinds of notices under the Act and understate what this particular ground requires. Tenants facing this kind of notice may also have separate compensation or right of first refusal protections.
  13. The correct answer is B (Conduct a new reserve fund study, since the studies are required at least every three years.): A corporation must conduct a reserve fund study within three years of completing the study required before it, and every three years after that, so reaching the three year mark since the last study means a new one is due. A five year cycle overstates the interval the Act actually requires. A corporation is not permitted to treat one study as sufficient forever, and no court order is needed to trigger the ongoing statutory obligation, since the timing is set directly by the regulation.
  14. The correct answer is C (It runs from the latest of the events listed in the Act, such as receiving full disclosure.): For a new unit purchase from a declarant, the ten day rescission period runs from the latest of several listed events, which typically includes when the purchaser actually receives the required disclosure materials, rather than from a single fixed date such as signing, taking possession, or registration of the declaration alone. Treating any one of those single events as the only trigger risks telling the purchaser the window opened, or closed, at the wrong time. The purchaser or the purchaser's lawyer must give written notice of rescission before accepting a deed in registerable form.
Case real_e_06

A purchaser is buying a commercial office building in Ottawa for $2,000,000 from a corporation that the purchaser's lawyer has confirmed is not resident in Canada for tax purposes. Before closing, the vendor obtains and delivers to the purchaser a certificate issued under section 116 of the Income Tax Act that fixes the certificate limit at $1,700,000. The building has always been rental property in the vendor's hands and is capital property, not inventory real property, to the vendor.

  1. The correct answer is C (Twenty five per cent of the three hundred thousand dollars above the certificate limit.): The purchaser must pay twenty five per cent of the excess of the purchase cost over the certificate limit, and here that excess is the difference between the two million dollar price and the seventeen hundred thousand dollar limit, not the whole price. Fifty per cent applies to a narrower category such as non capital or depreciable property, which does not describe this rental building held as capital property. Delivering a certificate reduces the withholding amount rather than eliminating it entirely, because the certificate limit is below the purchase price.
  2. The correct answer is D (It fixes a certificate limit that measures and can reduce the purchaser's withholding duty.): The Minister issues a certificate fixing a certificate limit once satisfied the vendor has paid an amount on account of tax or furnished acceptable security, and that limit is what measures and can reduce the purchaser's own withholding obligation. The certificate does not guarantee that every future tax liability on the sale has been paid in full, and it does not remove the purchaser's lawyer's own need to confirm residency before closing. Nothing about the process shifts the purchaser's statutory withholding duty onto the vendor's lawyer instead.
  3. The correct answer is D (None, because the purchaser reasonably concluded the vendor was a Canadian resident.): The withholding duty attaches where the purchaser did not conclude, after reasonable inquiry, that the vendor was resident in Canada, so where the purchaser genuinely and reasonably reached that conclusion, no withholding obligation arises even though the vendor turns out to be non resident. This means the purchaser does not owe twenty five per cent or fifty per cent of the price regardless of the reasonable conclusion reached, and the obligation is not something that only springs up later if the vendor eventually admits the truth.
  4. The correct answer is D (Fifty per cent, since inventory real property triggers the higher withholding rate.): Where the property acquired from a non resident is not capital real property, for example inventory real property, or is depreciable property, the purchaser's withholding rises to fifty per cent instead of the twenty five per cent that applies to ordinary capital real property. There is no separate ten per cent rate tied to transaction size under this provision, and inventory property is not excluded from withholding altogether, since it is precisely the kind of property that triggers the higher rate.
  5. The correct answer is C (For the forty years immediately preceding the current dealing with the land.): A person dealing with registry land need not show a good and sufficient chain of title for more than the forty years immediately preceding the dealing, which the Registry Act defines as the title search period. Twenty years understates that period, and sixty years overstates it well beyond what the statute actually requires. There is no rule requiring the lawyer to trace title all the way back to an original Crown grant, since the forty year period is specifically designed to shorten that kind of exhaustive historical search.
  6. The correct answer is A (Quarterly, on the prescribed dates the timing regulation sets out for large holdings.): For a disposition of a beneficial interest that results from acquiring or increasing an interest in a trust or partnership with fifty or more holders, tax is payable quarterly on the prescribed dates instead of on the thirtieth day after the disposition. There is no rule requiring immediate payment on closing for a large partnership, and the ordinary thirty day rule is exactly what this exception replaces once the fifty holder threshold is met. Payment is not deferred all the way until the partnership eventually winds up.
  7. The correct answer is D (No general ceiling exists, since parties may stipulate for any rate of interest they agree upon.): The Interest Act allows any person to stipulate for, allow and exact any rate of interest or discount that is agreed on, which is why there is no general statutory cap on mortgage interest rates in Canada. There is no federal regulation fixing one universal percentage ceiling, and the Ontario Mortgages Act does not itself impose a single maximum rate applicable to every private mortgage. There is also no rule requiring court pre approval before a lender and borrower can agree on a rate above five percent.
  8. The correct answer is D (In writing, using plain language, disclosed before the borrower signs the agreement.): A licensed mortgage broker or agent must disclose the material risks of the mortgage agreement in writing and in plain language before the client signs, so the disclosure must be both documented and timed before signature rather than left to a verbal exchange. Providing the writing only after signature defeats the purpose of the requirement, since the borrower needs the information before committing. A general notice posted at the office is not the kind of individualized, timely disclosure the borrower is entitled to receive.
  9. The correct answer is D (To pay the principal and interest then due, plus three months further interest, to redeem.): Where principal or interest secured by a mortgage is not payable until more than five years after the date of the mortgage, once those five years have passed the person entitled to redeem may pay the principal and interest then due, plus three months further interest in lieu of notice, and no further interest is chargeable after that. This right does not require paying the entire remaining two years of interest, and it does not depend on the lender's written consent or on first obtaining a court order, since the Act itself creates the right. The section specifically excludes mortgages given by corporations, which is not the situation here.
  10. The correct answer is C (Thirteen per cent, made up of a five per cent federal part and an eight per cent provincial part.): In a participating province the harmonized sales tax applies at a combined rate of thirteen per cent in Ontario, made up of a five per cent federal part and an eight per cent provincial part, so a fifteen per cent figure overstates the true combined rate and a ten per cent figure understates it. Ontario does have a provincial component of the harmonized tax, unlike a non participating province where only the five per cent federal GST applies, so treating Ontario as GST only would also be incorrect on a taxable supply.
  11. The correct answer is A (No tax is owed, since a sale of an existing home by a non builder is exempt.): A supply by way of sale of a residential complex made by a person who is not a builder of the complex is an exempt supply, so a typical resale from an individual owner who is not a builder and has not claimed input tax credits on it does not attract tax on the price. It is not correct that tax applies to every resale regardless of the seller's status, since the exemption turns precisely on that status, and there is no separate purchaser signed exemption certificate needed to make an already exempt supply exempt.
  12. The correct answer is B (Claim the new housing rebate, since the purchase is taxable and the residence requirement is met.): A builder who sells a new or substantially renovated home is making a taxable supply, so the buyer pays tax on the price, but an individual who buys the home for use as their own or a relation's primary residence and meets the other conditions can claim the new housing rebate to recover part of what was paid. It is incorrect to say nothing can be claimed, and new construction from a builder is not simply tax exempt the way a resale from a non builder is. The rebate is also not conditioned on reselling the unit within a year, since it is meant for a purchaser keeping the home as a residence.
  13. The correct answer is A (A statement that the transferor and transferee are related persons under section 251 of the Income Tax Act, since a corporation and the individual who controls it are related persons for that purpose.): The regulation lists related persons under section 251 of the Income Tax Act, which includes a corporation and the person who controls it, as one of the recognized exceptions to the ordinary two lawyer rule, supported by a compliance with law statement describing that relationship. The same person and change of tenure statement requires the transferor and transferee to be the literal same legal person, such as an individual severing a joint tenancy, and a corporation is a separate legal person from the individual who controls it even though beneficial ownership has not changed, so that statement does not fit this transaction. Stating that separate, unrelated firms are involved is not a recognized alternative statement at all. The estate trustee alternative and the government body exemption do not describe this fact pattern, since no estate and no government body is involved here.
  14. The correct answer is C (The fund is topped up from the Consolidated Revenue Fund whenever its balance drops below one million dollars.): The Land Titles Assurance Fund is continued under the Land Titles Act and is topped up from the Consolidated Revenue Fund whenever its balance falls below one million dollars, which keeps it solvent to pay legitimate claims. It is not a purely private insurance arrangement, and it is not funded through a fixed annual fee charged to every lawyer. The fund is also not limited to commercial property, since it protects owners of both residential and commercial land who are registered under the Act.
  15. The correct answer is B (The owner may claim compensation from the Land Titles Assurance Fund for the registrar's error.): A person is entitled to compensation from the Assurance Fund where the person is wrongfully deprived of land by a registrar's misdescription, omission or error, which squarely covers the situation described. It is wrong to say a registrar's clerical mistake is never compensable, since that is one of the specific grounds the Act lists for a claim. Suing a previous owner personally or relying only on separately purchased title insurance ignores the statutory remedy that the Land Titles Act itself provides for exactly this kind of loss.
  16. The correct answer is D (The application is still within time because the six year limit has not yet expired.): An application for compensation from the Assurance Fund arising from a fraudulent instrument must be made within six years from the time the loss was suffered, so an application brought four and a half years after the loss is still within the statutory window. There is no one year deadline in this context, and the client does not need a prior criminal conviction against the fraudster before applying to the Director of Titles. Loss caused by a fraudulent instrument is expressly one of the grounds the Act recognizes for compensation, provided the applicant also shows the required due diligence.
  17. The correct answer is C (The registered owner's title is subject to that enforceable writ because it is recorded on the register.): Under the Land Titles Act, a registered owner's title is subject to enforceable writs of execution against that owner that have been recorded on the register, which is exactly the situation the purchaser's lawyer has identified. There is no rule that a recorded writ loses effect simply because the owner has held the land for a year, and a writ can absolutely affect registered real property, not just personal property. A writ does not vanish from the register automatically on any fixed anniversary, so the lawyer must address it directly before closing.
  18. The correct answer is B (The lawyer searches the electronic index of writs maintained by the sheriff for the relevant names.): Writs of execution are filed with the sheriff and entered into an electronic index the sheriff maintains, which is why the purchaser's lawyer searches that index for the names of the parties who have owned the land, rather than relying on the vendor's own word. The Assurance Fund is a compensation mechanism for loss, not a registry of pending writs, so it is not the place to check for outstanding judgments. The municipal tax roll records realty taxes, not execution writs, so it cannot substitute for a proper sheriff's index search.
  19. The correct answer is B (The omission creates real risk, since an unnoted writ against a past owner may surface later.): Because title under the Land Titles Act can be affected by a writ against a prior owner if a note of that writ was entered on the register, skipping the search against a name that owned the property during a relevant period leaves a genuine gap in the due diligence, even though a writ against a prior owner not noted on title is not enforceable. Claiming that only judgments against the current owner ever matter overlooks the way the register works for recorded writs tied to earlier ownership. There is no automatic five year expiry rule for these writs, and relying on the prior owner to volunteer the information is not a substitute for a proper search.
  20. The correct answer is C (Realty taxes are a special lien on the land ranking ahead of almost every other claim.): Taxes are a special lien on the land that ranks in priority to every claim, privilege, lien or encumbrance of nearly every other person, which is why a purchaser's lawyer treats the tax certificate as such an important part of due diligence. It is not true that realty taxes rank behind registered mortgages, and they are not merely a personal debt of the seller that stays away from the land itself. A change of ownership at closing does not automatically wipe out an outstanding tax arrears, so confirming the account balance protects the purchaser from inheriting that liability.
  21. The correct answer is B (Because a purchaser can inherit utility arrears or find a needed service disconnected at closing.): The Law Society's residential guidelines treat utility status as one of the off title inquiries a lawyer discusses with the client because a purchaser can be exposed to arrears on the account or to a service that is disconnected right at closing. Utility companies do not automatically register a formal lien against title the way a tax lien arises, and utility status has nothing to do with confirming the boundary between neighbouring properties. Unpaid utility charges are not given the same statutory priority as realty taxes under the Municipal Act, 2001, which is part of why the two searches are kept separate.
  22. The correct answer is B (Raise the open permit as a requisition and discuss the risk with the client before proceeding.): A purchaser's lawyer who finds an open building permit should raise it as a requisition and discuss the practical risk with the client, since an unclosed permit can signal unfinished or unapproved work that may later create a compliance problem. There is no rule limiting relevance to permits issued in the past year, and an open permit does not close itself simply because ownership changes hands. Reporting the issue only to the vendor's lawyer and stopping there would leave the purchaser without the benefit of the lawyer's own advice on how serious the problem is.
  23. The correct answer is B (The unresolved compliance order is a real title risk that could bind the purchaser after closing.): A compliance order directing that a contravention be corrected can bind the owner of the property and affect its use, so an unresolved order with a passed deadline is a genuine title risk the purchaser could inherit after closing rather than something tied only to the person who first received it. Listing a property for sale does nothing to cancel an outstanding order, and a pending sale does not remove a building inspector's ongoing authority to enforce compliance. The lawyer needs to requisition resolution of the order or otherwise address the risk before advising the client to close.
  24. The correct answer is C (Zoning is set by municipal by-law, and non-compliance may be solved by a by-law amendment or minor variance.): Zoning is imposed by municipal by-law under the Planning Act, and where a property does not comply with a by-law for a permitted use, the by-law can be amended or a minor variance can be granted by the committee of adjustment. It is not accurate to say zoning is set entirely at the provincial level with no municipal process to change it, since municipalities administer their own zoning by-laws. Zoning applies regardless of a building's age, so an older house is not automatically exempt, and there is a real process available to the client rather than no path forward at all.
  25. The correct answer is D (The regulation on surveys, plans and descriptions of land made under the Land Titles Act.): Where land is in the Land Titles system, the legal description entered on the parcel must comply with the Land Titles Act, and a regulation made under that Act sets out requirements for surveys, plans and descriptions of land. Surveys are not a form of building permit application, so the Building Code regime does not govern their format. The federal Interest Act deals with interest rates on debt obligations and has nothing to do with survey standards, and it is incorrect to say there is no governing regulation at all.
  26. The correct answer is C (Yes, an omission to pay required insurance can also trigger the statutory power.): The statutory power of sale under section 24 becomes exercisable at any time after three months have passed from a default in payment of money due under the mortgage, or after an omission to pay insurance the mortgagor was required to pay. An insurance failure on its own, once three months have passed, is enough to bring the statutory power into play, so it is not limited to missed principal or interest payments. There is no need to start a separate court action just to address an insurance default under this provision. The power does not additionally require a missed payment on top of the insurance failure.
  27. The correct answer is D (No, the default must continue for at least fifteen days before notice is given.): Where a mortgage contains its own power of sale clause triggered by a stated default, the notice of exercising that power may not be given until the default has continued for at least 15 days. Serving notice after only seven days does not satisfy that minimum waiting period, so the notice was given too early. There is no rule allowing notice as soon as a payment is first missed, and 45 days of default is the statutory power of sale timeline in a different part of the Act, not the contractual clause described here. A contractual power of sale clause does not automatically shorten the minimum waiting period the Act requires before notice can be given.
  28. The correct answer is C (February 20, fifty days after the default under this contractual clause.): Under a mortgage's own power of sale clause, notice cannot be given until the default has continued for at least 15 days, and the sale cannot be completed until at least 35 days after that notice is given, for a combined minimum of 50 days from the original default. Counting 15 days from January 1 gives January 16 as the earliest date notice may be served, and counting a further 35 days from that date gives February 20 as the earliest date the sale can be completed. Thirty five days alone only accounts for the post notice waiting period and ignores the earlier 15 day default requirement. Eighty days overstates the combined minimum period the Act actually requires.
  29. The correct answer is C (No, a title acquired under a notice given in professed compliance is protected.): Where a notice has been given in professed compliance with Part III of the Mortgages Act, the purchaser's title is not liable to be impeached on the ground that the notice or default provisions were not complied with, and anyone harmed by that non compliance has a remedy against the person who exercised the power of sale instead. Automatically defeating the purchaser's title over any defect would undermine the protection this section is specifically designed to provide. The protection does not require the purchaser to personally confirm each step the mortgagee took before closing. The protection is not conditioned on the purchaser having paid full fair market value for the property.
  30. The correct answer is B (No, the receipt is a sufficient discharge without tracing how funds were applied.): A receipt for purchase money given by the person exercising the statutory power of sale is a sufficient discharge to the purchaser, who is not bound to see to the application of the money among the mortgagor, subsequent encumbrancers, or anyone else entitled to a share of it. Treating the purchaser as remaining responsible until every claimant is separately paid ignores what this receipt provision is meant to achieve. There is no requirement for the purchaser to personally collect written confirmation from each subsequent encumbrancer. The protection does not depend on whether the sale price happened to exceed the total of the registered claims against the property.
  31. The correct answer is B (No further proceeding may start until the time named in the notice passes.): Where a mortgage contains a provision for a demand or notice requiring payment or declaring an intention to exercise a power of sale, no further proceeding and no action to enforce the mortgage may be started until the time named in the demand or notice has passed, unless an order permitting it is obtained from a judge. Treating a clause like this as merely optional guidance misreads its binding effect under the Act. There is no rule doubling the stated period before an action can start. Personal service of the mortgagor does not itself override the waiting period the demand or notice sets out.
  32. The correct answer is C (One hundred dollars, paid into court as security for costs.): After an action to enforce the mortgagee's rights has been started, the mortgagor may pay $100 into court as security for costs and apply to the court, and, conditional on performing the covenant or paying what is due along with the costs of the action, the court must dismiss the action where judgment has not yet been recovered. Requiring the mortgagee's full legal costs upfront, before the court has even ruled, is not what the section demands at this stage. Ten per cent of the outstanding principal has no basis in this provision. Some payment into court is required to trigger this relief, so treating it as unnecessary misstates the rule.
  33. The correct answer is D (The notice is deemed given on the Monday it was mailed.): Notice under the Mortgages Act may be given by personal service or by registered mail to a person's usual or last known address, and a notice mailed in Ontario is deemed given on the day it was mailed. The mortgagor's later reading of the notice does not change when it is deemed to have been given for the purposes of the Act. There is no separate ten business day deemed delivery rule that applies here. Mailing to the correct address is itself a recognized method of giving this notice, so personal service is not the only way it can be validly given.
  34. The correct answer is C (No general limit, since any agreed rate may generally be charged.): Except as otherwise provided by an Act of Parliament, any person may stipulate for, allow, and exact any rate of interest or discount that is agreed on, which is why there is no general statutory cap on mortgage interest rates in Canada. There is no fixed federal ceiling that applies across every mortgage regardless of its terms. Nothing in this general rule ties the absence of a cap to the size of the loan. The absence of a general cap is not limited to federally regulated banks, it applies to private lenders as well.
Case wills__04

A lawyer drafted a will for a testator who wanted to leave $15,000 to her cousin and $8,000 to a family friend. The testator signed the will in the presence of three witnesses who were all present at the same time: the cousin, who receives the $15,000 gift; a neighbour, whose spouse is the family friend receiving the $8,000 gift; and an unrelated colleague, who receives nothing under the will. All three witnesses then signed the will in the testator's presence, and no one has raised any concern about pressure or wrongdoing.

  1. The correct answer is B (It is not void, because two other witnesses were not caught by the gift.): A gift to an attesting witness is void as to that witness, but the statute creates an exception where the will is attested by at least two other witnesses who are not caught by the rule. Here the neighbour and the colleague are both unaffected by the gift to the cousin, so the exception saves the cousin's gift. The forfeiture rule is not automatic once a qualifying number of independent witnesses have also attested.
  2. The correct answer is B (It is not void, because two witnesses remain unconnected to that gift.): The forfeiture rule catches a gift to a witness or to that witness's then spouse, so the gift to the family friend is caught because the neighbour who witnessed the will is married to her. The same exception applies again here, because the cousin and the colleague are both unconnected to the family friend's gift and were also attesting witnesses. Since two independent witnesses remain, the gift to the family friend is not void.
  3. The correct answer is D (No, a witness who receives a gift remains competent to prove the will.): The statute is explicit that a beneficiary witness remains a competent witness to prove the will, even though the underlying gift to that person may be affected. Competence to give evidence about execution and entitlement to keep a gift are treated as two separate questions. No rule requires a judge to be present for the cousin's evidence to be admissible.
  4. The correct answer is B (Two witnesses who are both present when the testator signs or acknowledges.): A typewritten will requires only two attesting witnesses who are present at the same time when the testator signs or acknowledges the signature, and who then sign the will themselves in the testator's presence. Using a third witness, as this lawyer did, is a common practice for extra safety but is not legally required. Notarization and the number or size of assets in the estate have no bearing on the number of witnesses needed.
  5. The correct answer is C (The will stands, because a signature beside the end can still give it effect.): Ontario law does not require the signature to sit at the exact physical bottom of the page, and a signature placed at, after, beside or opposite to the end of the will is sufficient as long as it is clear the testator meant to give effect to the document as a will. A margin signature placed this way is not treated as an unwitnessed alteration, since nothing about the wording itself was changed after signing. Retyping the document is unnecessary on these facts.
  6. The correct answer is B (No, a witness who later becomes incompetent does not invalidate the will.): The statute directly addresses this situation, providing that a will is not invalid merely because a witness was incompetent at the time or has since become incompetent to prove its execution. Other evidence, such as the surviving witness's testimony or an affidavit of execution, can still be used to prove the will was properly made. There is no rule requiring both witnesses to remain available before probate can be granted.
  7. The correct answer is A (Yes, a holograph will in the testator's own handwriting needs no witnesses.): A testator may make a valid will wholly in his or her own handwriting and signature, with no other formality and no witnesses required at all. This is the holograph will exception to the usual attestation rules, and it exists precisely for situations like a private handwritten note kept without anyone else present. No notarization step is needed to make a holograph will effective.
  8. The correct answer is C (Yes, a minor's will made for a named marriage that later happens is valid.): A will made by a person under eighteen is normally invalid, but the statute carves out an exception where the person is contemplating marriage to a named person, the will says so, and the marriage later takes place as described. Since all three conditions are satisfied here, the will made before her eighteenth birthday is valid without needing to be re-signed afterward. The contemplation of marriage exception is separate from, and broader than, the exception for members of the Canadian Forces.
  9. The correct answer is C (It may order the note effective if satisfied it shows his true intentions.): Since 2022, the Superior Court of Justice has a substantial compliance power that lets it declare a document effective as a will where it is satisfied the document sets out the deceased's genuine testamentary intentions, even though it was never properly executed. This power is discretionary rather than automatic, so the family still needs to satisfy the court on the evidence rather than simply filing the note. No notarization step is part of this substantial compliance process.
  10. The correct answer is C (The gift and the executor appointment to her are revoked, the rest stands.): Where a marriage ends by a judgment absolute of divorce, the will is read as though the former spouse died before the testator, which revokes both the gift to that spouse and the appointment of that spouse as executor. The rest of the will, including any other gifts, continues to operate normally rather than being torn up entirely. Both the property gift and the executor appointment fall together under this rule, so only one being revoked is not an accurate outcome.
  11. The correct answer is B (No, marriage no longer revokes an existing will under current Ontario law.): Ontario repealed the rule that used to say marriage revokes an existing will, so getting married today no longer cancels a will made beforehand. The children therefore still take under the earlier will exactly as written, and the new spouse would instead need to consider a separate claim such as an equalization entitlement. This outcome does not depend on whether the will happens to mention the new spouse.
  12. The correct answer is C (The gift is revoked, since they were separated as defined when he died.): Since 2022, a gift to a spouse is revoked on death if the spouses were separated at the time, and a valid separation agreement under the Family Law Act is one of the listed events that establishes separation for this rule. The couple's continued living apart because of the marriage breakdown satisfies the rest of the test, so the gift is revoked even though they were never divorced. Whether the spouse later remarries someone else has no bearing on this outcome.
  13. The correct answer is A (No, a will is only revoked by a later will, writing, or destruction.): Ontario law lists a closed set of ways to revoke a will, being a later qualifying will, a written document declaring revocation and made with the same formalities as a will, or destruction of the will with the intention to revoke it. A spoken instruction to a lawyer, without any of those steps actually being carried out, does not revoke anything. Whether the lawyer took notes of the call is irrelevant, since none of the recognized methods of revocation were used.
  14. The correct answer is B (It is not effective, since it lacks the formalities needed for a will.): An alteration made to a will after it was signed has no legal effect unless it is made with the same formalities required to make a will, which generally means it must itself be signed and witnessed. Simply crossing out a name and initialling the margin without witnesses does not satisfy that requirement. Because the alteration fails, the words it tries to change are treated as unclear rather than the gift being cancelled outright, so the original wording is what a court would look to restore.
  15.
