The correct answer is A (The spouse takes the shortfall needed to reach the preferential share first.): Where a person dies partly testate and partly intestate and the spouse receives less than the preferential share under the will, the spouse is entitled to take the shortfall out of the property that passes on intestacy. Here the spouse's $100,000 gift is $250,000 short of the $350,000 preferential share, so she takes that shortfall from the insurance proceeds before anyone else shares in them. The child and the charity only share in whatever remains once the spouse's preferential share has been topped up.
  16. The correct answer is A ($350,000, which applies for deaths occurring on or after March 1, 2021.): The regulation setting the preferential share was increased so that estates of persons who die on or after March 1, 2021 use a preferential share of $350,000, up from the earlier $200,000 figure. The $50,000 figure belongs to a completely different rule, the estate administration tax exemption threshold, and should not be confused with the preferential share. The preferential share still applies regardless of how many children survive the deceased, it simply determines what the spouse receives before the residue is split among them.
  17. The correct answer is C (The spouse takes one half of the residue and the child the other half.): Where an intestate is survived by a spouse and exactly one child, the remaining residue after the preferential share is split equally, with the spouse taking one half and the child taking the other half. The one third and two thirds split only applies where there is more than one child surviving. The spouse having already received the preferential share does not exhaust her entitlement to also share in the residue with the child.
  18. The correct answer is A (The sister and the late brother's children share equally by representation.): Where there is no spouse, issue or surviving parent, the estate is divided equally among the surviving brothers and sisters, and the share that would have gone to a predeceased sibling is instead divided equally among that sibling's own children. Here the sister takes one half outright, and the predeceased brother's two children split his half between them. Escheat to the Crown only applies once there is no spouse, issue, parent, sibling, niece, nephew, or next of kin at all, and this family still has surviving relatives within that list.
  19. The correct answer is B (No tax is payable, since the value does not exceed the $50,000 exemption.): An estate is exempt from estate administration tax where its value does not exceed $50,000, so an estate valued at $42,000 falls entirely within that exemption and no tax becomes payable when the certificate is issued. Nothing in the exemption depends on what type of asset makes up the estate, and it certainly does not depend on whether the estate trustee is also a beneficiary. Once the certificate issues, the tax becomes payable automatically only if the exemption does not apply in the first place.
  20. The correct answer is B ($2,850, calculated as $15 times the 190 whole thousands above $50,000.): The estate value is first rounded up to the nearest $1,000, turning $239,250 into $240,000, and the first $50,000 of that value is taxed at nothing. The remaining $190,000 is taxed at $15 for every $1,000, which works out to $15 multiplied by 190, giving a total of $2,850. No separate court order is needed for this ordinary statutory calculation, since the tax is simply assessed against the deposited estimate when the certificate is applied for.
  21. The correct answer is B (Suspend the administration of the estate, wholly or partly.): On an application by or on behalf of the dependants, the court may suspend the administration of the estate, wholly or partly, for the time and to the extent the court decides. That power protects the dependant while the support claim is decided. Ordering a bond or dismissing the application would not preserve the fund in the same way.
  22. The correct answer is B (An application for the interpretation of the will under Rule 14.05.): A proceeding for the interpretation, rectification, enforcement or setting aside of a deed, will, contract or other instrument about property in Ontario, or the personal property of a deceased person resident in Ontario at death, may be brought under Rule 14.05(3)(c). The Succession Law Reform Act has no rectification section, and the substantial compliance power in that Act addresses a document that was not properly executed rather than a dispute about meaning. A motion for the return of a certificate deals with a different problem.
  23. The correct answer is C (Validate it under the substantial compliance power, if it sets out his intentions.): The court may validate a document or writing that was not properly executed or made if it sets out the deceased's testamentary intentions or an intention to revoke, alter or revive a will, under the substantial compliance power added to the Succession Law Reform Act in 2022. The power may be used where the formal requirements for execution were not met, and it cannot be used to validate an electronic will. A holograph will must be wholly in the testator's own handwriting, so a typed document is not one, and a document signed by a single witness is not admitted without an order.
Case estate_03

Walter, 84 years old, decided to make a new will without instructing a lawyer. At his kitchen table, he signed the will at its end while his neighbours Priya and Evan watched together, and Priya and Evan then each signed as witnesses in Walter's presence. The will leaves $50,000 to Priya and the residue to Walter's daughter. No attestation clause was included, and Walter placed his signature in a short paragraph written just after the dispositive clauses, but clearly meant to give effect to the whole document. Walter died two months later without changing the will.

  1. The correct answer is C (Yes, a witness who receives a void gift remains competent to prove execution of the will.): If a will is witnessed by a person to whom it gives a beneficial gift, the gift is void as far as it concerns that witness, but the witness remains a competent witness to prove execution of the will. The rule protects the witnessing itself from being undone by a conflict of interest in one gift. Walter's will was properly signed and witnessed by two people present at the same time, so the formalities are satisfied regardless of the void gift. Priya's evidence about the signing can still be used to prove the will.
  2. The correct answer is D (The gift is void as far as it concerns Priya, the attesting witness.): Where a will is witnessed by a person to whom the will gives a beneficial gift, that gift is void as far as it concerns the witness, the witness's spouse, or anyone claiming under them. The will itself is not invalidated, and the witness remains competent to prove its execution. Priya's own understanding of the will and her willingness to sign do not save the gift, since the rule applies regardless of her intentions. There is no rule holding a void gift in trust for the witness.
  3. The correct answer is B (No, no particular form of attestation clause is necessary for a valid will.): Where witnesses are required for a will, no particular form of attestation clause is necessary for the will to be validly executed. The formalities that matter are the testator's signature and the two witnesses signing in the testator's presence, which Walter's will satisfies. A missing clause can make proving execution harder in practice, which is why an affidavit of execution is usually obtained for probate, but it does not make the will invalid. No judge's certificate is required for the will itself to be valid.
  4. The correct answer is B (No, the will is valid because the signature shows an intention to give effect to it.): A will is valid as to the position of the signature if it is placed at, after, following, under, beside or opposite the end of the will so that it is clear the testator intended to give effect to the writing as a will. Walter's signature in a short paragraph after the dispositive clauses meets that standard because it plainly shows his intention. There is no rule requiring the signature to sit directly beneath the final sentence or at the top of the document. A separate codicil is not needed to fix a signature that already gives effect to the will.
  5. The correct answer is A (The signing and the witnessing must be contemporaneous, using audio-visual communication technology.): The presence requirement for witnessing a will can be met using audio-visual communication technology if at least one person acting as a witness is a licensee under the Law Society Act, and the signing and the witnessing are contemporaneous. Only one witness needs to hold that licence, not both, so the cousin does not need any legal qualification. No prior judicial approval is required for this method of execution. Complete identical copies may be signed in counterpart, and minor differences in format do not affect validity.
  6. The correct answer is B (No, a will is not invalid because a witness later cannot prove execution.): If a person who witnessed a will was incompetent at the time, or later becomes incompetent, as a witness to prove execution, the will is not invalid for that reason. This protects an otherwise properly executed will from being undone by something that happens to a witness long after the signing. There is no requirement to reexecute the will or to obtain repeated fresh affidavits from the remaining witness. The surviving witness, or other evidence of due execution, can still be used to prove the will.
  7. The correct answer is A (Yes, a creditor and a person named as executor are both competent witnesses.): A creditor whose debt is charged on estate property by the will remains a competent witness, and a person is not incompetent as a witness merely because that person is named as executor. Both the sister and the lawyer can therefore validly attest the will while also holding their other roles. There is no rule barring a creditor with a charge from witnessing, and there is no need for a named executor to resign before signing as a witness.
  8. The correct answer is D (Yes, a wholly handwritten and signed document is valid with no witness needed.): A testator may make a valid will wholly in his or her own handwriting and signature, with no other formality and with no witness required. The unsigned typed page does not affect the validity of the handwritten portion, since it was never incorporated as part of a properly executed document. Probate is the process of proving a will that is already valid, not a requirement for validity itself. A holograph will is an exception to the usual witnessing rule, not an addition to it.
  9. The correct answer is D (Her will is not valid, because she is under 18 and no exception applies.): A will made by a person under 18 is not valid unless the person is or has been married, is contemplating marriage to a named person as the will states, is a member of a listed component of the Canadian Forces, or is a sailor at sea or on a voyage. None of those exceptions applies to this student, so her will is not valid as written. Parental signatures do not substitute for one of the statutory exceptions, and turning 19 does not retroactively validate a will made while she was under 18 and outside the exceptions.
  10. The correct answer is C (Yes, the will is valid because his named marriage plan later took place.): A will made by a person under 18 is valid if, at the time of making it, the person was contemplating marriage to a named person and the will states that, and the marriage later takes place. Because this testator named his partner and the wedding followed as planned, his will falls within that exception. Parental consent is not part of the exception, and the statute does not require the marriage to have already happened before the will is signed, only that it later takes place.
  11. The correct answer is B (The change has no effect unless made with the same formalities as a will.): An alteration made in a will after it was made has no effect unless it is made with the same formalities needed to make a will, such as the testator's signature witnessed in the usual way, or, where the will did not need witnesses, the testator's signature placed near the alteration or a signed memorandum referring to it. An unwitnessed marginal change with no signature does not meet that standard, so it has no effect and only invalidates words it makes unclear. Death without formal revision does not cure the missing formality, and recency of the handwriting is not the test.
  12. The correct answer is B (The internal law of Ontario, where the land is situated, governs that part.): As to an interest in land, the manner and formalities of making the will, and its essential validity and effect, are governed by the internal law of the place where the land is situated. The cottage is in Ontario, so Ontario internal law governs that part of the will, even though the testator was domiciled in Manitoba. Movables such as the bank account are instead governed by the law of the place of domicile at death. There is no separate federal law of wills that displaces this provincial rule.
  13. The correct answer is B (Yes, it is valid because it met the law of the place of habitual residence.): A will of movables is valid and admissible to probate if, at the time of making, it complied with the internal law of the place where it was made, where the testator was then domiciled, where the testator then had a habitual residence, or where the testator was then a national under a shared national law. France was both the place of making and her habitual residence at that time, so the will meets one of the recognized connecting factors and is valid as to the portfolio in Ontario. Citizenship alone was not the basis relied on here, and there is no rule requiring a rule to be met twice.
  14. The correct answer is A (No, because destruction only revokes a will if done with intent to revoke it.): A will is revoked by burning, tearing or otherwise destroying it, done by the testator or by someone else in the testator's presence and by the testator's direction, with the intention of revoking it. Intention to revoke is an element of the destruction itself, so a tearing done without that intention, or immediately regretted before any lasting revocation is intended, does not revoke the will. Taping the pieces back together is not itself a new will, since it does not meet the formalities for making one. Tearing alone, without the required intention, does not permanently destroy the will's legal effect.
  15. The correct answer is A (No, marriage no longer revokes a will under Ontario law since 2022.): The old rule that a will is revoked by the marriage of the testator was repealed effective January 1, 2022, so in Ontario today getting married does not revoke an existing will. Her 2018 will therefore remains in effect after her 2026 marriage, and her sister continues to inherit under it. There is no automatic suspension of a will pending a later divorce, and no timing exception based on how soon before the marriage the will was made, since the repeal removed the whole rule rather than narrowing it.
  16. The correct answer is A (Both are revoked, and the will is read as if she died first.): Unless the will shows a contrary intention, if the testator's marriage is later terminated by a judgment absolute of divorce, the will is read as if the former spouse had died first, so a gift to the former spouse and the appointment of the former spouse as executor are both revoked. The rest of the will still stands. There is no exception that revives the gift or appointment simply because divorce alone might not otherwise change anything, and there is no requirement that the former spouse remarry within a set time for the revocation to apply.
  17. The correct answer is D (The gift is revoked, because they were separated as defined at his death.): Since 2022, a gift to a spouse in a will is treated as revoked if the spouses were separated at the testator's death, which includes a case where a valid separation agreement under Part IV of the Family Law Act was signed and, at the time of death, the spouses were living separate and apart because of the breakdown of the marriage. Those conditions are met here, so the gift to her is revoked even though there was no divorce. Separation revocation does not partially reduce a gift by half, and it operates without any need to destroy the physical will.
  18. The correct answer is B (The firm is bound, because the order was made in the usual way of the business.): Every partner is an agent of the firm and of the other partners for the purposes of the partnership business, and an act done in the usual way of carrying on that kind of business binds the firm, unless the third party actually knows the partner lacks authority. Since the supplier had no reason to doubt the signing partner's authority and the order fits the firm's ordinary business, the firm is bound without needing the other partner's prior consent. There is no rule requiring both partners to co-sign routine purchase orders for the firm to be liable. Ratification after the fact is unnecessary where the agency authority already existed at the time of signing.
  19. The correct answer is D (Every partner, because wrongful acts in the ordinary course create joint and several liability.): Where a partner commits a wrongful act or omission in the ordinary course of the firm's business, the firm becomes liable, and every partner is then liable jointly with the co-partners and also severally for that liability, which is a different rule than the joint-only liability that applies to ordinary contractual firm debts. This means the client can pursue the full loss against any partner individually, not only the one who caused the damage. Limiting recovery to the negligent partner alone ignores the statutory extension of liability to the whole firm. Negligence arising from ordinary partnership business is squarely within what the Partnerships Act addresses, not outside its scope.
  20. The correct answer is B (This is permitted, provided the firm also registers its name under the Business Names Act.): A limited liability partnership may carry on business in Ontario only to practise a profession governed by an Act that expressly permits an LLP structure, and only where the governing body of that profession requires a minimum amount of liability insurance, both of which appear to be satisfied here. An LLP must still register its firm name under the Business Names Act like any other partnership operating under a name, so skipping that registration step would leave the structure incomplete. The insurance requirement operates at the governing body level rather than requiring each individual partner to buy a separate personal policy. Regulated professions are exactly the setting the LLP structure was designed for, not an exception to it.
  21. The correct answer is B (The supervising partner remains liable, but the uninvolved partner is shielded from that specific claim.): A partner in a limited liability partnership is not liable for debts arising from another partner's or an employee's negligent or wrongful act, but remains liable for the partner's own negligence and for anyone under that partner's direct supervision. This means the supervising partner keeps exposure for the junior employee's error, while the uninvolved partner benefits from the liability shield for that specific claim. Saying the LLP structure provides no protection at all overstates the risk that remains, just as saying it shields every partner from everything overstates the protection available. The presence of mandatory insurance does not itself create personal liability for a partner who had no supervisory role in the matter.
Case busine_03

Priya and Dev want to form a limited partnership to develop a small rental property. Priya will manage daily leasing decisions and sign contracts on behalf of the venture, while Dev has agreed to contribute $150,000 in cash and a parcel of land, but no personal services, in exchange for a share of profits. Priya prepares a declaration for filing with the Registrar, to be signed by the general partners. Dev is anxious that the declaration will publicly disclose his name and the size of his contribution online, and he also wants confirmation that his personal assets will be protected once the partnership faces its first debt to a supplier.

  1. The correct answer is B (Only the general partners must sign the declaration before it is filed.): Under the Limited Partnerships Act, a declaration is only effective once it is signed by all of the general partners, since they take on unlimited liability and confirm the details being filed with the Registrar. Limited partners are not required to sign the declaration itself, because their identities and contributions are instead recorded in a separate record of limited partners kept at the partnership's principal place of business. There is no requirement that a lawyer co-sign a limited partnership filing before it can be validly formed. The partnership only comes into existence once a properly signed declaration is accepted for filing.
  2. The correct answer is A (Dev risks becoming liable as a general partner for taking part in control.): A limited partner who exercises the rights and powers of a limited partner while also taking part in the control of the business risks losing the liability shield and being treated as a general partner for the partnership's obligations. Receiving a share of profits does not by itself protect Dev, because the relevant test looks at whether he took part in controlling the business, not at how he is paid. Losing limited status is a risk rather than an automatic outcome in every case, but personally negotiating and signing leases is a strong example of taking part in control. Describing the consequence as merely losing a voting right understates the real exposure Dev now faces.
  3. The correct answer is D (The declaration expires after five years, and Dev's name stays in a private record.): Under the 2021 amendments to the Limited Partnerships Act, a declaration accepted for filing expires five years after the date of acceptance unless it is renewed or replaced by a declaration of dissolution. Limited partners are no longer named on the public declaration itself, since the general partners must instead maintain a current record of the limited partners at the partnership's principal place of business in Ontario. A declaration does not remain effective indefinitely, and a two year term does not reflect the actual statutory period. Dev's contribution and identity stay in that private record rather than becoming part of the public filing.
  4. The correct answer is A (No, a limited partner may contribute money and property, but not services.): The Limited Partnerships Act specifically permits a limited partner to contribute money and other property, but it does not permit a contribution of services in that capacity. Dev's proposed weekend work managing tenant complaints cannot substitute for the cash and land already agreed, regardless of how the general partner feels about it. Written consent from Priya could not cure this defect, because the restriction on service contributions comes directly from the statute rather than from anything the partners could agree between themselves. The statute does allow property as well as cash, so limiting Dev to cash alone also misstates the actual rule.
  5. The correct answer is C (The certificate is conclusive proof of incorporation, absent a proceeding to cancel it for cause.): The Director's endorsement of the articles constitutes the certificate of incorporation, and that certificate is conclusive proof that the corporation was incorporated on the date shown, except in a proceeding brought to cancel the certificate for cause. A minor processing irregularity discovered years later does not, on its own, give an ordinary creditor a standalone right to challenge the corporation's existence outside such a proceeding. There is no five year seasoning period that must pass before the certificate becomes conclusive, since conclusiveness attaches from the date the certificate issues. Treating the certificate as merely presumptive would undermine the certainty the statute is designed to provide to anyone dealing with the corporation.
  6. The correct answer is B (No, an individual under 18 may not act as an incorporator under the Act.): The Business Corporations Act disqualifies an individual who is under 18 years of age from incorporating a corporation, along with a person found incapable of managing property or who has the status of bankrupt. Earning income from a part time job does not remove the age restriction, since the disqualification is based on age alone in this scenario, not on financial independence. There is no exception that allows a parent to co-sign or guarantee the articles in place of a qualified incorporator. The lack of prior experience managing property is irrelevant here, because the entrepreneur has not actually been found incapable of managing property by anyone.
  7. The correct answer is A (The corporation must file a notice of change within 15 days after the change occurs.): The Corporations Information Act requires every corporation to file a notice of change for every change in the information filed under that Act, including its official email address, within 15 days after the day the change takes place. This is a shorter window than the 60 day period that applies to certain initial filings, so confusing the two deadlines could leave the corporation offside. An email address is exactly the kind of information the Act requires to be kept current, so there is no exemption for administrative details of this sort. The obligation only arises once the change has actually taken place, not before it happens.
  8. The correct answer is C (The initial return is due within 60 days after the date of incorporation.): Every Ontario corporation, other than an extra-provincial corporation or an exempted class, must file an initial return with the Minister within 60 days after the date of incorporation, amalgamation or continuation, which sets a clear and separate deadline from any later filing. The six month, fiscal year end based deadline actually applies to the corporation's annual return, not its initial return, so applying that timeline here would miss the correct trigger date entirely. The 15 day period is the deadline for reporting a later change in filed information, not for the initial return itself. Treating the initial and annual returns as due at the same moment ignores that they are triggered by different events.
  9. The correct answer is C (No, a status word such as Limited or Corporation, or an abbreviation, must be included.): The Business Corporations Act requires that the word Limited, Limitee, Incorporated, Incorporee or Corporation, or an accepted abbreviation such as Ltd., Ltee, Inc. or Corp., form part of the name of every corporation. A distinctive brand name on its own, no matter how recognizable, does not satisfy this requirement without one of those words or abbreviations attached. The corporation may use either the full word or its abbreviated form, so there is no rule limiting it to only the unabbreviated version. Avoiding conflict with an existing trademark is a separate concern from the statutory requirement to include a corporate status designation in the name.
  10. The correct answer is C (No, a name that closely resembles an existing corporate name is generally prohibited.): A corporation must not have a name that is the same as, or similar to, the name of a known body corporate, trust, association, partnership, sole proprietorship or individual already in existence, subject to a limited exception for a proposed number name. This means a name that merely resembles an existing corporation's name, not just an identical one, can still be refused, so the client's proposed name carries real risk of rejection. Operating in a different city does not remove this restriction, because the prohibition is not limited by geography within the province. The Director does compare a proposed name against existing names as part of assessing whether the filing can proceed.
  11. The correct answer is A (The by-law ceases to be effective once the shareholders reject it at that meeting.): A directors' by-law is effective from the date of the directors' resolution until the shareholders confirm, confirm as amended, or reject it at the next meeting, and if the shareholders reject it, the by-law ceases to be effective from that point. This means the by-law was genuinely valid and operating for a period before the meeting, contrary to the idea that shareholder approval was needed before it could take effect at all. Rejection does not last forever as a form of automatic permanence for directors either, since a by-law rejected by shareholders stops applying rather than continuing indefinitely. Rejection also does not automatically substitute some alternative version the shareholders might have preferred, since no replacement by-law arises simply from voting one down.
  12. The correct answer is A (Making by-laws, appointing officers and auditors, and authorizing securities are typical matters.): At the meeting held after incorporation, the directors may make by-laws, adopt forms of security certificates and corporate records, authorize the issue of securities, appoint officers, appoint an auditor to serve until the first shareholders' meeting, make banking arrangements, and transact other organizational business. Electing replacement directors is a shareholder function tied to the first shareholders' meeting rather than something dealt with at this organizational directors' meeting. Approving annual financial statements is also a matter for a later shareholders' meeting once there are actual financial statements to review. Calling the first shareholders' meeting may occur, but it is only one of several matters the directors can address at this stage, not the sole purpose of the meeting.
  13. The correct answer is D (This is not enough notice, since five days is the general minimum required.): An incorporator or a director may call the first directors' meeting by giving not less than five days notice to each director, stating the time and, where applicable, the place of the meeting, so two days notice falls short of what the Act requires. There is no rule allowing any arbitrarily short notice period to suffice for this organizational meeting, since a specific minimum is set out in the statute. A thirty day period overstates the actual requirement and would create an unnecessary delay before the corporation could begin organizing itself. The residence of the directors within Ontario has no bearing on the length of notice that must be given for this particular meeting.
  14. The correct answer is A (The clause should be removed, since Ontario no longer imposes a residency requirement.): Ontario's former residency requirement for directors, once found in the Business Corporations Act, was repealed effective July 5, 2021, so an Ontario corporation is no longer required to have any fraction or minimum number of resident Canadian directors. The associate's clause reflects an older CBCA style rule that never actually described the current Ontario requirement, so simply restating a different percentage or a minimum of one director would still be incorrect for an Ontario corporation. The best correction is to remove the residency requirement from the Ontario articles entirely, since there is nothing left in the statute to comply with. This kind of outdated assumption is exactly the type of error a careful review before filing is meant to catch.
  15. The correct answer is C (No, only a person who currently has the status of bankrupt is disqualified.): The Business Corporations Act disqualifies a person who has the status of bankrupt, along with anyone under 18 or found incapable of managing property, but a person who has already been discharged from bankruptcy no longer has that status and is therefore not disqualified on that basis. Treating a past bankruptcy as a permanent bar misreads the statute, since the disqualification is tied to the person's current status rather than their history. There is no five year waiting period built into the Act that must elapse after discharge before eligibility resumes. Bankruptcy status is directly relevant under the Act, so it would be equally wrong to say it has never mattered to director eligibility.
  16. The correct answer is B (It becomes effective at the time specified in the letter, two weeks from today.): A resignation becomes effective when a written resignation is received by the corporation, or at the time specified in the resignation, whichever is later, so where the director has specified a later effective date, that later date controls. Treating receipt of the letter today as the effective date ignores the later specified time that the director expressly chose. There is no requirement that the board formally accept a resignation before it takes effect, since the Act ties effectiveness to receipt or the specified time rather than board approval. Waiting until the next annual shareholders' meeting has no basis in the statutory rule governing when a director's resignation takes hold.
  17. The correct answer is A (An ordinary resolution passed at the meeting is usually enough to remove the director.): Subject to the cumulative voting rules, shareholders may by ordinary resolution at an annual or special meeting remove any director or directors from office, so an ordinary resolution is generally what is needed rather than a higher threshold. Requiring unanimity would impose a far stricter standard than the statute sets, and nothing in the facts suggests the articles have raised the bar in that way. A court order is not the normal mechanism for an everyday director removal decided by the shareholders themselves. A special resolution requiring two thirds of the votes cast describes a different kind of corporate action entirely, not the ordinary process for removing a director.
  18. The correct answer is A (Only if it is registered as a charity, a separate status.): Section 149(1)(l) exempts a non-profit organization that is organized and operated exclusively for purposes other than profit, with no part of its income available for the personal benefit of a proprietor, member or shareholder. That exemption does not authorize the organization to issue official donation receipts. Registration as a charity is a separate status granted under Part V of the Act, and only a registered charity may issue official receipts. The absence of profit making and the ability to receive gifts are not the test for receipting.
  19. The correct answer is B (It is a non-share corporation, and members are not liable for its debts.): The Not-for-Profit Corporations Act, 2010 is a non-share statute, so members hold memberships rather than shares, and there is no stated capital or dividend rule. The Act provides that members are not, in that capacity, liable for any liability of the corporation or for any act or default of the corporation, except as the Act otherwise provides. The other options describe a share corporation or impose a liability that the Act does not impose. The Act also gives dissenting members a right to be paid the fair value of their membership interests in the cases it specifies.
  20. The correct answer is C (It is a separate federal regime operating alongside the provincial statute.): Section 427 of the Bank Act allows a bank to take security on the property of a person engaged in certain listed businesses and activities, and it operates as a separate federal regime alongside the Ontario Personal Property Security Act. A bank commonly takes both forms of security. The federal section does not displace the provincial registry for the bank's other collateral, and it is not limited to loans made to individuals. It is a federal provision, and its requirements are set out in the Bank Act rather than in the Ontario statute.
Case commer_03

Northgate Fabrication Ltd. runs a metal shop in Hamilton with twelve employees. In January, Meridian Bank registered a financing statement against all of Northgate's present and after acquired personal property and advanced a $200,000 operating loan. In March, Northgate bought an $80,000 stamping press from Apex Machinery on credit under a signed security agreement describing the press. Apex registered its financing statement on March 1 and gave Meridian written notice describing the collateral by item or type on March 2. Northgate took possession of the press on March 5. By August, Northgate owed its employees six weeks of unpaid wages and had defaulted on both the Meridian loan and the Apex debt. A lawyer is now advising Northgate's principal on how these competing claims will rank.

  1. The correct answer is D (Apex perfected its security interest before Northgate obtained possession of the press.): A purchase money security interest in equipment has priority over an earlier registered general security interest if it is perfected before or within fifteen days after the debtor obtains possession of the collateral. Registration alone is not the trigger; the timing of perfection relative to possession is what matters. The written notice requirement applies to inventory financing, not to equipment, so sending notice here was not what created the priority. A signed agreement is a precondition to attachment, but attachment alone does not establish priority over an earlier registered interest.
  2. The correct answer is A (As soon as Northgate acquires rights in the newly acquired equipment.): A security agreement covering after acquired property attaches to new collateral as soon as the debtor acquires rights in it, without any further filing or signature. The ten day rule that limits attachment under an after acquired property clause applies only to consumer goods, and the stamping press is business equipment, not a consumer good. Meridian does not need to register a fresh financing statement each time Northgate buys new equipment, because the original registration already covers after acquired collateral of that kind. No second signature is needed once the original security agreement describes the collateral broadly enough to include it.
  3. The correct answer is B (Up to $2,000 owed for wages earned in the six months before bankruptcy.): Bankruptcy law gives wage claims a secured priority against the bankrupt's current assets, but that priority is capped at $2,000 per employee for amounts earned in the six months before the bankruptcy, not the full wage claim. A cap of $10,000 and a full year period do not match the statutory limit for this priority. Wage claims are not left ranking behind every secured creditor, because the statute gives them their own secured status up to the capped amount. The priority exists precisely so a modest, capped wage claim is protected even where a bank holds a broader perfected security interest.
  4. The correct answer is A (Obtain a written discharge or release of Apex's registered interest before closing.): A buyer who purchases equipment outside the seller's ordinary course of business generally takes the collateral subject to a perfected security interest that continues against it, so the safe course is to obtain a written discharge or release from the secured party before closing. Waiting for a registration to lapse on its own is not a reliable substitute, since the secured party can renew it before expiry. No fixed thirty day waiting period removes a perfected interest from equipment sold outside the ordinary course. Meridian's consent does not affect Apex's separate registered interest, because the two security interests are held by different secured parties.
  5. The correct answer is C (Liability as a general partner because of taking part in control of the business.): A limited partner who exercises the rights and powers of a limited partner is not liable as a general partner, but a limited partner who also takes part in controlling the business loses that protection and becomes liable as a general partner. Simply contributing capital does not automatically shield a limited partner once that line is crossed, so the contribution cap no longer applies. Written consent from the general partners has nothing to do with whether a limited partner has taken part in control. The statute protects passive investors, not partners who are actually running day to day operations.
  6. The correct answer is D (Five years from the date the Registrar accepts the registration.): A business name registration under the Business Names Act is effective for five years from the date the Registrar accepts it for registration, not one year or ten years. After that period the registrant must renew the registration, either before it expires or within a short window after expiry on payment of a late fee. The registration does not simply continue indefinitely because the business keeps operating, since the Act ties the effective period to a fixed number of years rather than to ongoing use. Missing the renewal window means the registration lapses even if the business is still active under that name.
  7. The correct answer is D (Every partner is jointly and severally liable for the firm's wrongful act.): When a partner commits a wrongful act or omission in the ordinary course of the firm's business, the firm is liable and every partner becomes liable jointly and severally for that liability, which lets the client pursue any one partner for the full amount. This differs from the rule for ordinary firm debts, where partners are liable jointly rather than jointly and severally. The other partners are not shielded simply because they were not personally negligent, since partnership liability for a co-partner's wrongful act extends to all of them. Suing an insurer does not replace the underlying partnership liability the statute creates.
  8. The correct answer is D (The negligent partner remains liable; the uninvolved partner is protected from that debt.): A partner in a limited liability partnership is not liable for debts of the partnership arising from another partner's negligent or wrongful act, but remains liable for the partner's own negligence and for anyone under the partner's direct supervision. That protects the uninvolved partner here, since that partner neither committed the negligent act nor supervised the partner who did. The LLP structure does not make both partners jointly liable for each other's individual negligence, which is exactly the exposure the LLP form is designed to remove. The firm's LLP status also does not mean no one is personally liable, since the partner who gave the advice keeps that liability.
  9. The correct answer is A (Ontario has no director residency requirement; the old rule was repealed in 2021.): Ontario's Business Corporations Act once required a fraction of directors to be resident Canadians, but that requirement was repealed effective July 2021, so there is currently no director residency rule under Ontario law. The twenty five per cent figure and the one director minimum both describe the separate federal Canada Business Corporations Act rule, not Ontario's own statute. There is no special majority rule that applies only to private Ontario corporations, since the residency requirement was removed for every Ontario corporation regardless of whether its shares are offered to the public. A lawyer advising on an Ontario incorporation should not carry over the federal rule by mistake.
  10. The correct answer is D (At least one of the three directors must be a resident Canadian.): Under the federal residency rule, at least twenty five per cent of a corporation's directors must be resident Canadians, but where a corporation has fewer than four directors, the rule is expressed as a flat minimum of at least one resident Canadian director. Rounding twenty five per cent of three down to zero would defeat the purpose of the rule, which is why the statute sets a flat floor for small boards instead. The general residency requirement still applies to a board this size, so having no resident Canadian director would not satisfy the CBCA. A majority is only required for corporations in the prescribed sectors with the higher threshold, not for an ordinary board of three.
  11. The correct answer is B (Yes, reasonable reliance on a qualified professional's report is a statutory defence.): The statute gives a director a reasonable diligence defence where the director exercised the care, diligence and skill a reasonably prudent person would exercise, including reliance in good faith on a report from an accountant or another professional whose profession lends credibility to the statement. The defence does not require the director to personally re-verify the professional's calculations, since the whole point of the defence is permitting reasonable reliance on expert work. It is also not limited to financial statements prepared by an officer, since a professional's report is expressly covered as well. A director is not automatically responsible for every flaw in a report a qualified professional supplied in good faith.
  12. The correct answer is B (At the meeting where the proposed contract is first considered.): A director who is a party to, or has a material interest in, a material contract with the corporation must disclose the nature and extent of that interest at the meeting where the proposed contract is first considered, or at the first meeting after the interest arises. Waiting thirty days after signing comes too late, since the disclosure obligation attaches at the point the contract is being considered, not after it is finalized. The obligation is not triggered only when a shareholder happens to ask, because the Act imposes the duty independently of whether anyone raises the question. An annual shareholders meeting is also not the trigger, since disclosure runs to the corporation at the directors' level.
  13. The correct answer is A (Yes, since execution against the corporation has already been returned unsatisfied.): Directors are jointly and severally liable to employees for debts not exceeding six months of wages that become payable while they hold office, but a director is only liable once the corporation has been sued and execution against it has been returned unsatisfied, or a parallel insolvency event has occurred. Four months of wages falls within the six month cap, so the amount is recoverable once that precondition is met. The liability does not depend on proving bad faith, since it is a statutory liability tied to unpaid wages rather than to director misconduct. The twelve month period in the wrong option belongs to the separate vacation pay component of the same liability, not to ordinary wages.
  14. The correct answer is C (The shareholder takes on the director's liabilities to the extent the agreement restricts director power.): A shareholder who is a party to a unanimous shareholder agreement takes on the rights, powers, duties and liabilities of a director to the extent the agreement restricts the directors' discretion, and the directors are relieved of their own duties and liabilities to that same extent. The shareholder is not simply protected, because the whole purpose of the agreement is to shift decision making authority, and liability follows that authority. The directors do not keep full liability either, since the statute explicitly relieves them once the agreement restricts their powers. Liability does not simply disappear for everyone, because the statute transfers it to the shareholder rather than erasing it.
  15. The correct answer is B (Yes, a former director of the corporation is included in the definition.): The statutory definition of complainant expressly includes a director or officer, or a former director or officer, of the corporation or of any of its affiliates, so a former director fits squarely within that definition. Limiting the remedy to a current director or officer would leave out exactly the category the definition was written to cover. Restricting it to shareholders is also wrong, since directors and officers are named as a separate category from security holders. The remedy is not limited to offering corporations either, since the Ontario Securities Commission's own additional right to apply is reserved for offering corporations, not the complainant definition itself.
  16. The correct answer is A (Give the directors fourteen days' notice of the intention to apply.): Before bringing or intervening in a derivative action, a complainant must give fourteen days' notice to the directors of the intention to apply, unless every director of the corporation is named as a defendant in the action, in which case that notice is not required. Thirty days is not the statutory period, so relying on that longer figure would misstate the requirement. Consent from the defendant directors is not needed, since the derivative action exists precisely to proceed even where directors will not act on their own. Waiting for an annual meeting is unnecessary, because the derivative action route lets a shareholder proceed without waiting for a scheduled meeting.
Case profes_06

A lawyer in a two-lawyer firm is retained by Marisol, a private investor, to advance $150,000 to Halbrook Contracting Inc. as a second mortgage on the company's commercial building. Marisol has no ownership interest in Halbrook and has never lent money through the firm before. Halbrook's principal asks whether the same lawyer can act for Halbrook on the same transaction to save legal costs, since both sides trust the lawyer and want a fast closing before the fiscal year ends. The property sits in downtown Ottawa, where several other real estate lawyers are readily available for retainer. No family or business relationship connects Marisol and Halbrook.

  1. The correct answer is C (No, none of the exceptions in the mortgage conflict rule applies to this advance.): A lawyer or lawyers practising together must not act for both lender and borrower on a mortgage unless one of the listed exceptions applies, such as the lender being a lending client, the loan being $75,000 or less, or no other lawyer being conveniently available. None of those exceptions fits here, since Marisol has never lent through the firm, the advance is $150,000, and other lawyers practise nearby. Consent, written disclosure, and a faster closing do not substitute for meeting one of the actual exceptions the rule sets out.
  2. The correct answer is B (A signed Investment Authority and a Report on Investment for the lender.): By-Law 9 requires a licensee acting for a private lender to keep a file containing a completed Investment Authority signed and dated by the lender before the first advance of money, along with a copy of the Report on Investment given to that lender. A billing arrangement, an accountant's letter about repayment, and a tax filing are not among the documents the by law requires for the private lender file.
  3. The correct answer is A (The signed authority comes before the advance, and the report follows it.): The Investment Authority must be signed and dated by the private lender before the first advance of money, and the Report on Investment then documents that completed transaction for the lender. Signing the report first reverses the order the by law requires, and treating the timing as flexible or optional ignores that the authority is a precondition to advancing the funds rather than a record created only if requested later.
  4. The correct answer is D (A fresh Investment Authority Marisol signs before the postponement happens.): Whenever a change reduces a private lender's security, such as a subordination or postponement, the licensee must add a fresh Investment Authority signed by the lender before the act is done, together with an updated Report on Investment delivered to the lender. Halbrook's assurance about the new lender, a new appraisal, and confirmation that the other loan is funded do not replace Marisol's own signed authorization to accept the reduced priority.
  5. The correct answer is B (Rule 3.4-10, subject to the firm imputation exception in rule 3.4-11.): The prohibition on acting against a former client without consent is set out in rule 3.4-10, with the firm imputation exception found in rule 3.4-11. Rule 3.4-2 is instead the general conflict rule permitting representation with fully informed consent, rule 3.4-3 bars representing opposing parties in a live dispute, and rule 3.5-2 is about preserving a client's property and has nothing to do with former client conflicts.
  6. The correct answer is C (It must be fair, reasonable and disclosed to the client on time.): Rule 3.6-1 provides that a lawyer shall not charge or accept any amount for a fee or disbursement unless it is fair and reasonable and has been disclosed to the client in a timely fashion. A published website rate is not itself the legal standard, the Law Society does not pre-approve individual bills, and nothing requires a fee to undercut every competitor's price in the market.
  7. The correct answer is A (Equitably between the two clients, absent some other agreement.): Where a lawyer acts for two or more clients on the same matter, rule 3.6-4 requires the fees and disbursements to be divided equitably between them unless the clients have agreed otherwise. Listing one client's name first on the retainer letter has no bearing on the split, a credit score is irrelevant to dividing a legal bill, and leaving the division to chance is not an equitable allocation.
  8. The correct answer is B (Raise the issue with the chief legal officer or chief executive officer.): When a lawyer acting for an organization knows the organization is acting dishonestly or illegally, rule 3.2-8 requires escalating past the instructing person to the chief legal officer, or to the chief legal officer and chief executive officer, and higher still if needed, before withdrawal becomes necessary. Withdrawing at once skips that escalation step, going straight to the police is not the rule's first step, and simply accepting the dismissive manager's answer ignores the lawyer's duty to the organization itself.
  9. The correct answer is A ($1 million for each claim and $2 million in the aggregate.): By-Law 6 requires a licensee in private practice to maintain a policy with a limit for each single claim of not less than $1 million and an aggregate limit for all claims of not less than $2 million per year. The lower figures understate the required minimum, the higher figures overstate it, and treating $1 million as a single shared total ignores that the aggregate limit is separately set at $2 million.
  10. The correct answer is B (Notify the insurer promptly, even before any demand is made.): The Rules of Professional Conduct and LAWPRO's own guidance both call for prompt notice of any circumstance that could lead to a claim, not only after a demand is actually made, because early notice gives the best chance to fix the problem and preserves coverage. Waiting for a demand, a court proceeding, or an explicit threat all describe later triggers than the circumstance based notice the rule and the insurer's guidance actually call for.
  11. The correct answer is C (No, representing opposing parties in a dispute is never permitted.): Rule 3.4-3 is an absolute prohibition stating that a lawyer shall not represent opposing parties in a dispute, and this bar stands apart from the general consent based conflict rule in rule 3.4-2 that applies to other conflicts. Written consent, separate file keeping, and identical billing rates address different concerns entirely and cannot open the door to acting for both sides of the same live dispute.
  12. The correct answer is B (From one month after the date the bill was delivered to the client.): The Solicitors Act permits a solicitor to charge interest on unpaid fees, charges or disbursements calculated from a date that is one month after the bill was delivered. Rule 3.6-1.1 adds that interest may only be charged as the Act or other law permits, which rules out the date of signing, the date the work finished, and the broader claim that the Act does not permit interest on an unpaid account.
Case profes_07

Devon has practised alone for thirty years and plans to close his general practice in six months, once he turns seventy. No other lawyer has agreed to take over the practice yet. His filing room holds several clients' original wills and powers of attorney, along with routine correspondence and old invoices going back a decade. His trust account currently holds a small balance for a client he has been unable to locate for over two years despite repeated efforts. Devon has never created a written contingency plan for his practice, and he wants to know what steps the closure requires before he stops taking new files.

  1. The correct answer is D (Review the active client list and begin notifying clients of the closure.): The Law Society's guide to closing a practice begins the active file steps by reviewing the active client list and notifying clients, so files can be completed or transferred in an orderly way before the practice stops. Cancelling insurance before the wind up is finished leaves past work unprotected, shredding closed files ignores retention obligations, and the Law Society does not assign a retiring lawyer's files to another licensee on its own.
  2. The correct answer is D (The original wills and powers of attorney belonging to clients.): The Law Society's guidance states that a lawyer must not destroy original client documents such as wills and powers of attorney, because these belong to the client and cannot be replaced once lost. Routine correspondence, paid invoices, and internal file notes are subject to ordinary retention periods and may eventually be destroyed, but original testamentary documents are treated differently because of what they represent.
  3. The correct answer is A (The plan must be current and reviewed at least once every year.): By-Law 7.1 requires a licensee's contingency plan to be current at all times and reviewed at least once every year, not simply reviewed once and left unchanged. A co-signing licensee on every retainer letter is not what the by law demands, and a sole practitioner is not exempt from the requirement just because the practice happens to hold little or no money in trust.
  4. The correct answer is B (Apply to transfer the balance to the Law Society's Unclaimed Trust Fund.): Where a client cannot be located after reasonable efforts and two years have passed, the licensee applies to transfer the remaining trust balance to the Law Society's Unclaimed Trust Fund before closing the account. Moving the money into the general account would be an improper use of trust funds, leaving it indefinitely prevents the account from ever being closed, and giving it away is not a route the guidance authorizes for unclaimed client money.
  5. The correct answer is B (Only if the firm shows it took adequate and timely steps to screen the information.): Rule 3.4-11 allows another lawyer in the same firm to act against a former client if the client consents, or if the firm establishes that it took adequate and timely measures ensuring no risk of disclosure of the former client's confidential information to the lawyer handling the new matter. A blanket firm wide bar overstates the rule, simply never having worked on the file is not itself enough without those measures, and a court order is not what the rule requires.
  6. The correct answer is A ($7,500 in the aggregate, subject to the by law's listed exceptions.): By-Law 9, section 4(1) states that a licensee shall not receive cash of more than $7,500 in the aggregate from a person in respect of any one client file, subject to the exceptions listed in the by law. The federal reporting figure is a different threshold used elsewhere, a per payment limit with no aggregate cap misstates how the by law is structured, and identification alone does not remove the dollar limit.
  7. The correct answer is C (Cash may be accepted, but a cash deposit is refunded in cash.): The Law Society's guidance confirms that a licensee is never required to accept cash and may decide for themselves whether to take it, while By-Law 9, section 6(d) requires that where the original deposit was made in cash, any refund must also be issued in cash. Treating acceptance as mandatory, requiring a cheque refund instead, and banning cash outright all misstate how the guidance and the by law actually work together.
  8. The correct answer is B (Fees may only be taken from trust as the by-laws under the Law Society Act permit.): Rule 3.6-10 provides that a lawyer shall not appropriate any funds of the client held in trust for or on account of fees except as permitted by the by-laws under the Law Society Act, which generally requires a proper billing process rather than an informal transfer based on the lawyer's own belief. A genuine but undocumented belief that fees are owed does not, on its own, satisfy the by-law's requirements for taking money from trust. There is no rule that automatically permits appropriating trust funds simply because a file has reached a particular age. Telling the client after the fact does not substitute for following the proper process before the funds are taken.
  9. The correct answer is C (Repay the excess money to the client as soon as practicable after the assessment.): Rule 3.6-11 provides that if the amount of fees or disbursements is reduced on an assessment, the lawyer must repay the money to the client as soon as practicable, placing the repayment obligation on the lawyer rather than requiring the client to take any further step. Keeping the excess as compensation for having gone through the assessment process directly contradicts the rule's repayment requirement. There is no requirement that the client formally demand repayment in writing before the lawyer's obligation to repay arises. Applying the excess as a credit toward possible future work is not the repayment the rule contemplates, since the client may never retain the lawyer again and is owed the money now.
  10. The correct answer is D (It appropriates trust funds for fees without the billing the by-laws require.): Transferring client trust money to the general account for fees before any bill has actually been delivered bypasses the billing process the by-laws contemplate for taking fees from trust, which is exactly what rule 3.6-10's prohibition on appropriating trust funds is meant to prevent. That the underlying work was genuinely performed does not by itself authorize moving the money before it has been properly billed. An objectively reasonable dollar amount does not cure the absence of the billing step the process requires. Promising that a bill will eventually follow does not retroactively make an earlier, undocumented transfer of trust money proper.
  11. The correct answer is A (An order on requisition from a local registrar, since it is within one month of delivery.): Where the retainer is not disputed and there are no special circumstances, the client may obtain an order for assessment of a bill already delivered by requisition from a local registrar of the Superior Court of Justice, provided the request is made within one month from delivery, which the three week timeline in this scenario satisfies. A full motion with supporting affidavits is a more elaborate process than the requisition route the statute makes available for this straightforward situation. The Law Society Tribunal does not rule on whether a particular bill is fair, since that determination belongs to the assessment process under the Solicitors Act. The Law Society itself does not reduce bills, so a complaint asking it to do so would not achieve what the client is seeking.
  12. The correct answer is C (Yes, because twenty two days is still within the one month period from delivery.): The Solicitors Act allows the client to obtain an order on requisition for assessment of an already delivered bill within one month from its delivery where the retainer is undisputed and no special circumstances apply, and twenty two days comfortably falls inside that one month window. Fourteen days is a different figure entirely, referring instead to the time the solicitor has to deliver a bill once an order for its delivery and assessment has been made. Waiting a full year describes the outer limit that applies after a verdict or judgment or without special circumstances, not the ordinary requisition window used here. No special circumstances approval is needed at all for a request made comfortably within the one month period.
  13. The correct answer is B (No reference shall be directed after a judgment or beyond twelve months, absent special circumstances.): The Solicitors Act provides that no reference for assessment shall be directed on an application by the party chargeable with the bill after a verdict or judgment has been obtained, or after twelve months from delivery of the bill, except under special circumstances proved to the satisfaction of the court or judge, and both of those bars appear to apply here. It is not correct that a reference must automatically be directed on any client's request at any time, since the statute plainly limits that right. The bar is not absolute and without exception, because special circumstances can still allow a reference even after the twelve month period or a judgment. The lawyer's personal consent is not what the statute requires to overcome the bar, since the decision rests with the court or judge on the special circumstances test.
  14. The correct answer is D (Within fourteen days from service of the order.): Section 6 of the Solicitors Act requires that when a client or other person obtains an order for the delivery and assessment of a solicitor's bill, the bill must be delivered within fourteen days from service of the order, after which it stands referred to an assessment officer. Thirty days overstates the window the statute actually gives the lawyer to comply once served. Seven days understates the true fourteen day period the statute allows. Sixty days is far longer than what section 6 permits and could lead a lawyer to mistakenly believe there is more time to respond.
  15. The correct answer is A (Contingency fee agreements are prohibited in criminal or quasi-criminal matters and in family law matters.): The Solicitors Act and rule 3.6-2 both prohibit a lawyer from entering into a contingency fee agreement in respect of a proceeding under the Criminal Code or any other criminal or quasi-criminal matter, and in respect of a family law matter, which rules out the proposed criminal charge arrangement. Contingency fees are not permitted without restriction in every matter, since the criminal and family law prohibitions are absolute regardless of the client's wishes. The prohibition does not extend to every civil matter generally, since contingency fees remain available in many other civil contexts subject to the statute's requirements. Contingency fees are not limited only to estates matters, since they are commonly used in other permitted areas such as personal injury litigation.
  16. The correct answer is D (Yes, a contingency fee agreement is available for this kind of civil litigation matter.): A solicitor may enter into a contingency fee agreement where remuneration is contingent in whole or in part on the successful disposition or completion of the matter, and personal injury litigation is a classic example of the kind of civil matter where this arrangement is used and permitted. It is incorrect to say contingency fees are never available for personal injury work, since the statute expressly contemplates this use outside the excluded categories. Nothing in the statute requires a large non refundable retainer up front as a precondition to a contingency fee agreement. The statement that contingency fees are reserved exclusively for family and criminal matters is backwards, since those are precisely the two categories where contingency fees are prohibited, not permitted.
  17. The correct answer is A (A joint application must be brought within ninety days for Superior Court approval.): The Solicitors Act allows a solicitor to enter into a contingency fee agreement above the regulated maximum percentage only if, on the joint application of the solicitor and the client brought within ninety days after the agreement is executed, the agreement is approved by the Superior Court of Justice, and an agreement subject to that approval is not enforceable unless approved. The client's signature alone does not make an above cap percentage enforceable, since court approval is still required. Successful settlement of the underlying case has no bearing on whether the fee percentage itself becomes enforceable under the statute. The application for approval is a joint one made within ninety days, not something the lawyer alone may pursue on a longer five year timeline.
  18. The correct answer is D (No, because a contingency fee agreement must be in writing.): The Solicitors Act requires that a contingency fee agreement be in writing, so a purely verbal understanding reached over the phone does not satisfy the statute's requirements for this kind of fee arrangement. A verbal agreement is not treated as equally enforceable here, since the writing requirement is a specific precondition the statute imposes for contingency arrangements. A later email confirming the percentage might help but does not retroactively satisfy the requirement that the agreement itself be in writing from the outset in the manner the statute contemplates. It is also incorrect to say contingency fee agreements are never permitted, since they clearly are permitted in many civil matters, just not without a proper written agreement.
  19. The correct answer is A (The skill, labour and responsibility involved in the instrument, not its length.): Section 13 of the Solicitors Act provides that in assessing a bill for preparing and executing an instrument, the assessment officer shall consider not the length of the instrument but the skill, labour and responsibility involved in it, which directly rejects the argument that a shorter document alone justifies a lower fee. Focusing only on page count is exactly the approach the statute tells the assessment officer not to take. The solicitor's usual hourly rate for other clients is not the statutory test the assessment officer is directed to apply to this particular bill. The client's subjective satisfaction with the document is not the standard the statute sets for assessing the fee either.
  20. The correct answer is D (No, sections 17, 18 and 19 do not apply, since contingency agreements have their own rules.): The Solicitors Act expressly states that sections 17, 18 and 19 do not apply to contingency fee agreements, and it instead sets out separate, special rules for the assessment of a contingency fee agreement, so applying the general sections here would be the wrong approach. It is therefore incorrect to say those general sections apply in exactly the same way to every kind of fee agreement, since the statute expressly carves out contingency agreements from that regime. It is also incorrect that a contingency fee agreement cannot be assessed at all, since the statute simply directs a different assessment path for it. Whether the client specifically requests those general sections does not change which provisions actually govern a contingency fee agreement under the statute.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Remit twenty five per cent of the excess purchase cost over the certificate limit.): Where a purchaser acquires taxable Canadian property, which includes most Ontario real property, from a non resident who has not obtained a section 116 certificate, and the purchaser did not conclude after reasonable inquiry that the vendor was resident in Canada, the purchaser must pay twenty five per cent of the excess of the purchase cost over the certificate limit to the Receiver General within the time the Act sets out. The fifty per cent rate applies to a narrower category such as certain non capital or depreciable property, not to ordinary capital real property. Section 116 clearly extends to real property, and the purchaser is never required to remit the entire purchase price.
  2. The correct answer is D (Apply for a clearance certificate and pay tax or post security acceptable to the Minister.): The non resident vendor, or the purchaser, may apply to the Minister for a clearance certificate before or after the disposition, and if the Minister is satisfied that the vendor has paid an amount on account of tax or furnished acceptable security, the Minister issues a certificate fixing a certificate limit, which then measures and can reduce the purchaser's withholding obligation. It is not true that nothing can be done, since the certificate process exists specifically for this purpose. A vendor cannot simply self declare residency to avoid withholding, and a purchaser's lawyer has no authority to privately waive a statutory withholding obligation.
  3. The correct answer is B (A government issued photo identification document, other than a municipal one.): Verification of an individual may be done with a government issued photo identification document, other than one issued by a municipal government, or with an established Canadian credit file, or with two qualifying pieces of information from different sources, so a photo identification document is one of the accepted methods when a long standing credit file is not available. A single utility bill on its own, an unsupported verbal statement, or an employer's letter about salary do not by themselves satisfy the verification methods the by-law actually sets out.
  4. The correct answer is A (Obtain the source of funds and verify the organization, generally no later than thirty days after.): Where a licensee engages in or gives instructions about receiving, paying or transferring funds, the licensee must also obtain information about the source of the funds and, for an organization, must verify its identity, generally no later than thirty days after first engaging in those funds handling activities. It is not accurate to say there is no deadline at all, and the source of funds inquiry cannot simply be skipped in favour of verification alone. A rigid twenty four hour deadline is also stricter than what the by-law actually allows for verifying an organization.
  5. The correct answer is C (The client's home address, home telephone number, and occupation, among the other required details.): When retained to provide professional services to a client, a licensee must obtain the client's full name, business address and telephone number if applicable, and where the client is an individual, the client's home address and home telephone number, along with the client's occupation, so several categories of information are required together rather than just one on its own. Address and telephone number are not optional once the client is an individual, and the by-law does not ask for five years of detailed banking history simply to open a real estate file.
  6. The correct answer is D (Yes, this related persons situation is one of the recognized limited exceptions to the rule.): A single lawyer is only permitted to represent both the transferor and the transferee of real property in very limited situations, and a transfer between persons related within the meaning of section 251 of the Income Tax Act is one of those recognized exceptions. It is therefore inaccurate to say a single lawyer can never act for both sides, since the exceptions exist for exactly this kind of situation. A signed agreement of purchase and sale on its own is not what creates the exception, and being related family members does not automatically bar sharing a lawyer, since the related persons category is actually a permitted exception rather than a prohibition.
  7. The correct answer is B (No, that personal right of possession ended once they ceased to be spouses.): Where only one spouse has an interest in a matrimonial home, the other spouse's right of possession is personal as against the titled spouse and ends when they cease to be spouses, unless a separation agreement or court order provides otherwise, and neither exists here. The right does not survive divorce automatically and indefinitely, and it is not kept alive by the non titled spouse continuing to pay expenses. Physical separation alone does not end the right, because the couple remained spouses in law until the divorce was granted.
Case real_e_07

A general contractor completed a substantial renovation of a commercial property, and a certificate of substantial performance of the contract was published on June 1. A subcontractor that supplied electrical work last performed services on the project on May 20 and was never paid its final invoice for that work. The subcontractor's lawyer is asked to advise on preserving and perfecting a construction lien, and on the holdback the owner was required to retain from payments made to the general contractor over the course of the project.

  1. The correct answer is B (No later than sixty days after the certificate of substantial performance was published.): Unless preserved, a lien expires at the end of the sixty day period after the earlier of the publication of a certificate of substantial performance and the completion, abandonment or termination of the contract, so measuring from the June 1 publication date, the subcontractor's lawyer must preserve the lien within sixty days of that event. A thirty day figure understates the true preservation deadline, while ninety days and one year both describe different, longer periods that apply to other steps in the lien process rather than to preservation itself.
  2. The correct answer is A (A holdback equal to ten per cent of the price of the services or materials supplied.): Each payer under a contract where a lien may arise must retain a holdback equal to ten per cent of the price of the services or materials as they are actually supplied, until all liens that may be claimed against that holdback have expired, been satisfied, or been otherwise provided for. A five per cent figure understates the required holdback, while fifteen and twenty per cent both overstate the percentage the Act actually sets. This ten per cent fund exists specifically to protect subcontractors and other lien claimants further down the payment chain.
  3. The correct answer is B (The lien expires unless perfected within ninety days of the last day it could be preserved.): A preserved lien expires unless it is perfected before the end of the ninety day period following the last day on which it could have been preserved, so simply registering the claim for lien is not the final step if nothing further happens within that ninety day window.
