An older forty five day period no longer describes the current perfection deadline under the Act, so relying on it would understate how much time actually remains. A preserved lien does not last indefinitely with no further action required, and it does not automatically become a court judgment on its own after any fixed number of days.
  4. The correct answer is D (Commencing an action to enforce the lien and registering a certificate of that action on title.): Where a lien attaches to the premises, it is perfected when the lien claimant commences an action to enforce the lien and registers a certificate of that action on title, unless an order vacates the registration, and this two part step is what actually accomplishes perfection rather than simply preserving the claim again. Sending another demand letter, refiling the same claim for lien a second time, or notifying a surety company are all practical steps a subcontractor might take for other reasons, but none of them is the statutory act that perfects a preserved construction lien.
  5. The correct answer is A (A separate right to rescind within ten days of receiving that further disclosure.): A similar ten day rescission right applies within ten days of receiving a disclosure statement describing a material change, distinct from but parallel to the rescission right tied to the original disclosure statement the purchaser received earlier. It is therefore incorrect that only the very first disclosure carries any rescission right. The remedy this later right gives is rescission of the agreement itself, not merely a price renegotiation, and nothing in the Act converts it into an automatic closing date extension.
  6. The correct answer is C (The vendor is in breach of the agreement, since the stated closing date was binding.): Where an agreement makes time of the essence, the closing must happen on the stated closing date, and a party who fails to close on time, without the agreement being extended or a court relieving against the strictness, is in breach of the agreement. Treating the closing date as merely a loose guideline misstates what time of the essence actually means in this context. The agreement is not automatically cancelled with both parties simply walking away, and the ready, willing and able purchaser is not required to wait a fixed further period before any remedy becomes available.
  7. The correct answer is B (That the purchaser was ready, willing and able to deliver the funds as required.): Because closing is an exchange of documents and money, each side must be ready, willing and able to close and must tender performance, meaning the purchaser must be in a position to deliver the required funds as agreed, and a party who was not actually ready cannot later complain that the other side failed to close. An informal text message alone does not establish that readiness, and having the real estate agent attend somewhere is not what the tender requirement is about. Moving furniture into the home before closing is not evidence of financial readiness and is not part of what valid tender requires.
  8. The correct answer is B (Authorization to submit electronically, with the system operated in practice through Teraview.): Only an authorized electronic document submitter may deliver an electronic document by direct electronic transmission, and Ontario's electronic land registration system is operated in practice through the Teraview software, so both the authorization requirement and the specific platform matter to a lawyer preparing to submit documents. It is incorrect to say no authorization is needed or that any ordinary web browser will do, and the system does not rely on fax transmission or a generic government email account, nor does routine registration require a court order permitting the filing.
  9. The correct answer is C (Obtain the client's written authorization that the lawyer will act as agent.): The Law Society advises that a lawyer obtain the client's written authorization before entering into a Document Registration Agreement, confirming that the client understands the lawyer is contracting as agent for the client and that the client will have both the benefits and the burdens of the covenants in that agreement. Treating this as purely administrative and skipping client authorization misses an important step the guidelines specifically call for. There is no practice of obtaining a personal guarantee from the other lawyer, and authorization must come before entering the agreement, not after registration has already happened.
  10. The correct answer is A (Interest begins to accrue on the unpaid amount from the date it was due.): Interest begins to accrue on an amount not paid when due under the prompt payment rules, at the prejudgment interest rate under the Courts of Justice Act or a greater rate the contract specifies, so interest runs automatically from the due date rather than only once a court judgment is obtained. Termination of the contract is not the contractor's only available remedy for late payment. Interest accrual does not depend on the parties' contract addressing it, since the Construction Act itself supplies this default consequence.
  11. The correct answer is A (No later than seven days after the contract is terminated.): No later than seven days after a contract is terminated, the owner, the contractor, or another person whose lien is subject to expiry must publish a notice of termination in the prescribed form and manner, and the termination is then treated as occurring on the date that notice was published for expiry purposes. A thirty day or sixty day figure both overstate this specific publication deadline, and it is incorrect to say no deadline applies at all, since the seven day requirement is what starts the expiry clock running from a clearly identifiable date.
  12. The correct answer is D (Within one hundred twenty days of receiving the reserve fund study.): Within one hundred twenty days of receiving a reserve fund study, the board must review it and propose a plan for future funding, and only after that does the board send owners a notice within a further fifteen days before eventually implementing the plan thirty days after that notice requirement is met. Thirty and sixty day figures both understate the actual review and proposal period the regulation allows, and a full year is well beyond what the timeline requires for this particular first step.
  13. The correct answer is B (By registering a claim for lien on title in the proper land registry office.): A lien is preserved by registering a claim for lien on title in the proper land registry office where the lien attaches to the premises, during the supply of services or materials or at any time before the lien expires, so this registration step is what actually preserves the lien in the situation described. Mailing a letter to an insurance company, posting a notice at the site, or filing a complaint with a financial services regulator are none of them the statutory method the Construction Act sets out for preserving a lien that attaches to the premises.
  14. The correct answer is C (A second ten per cent holdback, running from the date of substantial performance onward.): Where a contract has been certified or declared substantially performed but services or materials remain to be supplied, the payer must also retain a second holdback equal to ten per cent of the price of those remaining services or materials, running from the date of substantial performance, so a further holdback is required rather than none at all. This second holdback is not reduced to five per cent, and it is not doubled to twenty per cent either, since the Act applies the same ten per cent figure to this second holdback as it does to the first.
  15. The correct answer is A (Ask the Authority to appoint a registry adjudicator, who must be appointed within a further seven days.): If the registry adjudicator the parties agreed on does not consent within four days after the notice of adjudication is given, the party who gave the notice must ask the Authority to appoint a registry adjudicator, and the Authority must appoint one, subject to consent, no later than seven days after receiving that request. Starting over in court abandons the adjudication route rather than following the Act's own mechanism, and there is no rule requiring the parties to simply wait indefinitely or to privately pick a replacement adjudicator without the Authority's involvement.
  16. The correct answer is A (It binds the parties until a court determination, an arbitration determination, or a written agreement.): An adjudicator's determination binds the parties to the adjudication until a court determination, an arbitration determination, or a written agreement between the parties changes that outcome, and the determination and reasons are admissible as evidence in court proceedings. It is therefore not accurate to describe the determination as permanently unchangeable by any later court or arbitration process, and it is equally inaccurate to say the determination has no binding effect at all, since it plainly binds the parties in the interim. The determination binds both parties to the adjudication, not only the one who requested it.
  17. The correct answer is C (Other registered parties such as encumbrancers, execution creditors and lien claimants.): The mortgagee must give the notice of exercising the power of sale to the mortgagor and to the other persons the Act lists, including subsequent registered owners, encumbrancers, execution creditors and construction lien claimants as specified, subject to the exceptions set out in the section. Limiting notice to only the municipality, only the original builder, or only the mortgagor's employer would all leave out the actual categories of interested parties the Act requires notice to be given to before a sale can proceed.
  18. The correct answer is D (A power of sale is exercised out of court, while a judicial sale proceeds under court supervision.): A power of sale is the mortgagee's contractual or statutory right to sell without going to court, subject to the applicable notice rules, while a judicial sale happens inside a mortgage action, where the court orders the property sold and the proceeds are applied under the court's supervision. It is not accurate to say a power of sale always takes longer, since it is generally the out of court route precisely because it can move faster than full litigation. A judicial sale by definition involves the court, so it is incorrect to say it needs nothing filed with the court, and the two remedies are clearly not identical.
  19. The correct answer is C (Two point one per cent, the guideline figure set for the year 2026.): The guideline is the maximum a landlord can increase most tenants' rent during a year without Landlord and Tenant Board approval, and the guideline for 2026 is two point one per cent, while one point nine per cent is instead the separate figure that applies to increases taking effect in 2027. A three point five per cent figure does not match either year's actual guideline, and Ontario has not suspended the guideline system in 2026, since the guideline continues to apply and simply changes in amount from year to year.
  20. The correct answer is A (Explain which searches will not be done and personally review the policy exclusions and exceptions.): Where title insurance is used, the lawyer must still tell the client which searches will not be done, check what off title matters are not covered by the policy, obtain waivers where appropriate, and personally review the search reports and the draft policy exclusions and exceptions. It is not correct that the policy eliminates every professional obligation of the lawyer, and simply forwarding the unread policy to the client would ignore that continuing duty. Title insurance is a widely accepted alternative in Ontario practice, so refusing to close on that basis alone is not the right response.
  21. The correct answer is C (The land is not on a registered plan of subdivision, so a consent is needed first.): Section 50 of the Planning Act prohibits conveying land unless it is described in accordance with a registered plan of subdivision, or the transaction otherwise fits an allowed exception, so land carved out of a larger lot with no registered plan generally needs a consent before it can be conveyed. There is no dollar threshold that removes small transactions from this rule, and the rule is not limited to commercial land, since it applies broadly to land generally, including rural residential lots. Having separate lawyers for each party has nothing to do with whether a consent is required under the Act.
  22. The correct answer is A (The council of the relevant municipality, or a body the council has properly delegated its power to.): A consent is generally given by the council of the relevant municipality, depending on where the land is located, and a council may delegate its consent powers by by-law to a committee of council, an appointed officer, a planning authority or the committee of adjustment. It is not accurate to say only the Minister can grant consents, since municipal councils and their delegates handle most applications. A lawyer has no authority to grant a Planning Act consent personally, and this is not a function performed by a judge of the Superior Court of Justice.
  23. The correct answer is B (The conveyance did not create or convey any interest in the land at all.): A conveyance made in contravention of section 50 of the Planning Act does not create or convey any interest in the land, so the buyer did not actually acquire the interest the parties intended, subject only to the narrow exception for an agreement made expressly effective only if section 50 is complied with. Describing the problem as merely exposing the vendor to a fine understates the consequence, since the conveyance itself is legally ineffective, not just penalized. There is no rule that an invalid conveyance becomes effective after any fixed number of years simply by the passage of time.
  24. The correct answer is D (Consent may be given by an upper tier or single tier municipal council, or by the Minister.): Depending on where the land is located, a consent may be given by the council of the upper tier municipality, the council of a single tier municipality, the council of a prescribed single tier municipality in a territorial district, or the Minister. The federal government has no role in granting a Planning Act consent, and neither a licensed surveyor nor a local school board is an authority named in the Act for this purpose. Understanding which body applies depends on the specific municipal structure where the land sits.
  25. The correct answer is D (Yes, because a purchaser authorized in the agreement to apply may make the consent application.): The Planning Act allows an owner, a chargee or a purchaser of land, or a duly authorized agent, to apply for a consent, and a purchaser who has entered into an agreement of purchase and sale that authorizes the application qualifies under the Act. It is therefore incorrect to say only the registered owner may apply, since a purchaser with proper authorization is expressly included. A real estate agent has no special status to submit the application on their own authority, and the application is made to the council or the Minister, not to the municipality acting as an applicant itself.
  26. The correct answer is A (Apply to the committee of adjustment for a minor variance from the height requirement instead.): A minor variance changes how the zoning by-law applies to a specific property, such as a height limit, while a consent severs land so it can be conveyed separately, so a garage height problem calls for a minor variance application to the committee of adjustment rather than a consent. Treating the two application types as interchangeable misunderstands their different legal purposes under the Planning Act. There is no process for the Minister to grant a blanket exemption from an entire zoning by-law for a single property, and a minor variance application is in fact available to address exactly this kind of problem.
  27. The correct answer is D (Whether the variance is desirable and keeps the general intent of the by-law and official plan.): The committee of adjustment may authorize a minor variance if, in its opinion, the variance is desirable for the appropriate development or use of the land and the general intent and purpose of the by-law and the official plan are maintained. A neighbour's personal written consent is not the statutory test the committee applies, even though neighbours may be notified and heard. Resale value and cost to the applicant are not the legal criteria set out in the Planning Act for deciding whether to grant a minor variance.
  28. The correct answer is B (Appeal to the Tribunal within twenty days of the decision by filing a notice with the secretary treasurer.): An applicant, the Minister, or a specified person or public body with an interest in the matter may appeal a committee of adjustment decision to the Tribunal within twenty days of the decision, by filing a notice of appeal with the committee's secretary treasurer and paying the required fee. It is incorrect to say no appeal exists at all, since the Planning Act sets out this specific route. Ninety days to the Minister and six years to the Small Claims Court are both wrong descriptions of the actual appeal mechanism and time limit that apply here.
  29. The correct answer is D (The appeal is likely out of time and at real risk of being rejected for missing the deadline.): Because an appeal from a committee of adjustment decision must be filed within twenty days of the decision, filing on day twenty-six is past that statutory limit and puts the appeal at real risk of being treated as out of time. The twenty day period runs from the date of the decision itself, not from any later event such as the start of construction. Paying the required fee is necessary but does not cure a late filing, and it is incorrect to say there is no time limit at all for this type of appeal.
  30. The correct answer is D (Common expenses, any planned assessments, the reserve fund amount and any legal action against the corporation.): The status certificate must contain the items listed in the regulation, including common expenses for the unit, any assessments levied to increase the reserve fund and the reason for them, the reserve fund amount within the prescribed period, and any legal action against the corporation, among other required items. It is not accurate that the regulation stops at disclosing the common expense figure alone, and a simple list of board members' personal phone numbers is not what the certificate is meant to provide. Financial information about the reserve fund and legal actions is exactly the kind of detail the certificate is designed to disclose to a purchaser.
  31. The correct answer is B (The corporation is deemed to have given a certificate stating there are no unpaid common expenses.): The corporation must give the status certificate within ten days after receiving the request and the fee, and if it does not, it is deemed to have given a certificate stating, among other things, that there are no unpaid common expenses and no reserve fund assessments have been levied since the current budget. Missing the deadline does not automatically cause the purchaser to lose the right to complete the purchase, and it does not void the sale on its own. The ten day period is a firm statutory requirement, not merely a suggested guideline the corporation can ignore without consequence.
  32. The correct answer is C (The charge exceeds what the regulation allows, since the maximum permitted fee is one hundred dollars.): The fee a corporation may charge for a status certificate, including all material required to be included in it, must not exceed one hundred dollars inclusive of applicable taxes, so a charge of one hundred and fifty dollars exceeds the statutory maximum. Corporations do not have unrestricted discretion to set whatever fee they wish, and there is no rule setting a minimum fee of two hundred dollars. Status certificates are not free, since the regulation expressly contemplates a fee being charged, just one capped at the amount the regulation states.
  33. The correct answer is C (The charges the borrower becomes responsible for by entering the arrangement.): Where the mortgage is not a term mortgage, such as a line of credit, the brokerage must disclose the charges or penalties for failing to pay as agreed, the charges for which the borrower becomes responsible by entering the arrangement, and other prescribed information. Limiting the disclosure to only the early payoff charges leaves out the broader charges the section actually requires to be disclosed. Other clients' credit histories have nothing to do with this particular borrower's disclosure. The brokerage's own internal staffing costs are not a matter the Act requires to be disclosed to a borrower.
  34. The correct answer is B (The advertisement is not permitted without including the required information.): No person or entity may authorize an advertisement for a mortgage that purports to contain information about the cost of borrowing or another prescribed matter unless the advertisement contains the required information in the required form and manner. Quoting a rate while omitting the other required cost of borrowing details does not satisfy this rule, regardless of whether a contract is ultimately signed. The advertising disclosure rule applies to brokerages authorizing advertisements, not only to lenders directly. Nothing in the Act carves out online advertisements as a category exempt from this requirement.
  35. The correct answer is A (Until every lien that may be claimed against it has expired or been satisfied.): Each payer under a contract or subcontract under which a lien may arise must retain the holdback until all liens that may be claimed against it have expired, been satisfied, or been otherwise provided for. There is no fixed one year period tied to when construction began that governs when the holdback may be released. A municipal occupancy certificate addresses building code compliance, not the separate lien holdback obligation under this Act. A flat thirty day period after the final invoice does not match the expiry or satisfaction standard the Act actually sets.
  36. The correct answer is D (October 31, sixty days after the certificate was published.): A lien expires at the end of the 60 day period after the earlier of the publication of a certificate of substantial performance and the completion, abandonment, or termination of the contract, unless it is preserved before that date. Counting 60 days from September 1 gives October 31 as the last day the lien can exist without being preserved. Fifteen and 30 days are far shorter than the 60 day period the Act actually allows for this purpose. One hundred twenty days overstates the expiry period by double the correct figure.
  37. The correct answer is B (Simply commence the action to enforce the lien.): Where the lien does not attach to the premises, perfection happens once the claimant commences the action to enforce the lien, without the additional step of registering a certificate of action on title that applies where the lien does attach to the premises. Requiring a certificate of action here conflates the two different perfection routes the Act sets out. There is no newspaper notice requirement anywhere in the perfection provisions. A fresh consent from the owner is not a precondition to starting the action that perfects this kind of lien.
  38. The correct answer is A (Shelter under the other claimant's already perfected lien.): A preserved lien can also be perfected by sheltering under another lien claimant's perfected lien, following the rules set out in the Construction Act for doing so. Saying each lien must always be perfected through its own separate action overlooks this specific sheltering option the Act provides. Perfection does not happen automatically just because some other unrelated lien on the project has been perfected, sheltering has its own rules to follow. Consolidation by the court is a separate procedural step and is not what the Act describes as the route to sheltering.
  39. The correct answer is D (July 6, thirty five days after the invoice was given to the owner.): If the owner does not pay, the contractor must pay subcontractors no later than 35 days after giving the proper invoice to the owner, unless the contractor gives a valid notice of non payment within the allowed time. Counting 35 days from June 1 gives July 6 as the deadline that applies here. Seven days applies only where the contractor has actually been paid and is passing that payment along, which did not happen in this scenario. Twenty eight days is the deadline that applies to the owner's own payment of the contractor, not to the contractor's payment of its subcontractors.
  40. The correct answer is A (The determination is of no force or effect, since it was made too late.): The adjudicator must make a determination no later than 30 days after receiving the required documents, subject to a 14 day extension with the consent of the parties or a longer extension by written agreement, and a determination made late is of no force or effect. Counting from July 1, even with the full 14 day extension the outer date would be well before August 20, so an unextended determination on that date exceeds the deadline. Adjudicators do not have open ended discretion over how long a determination may take. The determination's invalidity here does not depend on either party applying to court, it follows directly from the missed statutory deadline.
  41. The correct answer is B (The determination is admissible evidence, and each party bears its own costs.): An adjudicator's determination and reasons are admissible as evidence in court, and the parties bear their own costs of the adjudication unless the adjudicator orders otherwise under the costs provisions. Treating the determination as inadmissible misstates its evidentiary status entirely. There is no requirement for the adjudicator to personally consent before the determination can be used as evidence in a later proceeding. The Authority does not routinely pay the parties' costs, the default rule is that each side covers its own unless the adjudicator directs otherwise.
Case wills__05

A testator died on March 1 leaving a will that gave his entire estate to a local charity. For the three years before his death, he had been paying his adult daughter's rent and medical costs because a permanent disability prevents her from working. He had also been giving his unemployed brother $500 a month for the eighteen months before his death to help cover living costs. Letters probate were granted on May 1. By the following January, seven months after the grant, most of the estate had been distributed to the charity, but a $40,000 parcel of land had not yet been transferred.

  1. The correct answer is A (Both of them qualify as dependants he was supporting before his death.): A dependant includes a child of the deceased and a brother or sister of the deceased, but only where the deceased was providing support, or was under a legal obligation to provide support, immediately before death. Both relatives here were receiving regular financial support from the testator right up until his death, so both can qualify. The fact that the will leaves everything to a charity does not remove either relative's status as a dependant, it is exactly the situation the support claim exists to address.
  2. The correct answer is A (By November 1, six months after the letters probate were granted.): The statute sets the ordinary limitation period for a dependant support application at six months after the grant of letters probate, which here means six months after May 1, landing on November 1. The date of death itself is not the trigger for this deadline, only the date the grant is issued. A deadline does apply here regardless of how the underlying support was structured, whether as monthly payments or otherwise.
  3. The correct answer is D (The court may still allow a claim against the undistributed land if fair.): Even after the ordinary six month deadline has passed, the court may allow a late application if it considers it proper, but only as to any part of the estate that is still undistributed at the date of the application. Since the $40,000 land has not yet been transferred, that undistributed portion remains available for a late claim. Neither the charity's consent nor an application by the estate trustee is a precondition to the court exercising this discretion.
  4. The correct answer is D (The dependant's parent, or certain public agencies providing support.): The statute allows the application to be made by the dependant or by the dependant's parent, and it also permits certain listed public agencies to apply if they are providing, or have provided, income support or similar benefits to that dependant. A retained lawyer acts on the dependant's instructions rather than being a separate applicant in his or her own right. Neither the estate trustee nor another beneficiary has any special standing under this provision to bring the claim.
  5. The correct answer is A (No, survivorship assets and a TFSA with a beneficiary designation are excluded.): Assets that pass to a surviving joint owner by right of survivorship, and registered accounts such as a TFSA with a valid beneficiary designation, are both excluded from the value of the estate for this tax. Neither category needs a court order or election to be excluded, the exclusion follows automatically from how the asset is legally held or designated. Combining these two facts, both assets described here fall outside the value that the estate trustee must report.
  6. The correct answer is D (Within 180 calendar days after the estate certificate was issued.): The initial estate information return must be received by the Ministry of Finance within 180 calendar days after the estate certificate is issued, not the date of death. This deadline applies even where the calculated value of the estate turns out to be zero, since a return is still required to be filed in that situation. Thirty days and one year are both incorrect figures for this specific filing deadline.
  7. The correct answer is B (File an amended return within 60 calendar days of becoming aware of that.): Where an estate representative becomes aware within four years of the certificate being issued that a return was incorrect or incomplete, an amended return must be received within 60 calendar days of becoming aware of that fact. This applies directly to a case where an earlier estimate turns out to have been too low once the true value becomes known. The obligation is to amend the existing return promptly, not to wait until the estate is wound up or to start over with a wholly new filing.
  8. The correct answer is D (Four years after the day the tax became payable, being the issue date.): The Minister may assess or reassess an estate for tax payable within four years after the day the tax became payable, and that day is the day the estate certificate is issued. On these facts that means the ordinary deadline runs four years from January 15, 2023. Two years and six years are both incorrect figures for this deadline, and a deadline does exist under the ordinary rule even though separate exceptions can extend it in cases of misrepresentation or fraud.
  9. The correct answer is D (Yes, the estate does not exceed the $150,000 prescribed small estate limit.): A small estate is defined as one that does not exceed the amount prescribed by regulation, which is currently $150,000, so an estate valued at $148,000 qualifies for the simplified procedure. The $50,000 figure belongs to a different rule, the estate administration tax exemption threshold, and should not be confused with the small estate ceiling. Nothing in the small estate rules requires every beneficiary to be an adult, though special notice steps apply where a beneficiary is a minor.
  10. The correct answer is D (The trustee must first give 30 days notice to the parent and Children's Lawyer.): An application for a small estate certificate cannot be filed until at least 30 days after the applicant has sent the application and the will to every person entitled to share in the distribution, and where a beneficiary is under 18 the documents must also go to a parent or guardian and to the Children's Lawyer. Filing tomorrow, before that notice has even been sent, would be premature regardless of the estate's size. A minor beneficiary does not block the small estate process entirely, it simply triggers this additional notice requirement before filing.
  11. The correct answer is A (A renunciation signed by the person named as estate trustee who is not acting.): Where a person named in a will as estate trustee does not join the application, the applicant must obtain a signed renunciation from that person before the certificate can be issued. This is a simple administrative step rather than something requiring a court order, and it says nothing about that person's capacity, only that they are choosing not to act. No medical evidence is needed for a person who is simply uninterested rather than incapable.
  12. The correct answer is A (A renunciation, since the sibling is entitled in equal right but not joining.): Where there is no will, the applicant must provide a renunciation from every person entitled in priority or in equal right to be named estate trustee who has not joined the application, and here the other adult child is entitled in equal right. A simple signed renunciation is what is required, not a court order permanently reassigning that entitlement. No affidavit about the siblings' relationship is part of this requirement at all.
  13. The correct answer is A (There is no fixed percentage, only a fair and reasonable allowance set by a judge.): The governing statute entitles a trustee or personal representative to a fair and reasonable allowance for care, pains, trouble and time spent, as fixed by a judge of the Superior Court of Justice, rather than a set percentage written into the statute itself. Courts often start from a guideline percentage developed through case law, but that guideline is adjusted against several discretionary factors rather than applied as a rigid rate. Compensation remains available even where the will is silent, since the statutory allowance does not depend on the will authorizing an amount.
  14. The correct answer is D (At least 75 days, since the beneficiary is being served outside Ontario.): The rules governing the passing of accounts require the notice of application and a draft of the judgment sought to be served at least 60 days before the hearing for a person served in Ontario, but that period increases to at least 75 days for a person served outside Ontario. The 10 day period belongs to a different procedure entirely, the notice for a motion for directions in a contentious estate proceeding. A fixed minimum period does apply here, it is not simply whatever arrives before the hearing date.
  15. The correct answer is D (Understand relevant information, or appreciate the foreseeable consequences of it.): A person is incapable of managing property only where the person cannot understand information relevant to a property decision, or cannot appreciate the reasonably foreseeable consequences of making or not making that decision. This is a functional test focused on understanding and appreciation of consequences, not a test of financial skill compared to a professional, and it does not require perfect recall of financial history or exact asset values. A client can be perfectly capable while still being less sophisticated than a professional trustee.
  16. The correct answer is C (He cannot witness it, since a spouse of the named attorney is barred.): The statute bars several categories of people from witnessing a continuing power of attorney for property, including the attorney and the attorney's spouse or partner, so the son cannot witness a document naming his own spouse as attorney. Written consent from the grantor does not override this statutory bar. The bar is not a blanket rule against any relative of the grantor witnessing, since a child of the grantor is only barred in that separate capacity, not because relatives in general are excluded.
  17. The correct answer is D (It terminates the first power of attorney, since a new one revokes the earlier grant.): A continuing power of attorney terminates when the grantor executes a new continuing power of attorney, unless the grantor has provided for multiple continuing powers of attorney to operate together. Nothing here suggests the grantor intended to keep both documents alive at once, so the later one simply supersedes the first. The remote execution of the first document was valid in its own right, since audio visual signing is permitted where a witness is a licensee, but that validity does not stop a later document from ending it.
  18. The correct answer is D (That the attorney must account for dealings and that she may revoke it while capable.): A person is capable of giving a continuing power of attorney if she knows, among other things, that the attorney must account for dealings with her property and that she may revoke the power of attorney while she remains capable of doing so. Signing the document does not turn the attorney into a court appointed guardian, and no court order is needed before the attorney can act. The capacity test exists precisely because the power takes effect without ongoing court supervision unless something later goes wrong.
  19. The correct answer is D (No, because a continuing power of attorney terminates immediately on the grantor's death.): A continuing power of attorney is terminated by the grantor's death, along with several other listed events such as the attorney's own death, incapacity or resignation. There is no statutory grace period letting the attorney keep paying bills for a wind up week, and authority does not carry over until an estate trustee is appointed. Once the grantor dies, only a validly appointed estate trustee has authority over the property, so the attorney should have stopped acting immediately.
  20. The correct answer is B (No, a person paid to provide care services cannot act as attorney for personal care.): A person may not act as attorney for personal care if that person provides health care or residential, social, training or support services to the grantor for compensation, unless the person is also the grantor's spouse, partner or relative. The support worker here fits squarely within the barred category and has no qualifying family relationship, so the bar applies regardless of trust or a doctor's opinion. Being unrelated is not itself the problem, since unrelated people can normally act as attorney, it is the paid caregiving relationship that bars this particular worker.
  21. The correct answer is D (Understand relevant information, or appreciate the foreseeable consequences of a decision.): A person is incapable of personal care where the person cannot understand information relevant to a decision about health care, nutrition, shelter, clothing, hygiene or safety, or cannot appreciate the reasonably foreseeable consequences of making or not making that decision. This is the same understanding and appreciation framework used for property decisions, just applied to personal care topics instead. Being unable to live independently, recall every provider's name, or communicate a firm preference are not themselves the statutory test.
  22. The correct answer is C (No, a provision giving property to a spouse does not apply to her.): Any provision in the intestacy rules that gives property to a person's spouse does not apply to the spouse if the spouses were separated at the time of the person's death, using the same separation test used for revoking gifts under a will. Because they were separated and never resumed cohabitation, she is excluded from taking as his spouse on intestacy. No court decree of separation is needed for this rule to apply, and the length of the marriage does not change the result.
  23. The correct answer is A (Her husband is entitled to the whole estate, worth $600,000, absolutely.): Where a person dies intestate and is survived by a spouse and no issue, the spouse is entitled to the whole property absolutely. Since this woman has no children, her husband takes the entire $600,000 estate rather than sharing it with parents or other relatives. The preferential share rule only comes into play where the intestate is survived by both a spouse and issue, which is not the case here. Escheat to the Crown applies only where there is no spouse and no other qualifying relative at all.
  24. The correct answer is B (She takes the whole net estate, since it is below the preferential share.): Where a person dies intestate with a spouse and issue, the spouse takes the whole estate if its net value is not more than the preferential share, which is $350,000 for deaths on or after March 1, 2021. Because this estate's net value of $310,000 is below that figure, the wife takes the entire net estate and the child takes nothing under the intestacy rules. The $200,000 figure applied only to deaths before March 1, 2021, and a spouse cannot receive a fixed preferential share that exceeds what the estate actually contains.
  25. The correct answer is A ($350,000, for estates of persons who died on or after March 1, 2021.): The prescribed preferential share is $200,000 for the estates of persons who died before March 1, 2021, and $350,000 for the estates of persons who died on or after that date. A death in April 2026 falls under the current $350,000 figure. The amount is set by regulation rather than indexed automatically each year, and it is a separate figure from the $150,000 small estate certificate limit, which serves an entirely different purpose.
  26. The correct answer is A (She takes the shortfall from the intestate property up to the preferential share.): Where a deceased left a will for some property and died intestate for other property, and is survived by a spouse and issue, the spouse takes the shortfall out of the intestate property if the will gives less than the preferential share. Because the will gave her only $100,000 against a $350,000 preferential share, she is entitled to top up to $350,000 from the intestate rental property. She is not limited to the will's $100,000, but she is also not automatically entitled to the whole property regardless of value, since the top up stops once the preferential share is reached.
  27. The correct answer is D (The husband takes half of the residue, and the child takes the other half.): Where a person dies intestate leaving a spouse and one child, the spouse takes one half of the residue of the property after the preferential share is paid, and the child takes the other half. Because there is only one child here, the one third and two thirds split applies only where there is more than one child, so the husband and this single child instead each receive half of the $300,000 residue. There is no rule that gives the child the whole residue, or that splits it three to one.
  28. The correct answer is B (The wife receives one third, and the children share the remaining two thirds.): Where a person dies intestate leaving a spouse and more than one child, the spouse takes one third of the residue after the preferential share is paid, and the children share the other two thirds. The one half split instead applies only where there is a single child. Because this family has three children, the equal one quarter split and the two thirds to the spouse split are both wrong, and the statutory one third and two thirds division governs.
  29. The correct answer is D (The daughter takes half, and the two grandchildren share their parent's other half.): Where the deceased leaves issue but no spouse, the property is distributed equally among the issue of the nearest degree in which there are issue surviving, and the share of a predeceased issue goes to that person's own issue in the same way. The daughter, as the surviving child, takes one half directly, and the two grandchildren together take the other half that their deceased parent would have received, splitting it equally between themselves. An equal three way split ignores that the estate is first divided at the level of the deceased's children, not at the level of all descendants together.
  30. The correct answer is A (The property is divided equally between his two surviving parents.): Where there is no spouse or issue, the property is divided equally between the deceased's parents, and only where one parent has died does the surviving parent take absolutely. The parents' own separation from each other has no bearing on this rule, which looks only at their relationship to the deceased. There is no test based on proximity of residence or relative income, and both surviving biological parents share the estate equally on these facts.
  31. The correct answer is B (The brother takes half, and the sister's two children share her half.): Where there is no spouse, issue or parent, the property is divided equally among the surviving brothers and sisters, and the share of a predeceased brother or sister is divided equally among that person's children. The brother therefore takes one half directly, and the deceased sister's two children divide her half between themselves. Living siblings do not take the whole estate to the exclusion of a predeceased sibling's children, and an equal three way split again ignores that the division starts at the level of the deceased's siblings.
  32. The correct answer is B (It is divided equally among all five nephews and nieces, without representation.): Where there is no spouse, issue, parent, brother or sister, the property is divided equally among the nephews and nieces, without representation. This differs from the earlier rule for siblings, where a predeceased sibling's children take their parent's share by representation. At this level, all five nephews and nieces simply share equally, regardless of which sibling was their parent, and there is no distinction in shares between nephews and nieces or preference for the eldest sibling's children.
  33. The correct answer is B (The half blood cousin inherits equally with the whole blood cousins of the same degree.): Where there is no spouse, issue, parent, brother, sister, nephew or niece, the property is divided equally among the next of kin of equal degree of consanguinity, without representation, and relatives of the half blood inherit equally with those of the whole blood in the same degree. Because all of these cousins are of equal degree, the half blood cousin shares fully and equally with the whole blood cousins, is not excluded, and does not take a reduced or a priority share because of the half blood relationship.
  34. The correct answer is C (The property becomes the property of the Crown, subject to the Escheats Act.): Where there is no spouse, issue, parent, brother, sister, nephew, niece or next of kin, the property becomes the property of the Crown, and the Escheats Act, 2015 applies. Friends and caregivers have no claim under the intestacy rules simply because no blood or spousal relative can be found. The court does not simply hold the estate indefinitely on the chance a relative later appears, and the estate trustee has no power to redirect an escheated estate to a charity of choice.
  35. The correct answer is C (The child inherits as if born during the father's lifetime and surviving him.): A child conceived before the deceased's death and born alive after the death inherits as if the child had been born in the deceased's lifetime and had survived the deceased. Being conceived but not yet born at the moment of death does not exclude the child from sharing in the intestate estate. There is no token share set by the Public Guardian and Trustee for a child conceived before death, and no thirty day paternity deadline applies to this category of child.
  36. The correct answer is C (She is presumed capable of the medical consent, but not the contract.): A person who is 18 or older is presumed capable of entering into a contract, while a person who is 16 or older is presumed capable of giving or refusing consent in connection with personal care. At 17, she is old enough to be presumed capable of consenting to the medical procedure but not yet old enough to be presumed capable of the gym contract. The two presumptions use different ages, so treating both decisions the same way, in either direction, misstates the rule.
  37. The correct answer is A (Yes, because he cannot appreciate the reasonably foreseeable consequences of his decisions.): A person is incapable of managing property if the person is not able to understand information relevant to a decision about the property, or is not able to appreciate the reasonably foreseeable consequences of a decision or a lack of decision. Being able to recite bank balances shows he can understand some information, but his inability to appreciate the foreseeable consequences of ongoing large cash gifts still satisfies the second branch of the test. Incapacity is a finding that can exist before any guardian is appointed, and recognizing family members is not part of the statutory test.
  38. The correct answer is B (That the attorney must account for dealings with the client's property.): A person is capable of giving a continuing power of attorney for property if he or she knows the kind and value of the property, is aware of obligations to dependants, knows what the attorney will be able to do, knows the attorney must account for dealings with the property, knows the power can be revoked while capable, and appreciates that the property's value may decline or the authority may be misused. Court approval is not a precondition to signing, and there is no rule limiting attorneys to lawyers, and the document has no automatic effect on the client's beneficiaries after death, since it ends at death.
  39. The correct answer is B (Both her daughter and the attorney's spouse are barred as witnesses.): A continuing power of attorney for property must be witnessed by two people, and the attorney or the attorney's spouse or partner, the grantor's spouse or partner, a child of the grantor or a person the grantor has demonstrated a settled intention to treat as a child, a person under guardianship, and a person under 18 must not act as witnesses. Her daughter is the grantor's child, and the attorney's spouse falls within the attorney's own prohibited relationship, so both are barred. There is no general bar on a doctor or a neighbour acting as a witness, and it is not true that any adult may witness regardless of relationship.
  40. The correct answer is D (The power of attorney is terminated because the attorney resigned with no substitute.): A continuing power of attorney is terminated when the attorney resigns, unless another attorney is authorized to act or a substitute is named to act instead. Because no other attorney or substitute is provided for in this document, the resignation ends the power of attorney rather than leaving it suspended or continuing automatically. There is no rule under the Act that passes the authority automatically to the grantor's nearest relative, and termination does not wait for a future finding of incapacity.
  41. The correct answer is D (At least one director must be a resident Canadian, since the board has fewer than four.): Under the CBCA, subject to the higher threshold for prescribed sectors, at least 25 percent of the directors of a corporation must be resident Canadians, but if a corporation has fewer than four directors, at least one director must be a resident Canadian instead of applying a strict percentage. Since this board has only three directors, the applicable floor is one resident Canadian director, not a fractional 25 percent calculation that would round down to nothing meaningful. Saying no resident Canadian director is required at all ignores that the CBCA residency rule remains fully in force federally. A majority threshold only applies to corporations caught by the higher prescribed sector rule, which is not described in these facts.
  42. The correct answer is C (The director may have a statutory defence based on reasonable reliance on the accountant's report.): The Act provides a statutory reasonable diligence defence: a director is not liable and has complied with the duty of care if the director exercised the care, diligence and skill a reasonably prudent person would exercise, including reliance in good faith on a report from an accountant or other person whose profession lends credibility to the statement. This means the director's reliance here is exactly the kind of conduct the defence is meant to protect, rather than something irrelevant to the analysis. Saying directors may never rely on outside reports directly contradicts the statutory language allowing that reliance. Nothing in the defence requires the accountant to have personally guaranteed the figures in writing before the director can rely on the report in good faith.
  43. The correct answer is D (Disclosure must be made at the meeting where the contract is first considered.): A director who is a party to a material contract with the corporation, or who has a material interest in another party to that contract, must disclose the nature and extent of the interest in writing or request that it be entered in the minutes, and for a director this disclosure is made at the meeting at which the proposed contract is first considered. Waiting until after the contract has been fully performed would defeat the purpose of giving the other directors the information before they vote on it. Merely abstaining from the vote does not satisfy the separate statutory disclosure obligation, since disclosure and voting conduct are treated as distinct requirements. There is no requirement to make this kind of conflict disclosure through a public Ontario Business Registry filing.
  44. The correct answer is B (Directors can be liable for debts not exceeding six months of unpaid wages.): Directors are jointly and severally liable to the employees of the corporation for all debts not exceeding six months' wages that become payable while they are directors for services performed for the corporation, which is a narrower period than the twelve month figure, which instead applies to accrued vacation pay under the same section. Saying directors have no personal liability at all for unpaid wages misstates this specific statutory exposure that exists once the other conditions for liability are met. Limiting exposure to only the final week worked understates the actual six month period the statute sets out. The employee's claim in this scenario falls within the type of debt this director liability provision is designed to address.
  45. The correct answer is D (The director may now be liable, since execution against the corporation was returned unsatisfied.): A director is liable under this provision only if the corporation is sued in the action against the director and execution against the corporation is returned unsatisfied in whole or in part, among other listed triggering events such as liquidation or bankruptcy, and that condition has now been met here. There is no requirement that the employee first pursue every other creditor of the corporation before turning to the director personally. Formal dissolution of the corporation by the Director is not a precondition to this kind of claim, since liquidation, winding up or unsatisfied execution are the relevant triggers instead. The employee could not simply skip suing the corporation altogether and proceed straight against the director, because that step is part of what establishes the director's liability in the first place.
  46. The correct answer is A (Indemnification is available, since the officer acted honestly and in good faith for the corporation.): A corporation may indemnify a former officer against reasonably incurred costs, charges and expenses connected to a proceeding arising from that association, but indemnity is not permitted unless the individual acted honestly and in good faith with a view to the best interests of the corporation, a condition the facts describe as satisfied here. The indemnification power extends expressly to former officers, so having already left the role does not itself defeat the claim. Good faith conduct remains a genuine precondition rather than something that can be ignored, so it is inaccurate to say indemnification follows automatically from a costs claim, without regard to the officer's conduct. Court approval is only one available route for approving an indemnity in specific circumstances, not a mandatory step required in every single indemnification case.
  47. The correct answer is D (This declaration is deemed to be a unanimous shareholder agreement under the Act.): Where the registered holder of all the issued shares makes a written declaration restricting the directors' powers to manage the business and affairs of the corporation, that declaration is deemed to be a unanimous shareholder agreement, even though only one person is involved as the sole shareholder. There is no requirement that a single shareholder be unable to bind the directors this way, since the statute specifically contemplates this exact one shareholder scenario. Court approval is not a precondition to a unanimous shareholder agreement taking effect under this rule. The declaration does not need a second shareholder to sign later, because it already qualifies as a unanimous shareholder agreement on its own.
  48. The correct answer is A (The purchaser may rescind the share purchase within 60 days of receiving a complete copy.): A transferee of shares subject to a unanimous shareholder agreement is deemed to be a party to the agreement, but a purchaser for value without notice of the agreement may rescind the share acquisition contract within 60 days after actually receiving a complete copy of the agreement. This means the purchaser does have a real, but time limited, right to unwind the purchase rather than being bound with no recourse at all. There is no rule allowing rescission at any time with no deadline whatsoever, since the 60 day window is a specific and meaningful limit. A court application within seven days is not the mechanism the Act actually provides for this situation.
  49. The correct answer is B (The full consideration received must be added to the appropriate stated capital account.): Because shares are without nominal or par value, a corporation must maintain a separate stated capital account for each class and series of shares it issues, and must add the full amount of the consideration received, as determined by the directors, to the appropriate account. There is no par value account to use instead, since Ontario shares generally do not carry par value at all under the current regime. Leaving the consideration unrecorded anywhere would not comply with this accounting requirement built into the Act. Adding only half of the amount received also falls short of the full amount the statute requires to be credited.
  50. The correct answer is C (The holders have equal rights, including voting rights and rights on dissolution.): Where a corporation has only one class of shares, the rights of the holders are equal in all respects and include the right to vote at all meetings of shareholders and the right to receive the remaining property of the corporation on dissolution. This means both key rights, voting and sharing in property on dissolution, attach together to that single class rather than being split apart. There is no rule allowing directors to informally decide shareholder rights on a year by year basis outside of what the articles establish. Splitting the two rights apart, so that only one applies and not the other, describes a multiple class structure rather than the single class situation described here.
  51. The correct answer is B (The directors must determine the fair value of the past service before issuing the shares.): Where a share is not issued for money, the directors must determine either the fair value of the property or past service, or that it has a fair value not less than the amount of money the corporation would have received had the share been issued for money, before the shares are issued. Leaving the valuation entirely to the consultant would bypass this director responsibility that the Act specifically assigns. An informal agreement between the parties, without the directors making this determination, does not satisfy the statutory requirement. The valuation must occur before issuance rather than being deferred to some later, unspecified date, since a share must not be issued until the consideration is fully paid.
  52. The correct answer is D (A note evidencing the shareholder's own debt is not valid property for shares.): A promissory note or other document evidencing indebtedness of the person to whom shares are to be issued does not constitute property for the purpose of paying for shares under the Act, so a corporation cannot accept a shareholder's own note as full payment. This rule applies regardless of whether the note carries a reasonable interest rate, since the defect lies in the nature of the instrument itself rather than its terms. Adding a third party co-signer to the note is not the mechanism the Act uses to solve this particular problem, and the statute does not create such an exception. Treating the note as automatically valid property would directly contradict the specific statutory exclusion that applies here.
  53. The correct answer is D (The dividend cannot be declared, because the balance sheet based solvency test would be failed.): The directors must not declare, and the corporation must not pay, a dividend if there are reasonable grounds for believing either that the corporation could not pay its liabilities as they become due, or that the realizable value of its assets would be less than the aggregate of its liabilities and stated capital, and this second, balance sheet style test is failed on these facts even though the first test might be satisfied. Focusing only on whether current bills can be paid ignores that the statute sets out two separate grounds, either of which blocks the dividend. Having more than one class of shares is not itself what makes the dividend unlawful here, since the actual problem is the asset and liability comparison. Shareholder approval, even if unanimous, does not override this statutory solvency restriction on what the directors may lawfully declare.
  54. The correct answer is D (The next annual meeting must be held not later than 15 months after the last one.): The directors must call an annual meeting of shareholders not later than 18 months after the corporation comes into existence, and afterwards not later than 15 months after holding the last preceding annual meeting, so the 15 month figure is the one that applies once a first annual meeting has already taken place. The 18 month period is specific to the corporation's very first annual meeting after incorporation, not to later ones. A 12 month deadline understates the actual period the Act allows between annual meetings. There is a real outer deadline that continues to apply after the first annual meeting, contrary to the suggestion that no further deadline exists once that first meeting has occurred.
  55. The correct answer is C (This majority written resolution is valid, but non-signing shareholders must be notified within 10 business days.): For a corporation that is not an offering corporation, a written resolution signed by holders of at least a majority of the shares entitled to vote can satisfy the requirements otherwise met by an ordinary resolution passed at a meeting, but within 10 business days after that majority resolution is signed, the corporation must give written notice of the resolution to the shareholders entitled to vote who did not sign it. This majority route is a genuine alternative to the older rule requiring signatures from every shareholder entitled to vote, so unanimity is not always necessary. Saying no further notice is ever required ignores this specific statutory notice obligation aimed at protecting shareholders who did not sign. There is no requirement to also hold a separate later meeting to approve what the majority written resolution has already validly accomplished.
  56. The correct answer is B (Yes, because a former director of the corporation qualifies as a complainant under the Act.): The statutory definition of complainant expressly includes a director or officer, or a former director or officer, of the corporation or any of its affiliates, so a former director retains standing to bring this kind of application. Limiting the definition to current directors or officers ignores that the former role is specifically preserved for standing purposes. Security holder status is one route to becoming a complainant, but it is not the only route, since former directors are separately included. There is no precondition that the remaining directors already be removed before a complainant can bring the application in the first place.
  57. The correct answer is D (An oppression application, since it targets conduct that unfairly disregards a security holder's interests.): The oppression remedy allows a complainant to apply where the business or affairs of the corporation are being carried on, or the powers of the directors are being exercised, in a manner that is oppressive or unfairly prejudicial to, or that unfairly disregards, the interests of a security holder, which matches this pattern of ongoing conduct without one isolated illegal act. A derivative action is aimed at harm to the corporation itself that is then pursued on the corporation's behalf, not at harm suffered personally by a shareholder. Dissent applications concern a shareholder's right to be bought out following specific fundamental changes, not general dissatisfaction with management. No criminal offence needs to be shown for an oppression application to be available.
  58. The correct answer is C (The court may vary a unanimous shareholder agreement or appoint additional directors as a remedy.): On an oppression application the court may make any order it thinks fit, including regulating the corporation's affairs by amending a unanimous shareholder agreement, appointing directors in place of or in addition to the existing directors, and several other listed remedies aimed at fixing the underlying situation rather than only awarding money. Limiting the court to a damages award ignores this much broader statutory list of available remedies. Winding up the corporation is only one possible remedy among many, not the sole option available on an oppression finding. Forcing a share sale by the majority is not the only structural remedy contemplated, since the court has considerable flexibility to fashion a fitting order.
  59. The correct answer is A (The payment cannot proceed if it would leave the corporation unable to pay its liabilities.): The corporation must not make a payment to a shareholder under the relevant oppression remedy provisions if there are reasonable grounds for believing that the corporation is or would become unable to pay its liabilities as they become due, or that the realizable value of its assets would fall below its liabilities. This means a court ordered payment is still constrained by this solvency style test rather than being automatically enforceable regardless of the corporation's finances. A director vote alone does not satisfy this statutory restriction on the payment. Unanimous shareholder consent is not the mechanism the Act uses to police this kind of payment either.
  60. The correct answer is D (The complainant must give the directors 14 days notice of the intention to apply for leave.): No derivative action may be brought and no intervention may be made unless the complainant has given 14 days notice to the directors of the corporation of the intention to apply, alongside satisfying the good faith and interests of the corporation requirements the court must also be satisfied of. A unanimous shareholder resolution is not a precondition to seeking leave for a derivative action under this provision. Filing a complaint with the Ontario Securities Commission has no bearing on this particular procedural requirement for a derivative action. There is no requirement to wait for the next annual meeting before applying for leave to bring the action.
  61. The correct answer is A (Only the resolution adding the new restriction on share transfers.): A shareholder may dissent and claim the fair value of shares where the corporation resolves to amend its articles to add, remove or change restrictions on the transfer of shares of a class, which is exactly what the share transfer amendment does. An executive bonus increase is an ordinary compensation decision that does not touch the articles or any other listed fundamental change, so it does not trigger the dissent right. Both resolutions cannot trigger the right, because the dissent remedy is tied to a specific list of fundamental changes rather than to every shareholder vote. Neither resolution being covered is also wrong, since the share transfer amendment falls squarely within the listed triggers.
  62. The correct answer is C (A special resolution of the holders of each class entitled to vote.): An amalgamation agreement is adopted only once the shareholders of each amalgamating corporation have approved it by special resolution of the holders of the shares of each class or series entitled to vote on it. A simple majority is the threshold for an ordinary resolution, not the higher threshold the statute sets for an amalgamation. Unanimous written consent is not required, since a special resolution passed at a properly called meeting is sufficient without needing every shareholder to sign. Board approval alone is also not enough, because the statute requires the shareholders themselves to approve the fundamental change before it is adopted.
  63. The correct answer is D (Yes, because the subsidiary is wholly owned by the amalgamating holding corporation.): A holding corporation and one or more of its subsidiaries may amalgamate and continue as one corporation without complying with the usual amalgamation agreement and shareholder approval rules, provided the amalgamation is approved by a resolution of the directors of each amalgamating corporation and all of the issued shares of each amalgamating subsidiary are held by the other amalgamating corporations. That shortcut does not depend on shareholder count, so a fifty shareholder threshold is not the relevant condition here. It is also not limited to public corporations, since the shortcut exists specifically for wholly owned subsidiary structures. Claiming a full special resolution is always required ignores this specific statutory exception.
  64. The correct answer is B (An arrangement approved by special resolution and then by the court.): The statutory definition of an arrangement covers a transfer of all or substantially all the property of a corporation to another body corporate in exchange for securities, money or other property, and once the shareholders approve the arrangement by special resolution, the corporation may apply to the court for an order approving it. An ordinary board approved asset sale does not capture this transaction, since it involves an exchange of securities and money that the statute treats as an arrangement. A voluntary winding up ends the corporation's existence, which is not what this reorganization is trying to achieve. Continuance into another jurisdiction changes where the corporation is incorporated, an entirely different kind of fundamental change.
  65. The correct answer is C (Yes, since an application for revival can be made up to twenty years after dissolution.): An application for revival of a dissolved corporation must not be made more than twenty years after the date of dissolution, so a shareholder applying fifteen years after dissolution is still within the statutory window. The ten year figure understates the real deadline set by the Act. A dissolved corporation is not permanently beyond revival, since the Director has discretion to revive it on the application of an interested person within that twenty year period. The right to apply is also not limited to former directors, because an interested person expressly includes a director, officer and shareholder of the corporation.
  66. The correct answer is A (The property generally remains forfeited unless returned under specific legislation.): Where a corporation dissolved before the relevant forfeiture legislation came into force is revived after the third anniversary of that legislation, or a corporation dissolved on or after that day is revived after the third anniversary of its own dissolution, property that already forfeited to and vested in the Crown remains forfeited and is not returned except as the forfeiture legislation itself provides. Revival does not automatically restore forfeited property, so treating revival and restoration as the same event misstates the rule. An auction is not the statutory mechanism either, since the Act deals with whether the property returns at all. A court order is also not the general route back, since return is governed by the specific legislation.
  67. The correct answer is D (An asset purchase, since liabilities are only assumed if specifically identified.): An asset purchase transfers identified assets and, if agreed, identified liabilities, so the purchaser can choose which obligations to take on and leave existing environmental claims and pending lawsuits with the seller's corporation. A share purchase keeps the target corporation intact along with its existing liabilities, so historical exposure does not simply disappear when the shares change hands. The ease of contract transfer that a share purchase offers is a real advantage of that structure, but it does not serve the purchaser's stated priority of avoiding liabilities. The two structures do not work identically for this purpose, since allocating liability is exactly the feature that distinguishes an asset purchase from a share purchase.
  68. The correct answer is A (A purchase money security interest can be perfected within fifteen days of possession.): A purchase money security interest in equipment other than inventory can be perfected within fifteen days after the debtor obtains possession of the collateral and still have priority, so a search conducted just after the equipment was acquired can miss a purchase money interest that has not yet been registered. The registry's business hours have nothing to do with why a valid interest might not yet appear. There is no minimum dollar threshold for what gets registered on the PPSA registry, so that is not the explanation either. Financing statements are not automatically removed after thirty days, since registrations remain effective for the period stated on the registration itself.
  69. The correct answer is B (A certificate of status from the Ontario Business Registry.): A certificate of status is the search product designed to confirm a corporation's current standing and incorporation details in a form that carries evidentiary weight, which is why a lawyer needing that level of certainty orders it rather than relying only on a profile report. Ordering a second profile report through a different index does not change what a profile report actually confirms, so it would not solve the underlying problem. A notice of change is a filing the corporation itself makes to update its own information, not a search product a purchaser's lawyer orders. An annual return reflects what the corporation filed rather than providing the confirmatory search result the lawyer needs here.
  70. The correct answer is B (No, because its own wording shows the parties did not intend to be bound.): Whether a letter of intent binds depends on its own words, and language stating that it is non-binding, subject to a definitive agreement, and that no obligations arise except for specified items such as confidentiality points strongly toward an unenforceable letter. Ontario has no rule making every signed letter of intent binding, since the common law test looks at intention as shown by the actual wording rather than treating signature alone as decisive. Containing essential commercial terms can support a finding that a letter is binding in other cases, but it does not override the explicit non-binding language used here. Letters of intent are not categorically unenforceable in Ontario, since one showing a genuine intention to be bound can be enforced on its own terms.
  71. The correct answer is A (It is void, since employers are prohibited from entering into non-compete agreements.): The Employment Standards Act prohibits an employer from entering into an employment contract or other agreement with an employee that is, or that includes, a non-compete agreement, which makes that kind of clause void rather than merely restricted. Voluntary signature by the employee does not cure the problem, because the prohibition is a statutory one that does not turn on the employee's consent. A one year time limit does not save the clause either, since the statute is a prohibition on this category of agreement rather than a rule about permissible duration. Extra compensation likewise does not make the clause enforceable, because the Act bars the agreement itself rather than requiring an additional payment to justify it.
  72. The correct answer is B (Value must be given and a signed agreement must describe the collateral.): An escrow or holdback securing a seller's indemnity is a security interest in personal property, and attachment requires that value be given, that the debtor have rights in the collateral, and that one of the statutory attachment acts occur, most commonly a signed security agreement with a description of the collateral sufficient to identify it. Simply labelling the holdback as security in the closing documents does not by itself satisfy the attachment requirements if the underlying signed description is missing. Registering anything with the land registry has nothing to do with personal property held in escrow. Physical delivery of the funds by the seller is also not what attachment requires, since the funds are typically already held by an escrow agent.
  73. The correct answer is C (Section 73.3 of the Ontario Securities Act, not National Instrument 45-106.): National Instrument 45-106 itself states that its general accredited investor exemption does not apply in Ontario, and in Ontario the accredited investor exemption is instead found in section 73.3 of the Securities Act. Treating the National Instrument section as the operative provision for an Ontario trade would misstate where the exemption actually comes from in this province.
