There is no standalone Ontario regulation for this exemption separate from the Securities Act itself, so that option overstates the disconnect from the national instrument framework. Section 2.9 is a different exemption entirely, the offering memorandum exemption, and confusing the two would lead a lawyer to rely on the wrong provision.
  74. The correct answer is A (For eight years counted from the date of the distribution.): Where an accredited investor relies on the income, net assets or financial assets tests, the person distributing the security must obtain the signed risk acknowledgement and retain it for eight years after the distribution, not two years. There is no indefinite retention obligation tied to how long the dealer stays registered, since the statute sets a fixed eight year period independent of the dealer's own registration status. Retention is also not tied to the investor's own account review cycle, because the obligation runs from the date of the distribution itself rather than from any later event involving the investor's account.
  75. The correct answer is D (A risk acknowledgement signed by the purchaser and an executive officer.): The family, friends and business associates exemption in Ontario requires the person making the distribution to obtain a risk acknowledgement signed by the purchaser and by an executive officer of the issuer other than the purchaser, along with a signature from the specifically related person depending on which relationship is relied on. A prospectus is exactly what this exemption is designed to avoid, so requiring one would defeat the purpose of relying on it. Approval from every other shareholder is not a condition of this exemption, since it turns on the purchaser's relationship and the risk acknowledgement. There is no minimum holding period built into this particular exemption, since holding period rules are a separate resale concept.
  76. The correct answer is B ($10,000 in acquisition cost across the preceding twelve months.): Under the offering memorandum exemption, a purchaser who is not an eligible investor is capped at $10,000 in acquisition cost for securities acquired under that exemption in the preceding twelve months. The $30,000 figure applies to a purchaser who does qualify as an eligible investor, a higher category than the purchaser described here. The $100,000 figure applies only where an eligible investor has also received advice from a portfolio manager, investment dealer or exempt market dealer that a larger investment is suitable, neither of which is true in this scenario. Delivering the offering memorandum does not remove the dollar limit, since the cap protects a purchaser who is not an eligible investor.
  77. The correct answer is D (No, because beneficial ownership must be limited to no more than fifty persons.): A private issuer must have securities, other than non-convertible debt securities, that are beneficially owned by not more than fifty persons, not counting employees and former employees, so fifty two non-employee beneficial owners already exceeds that limit. The fifty person cap counts everyone who is not an employee or former employee, so it is not limited to current employees as one wrong option suggests. Transfer restrictions are a necessary part of the private issuer definition, but they are not sufficient on their own, since the beneficial ownership limit is a separate condition that must also be met. Transfer restrictions are in fact required for a private issuer, so claiming a private issuer can have none of them gets the definition backwards.
  78. The correct answer is B (No later than ten days after the distribution.): An issuer or underwriter must file the report of exempt distribution in the jurisdiction where the distribution takes place no later than ten days after the distribution, using Form 45-106F1 in most jurisdictions. The thirty day period applies to a different category of distribution under a separate instrument, not to an ordinary accredited investor distribution reported under this rule. Sixty days is not a deadline that applies to this filing. There is no annual filing option for an ordinary single trade like this one, since the annual filing route is reserved for investment funds relying on certain specific exemptions, which is not the situation here.
  79. The correct answer is B (Yes, since all three distributions occurred within a ten day period.): A single Form 45-106F1 may cover distributions made on multiple dates as long as those distributions occur within a ten day period, and here all three distributions fall within an eight day span, so one combined report is permitted. Filing three separate reports is not required in this situation, since the rule specifically allows combining reports that fall within the ten day window. Whether the investors sign one combined subscription agreement is unrelated to whether the issuer can combine its regulatory filing. Combining reports is not barred outright, because the ten day window rule exists precisely to permit this kind of consolidated filing.
  80. The correct answer is C (No, because no signed agreement describing the collateral was ever created.): Attachment requires that value be given, the debtor have rights in the collateral, and one of the statutory attachment events occur, most commonly a signed security agreement containing a description of the collateral sufficient to identify it, and an oral description does not satisfy that written and signed requirement. The borrower already owning the equipment satisfies only one of the three elements needed for attachment, so that fact alone does not complete attachment. Giving value likewise satisfies only one element, and all three must be present together for the security interest to attach. An oral description might be a genuine belief on the lender's part, but the statute requires a signed writing to establish that element.
  81. The correct answer is A (Report the suspicious pattern to the purchaser or the lender, as applicable.): The Law Society's due diligence guidance for real estate transactions directs the lawyer to retain notes on the title search and to report the results, including suspicious patterns of transfers or value changes, to the purchaser or borrower if acting for them, to the lender if acting for the lender, and to the title insurer. Ignoring the pattern, staying silent unless asked, and cancelling the file without any discussion all fall short of that reporting duty.
  82. The correct answer is B (Within 25 days after the last day of the reported month.): By-Law 9, section 22(2) requires the monthly reconciliation and comparison record to be created within 25 days after the last day of the month it reports, which the Law Society's own guide confirms with a worked example. A 30 day window, the last business day of the same month, and a quarterly 45 day window are all different deadlines from the one the by law actually sets.
  83. The correct answer is A (Within 30 days of opening the new trust account.): By-Law 9, section 7(8)(a), as explained in the Law Society's trust reporting guide, requires the irrevocable written authorization for a newly opened trust account to be submitted within 30 days of the account being opened. The 25 day figure belongs to the separate monthly reconciliation deadline, the March 31 date applies to existing accounts and the Annual Report filing, and leaving the timing open ended misstates the fixed deadline the by law sets.
  84. The correct answer is C (Disclosure must be reasonably necessary and must not compromise privilege.): Rule 3.3-7 permits disclosure of confidential information only to the extent reasonably necessary to detect and resolve conflicts arising from a change of employment, and only where the disclosure does not compromise solicitor client privilege or otherwise prejudice the client. Disclosing whatever a managing partner requests, refusing any disclosure at all, and copying full files by mutual agreement each go beyond, or fall short of, that limited and conditional permission.
  85. The correct answer is A (The lawyer generally shall not testify unless a listed exception permits it.): Rule 5.2-1 provides that a lawyer who appears as advocate shall not testify or submit the lawyer's own affidavit evidence before the tribunal unless permitted by law, the tribunal, the applicable rules of procedure, or unless the matter is purely formal or uncontroverted. Treating the lawyer's own competency as a witness as enough on its own, or treating the lawyer as bound to testify whenever asked, ignores that the rule presumes the opposite, and a witness fee has no bearing on whether the rule applies.
Case profes_08

A client submitted a complaint against her lawyer through LSO Connects, alleging the lawyer misapplied trust funds meant for a real estate deposit. Two weeks later, while gathering records for the investigation, the lawyer discovers a separate problem: a client's cheque that had been deposited to trust and disbursed was returned days later marked non sufficient funds, leaving the trust account short by $6,200. The client also believes the lawyer overcharged her for the closing and wants the Law Society to reduce the bill. The complaint file is eventually closed, and the client is told she may ask for a review.

  1. The correct answer is B (Receipt is acknowledged within 48 hours, and the file stays confidential.): The Law Society generally acknowledges receipt of a complaint within about 48 hours, although volume can delay that, and complaints and investigations are otherwise kept confidential unless a public regulatory proceeding results. Publishing the lawyer's name at the outset, treating the complaint as a public court record, and requiring an in person hearing within 48 hours all overstate how the process actually begins.
  2. The correct answer is A (Replace the shortfall at once and report the misapplication through LSO Connects.): A lawyer who discovers a trust shortfall must replenish it immediately from the lawyer's own money and report the misapplication of trust funds to the Law Society through LSO Connects, and this obligation does not depend on whether an unrelated complaint is already under review. Waiting for the other matter to finish leaves client money exposed, borrowing from another client's ledger creates a second shortfall, and staying silent unless asked is not permitted reporting.
  3. The correct answer is B (The Law Society will not reduce the bill and may refer her to the Assessment Office.): The Law Society states plainly that it does not set fees and cannot reduce a bill a client believes is too high, and instead may refer the person to the Assessment Office of the Superior Court of Justice for that separate process. Ordering a refund, setting a fee cap for future work, and forwarding the dispute to an insurer are not remedies the complaints process provides for a fee disagreement of this kind.
  4. The correct answer is A (Request review through LSO Connects within 60 days of being told.): Once a complaint file is closed and the Law Society has advised that a review may be requested, the complainant must make that request through LSO Connects within 60 days of being notified. Filing a fresh complaint on the same facts, waiting a year to apply to the Tribunal, and needing the lawyer's own consent are not part of how a Commissioner review is actually requested.
  5. The correct answer is A (No, since the referring lawyer's own conflict caused the referral.): Rule 3.6-6.1 permits a referral fee only where several conditions are all met together, and one of those conditions is that the referral was not made because the referring licensee had a conflict of interest. A signed agreement, no increase in the client's total fee, and the receiving lawyer's genuine expertise are each part of the wider test, but none of them overcomes a referral that was prompted by the referring lawyer's own conflict.
  6. The correct answer is A (A signed written agreement, in the Law Society's form, confirming it was optional.): Rule 3.6-6.0 defines a referral agreement as a signed written agreement between the referring licensee, the receiving licensee and the client, in the Law Society's own form, confirming the client was advised of no obligation to accept the referral. An informal email, a purely verbal understanding, and a sworn affidavit between only the two lawyers each fall short of that three party signed written form.
  7. The correct answer is B (No, rewarding a non-licensee for referrals is barred, directly or indirectly.): Rule 3.6-7 forbids a lawyer from giving any financial or other reward to a non-licensee for the referral of clients, and rule 3.6-7.1 forbids doing indirectly what is prohibited directly, so client disclosure or a modest amount does not cure the problem. A mortgage broker is not a licensed lawyer or paralegal, so the fee sharing rules that apply between licensees do not extend to that broker.
  8. The correct answer is A (It has two divisions, a Hearing Division and an Appeal Division.): Section 49.20.1 of the Law Society Act establishes the Law Society Tribunal with two divisions, the Hearing Division and the Appeal Division, along with a chair and any temporary members. A single combined division, three separate profession based divisions, and treating the Tribunal as merely an informal bencher committee all misdescribe the statutory structure the Act sets out.
  9. The correct answer is D (An audit of records, which needs no suspicion of misconduct first.): Section 49.2 allows the Law Society to audit a licensee's financial records to check compliance with the by laws, while section 49.3 allows an investigation into conduct only once the Society has received information suggesting possible professional misconduct. Treating the two powers as identical, calling this a discipline hearing, and describing it as a Tribunal appeal all confuse the audit power with the separate conduct based processes.
  10. The correct answer is B (It continues at all times, without an end point tied to the retainer.): Rule 3.3-1 requires a lawyer to hold client information in strict confidence at all times, and that wording means the duty is not limited to the length of the retainer and continues after the file closes and the bill is paid. A fixed two year cutoff and tying confidentiality to a client's limitation period both invent an end point that the rule does not actually contain.
  11. The correct answer is B (Disclose only what is necessary to prevent the death or serious bodily harm.): Rule 3.3-3 permits a lawyer to disclose confidential information, but not more than is required, where the lawyer believes on reasonable grounds there is an imminent risk of death or serious bodily harm and disclosure is necessary to prevent it. Staying completely silent ignores the permitted exception, publishing full notes goes far beyond what is necessary, and a quiet withdrawal does nothing to address the imminent risk itself.
  12. The correct answer is C (Try to dissuade the client, but still produce the report if asked.): Where a report might cause harm if disclosed, the lawyer must attempt to dissuade the client from seeing it, but must still produce the report once the client insists, and should suggest the client review it with the physician who prepared it. Refusing outright ignores the client's right to their own information, handing it over without any discussion skips the required caution, and destroying the report is not an option the rule contemplates at all.
  13. The correct answer is B (No, contingency fee agreements are prohibited in family law matters.): Both the Solicitors Act and rule 3.6-2 state that a solicitor shall not enter into a contingency fee agreement in respect of a family law matter, alongside the same prohibition for criminal and quasi-criminal proceedings. Court approval, staying under the percentage cap, and comparing a divorce file to ordinary civil litigation are all irrelevant, since family law matters are excluded from contingency billing outright.
  14. The correct answer is D (Jointly apply to the Superior Court of Justice for its approval.): Section 28.1 of the Solicitors Act allows a solicitor to exceed the prescribed percentage cap only where the solicitor and client jointly apply to the Superior Court of Justice within 90 days after the agreement is executed and the court approves it, failing which the agreement is not enforceable. Filing with the Law Society, a second lawyer's signature, and simple lapse of time are not the approval mechanism the Act actually requires.
  15. The correct answer is A (The Law Society does not set fees and cannot reduce a client's bill.): The Law Society states plainly that it does not set fees for legal services and cannot reduce a bill the client thinks is too high, and that it may instead refer the person to the Assessment Office of the Superior Court of Justice. Simply filing a written complaint does not trigger a fee reduction by the Law Society, since that is not a power the Society exercises over billing disputes of this kind. The lawyer's agreement to a reduction is a private matter between the parties and is not how the Law Society's own limited role in fee disputes works. There is no ninety day rule under which the Law Society automatically reduces a bill on its own initiative.
  16. The correct answer is D (The Law Society may refer the person to the Assessment Office of the Superior Court.): The Law Society's own guidance explains that while it cannot set fees or reduce a bill itself, it may refer the person to the Assessment Office of the Superior Court of Justice, which is the body that actually examines whether a bill is fair through the statutory assessment process. There is no internal Law Society auditor role described for reviewing the substantive fairness of a lawyer's bill. The Law Society does not order the lawyer and client to negotiate directly as its stated response to this kind of complaint. Simply closing the file with no direction at all does not match the guidance, which specifically points the client toward the Assessment Office.
  17. The correct answer is D (No, payment does not bar assessment if the special circumstances of the case require it.): The Solicitors Act provides that payment of a bill does not prevent the court from referring it for assessment if the special circumstances of the case require it, so the client's earlier payment does not automatically close off this avenue. It is therefore incorrect to say payment always permanently bars a later assessment in every case. The method of payment, such as using a certified cheque instead of cash, has no bearing on whether an assessment remains available afterward. The lawyer's personal agreement is not the legal mechanism that allows an assessment after payment, since the statute ties the possibility to the special circumstances of the case rather than to the lawyer's consent.
  18. The correct answer is B (Account for all funds held or previously dealt with, and refund any remuneration not earned.): Rule 3.7-9 requires a lawyer, on discharge or withdrawal, to account for all funds of the client then held or previously dealt with, including refunding any remuneration not earned, which covers both the unused advance for disbursements and the portion of fees for work that was billed but never actually completed. Keeping the full advance simply because the file ended earlier than planned is not consistent with the rule's requirement to refund unearned amounts. There is no requirement that the client first retain successor counsel before the withdrawing lawyer must account for and refund unearned money. Applying client funds toward the lawyer's own professional dues has nothing to do with the accounting and refund obligation the rule imposes.
  19. The correct answer is B (The lawyer must refund the portion of the retainer that represents unearned remuneration.): Rule 3.7-9 requires a discharged lawyer to account for all client funds held or previously dealt with and to refund any remuneration not earned, so the lawyer must return the portion of the lump sum retainer that corresponds to litigation work never actually performed. The client's decision to discharge the lawyer does not change this refund obligation, since the rule applies regardless of which party initiated the end of the retainer. There is no discharge administration fee recognized under the rule that would let the lawyer keep the unearned portion. The unearned funds belong to the client, not to the successor lawyer directly, so they must be accounted for and refunded to the client rather than transferred elsewhere.
  20. The correct answer is B (A Form 9D Report on Investment, delivered to each private lender.): By-Law 9, s.24 requires the licensee acting for a private lender who holds a mortgage or charge in trust to prepare a written report on the investment, using Form 9D Report on Investment, and to deliver a copy to each lender, which applies here even though the mortgage has now been repaid. Form 9C Investment Authority is a different document, signed before an advance is made, rather than a report generated in connection with events such as repayment. Nothing in the by-law calls for a letter of apology regardless of how the investment actually performed. A tax receipt from the firm is not the document By-Law 9 requires in connection with reporting on a private lender's mortgage investment.
  21. The correct answer is A (Full name, business and home address and telephone number, and occupation.): Section 23(1) of By-Law 7.1 requires the licensee to obtain, for an individual client, the client's full name, business address and business telephone number if applicable, home address and home telephone number, and occupation or occupations. Collecting only the client's name without any address or occupation information falls well short of what the section actually requires. Obtaining only a home address, with no name or occupation, leaves out most of the information the by-law calls for. Obtaining only occupation information, without name or address, likewise fails to meet the basic identification requirement that applies to every retainer.
  22. The correct answer is B (Obtain the same identification information about the third party as about the client.): Section 23(1) of By-Law 7.1 provides that if the client is acting for or representing a third party, the licensee must obtain the same identification information about the third party that would otherwise be obtained about the client. Limiting identification to the client alone would miss the specific extension the by-law builds in for exactly this situation, where someone is acting on behalf of another person. Skipping identification of the actual client while identifying only the third party goes too far in the opposite direction and is not what the by-law requires. Treating this as requiring no additional identification at all ignores the by-law's specific rule for a client acting for or representing someone else.
  23. The correct answer is C (Names and addresses of anyone owning twenty five percent or more of the organization.): Section 23(2.1) of By-Law 7.1 requires the licensee to make reasonable efforts to obtain the names of each director, and the names and addresses of persons owning directly or indirectly twenty five percent or more of the organization or its shares, along with information about trustees, beneficiaries, settlors and ownership structure. A list of every employee the organization has ever hired goes far beyond what the by-law asks the licensee to gather about ownership and control. The organization's outside auditors and accountants are not among the categories of information section 23(2.1) requires the licensee to pursue. A list of every customer the organization has ever dealt with likewise has nothing to do with the ownership and control information the section calls for.
  24. The correct answer is C (No, an exemption applies where the licensee acts as agent for a licensee who already complied.): Section 22(2) of By-Law 7.1 exempts a licensee from the section 23 identification and verification requirements where the licensee is acting as agent for another licensee, or for a lawyer, who has already complied, which covers this exact situation and avoids duplicating work already properly done. Requiring every licensee who ever touches the file to independently repeat the full process ignores this specific exemption the by-law provides. Whether the agent licensee is being paid at a reduced rate has no bearing on whether the exemption applies. The exemption does not depend on the client asking for it, since it is a structural exemption built into the by-law itself.
  25. The correct answer is C (No, funds received from a financial institution are exempt from that additional identification step.): Section 22(3) of By-Law 7.1 exempts funds paid to or received from a financial institution, among other listed categories, from the additional identification requirements under sections 23(2) and 23(2.1) and the verification requirement under section 23(4), which covers this receipt of funds directly from a Canadian bank. It is not correct that every receipt of funds in any circumstance triggers the additional identification step, since the by-law specifically carves out certain sources such as financial institutions. The exemption does not depend on the dollar amount involved, so a threshold under one thousand dollars is not the basis for it. Personal familiarity between the licensee and a particular bank employee is not what triggers or removes the exemption under the by-law.
  26. The correct answer is D (Identify the senior managing officer, assess consistency with the retainer, and record the results.): Section 23(2.2) of By-Law 7.1 requires that where the licensee cannot obtain the ownership and control information or confirm its accuracy, the licensee must take reasonable measures to identify the organization's most senior managing officer, assess whether the client's information, source of funds and instructions are consistent with the purpose of the retainer, record the results and the date, and assess and record the risk of assisting fraud or illegal conduct. Simply declining the retainer immediately is not the step-by-step process section 23(2.2) actually prescribes. Ignoring the missing information and proceeding as though nothing unusual occurred is inconsistent with the specific fallback obligations the section imposes. There is no requirement in section 23(2.2) that the licensee report the situation directly to the police before completing these internal identification and risk assessment steps.
  27. The correct answer is A (At least six years following completion of the work for which the licensee was retained.): By-Law 7.1 requires the licensee to retain the client identification information and verification documents for the longer of the duration of the licensee and client relationship and as long as necessary to provide service to the client, and at least six years following completion of the work for which the licensee was retained. A two year period understates the six year minimum the by-law actually sets for this record once the relationship and service needs no longer apply. A one year period falls even further short of the required minimum retention period. A twenty year period substantially overstates what the by-law itself requires for this particular identification record.

Practice Test 7 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Not earlier than the fourteenth day after the notice of termination is given to the tenant.): Under section 59 of the Residential Tenancies Act, a notice of termination for non-payment of rent must set a termination date not earlier than the fourteenth day after the notice is given, for a tenancy other than a daily or weekly tenancy. The seven day period applies only to a daily or weekly tenancy, which nothing in these facts indicates. A twenty day period belongs to a different kind of notice entirely, such as one dealing with damage or interference with enjoyment, and a sixty day period is used for grounds like persistent late payment rather than a single month of unpaid rent.
  2. The correct answer is C (Not earlier than the tenth day after the notice of termination is given to the tenant.): A landlord may terminate a tenancy for an illegal act such as an illegal trade, business or occupation in the unit or complex, and for the listed drug related cases the notice must set a termination date not earlier than the tenth day after the notice is given, with longer periods applying to other kinds of illegal acts described in the section. Thirty and sixty day periods both overstate the minimum period for this specific drug related ground, and a full year has no basis in the Act for a notice of this kind.
  3. The correct answer is B (Claim the refund only for the qualifying buyer's half interest in the property.): Where only one of two purchasers on title qualifies, the refund is reduced to the share of the interest acquired by the qualifying purchaser, here half the interest. The Alberta ownership disqualifies the other buyer personally, and that disqualification cannot be undone by a waiver. The refund is not available for the whole property simply because the couple is buying together, and it is not denied outright, because one buyer does qualify.
  4. The correct answer is D (Two separate taxes apply, an Ontario tax and a Toronto municipal tax.): A purchase of property in the City of Toronto attracts two separate land transfer taxes, the Ontario tax under the Land Transfer Tax Act and a distinct municipal tax imposed under Chapter 760 of the Toronto Municipal Code. Both must be calculated and paid, so the client needs to budget for both amounts. Toronto has not replaced the provincial tax, and although both are declared through the same electronic system, they remain two separate calculations rather than one merged figure.
  5. The correct answer is B (The Non Resident Speculation Tax at a rate of 25 per cent.): A 25 per cent Non Resident Speculation Tax applies in addition to the general Ontario tax when designated land in the specified region is conveyed to a foreign entity or a taxable trustee. There is no separate federal surtax collected through the provincial land transfer return, and no municipal surcharge tied to citizenship exists under this scheme. The tax is not limited to condominiums and applies to the kind of residential land described.
  6. The correct answer is A (A signed Document Registration Agreement setting out the parties' escrow obligations.): Because purchase funds and documents cannot move electronically among the parties until registration, an escrow closing procedure is recommended, and the Document Registration Agreement is the standardized agreement that governs the parties' relationship until documents and funds have all been exchanged and registration is effected. An oral understanding does not give the same protection as the written escrow terms the profession has adopted. Signing for completeness only addresses the registration step itself, and a lender guarantee does not replace the escrow framework between the two lawyers.
  7. The correct answer is A (The Electronic Trust Transfer Requisition, Form 9c, for closing funds.): The Law Society maintains a standard Form 9c, the Electronic Trust Transfer Requisition for closing funds, as the document used to move closing funds electronically from a lawyer's trust account. A paper cheque and a verbal instruction do not satisfy the requirements for an electronic trust transfer under By-Law 9. An email about the deposit alone does not address the balance of the closing funds that must also move on closing day.
  8. The correct answer is B (No, review and signing of a requisition letter cannot be delegated to staff.): The Law Society's residential real estate guidelines list review and signing of a letter of requisition among the tasks that cannot be delegated to a non lawyer, because the lawyer bears responsibility for what is requisitioned. Training or experience does not change this requirement, and no course substitutes for the lawyer's own review. The reason is not that the other side must approve it first, since the requisition is the purchaser's own document addressed to the seller.
  9. The correct answer is D (Follow up under the firm's undertaking system and press for the discharge.): The Law Society's guidelines require a lawyer to maintain a system to follow up on outstanding undertakings, including those about mortgage discharges, so the purchaser's lawyer must actively pursue the seller's lawyer for compliance. An undertaking does not simply expire after a fixed period, and it remains an obligation the giving lawyer owes personally. Refinancing lenders will not accept a new mortgage behind an unregistered discharge, and it is the lawyer's professional responsibility to chase the outstanding undertaking rather than leaving the client to do so.
  10. The correct answer is A (The client's written authorization to register the electronic documents.): The Law Society's guidelines require lawyers to obtain and retain in their files the client's written authorization before registering electronic documents. The fact that an electronic document itself needs no signature under the Land Registration Reform Act does not remove the lawyer's own duty to get written client authorization first. Confirming the other lawyer received funds addresses a different closing step, and the original agreement of purchase and sale alone does not authorize the specific act of registration.
  11. The correct answer is C (No, only a lawyer can sign for completeness a document requiring law statements.): In a transaction using electronic registration, only a lawyer can sign for completeness any document requiring compliance with law statements, because those statements attest to matters within the lawyer's own knowledge and responsibility. Reviewing the document the next morning does not cure signing it before that review, and the act is not merely administrative given what it certifies. Years of clerking experience do not confer the authority to sign for completeness on a law statement document.
  12. The correct answer is B (No, the exception needs no other lawyer within 200 kilometres, not mere distance alone.): The remote location exception applies only where no other real estate lawyer practises within 200 kilometres and the clients cannot meet remotely, and 60 kilometres does not meet that distance threshold. Written agreement between the clients is relevant to the joint retainer rules generally, but it does not create the remote location exception on its own where the distance test is not met. The exception has not been repealed, it simply is not available on these facts.
  13. The correct answer is D (Confirm whether it is a matrimonial home and obtain the spouse's consent.): Because the seller and spouse currently reside together in the property, it may be a matrimonial home under the Family Law Act, so no spouse may dispose of an interest in it without the other spouse joining in the instrument or consenting to the transaction. Registration on title alone does not satisfy this requirement, since the right belongs to both spouses regardless of ownership. A marriage certificate or a valuation letter does not substitute for the consent or joinder itself.
  14. The correct answer is C (Both spouses must designate the condominium and register the designation on title.): One or both spouses may designate property as a matrimonial home in the prescribed form, and where both spouses register a joint designation, other property that would otherwise be a matrimonial home ceases to be one. Without that registered designation, both the condominium and the cottage could remain matrimonial homes since each is occupied by the family as a residence. A tax filing about principal residence has no effect under the Family Law Act, and a court application to abandon the cottage is not how the Act addresses this situation.
  15. The correct answer is A (No, a purchaser for value in good faith without notice is protected here.): The transaction may be set aside on application under the Family Law Act unless the person who acquired the interest did so for value, in good faith and without notice that the property was a matrimonial home, and those facts are met here. The protection does not depend on whether the purchaser is a corporation or an individual. There is no requirement that the purchaser personally interview a spouse, because a properly made spousal status statement is deemed sufficient proof unless the purchaser had notice to the contrary.
  16. The correct answer is D (Exercise caution, given the fraud risk, and follow the joint retainer rules.): The Law Society directs lawyers to exercise extreme caution when asked to act for both a borrower and a private lender because of the increased risk of fraud and exploitation, while still assessing whether the joint retainer is prudent and complying with the applicable rules. Acting for both is not automatically forbidden, but it is also not exempt from scrutiny simply because the loan is private. A waiver signed by only one party does not address the duty of candour owed to the other client.
  17. The correct answer is D (The principal amount and interest rate, calculated yearly or half yearly.): Where a mortgage involves blended payments of principal and interest, no interest is chargeable, payable or recoverable at all unless the mortgage contains a statement showing the principal amount and the rate of interest, calculated yearly or half yearly and not in advance. Stating only the number of payments does not satisfy this requirement, and no bank guarantee is needed for a private loan. A privately agreed rate is not automatically enforceable if the mortgage omits the statement the Interest Act requires.
  18. The correct answer is B (Pay the principal and interest due, plus three months interest instead of notice.): Where principal or interest is not payable until more than five years after the date of the mortgage, after those five years the person entitled to redeem may pay the principal and interest then due plus three months further interest in lieu of notice, and no further interest is chargeable after that. The rule does exactly the opposite of forcing the borrower to wait the full term, since it lets early payment happen. There is no right to force an extension, and the section applies to freehold and leasehold property generally, but it specifically does not apply to mortgages given by corporations.
  19. The correct answer is C (At least 15 days of default, then 35 days from notice to sale.): Where the mortgage itself confers a power of sale on a stated default, the notice of exercising that power may not be given until the default has continued for at least 15 days, and the sale may not occur until at least 35 days after the notice. The 45 day period instead applies to the statutory power of sale under Part III of the Act where the mortgage does not already contain its own power of sale, so it is not the period that governs here. A power of sale clause does not eliminate the separate default and notice periods that must both run.
  20. The correct answer is B (Within 15 days after receiving the mortgagor's written request for the statement.): Before a sale or an enforcement action, a mortgagor may require a written statement of the amount in default, and the mortgagee must answer within 15 days. If the mortgagee fails to answer, or answers incompletely or incorrectly without a reasonable excuse, the mortgagee's enforcement rights are suspended until it complies. The other periods listed apply to different steps in the mortgage remedies process, not to this statement request.
  21. The correct answer is D (An N13 notice, with a termination date of at least 120 days.): A landlord may give notice where possession is required to demolish, convert, or do repairs or renovations that require a building permit and vacant possession, and that notice, an N13, must give a termination date at least 120 days after the notice. An N12 addresses the landlord's own personal occupation rather than renovation work needing a permit, so it does not fit these facts. An N8 addresses persistent late payment, which is not mentioned here, and becoming the registered owner does not remove the requirement to give proper notice to existing tenants.
  22. The correct answer is A (A termination date at least 60 days after the notice, under section 58.): A landlord may give notice where the tenant has persistently failed to pay rent on the date it becomes due, and the termination date must be at least the 60 days set out in section 58, ending on the last day of a rental period or the end of the term. The 14 day period belongs to the N4 notice for actual non payment of rent, not to a persistent late payment pattern. The 20 day period with a 7 day correction applies to damage or interference notices, not to this ground.
  23. The correct answer is B (A termination date at least 20 days after the notice, void if repaired within 7 days.): For wilful or negligent undue damage, the notice must give a termination date not earlier than the 20th day after the notice, and it is void if the tenant repairs or replaces the damaged property, or pays the reasonable cost, within seven days. The 90 day and 60 day figures apply to other kinds of notices under the Act, not to damage. Written apologies are not what makes an N5 damage notice void, only actual repair, replacement or payment within the correction period.
  24. The correct answer is B (The board has up to 120 days to review and propose a plan.): Within 120 days of receiving a reserve fund study the board must review it and propose a funding plan, then send owners notice of the plan, and the plan is implemented only after a further 30 days following that notice, so nothing has yet been decided within three weeks. A special assessment is therefore possible but not guaranteed on this timeline. Status certificates in fact must disclose current plans to increase the reserve fund and any recent assessments, so the buyer's lawyer should review the certificate rather than assume no disclosure exists.
  25. The correct answer is C (Tax is payable within 30 days of the assignment, as a beneficial interest disposition.): An assignment of an agreement of purchase and sale for consideration is a disposition of a beneficial interest in land, and tax on such a disposition is payable on the thirtieth day after the disposition by the person who acquires or whose beneficial interest is increased. Waiting for the later registered transfer misses this separate obligation on the assignment itself. The tax is not conditioned on the assignment price exceeding the original price, and it is not due immediately in cash with no time allowed.
  26. The correct answer is C (The Year-End Form, since the Mid-Year Form's window to add new items has already closed.): Tarion's first-year warranty claim process uses two forms in sequence, a 30-Day Form that can list items found in the first 30 days of possession, with a further 10-day grace period, and a Year-End Form submitted during the last 30 days of the first year that lists all outstanding warranty items to date, including any item first discovered after the 30-Day Form window closed. Eight months after possession falls well after the 30-Day Form window has closed and before the Year-End Form is due, so the buyer's lawyer should advise the client to document the defect and include it on the Year-End Form when that window opens. The Major Structural Defect Form applies only to a major structural defect and only after the second year, not to an ordinary finish defect reported within the first year. No form is required until the seventh anniversary is wrong because that describes the major structural defect warranty period, not the ordinary first-year process that applies here.
  27. The correct answer is C (The certificate binds the corporation as against the purchaser regarding the information it contained.): The status certificate binds the corporation, as of the date it is given or deemed given, with respect to the information it contains, as against a purchaser who relies on it, which is exactly the protection this client obtained by closing on the strength of the certificate. It is incorrect to say the certificate stops mattering once the sale closes, since its binding effect is precisely what protects a purchaser after closing. There is no requirement that the purchaser personally attend any board meeting, and the certificate binds the corporation itself rather than individual board members personally.
  28. The correct answer is B (No statutory rescission right applies here, since that right is limited to purchases from a declarant.): The ten day statutory rescission right applies to a purchaser who receives a disclosure statement and the condominium guide on a purchase of a new or proposed unit from a declarant, and the Condominium Act gives no equivalent statutory right to a buyer purchasing an existing unit from an individual owner. It is a common misconception that every condominium sale carries an automatic cooling off period, but that is simply not what the Act provides for a resale transaction. Whether the purchase is for investment purposes or falls under a particular price does not create a rescission right that the Act does not grant.
  29. The correct answer is C (The ten days run from the latest of the listed triggering events, before accepting a registerable deed.): To rescind, the purchaser or the purchaser's solicitor must give written notice of rescission within ten days of the latest of the listed events, and the right must be exercised before the purchaser accepts a deed in registerable form. It is incorrect to say the clock only ever runs from the first disclosure statement, since a later triggering event such as a material change amendment can restart or extend the relevant ten day window. There is no rule allowing rescission up to a full year after closing, and the right must be exercised before, not after, accepting a registerable deed.
  30. The correct answer is B (Whether the property is or was ordinarily occupied by both spouses as their family residence.): Under the Family Law Act, every property in which a person has an interest that is, or at separation was, ordinarily occupied by that person and the person's spouse as their family residence is their matrimonial home, so joint ownership is not the test. It does not matter whether the property was purchased before or after the marriage, since the definition turns on occupation as the family residence rather than the timing of acquisition. Whether the mortgage is held jointly is also irrelevant to whether the house qualifies as a matrimonial home under the statute.
  31. The correct answer is A (The other spouse must join in the transfer or otherwise consent under the statute.): A spouse cannot dispose of or encumber an interest in a matrimonial home unless the other spouse joins in the instrument or consents to the transaction, or one of the other listed circumstances such as a court order or a release applies. Sole ownership on title does not remove this requirement, since the protection exists specifically to give the non-owning spouse a say regardless of how title is registered. There is no rule requiring an advance payment of half the sale price, and the length of the marriage has no bearing on whether consent is required.
  32. The correct answer is B (The purchaser is likely protected because they acquired the interest for value, in good faith and without notice.): A transaction made without the required spousal consent may be set aside, but that remedy does not apply against a person who acquired the interest for value, in good faith and without notice that the property was a matrimonial home, which is exactly the purchaser's position here. It is not true that any sale of a matrimonial home without consent is automatically void against everyone, since the statute expressly protects an innocent purchaser in the circumstances described. There is no requirement that the purchaser personally interview the spouse, and the length of the transferor's occupancy is not the relevant test for the purchaser's protection.
  33. The correct answer is B (Because a statement of spousal status under the Family Law Act is a required part of the transfer.): Every electronic transfer must contain a statement of spousal status under the Family Law Act, made by the transferor unless the transferor is a corporation, which is why this statement appears as a standard part of nearly every individual transferor's transfer. The statement is required precisely because the transferor is an individual, not only when the transferor happens to be a corporation, since corporations are the exception rather than the rule for this requirement. The statement relates to matrimonial home protection rather than to any federal income tax reporting purpose, and it is a mandatory element of the transfer, not an optional courtesy the lawyer includes on request.
  34. The correct answer is A (A separation agreement in which the other spouse released rights under Part II of the statute.): Among the circumstances that satisfy the consent requirement is a separation agreement by which the other spouse has released all rights under Part II of the Family Law Act, which is a recognized alternative to the other spouse joining in the instrument. A verbal assurance or an informal text message does not provide the kind of documented evidence the lawyer needs to support the compliance with law statement being registered. It is also incorrect that personal attendance at closing is the only way to satisfy the requirement, since the statute recognizes several distinct routes, including a properly documented release.
  35. The correct answer is B (They must comply with the joint retainer rules and confirm there is no conflict of interest.): Two lawyers at the same firm may represent a transferor and a transferee if they comply with the joint retainer rules and there is no conflict of interest, which is the recognized exception known as the two lawyer rule. It is not accurate that lawyers at the same firm may act for both sides with no further steps required, since compliance with the joint retainer rules is a real precondition. There is no requirement to obtain municipal permission for this kind of representation arrangement, and deciding disputes by chance has nothing to do with the professional obligations that actually apply.
  36. The correct answer is D (It is a recognized red flag that should prompt further inquiry into the transaction.): The Law Society's red flags list includes a deposit paid directly to the vendor rather than to a lawyer or brokerage in trust as one of its examples, so this pattern should prompt the lawyer to investigate further rather than being treated as routine. A red flag does not by itself prove fraud has occurred, since the guidance states that the presence of one or more indicators does not necessarily mean fraud is happening. There is no dollar threshold in the guidance that limits when this particular pattern becomes relevant to the lawyer's review.
  37. The correct answer is A (Inquire further into the pattern and report the concern to the client and relevant parties.): The Law Society's guidelines direct a lawyer to inquire about suspicious patterns of transfers and to check for suspicious changes in value revealed by recent arm's length transfers, then to report those matters to the client and, where relevant, to the lender or title insurer. Immediately withdrawing without any inquiry skips the investigative step the guidelines actually call for, and simply ignoring the pattern contradicts the express purpose of the red flags guidance. Real estate agents are not a substitute for the lawyer's own professional review of title patterns, since this duty belongs to the lawyer handling the file.
  38. The correct answer is C (Obtain the source of funds and use reasonable efforts to identify who owns twenty-five percent or more.): When a licensee gives instructions about receiving, paying or transferring funds, the licensee must also obtain information about the source of the funds and, for an organization, use reasonable efforts to obtain the names and addresses of persons owning twenty-five percent or more of the organization, along with information establishing ownership, control and structure. Stopping at the basic name and address collected earlier does not satisfy these additional money handling obligations. Identifying every employee in the organization goes well beyond what the rule requires, and a notarized set of financial statements is not the specific information the by-law calls for in this context.
  39. The correct answer is A (It is a requisition, and the reviewing and signing of it cannot be delegated away from the lawyer.): A requisition on title is the purchaser's written demand that the vendor fix a title problem or provide something needed to complete the transaction, and the Law Society's guidelines list reviewing and signing the letter of requisition among the tasks that cannot be delegated to a non lawyer. A status certificate is a different document entirely, issued by a condominium corporation rather than sent by a purchaser's lawyer, so that label does not fit here. A compliance order comes from a municipal building official, and a notice of sale relates to a mortgage remedy, so neither correctly names the document the purchaser's lawyer is actually preparing.
  40. The correct answer is C (April 12, forty two days after the invoice was given to the owner.): A subcontractor pays its own subcontractors within seven days of receiving payment, or, if the contractor does not pay, no later than 42 days after the proper invoice was given to the owner, unless a valid notice of non payment is given within the times the section allows. Counting 42 days from March 1 gives April 12 as the deadline that applies when the subcontractor has not been paid. Seven days applies only once the subcontractor has actually received payment to pass along. Twenty eight days is the separate deadline that applies to the owner's own payment obligation, not to this lower tier chain.
  41. The correct answer is C (The application fails, since the mortgagee is protected as a good faith lender.): Where a spouse disposes of or encumbers an interest in a matrimonial home without the required consent or joinder, the transaction may be set aside on application, unless the person holding the interest or encumbrance acquired it for value, in good faith, and without notice that the property was a matrimonial home. Since the mortgagee here meets each of those conditions, the application to set the transaction aside fails. Automatically granting the application ignores this specific statutory protection for a good faith lender. There is no freestanding duty on the mortgagee to independently investigate spousal status once it has acted in good faith and without notice, and the outcome does not turn on some unrelated document the spouse may have signed elsewhere.
  42. The correct answer is B (It is deemed sufficient proof that the property is not a matrimonial home.): A statement verifying the spousal status facts, made by the person making the disposition, is deemed sufficient proof that the property is not a matrimonial home unless the person to whom the disposition is made had notice to the contrary, and such a statement may be made by the transferor's own lawyer based on the lawyer's personal knowledge. There is no requirement for a second, independent lawyer to separately confirm the same facts. The statement takes its effect as part of the transfer itself rather than needing separate registration as its own instrument. Nothing in this rule requires the statement to be sworn before a judge.
  43. The correct answer is D (The spousal status statement, and consent if the property is a matrimonial home.): Before closing, the lawyer confirms the transferor's spousal status and obtains the statement that will appear in the electronic transfer, and where the property is a matrimonial home also obtains the non owning spouse's consent or joinder, or confirms one of the other facts that satisfies the requirement, such as a registered designation of another property. Looking only at marital status from years earlier ignores that the relevant question is the status connected to this property now. Whether the transferor previously sold some other matrimonial home is not what this closing agenda item addresses. It is the transferor's spousal status that is relevant to this particular statement, not the transferee's.
  44. The correct answer is D (No, an application may still be made within six years of the loss.): A person wrongfully deprived of land because of the registration of a fraudulent instrument is entitled to compensation from the Land Titles Assurance Fund where the person has shown the due diligence the Director specifies, cannot recover just compensation otherwise, and applies within the time limit, which is six years from the time the loss was suffered. Since only five years have passed here, the application is still within time. A two year or one year limit would be shorter than the actual period the Act allows for this kind of claim. The six year period runs from when the loss was suffered, not from how recently the fraudulent instrument happened to be registered.
  45. The correct answer is C (It is topped up from the Consolidated Revenue Fund.): The Land Titles Assurance Fund is continued under the Land Titles Act and is topped up from the Consolidated Revenue Fund when its balance falls below $1,000,000. There is no rule that suspends new registrations simply because the Fund balance drops. Individual registered owners are not assessed a special contribution to replenish the Fund when it runs low. The Fund is not closed to new claims when its balance is low, replenishment from the Consolidated Revenue Fund is exactly what keeps it available.
  46. The correct answer is C (The same period, within the time for a defence or before being noted in default.): A defendant who wants a sale instead of foreclosure must serve and file a request for sale, Form 64F, within the time for delivering a statement of defence or before being noted in default, which is the same period that applies to a request to redeem. Extending the deadline all the way to trial ignores the fixed period the Rules actually set for this step. Seven days after the claim is served is far shorter than the period the Rules allow. The deadline is set by the Rules of Civil Procedure themselves, not left to be set independently by the assigned judge.
  47. The correct answer is D (Treat it with suspicion, since a valid power of attorney needs two witnesses.): The Law Society's red flags list notes that a titled spouse may try to sell a matrimonial home using a forged power of attorney purporting to give the non titled spouse's consent, and that the power of attorney may not have been witnessed by two independent witnesses as section 10 of the Substitute Decisions Act, 1992 requires. A single witness on a document of this kind is exactly the defect the red flags list warns about, so the lawyer should treat it with suspicion rather than rely on it as presented. Accepting any signed power of attorney without further inquiry ignores this specific warning. The non titled spouse's own consent is what section 21 of the Family Law Act actually requires, so the titled spouse's signature alone does not satisfy it.
  48. The correct answer is B (The registrar, once the defendants have been noted in default.): Where a defendant has been noted in default and has not filed a request to redeem, the plaintiff may require the registrar to sign judgment for foreclosure with a reference or judgment for immediate foreclosure, without this step requiring a judge to hold a full hearing on the merits. The Director of Titles has a role in Land Titles Assurance Fund compensation claims, not in signing a judgment for foreclosure in a court action. Foreclosure actions of this kind are not decided by a jury. This step, signing the judgment once defendants are noted in default, is specifically a registrar's function under the Rules of Civil Procedure.
  49. The correct answer is D (That the attorney genuinely cares for her and may need to make decisions.): A person is capable of giving a power of attorney for personal care if she can understand whether the proposed attorney has a genuine concern for her welfare and can appreciate that she may need the attorney to make decisions for her. Nothing in the test requires the attorney to make flawless decisions, be a licensed professional, or obtain prior court approval before acting. The focus is on the grantor's own understanding of the relationship, not on guaranteeing a particular outcome.
  50. The correct answer is B (Only if there is evidence the grantor made similar gifts before becoming incapable.): Where the power of attorney does not authorize charitable gifts in advance, an attorney may still make one only if there is evidence of similar gifts the grantor made when capable, and the total of such gifts cannot exceed the lesser of 20 percent of the year's income and any limit stated in the document. A blanket rule that any charitable gift is always fine, or that no charitable gift is ever permitted, misstates the actual conditional test. The overall size of the property has no bearing on whether this particular gift is authorized.
  51. The correct answer is A (The attorney must keep accounts of all transactions involving the property.): Once the grantor is incapable of managing property, or the attorney has reasonable grounds to believe so, the attorney is under a duty to keep accounts of all transactions involving the grantor's property, whether or not any complaint has yet been raised. The accounts do not need to be filed automatically with the court every year, they are instead produced if a passing of accounts is later required. The duty to keep records begins during the grantor's incapacity, not only once the grantor has died.
  52. The correct answer is C (The Public Guardian and Trustee becomes her statutory guardian of property.): Where a certificate is issued under the Mental Health Act certifying that a patient of a psychiatric facility is incapable of managing property, the Public Guardian and Trustee becomes that person's statutory guardian of property. No court hearing is required to trigger this outcome, and it does not depend on a relative or physician stepping forward. The patient can later apply to the Consent and Capacity Board to review the underlying finding of incapacity if she disagrees with it.
  53. The correct answer is A (Whether a less restrictive option would meet the need without a capacity finding.): The court must not appoint a guardian of property if the need will be met by an alternative course of action that does not require a finding of incapacity and is less restrictive of the person's decision making rights. The size of the estate and whether the family agrees on a candidate do not answer that threshold question. A specific medical diagnosis is not itself the legal test either, since the statutory focus is on functional decision making ability and available alternatives.
  54. The correct answer is A (The nature and extent of her property, and the people she might be expected to benefit.): To have a sound disposing mind, a testator must understand the nature and effect of a will, recollect the nature and extent of her property, understand what she is giving under the will, and remember the people she might be expected to benefit, along with the claims of anyone being excluded. Nothing in the test requires her to grasp precise legal drafting language, exact current market values, or the eventual tax consequences of each gift. The focus is on general understanding and memory, not technical or financial precision.
  55. The correct answer is D (The burden shifts to the person propounding the will to prove capacity overall.): A testator is ordinarily presumed capable, but where suspicious circumstances are shown, the burden shifts so that the person propounding the will must prove testamentary capacity on a balance of probabilities. Suspicious circumstances trigger a shifted burden of proof, not an automatic finding that the will is invalid, so evidence must still be led and weighed by the court. The shift is specifically about capacity and knowledge and approval of the contents, not a separate standalone finding about undue influence.
  56. The correct answer is C (She is deemed to have capacity to retain and instruct counsel here.): Where the capacity of a person who does not have legal representation is in issue in a proceeding under the statute, the court may direct the Public Guardian and Trustee to arrange legal representation, and the person is deemed to have capacity to retain and instruct counsel. This deeming rule keeps her from being shut out of a proceeding about her own capacity simply because that very capacity is being questioned. Neither dismissing the proceeding nor requiring a relative to instruct counsel reflects what the statute actually provides.
  57. The correct answer is C (The day before the date of death, the earliest triggering event.): The valuation date is the earliest of several listed events, and on a death where the spouses were not separated, that event is the day before the date on which one spouse dies leaving the other surviving. The date of death itself, the appointment of an estate trustee, and the date a will is admitted to probate are all later or otherwise incorrect reference points for this calculation. Using the day before death avoids counting any property changes caused by the death itself in the net family property calculation.
  58. The correct answer is A (She is deemed to have elected to take under the will absent a court order.): The election must be filed with the Estate Registrar for Ontario within six months after the spouse's death, and if no election is filed in that time, the surviving spouse is deemed to have elected to take under the will, unless the court orders otherwise on application. This deeming rule does not strip her of everything, it simply locks in the will based option by default. The estate trustee has no power to pick between the two options on the surviving spouse's behalf.
  59. The correct answer is D (The joint tenancy is deemed severed before death, and the equalization claim has priority.): Where a spouse dies owning an interest in a matrimonial home as a joint tenant with a third person rather than with the other spouse, that joint tenancy is deemed severed immediately before the death, so the interest falls into the estate instead of passing by survivorship. The surviving spouse's equalization entitlement then has priority over gifts under the will and over the intestacy rules, so it ranks ahead of the business partner's competing claim to the interest. Nothing in these facts removes the equalization claim or places it behind ordinary creditors.
  60. The correct answer is B (Disposed of them for proceeds equal to their fair market value immediately before death.): Where a taxpayer dies, he is deemed to have disposed immediately before death of each capital property, including shares like these, and to have received proceeds equal to the fair market value of that property immediately before death. This deemed disposition is what typically triggers a capital gain, since it compares fair market value rather than the original purchase price. Absent a specific rollover, the property is not transferred tax free, and it is not simply treated as retained until an eventual future sale.
  61. The correct answer is B (Within 36 months after the death, or a longer period the Minister allows.): The spousal rollover for property transferred to a qualifying trust on death is only available if it can be shown, within 36 months after the death or a longer period the Minister considers reasonable on a timely application, that the property has become vested indefeasibly in the trust. Twelve months and six months are both incorrect figures for this specific rollover, and the six month figure in particular belongs to the unrelated estate administration tax return deadline. A time limit does apply here even though the trust itself is otherwise validly drafted.
  62. The correct answer is C (No general rollover exists for ordinary small business shares passing to a child.): The rollovers to a child in the federal tax legislation are specifically for farm and fishing property, and for shares of a family farm or fishing corporation, not for ordinary small business corporation shares with no farming or fishing activity. Since these shares do not meet the farm or fishing definitions, they remain subject to the ordinary deemed disposition rule on death, though the lifetime capital gains exemption may still reduce the resulting tax. The shares can certainly still be transferred to the daughter, the transfer itself is not what is being denied.
  63. The correct answer is A (It may be treated as a refund of premiums, reducing the deemed amount in his income.): Where the value that becomes receivable by the spouse reduces the amount the deceased annuitant is deemed to have received immediately before death, that reduction is described as a refund of premiums, and the legal representative and the spouse may jointly designate the payment for this treatment. The amount is not simply always fully taxable with no relief, nor is it automatically tax free, the refund of premiums mechanism is what determines the actual result. Reporting obligations here run through the deceased's own return and the joint designation, not solely through the spouse's separate return.
  64. The correct answer is A (The TFSA is excluded from the value, while the investment account is included.): A TFSA with a valid beneficiary or successor holder designation passes outside the estate and is not included in the value of the estate for this tax, while an ordinary investment account with no such designation and no joint owner falls into the estate and must be included at its date of death value. Both assets being investments does not by itself exclude either of them, the designation is what matters. The two assets are treated differently here precisely because only one of them carries a valid designation.
  65. The correct answer is B (No more than 36 months after the death, provided no other requirement is missed.): A graduated rate estate exists only where, among other conditions, the relevant time is no more than 36 months after the individual's death, along with the estate being a testamentary trust and meeting the social insurance number and self designation requirements. The status is not indefinite, it is not fixed at 12 months, and it does not depend on when a clearance certificate happens to be issued. Once 36 months have passed, the estate can no longer be treated as a graduated rate estate under this definition.
  66. The correct answer is C (She can become personally liable for unpaid tax up to the value of the property distributed.): A legal representative who distributes estate property without first obtaining a clearance certificate can be held personally liable for unpaid amounts owed under the Income Tax Act, up to the value of the property distributed. An accurate return does not remove this risk, since the certificate confirms all outstanding amounts have actually been paid or secured, not merely reported correctly. This is a personal liability risk for the representative herself, not simply a criminal offence, and it falls on the trustee rather than only on the beneficiaries.
  67. The correct answer is D (Twelve, because the formula adds one extra year to the years she actually owned it.): The formula that reduces a principal residence gain uses a variable equal to one plus the number of qualifying taxation years, which gives an extra year of exemption beyond the years actually owned and used as a principal residence. Here that means the variable is twelve, not simply the eleven years she actually owned and lived in the house. The formula does not subtract a year, and it is not limited to a sale occurring within the same taxation year as the purchase.
  68. The correct answer is D (Yes, this can qualify as an alter ego trust since she was over 65 when created.): An alter ego trust allows a rollover of capital property transferred into it during the settlor's lifetime, but only where the settlor had attained 65 years of age when the trust was created, and the settlor is entitled to receive all of the income of the trust during her lifetime with no one else entitled to receive income or capital before her death. Since she is 66, the age condition is satisfied, so a rollover into this kind of lifetime trust is genuinely available to her. No further age threshold beyond 65 applies once that condition is already met.
  69. The correct answer is A (The trust property must be identified, and the beneficiaries or purpose must be identified.): A valid express trust requires the three certainties, being certainty of intention, certainty of subject matter meaning the trust property is identified, and certainty of objects meaning the beneficiaries or charitable purpose are identified. Nothing in this common law requirement demands government registration or a licensed corporate trustee, an individual trustee is perfectly capable of holding a valid trust. A minimum twenty one year duration is not part of the three certainties either, that concept belongs to the separate rule against perpetuities.
  70. The correct answer is C (Whether he can understand relevant information or appreciate the foreseeable consequences.): A person is incapable of personal care if the person is not able to understand information that is relevant to making a decision concerning his or her own health care, nutrition, shelter, clothing, hygiene or safety, or is not able to appreciate the reasonably foreseeable consequences of a decision or lack of decision. Orientation questions about the date or location are not the statutory test, and disagreeing with a proposed plan does not by itself show incapacity. A diagnosis alone, without an assessment against this functional test, does not establish incapacity either.
  71. The correct answer is C (Understand whether the attorney has genuine concern, and appreciate she may need decisions made.): A person is capable of giving a power of attorney for personal care if the person has the ability to understand whether the proposed attorney has a genuine concern for the person's welfare, and appreciates that the person may need to have the proposed attorney make decisions on the person's behalf. There is no requirement to list every possible future medical treatment, and the test does not depend on a clean history free of any mental illness diagnosis or on a doctor's certificate of general capacity.
  72. The correct answer is A (No, a person paid to provide her support services may not act as attorney.): A person may not act as an attorney for personal care if the person provides health care to the grantor for compensation, or provides residential, social, training or support services to the grantor for compensation, unless the person is the grantor's spouse, partner or relative. Since this support worker is paid and is not her spouse, partner or relative, the worker cannot act as her attorney for personal care. Personal closeness or a written waiver of the fee does not remove the statutory bar, and meeting the family is not part of the test.
  73. The correct answer is B (Two witnesses are required, and each must sign the document as a witness.): A power of attorney for personal care must be executed in the presence of two witnesses, each of whom signs it as a witness. Preparation of the document by a lawyer does not remove the witnessing requirement, and the Act does not accept one witness or three witnesses as satisfying the rule. The same list of persons who may not witness a continuing power of attorney for property also applies to a power of attorney for personal care.
  74. The correct answer is C (Support, education and care of the incapable person's own dependants, if funds remain sufficient.): A guardian of property must first make expenditures reasonably necessary for the incapable person's own support, education and care, then for the support, education and care of the person's dependants if the property is and will remain sufficient for the person's own needs, and then to satisfy other legal obligations. Charitable gifts and gifts or loans to friends and relatives come later still, and each is subject to its own conditions. The guardian's own compensation is fixed separately and is not treated as a priority expenditure ahead of the incapable person's needs.
  75. The correct answer is B (No, total charitable gifts cannot exceed 20 percent of the year's income.): Total charitable gifts made by a guardian or an attorney under a continuing power of attorney cannot exceed the lesser of 20 percent of the income of the property in the year and any maximum set out in a power of attorney executed before incapacity. Evidence of similar giving when capable is a precondition for making a charitable gift at all, but it does not lift the 20 percent cap. Sufficiency of the property alone is also not enough, since the cap remains in place. Charitable giving is permitted in principle, subject to the conditions and the cap, so a blanket prohibition overstates the rule.
  76. The correct answer is C (The Public Guardian and Trustee becomes the statutory guardian of property.): Where a certificate is issued under the Mental Health Act certifying that a patient of a psychiatric facility is incapable of managing property, the Public Guardian and Trustee is the person's statutory guardian of property. This arises automatically from the certificate, without any family member applying and without the physician or the facility itself becoming the guardian. A separate court application under the Substitute Decisions Act is a different route to a guardian and is not needed for this statutory guardianship to arise.
  77. The correct answer is D (That the person may apply to the Consent and Capacity Board for a review.): The Public Guardian and Trustee becomes the statutory guardian as soon as it receives a copy of the assessor's certificate of incapacity, and it must inform the person of the finding and tell the person that he or she may apply to the Consent and Capacity Board for a review of the assessor's finding. There is no seven day deadline to retain a lawyer built into that notice requirement, the finding is not described as final and unreviewable, and the review route runs through the Board rather than a direct court appeal.
  78.
