The correct answer is D (No, because a less restrictive alternative that avoids a finding is available.): The court may appoint a guardian of property for a person who is incapable of managing property if decisions need to be made on the person's behalf by an authorized person, but the court must not appoint a guardian if the need will be met by a less restrictive alternative that does not require a finding of incapacity. Because he remains capable of granting a continuing power of attorney to his daughter, that less restrictive route should be used instead of a guardianship application. Forgetfulness alone does not compel an appointment, and being named executor of a will is unrelated to whether a guardian of property should be appointed while he is alive.
  79. The correct answer is A (The person is deemed to have capacity to retain and instruct counsel.): If the capacity of a person who does not have legal representation is in issue in a proceeding under the Substitute Decisions Act, the court may direct the Public Guardian and Trustee to arrange for legal representation, and the person is deemed to have capacity to retain and instruct counsel. This deeming provision exists precisely so the underlying capacity dispute can be litigated with the person's own instructions, rather than treating the person as incapable in advance or requiring the lawyer to first assess capacity. The lawyer, not the Public Guardian and Trustee, remains the instructing client's counsel.
  80. The correct answer is B (No, the Act has no requirement that a witness be specially trained.): There is no requirement in the Substitute Decisions Act that a witness be a trained witness, whether a nurse, a social worker or any other professional. The only special witness qualification in the Act applies to remote execution by audio-visual communication technology, which requires at least one witness to be a licensee under the Law Society Act, and that requirement does not apply to an ordinary in person execution. A family doctor is not specially required to witness the document either, subject only to the usual prohibited witness list.
  81. The correct answer is C (Its date of marriage value of $80,000 is deducted, leaving $70,000 counted.): Net family property is the value of property a spouse owns on the valuation date, after deducting the spouse's debts and liabilities and the value of property, other than a matrimonial home, that the spouse owned on the date of the marriage. Here that means deducting the $80,000 date of marriage value from the $150,000 valuation date value, leaving $70,000 counted toward net family property. The whole $150,000 is not counted because the deduction rule exists for exactly this situation, and the portfolio is not excluded altogether, since only a matrimonial home is denied the date of marriage deduction. Age has no role in this calculation.
  82. The correct answer is C (The day before the date of his death is the valuation date.): The valuation date is the earliest of several listed events, including the date before the date on which one spouse dies leaving the other surviving. On death, the valuation date is therefore the day before the death, not the day of death itself, and not the later date the estate certificate happens to be issued. A valuation date still exists even where the spouses were never separated, since death by itself is one of the triggering events under the definition.
  83. The correct answer is A (It is excluded property and is not counted in her net family property.): Property acquired by gift or inheritance from a third person after the date of the marriage is excluded from net family property, other than a matrimonial home. Because this inheritance was not used to acquire or improve the matrimonial home and was kept separate, it remains excluded property and is not counted. There is no rule including it in full or splitting it in half between the spouses, and exclusion does not depend on whether she spends it before the valuation date.
  84. The correct answer is D ($0, because the whole plan became receivable by his surviving spouse.): Where an annuitant under a registered retirement savings plan dies, the annuitant is deemed to have received a benefit equal to the amount by which the fair market value of the plan's property exceeds the value of the portion that becomes receivable by a spouse or common-law partner. Because his spouse became entitled to the entire $200,000, the excess is nil, so the deemed receipt is $0. There is no rule fixing the spousal portion at a flat half regardless of what actually becomes receivable, and the deeming applies to the deceased annuitant, not separately to the estate.
  85. The correct answer is B (She is deemed to have received the $175,000 immediately before her death.): Where the last annuitant under a registered retirement income fund dies, that annuitant is deemed to have received immediately before death an amount out of the fund equal to the fair market value of the fund's property at the time of death. The deeming attaches to the deceased annuitant herself, not to her estate, and it applies even where no beneficiary actually receives a payment, since the deemed amount arises from the death itself rather than from a later distribution. There is no rule that only half the value is deemed received.
  86. The correct answer is B (His partner becomes the holder, and the account keeps its tax free status.): A survivor, meaning an individual who was a spouse or common-law partner of the deceased immediately before death, can become the holder of the arrangement if named as successor holder, and the holder of the arrangement is the individual until death and, after the death, the survivor who acquires it. As successor holder, his partner steps directly into his position as holder, and the account continues as a tax free savings account rather than losing that status or requiring the estate to be fully administered first. A survivor named as successor holder becomes the holder outright, which is a different, more direct role than simply being named a beneficiary of the proceeds.
  87. The correct answer is D (Yes, the rollover is only available once the settlor has attained age 65.): The rollover on a transfer of capital property into an alter ego trust applies only where the trust was created after 1999 and the individual had attained 65 years of age when the trust was created, unless the transfer does not change beneficial ownership and no other beneficiary has an interest immediately after the transfer. Because this settlor is 58 and the trust gives him alone the income for life, the ordinary route to the rollover requires him to be 65, so at his current age the rollover is not available on that basis. The age condition applies to this kind of lifetime trust generally, not only to a joint spousal trust, and the age of any remainderman named is not the relevant condition.
  88. The correct answer is C (21 years, after which an unexercised option becomes void on its own terms.): For options to acquire an interest in land for valuable consideration, other than the lease renewal options excluded from that rule, the perpetuity period is 21 years, and an option exercisable more than 21 years after its creation is void on the expiry of that period. The 40 year period instead applies to an easement, profit a prendre or similar interest, and the life in being plus 21 years measure applies to the general perpetuity rule rather than to this specific option rule. There is a defined time limit on this kind of option, so it is not unlimited.
  89. The correct answer is B (No notice is required here, since all of the directors are named as defendants.): The 14 day notice requirement does not apply if all of the directors of the corporation are defendants in the action, which is exactly the situation described here. Saying the notice rule applies without exception overlooks this specific statutory carve out for an all directors defendant scenario. There is no alternative seven day notice period that substitutes for the usual 14 days in this context. The exception is tied to all of the directors being named, not merely a majority of them, so naming only a majority would not by itself remove the notice requirement.
  90. The correct answer is A (The court may direct that part of the amount recovered be paid directly to shareholders.): In connection with a derivative action, the court may make any order it thinks fit, including directing that any amount adjudged payable by a defendant be paid, in whole or in part, directly to former and present security holders instead of to the corporation. This means the corporation's treasury is not always the mandatory destination for every dollar recovered, contrary to a strict rule requiring full repayment to the corporation. Splitting the recovery among the directors has no basis in this remedial provision, which is focused on security holders and the corporation, not the directors personally. The court clearly retains discretion over how any recovered amount is distributed, rather than having no power at all in that respect.
  91. The correct answer is C (Yes, adding a restriction on share transfer is a recognized trigger for the dissent right.): A holder of shares entitled to vote on the resolution may dissent where the corporation resolves to amend its articles to add, remove or change restrictions on the issue, transfer or ownership of shares of a class or series, which squarely covers this kind of amendment. Amalgamation is only one of several listed dissent triggers, not the only one available under the Act, so limiting dissent to amalgamations alone is inaccurate. Dissent rights are not confined to changes affecting voting rights, since transfer restrictions are a separately recognized trigger in their own right. There is no requirement that the dissenting shareholder first dispose of other unrelated holdings before exercising this right.
  92. The correct answer is C (The written objection must be sent to the corporation at or before that meeting.): The dissenting shareholder must send a written objection to the corporation at or before the meeting at which the resolution is to be voted on, subject to a narrow exception where the corporation failed to give proper notice. Waiting 30 days after the meeting to send the objection would come too late under this rule, since the deadline is tied to the meeting itself rather than a fixed period afterward. There is no rule allowing the objection to be delayed until any time before the shares are eventually sold. Sending the objection only after the resolution has already been adopted misses the point of objecting before the vote takes place.
  93. The correct answer is A (The corporation must notify within 10 days, and the shareholder must demand payment within 20 days.): The corporation must send notice that the resolution was adopted within 10 days after adoption to each shareholder who filed an objection, and the shareholder must then send a written demand for payment of fair value within 20 days after receiving that notice. Reversing the two periods, so that notice takes 20 days and demand takes only 10, does not reflect the sequence the Act actually sets out. A flat 30 day period for both steps overstates the time the Act allows at each stage. A flat five day period for both steps understates the time actually available under the statute.
  94. The correct answer is B (Section 253 governs a compliance or restraining order application, not section 207.): The compliance and restraining order provision is found in section 253 of the Business Corporations Act, which allows a complainant or a creditor to apply where a corporation or certain individuals fail to comply with the Act, the regulations, the articles, the by-laws or a unanimous shareholder agreement. Section 207 instead deals with winding up a corporation by court order, so citing it here would point to the wrong provision entirely. Section 248 is the oppression remedy provision, which is a related but distinct remedy from the compliance order sought here. A genuine compliance order application does exist under the Act, so no such option correctly describes the law.
  95. The correct answer is D (Each amalgamating corporation's shareholders must approve the agreement by special resolution.): An amalgamation agreement is adopted when the shareholders of each amalgamating corporation have approved the amalgamation by a special resolution of the holders of the shares of each class or series entitled to vote on it, which is a higher threshold than an ordinary resolution. Director approval alone is not sufficient outside the narrow holding and subsidiary amalgamation shortcut, so this general scenario still needs shareholder involvement. An ordinary resolution understates the actual threshold the Act requires for adopting an amalgamation agreement. Shareholder approval is squarely required in this general amalgamation scenario, so saying none is needed misstates the process.
  96. The correct answer is D (This amalgamation can proceed without shareholder approval under the holding and subsidiary shortcut.): A holding corporation and one or more of its subsidiary corporations may amalgamate and continue as one corporation without complying with the usual agreement and shareholder approval provisions if the amalgamation is approved by a resolution of the directors of each amalgamating corporation and all of the issued shares of each amalgamating subsidiary are held by the other amalgamating corporations, which matches these facts exactly. Saying full shareholder approval is still needed ignores this specific statutory shortcut built for exactly this ownership structure. Amalgamating a holding corporation with its wholly owned subsidiary is expressly contemplated by the Act, not prohibited. Ontario Securities Commission approval is not part of this particular amalgamation mechanism.
  97. The correct answer is A (Dissent rights apply, and a copy of the new instrument must be filed within 60 days.): Continuance out of Ontario is a recognized dissent trigger, so shareholders entitled to vote on the continuance resolution may dissent under the applicable provision. A corporation that continues out of Ontario also agrees, as a condition of the electronic filing, to file with the Ministry a copy of the instrument of continuance issued by the other jurisdiction within 60 days after the date of issuance. Saying nothing further needs to be filed ignores this specific 60 day filing obligation that follows the continuance. A 15 day period understates the actual filing window the Ministry's own filing conditions establish for this step.
  98. The correct answer is C (Each affected class must approve the arrangement separately by special resolution before court approval.): Where an arrangement has been approved by the shareholders and by the holders of each class or series entitled to vote separately on it, in each case by special resolution, the arrangement has been adopted and the corporation may then apply to the court for an order approving it. A simple majority of the whole shareholder body does not meet this special resolution and separate class approval standard the Act requires. Shareholder approval is a genuine precondition to the court application, so saying none is required misstates the process. Director approval alone does not substitute for the shareholder approval this provision specifically requires.
  99. The correct answer is B (A special resolution is needed, and notice must be filed with the Director within 10 days.): The shareholders of a corporation may, by special resolution, require the corporation to be wound up voluntarily, appointing one or more liquidators at that meeting, and the corporation must file notice of the resolution with the Director within 10 days after it is passed. An ordinary resolution does not meet the higher threshold the Act sets for this fundamental step, and a notice obligation to the Director does exist rather than being absent altogether. A 60 day filing period overstates the actual 10 day deadline that applies here. Unanimity is not the standard the Act sets for this resolution, and same day filing is stricter than what the statute actually requires.
  100. The correct answer is B (The application may proceed, since revival must occur within 20 years of dissolution.): An application for revival must not be made more than 20 years after the date of dissolution, so an application 18 years after dissolution still falls within the permitted window. A 10 year bar does not reflect the actual limitation period the Act sets for revival applications. Revival is expressly available under the Act on the application of an interested person, so treating dissolution as permanently irreversible misstates the law. There is a genuine outer time limit on revival applications, so it is inaccurate to say no time limit applies at all.
  101. The correct answer is B (The corporation is deemed never to have been dissolved, subject to rights acquired during dissolution.): On revival the corporation is deemed for all purposes never to have been dissolved, subject to any terms and conditions the Director imposes and to the rights acquired by any person during the period of dissolution, which protects a third party who genuinely acquired rights while the corporation was dissolved. Saying all third party rights are simply erased ignores this express statutory protection for rights acquired during the dissolution period. The revival does operate retroactively in this deemed never dissolved sense, rather than only taking effect from the date of revival forward. Automatic restoration of every asset regardless of intervening rights overstates what the revival actually accomplishes.
  102. The correct answer is C (The elected amount cannot be less than the fair market value of any non-share consideration.): The elected amount under this rollover provision is bounded so that it cannot be less than the fair market value of any non-share consideration received, and cannot exceed the fair market value of the property disposed of, which is a meaningful constraint rather than an entirely open ended choice. Saying the parties can agree on any figure ignores these statutory floor and ceiling limits on the election. Requiring the elected amount to equal fair market value exactly misstates the rule, since the whole point of the election is to allow a different amount within the bounded range. The constraint is tied to fair market value rather than the taxpayer's original cost for the property.
  103. The correct answer is D (The new corporation is deemed to be the same corporation as, and a continuation of, each one.): For an amalgamation to which the relevant rollover provision applies, the new corporation is deemed to be the same corporation as, and a continuation of, each predecessor corporation for the specified purposes, so the amalgamated entity generally inherits the tax attributes of both predecessors rather than starting fresh. Saying only one predecessor's attributes carry forward misstates the deemed continuation treatment that applies to each predecessor. The amalgamation rollover is specifically designed to avoid treating the transaction as a disposition of the predecessors' assets. Fresh, unrelated tax attributes would defeat the purpose of the rollover treatment this provision provides.
  104. The correct answer is B (Employment relationships and contracts do not transfer automatically and generally need assignment or consent.): On an asset purchase, contracts, licences and employment relationships do not transfer automatically, so each must generally be assigned or novated, with consents obtained where the underlying contract requires them, which is quite different from how a share purchase works. Saying everything transfers automatically on closing overlooks this basic distinction between an asset purchase and a share purchase. Splitting the analysis so that only employees transfer automatically, or only contracts do, does not reflect how either category actually behaves on an asset deal. Both employment relationships and ordinary commercial contracts require active steps to carry them over to the purchaser.
  105. The correct answer is B (Shareholder authorization is required, and a shareholder may dissent from that authorizing resolution.): Subject to limited exceptions, the directors may sell, lease or exchange all or substantially all the property of the corporation only if authorized by the shareholders as required by the relevant provision, and a sale of all or substantially all the property is itself a recognized dissent trigger under the Act. This means director authority alone is not enough for a transaction of this scale, contrary to the idea that no further step is needed. Dissent rights genuinely do apply to this kind of authorizing resolution, so saying no shareholder may ever dissent misstates the protection available. An auditor's approval has no role in authorizing this kind of fundamental sale transaction.
  106. The correct answer is B (Value must be given, the debtor must have rights in the collateral, and a signed agreement.): A security interest is not enforceable against a third party unless it has attached, and attachment requires that value be given, that the debtor has rights in the collateral, and that one of the statutory requirements is met, most commonly a signed security agreement describing the collateral sufficiently. Focusing only on the signed agreement, without value having been given, leaves out a necessary element of attachment. Registration relates to perfection and priority rather than to attachment itself, so registration alone does not establish enforceability against the debtor. The debtor having rights in the collateral is necessary but not sufficient on its own, since the other attachment elements must also be present.
  107. The correct answer is A (Written notice must be given to the earlier registered secured party before the debtor gets possession.): A purchase money security interest in inventory has priority over an earlier security interest in the same collateral only if it was perfected at the time the debtor obtained possession and, before the debtor receives possession, written notice is given to every other secured party who had earlier registered a financing statement describing the same items or types of inventory. Saying no notice is required overlooks this specific and important precondition for inventory purchase money priority. Giving notice only after possession has already passed comes too late to satisfy the requirement. The notice must go to the earlier secured party, not merely to the debtor, since the whole purpose is to warn the existing registrant.
  108. The correct answer is D (The supplier's interest still has priority, since perfection occurred within 15 days of possession.): For collateral other than inventory, a purchase money security interest has priority over an earlier security interest in the same collateral if it is perfected before or within 15 days after the debtor obtained possession of the collateral, and 12 days falls comfortably within that window regardless of when the other lender registered. The supplier's priority does not depend on beating the other lender's registration date, since the 15 day grace period is what actually matters here. The earlier registered lender does not automatically win simply by registering first, because the purchase money priority rule is designed to override ordinary first to register priority in this situation. There is no equal sharing rule that applies once one party correctly qualifies for purchase money priority.
  109. The correct answer is D (No, Ontario law generally prohibits an employer from entering into this kind of non-compete agreement.): Ontario employment standards legislation provides that no employer shall enter into an employment contract or other agreement with an employee that is, or that includes, a non-compete agreement, subject to limited exceptions not suggested by these facts. Saying any non-compete agreement is fully enforceable directly contradicts this statutory prohibition on non-compete agreements in the employment context. The prohibition is not limited to agreements lasting longer than five years, since the statutory language targets non-compete agreements generally rather than singling out a duration threshold. Independent legal advice does not cure the statutory prohibition, since the defect lies in the nature of the clause itself rather than how it was negotiated.
  110. The correct answer is A (By the order in which each security interest attached.): Where no other provision of the Act applies, priority between unperfected security interests is determined by the order of attachment, not by any later event such as a demand for payment. Demanding payment is a remedy step that happens after a security interest already exists, so it is not what fixes priority between two unperfected interests. An equal proportional split is not how the statute resolves this kind of competition, since the Act sets a clear ordering rule rather than dividing the collateral evenly. Signing the loan agreement is a separate event from attachment itself, since attachment also depends on value being given and rights in the collateral, so the signing date alone does not control priority.
  111. The correct answer is B (Yes, because the original agreement already covers future advances.): A security agreement may secure future advances, so an original agreement that expressly covers future advances continues to secure a later advance made under the same borrowing relationship without any new documentation. Requiring a fresh agreement for every advance would defeat the purpose of a future advances clause, which exists precisely to avoid repeating that paperwork each time. There is no calendar year limit built into the future advances rule, since the clause covers advances made from time to time under the existing agreement. Re-registration is also not required before a new advance is covered, since the original registration and security agreement already extend to future advances made under it.
  112. The correct answer is A (The deemed trust beats the general interest, but not a purchase money interest in inventory.): A security interest in an account or inventory and its proceeds is subordinate to the interest of a beneficiary of a deemed trust arising under the Employment Standards Act, but a perfected purchase money security interest in inventory or its proceeds is specifically excepted from that subordination rule. The general security interest does not rank ahead of the deemed trust, because the statute deliberately gives the wage related deemed trust priority over an ordinary general security interest. The deemed trust does not rank behind every security interest, since the exception is limited to a perfected purchase money security interest in inventory. The three claims are not simply split equally, because the statute sets a specific ordering rather than proportional sharing.
  113. The correct answer is A (The repair shop's lien has priority over the perfected security interest.): A lien for materials or services furnished in the ordinary course of business with respect to goods subject to a security interest has priority over a perfected security interest, unless another Act provides that the lien does not have that priority, and nothing here strips that priority away. Being registered first does not save the perfected security interest in this situation, since the statute gives the repair lien priority independent of registration timing between the two interests. The two claims are not treated as equal, because the statute sets a clear priority in favour of the person who supplied the materials or services. Registration is also not what creates the repair lien's priority, since this kind of lien gets its priority directly from the statute.
  114. The correct answer is A (The lender's claim, since its interest attached before the equipment became a fixture.): A security interest in goods that attached before the goods became a fixture has priority as to the fixture over the claim of any person who has an interest in the real property, which covers exactly this situation. The new owner's later registered interest in the land does not automatically defeat the earlier attached security interest, since the statute preserves priority for a security interest that attached before fixing. Splitting the fixture's value between equally ranked claims is not how this priority rule works, because the statute establishes a clear winner rather than a proportional sharing arrangement. The landlord is not the relevant party to this priority contest, since the competing claim described here belongs to the new owner of the real property.
  115. The correct answer is C (No, because a guarantee must be evidenced in signed writing to be enforced.): No action may be brought to charge a person on a special promise to answer for the debt, default or miscarriage of another person unless the agreement, or a memorandum or note of it, is in writing and signed by the party to be charged or by someone lawfully authorized to sign for them. A purely verbal guarantee does not satisfy that written and signed requirement, so it is not enforceable in the same way a properly documented one would be. A witness confirming the verbal exchange does not substitute for the required writing, since the statute demands a signed document rather than corroborating testimony. Personal guarantees between individuals are not prohibited outright, since guarantees are common and enforceable transactions in Ontario as long as the writing requirement is satisfied.
  116. The correct answer is A (Yes, an indefinite term lease is included even if determinable within a year.): The statutory definition of a lease for a term of more than one year expressly includes a lease for an indefinite term, even if that lease is determinable by either party within one year, alongside leases that continue beyond one year through uninterrupted possession or renewal. Limiting the definition to a fixed multi year term ignores that the indefinite term category is separately listed in the statute. Saying an indefinite term lease falls outside the definition gets the rule backwards, since the statute pulls that exact arrangement in. The classification does not wait until a year has actually passed either, since the lease is caught from the outset by virtue of being an indefinite term arrangement.
  117. The correct answer is D (Register a financing statement against the customer under the PPSA.): Because the statute treats a lease for a term of more than one year as a security interest, the practical protection for the leasing company's interest is to register a financing statement under the PPSA, the same as any other secured party would. Relying on ownership alone leaves the leasing company exposed, since an unregistered interest can lose out to a person who represents the customer's creditors or to a later purchaser dealing with the equipment. The land registry deals with real property, not equipment leased to a business customer, so filing there does nothing to protect this kind of interest. Individually notifying creditors by mail is not the statutory mechanism, since registration on the personal property registry is what gives the interest its priority against third parties.
  118. The correct answer is D (The Fraudulent Conveyances Act, since the transfer intends to defeat a creditor.): The Fraudulent Conveyances Act voids a conveyance made with intent to defeat, hinder, delay or defraud creditors, and that intent based test does not require the transferor to already be insolvent, which fits this fact pattern where the business is still paying its ordinary creditors. The Assignments and Preferences Act targets transfers made when a person is insolvent, unable to pay debts in full, or on the eve of insolvency, none of which describes this still solvent business owner. The Bankruptcy and Insolvency Act preference rules only operate once there has been an actual bankruptcy, and nothing in the facts suggests one has occurred. The Execution Act sets out property exempt from seizure for an individual debtor, a completely different concept from voiding an intentional transfer.
  119. The correct answer is D ($8,578 in value, at the debtor's election under the current regulation.): The current prescribed exemption for one motor vehicle under the Execution Act regulation is $8,578, which the $17,362 figure overstates, since that larger number is actually the exemption for tools and other personal property used to earn income. The $12,997 figure is also a different exemption, the one that applies to a debtor's equity in a principal residence, not to a motor vehicle. There is no unlimited exemption for a motor vehicle, since the statute sets a specific prescribed dollar amount rather than exempting the vehicle outright. Where the vehicle's value exceeds the exempt amount, the vehicle remains subject to seizure and sale, with the debtor entitled to be paid the prescribed amount out of the proceeds.
  120. The correct answer is B (The twelve month period, since the sibling is a non-arm's length creditor.): A preference given to a creditor who is not dealing at arm's length with the insolvent person can be void as against the trustee if made in the twelve months before the date of the initial bankruptcy event, rather than the shorter three month period that applies to an arm's length creditor. A sibling is not an arm's length party, so the shorter three month period understates the real window that applies here. There is no separate six month period for family payments under this preference rule, so that figure does not match the statute. The preference rules do apply to a payment made to a relative, since a non-arm's length relationship is exactly the situation the longer limitation period is designed to catch.
  121. The correct answer is C (Yes, since non-arm's length transfers can be challenged up to five years back.): For a non-arm's length party, a transfer at undervalue can be challenged if it occurred in the one year before the initial bankruptcy event or in the further period stretching back five years before that one year period, provided the debtor was insolvent at the time or rendered insolvent by the transfer. A controlling shareholder is exactly the kind of non-arm's length party the longer period is designed to reach, so limiting the analysis to the one year window understates the trustee's real options here. Transfers to a shareholder are not excluded from this remedy, since a shareholder is a classic non-arm's length party under this rule. The trustee does not need to prove intentional fraud for this remedy, since insolvency at the time of an undervalue transfer is itself enough.
  122. The correct answer is A (Within fifteen days after the day the purchaser became bankrupt.): The unpaid supplier's right to repossess goods requires presenting a written demand for repossession within fifteen days after the purchaser becomes bankrupt or subject to receivership, and that right expires if not exercised within that window unless it is extended before expiry. Thirty days and sixty days both overstate the actual statutory period, which is fixed at fifteen days from the bankruptcy or receivership event, not from delivery. Counting from the original delivery date rather than from the bankruptcy event also misstates the trigger, since the fifteen day clock runs from when the purchaser becomes bankrupt. The separate thirty day figure in this scenario describes how recently the goods must have been delivered before the bankruptcy, a different requirement from the fifteen day demand deadline.
  123. The correct answer is A (No, an officer or director cannot have a claim secured under this priority.): The receivership wage priority parallels the bankruptcy wage priority, securing up to $2,000 per employee against the debtor's current assets, but the statute expressly provides that no officer or director of the person subject to receivership may have a claim secured under that provision. Officers are therefore not treated the same as ordinary employees for this particular priority, independent of what work they may have performed in some other capacity. The employees' six month wage window describes the period for which their own wages qualify, but it does not open the door for an officer to share in the fund. There is no separate $10,000 cap that applies in receivership instead of the $2,000 figure, since $2,000 is the actual statutory cap that parallels the bankruptcy rule.
  124. The correct answer is A (No, because delivery must occur at least fourteen days before signing or payment.): A prospective franchisee must receive the disclosure document not less than fourteen days before the earlier of signing the franchise agreement and paying any consideration relating to the franchise, and only nine days separate June 1 from June 10, which falls short of that minimum. Delivering the document at some point before signing is not enough on its own, because the statute sets a specific minimum number of days rather than requiring only that delivery happen first. Ten days would still be short of the required period, so that option also fails to meet the actual statutory minimum. Thirty days overstates the requirement, since the statute fixes the minimum period at fourteen days, not thirty.
  125. The correct answer is B (Two years after entering into the franchise agreement.): Where a franchisor never provides the disclosure document, the franchisee may rescind the franchise agreement, without penalty or obligation, no later than two years after entering into the agreement. The sixty day period is the shorter rescission window that applies where a disclosure document was provided but was late or deficient, a different situation from receiving no disclosure document at all. Ninety days does not appear in the statute as a rescission deadline for either ground. One year also understates the true outer limit, since the statute specifically extends the deadline to two years for the more serious failure of never delivering any disclosure document.
  126. The correct answer is D (The waiver clause is void, and the franchisee can still sue for the breach.): Any purported waiver or release by a franchisee of a right given under the Act is void, so the clause in this agreement does not stop the franchisee from pursuing a claim, and a party who breaches the duty of fair dealing in performing or enforcing the agreement can face an action for damages from the other party. Signing the waiver knowingly does not make it enforceable, because the statute voids this kind of waiver independent of the franchisee's awareness when signing. Good faith in performance and enforcement is expressly made a statutory duty under the Act, so claiming there is no remedy misstates the franchisee's actual legal position. There is no requirement to use an internal ombudsperson process instead of pursuing the statutory right of action.
  127. The correct answer is A (Members are not liable for the corporation's debts in that capacity.): The statute governing an Ontario non-share capital corporation provides that members are not, in that capacity, liable for any liability of the corporation or for any act or default of the corporation, except as the Act otherwise provides. Comparing a member's exposure to a general partner's exposure misstates the structure, since a non-share capital corporation is built around limited member liability rather than the unlimited exposure that partnership law imposes on general partners. There is no rule tying member liability to unpaid membership dues, since dues and corporate debt liability are separate concepts under this statute. Members are not automatically exposed whenever the corporation cannot pay a debt, because the point of incorporating on a non-share capital basis is to separate members from the corporation's own liabilities.
  128. The correct answer is C (No, only a registered charity can issue an official donation receipt.): Registration as a charity is a separate status from the tax exemption available to a non-profit organization, and a registered charity may issue official donation receipts while an organization that is merely exempt as a non-profit organization may not. Being tax exempt as a non-profit organization does not itself carry the ability to issue official receipts, since that ability depends specifically on holding registered charity status. There is no dollar threshold that unlocks receipt issuing power for an organization that has not registered as a charity, since the requirement is about status, not the size of the gift. Provincial government approval is not the relevant gatekeeper either, because registration as a charity for income tax purposes is a federal process administered under the Income Tax Act.
Case profes_09

Priya and Dov have run a manufacturing business together for fifteen years as equal shareholders. Priya's family has used the same lawyer for two decades for various personal and business matters, while Dov has never retained that lawyer before. The two of them jointly ask the lawyer to draft a buyout agreement under which Dov will eventually purchase Priya's shares over five years. Partway through drafting, Priya and Dov begin to disagree sharply about which valuation method should set the share price, with each insisting the other's preferred method understates what the company is actually worth.

  1. The correct answer is C (Stop advising either client on the dispute and refer them out.): Once a contentious issue arises inside a joint retainer, the lawyer must not advise either client on that issue and must refer both of them to other lawyers, unless the clients had earlier agreed the lawyer could continue advising just one of them. Continuing to advise both, favouring Priya because of the longer relationship, and simply picking a valuation method for the clients all ignore that the lawyer must step back once the issue becomes contentious.
  2. The correct answer is D (That no information from one client stays confidential from the other.): Rule 3.4-5 requires the lawyer, before accepting a joint retainer, to advise each client that the lawyer has been asked to act for both, that no information received from one can be treated as confidential against the other, and that the lawyer may have to withdraw completely if a conflict develops. A discounted rate, judicial review of the agreement, and restricting who may ask questions are not part of that required advice.
  3. The correct answer is D (Tell Dov about the relationship and recommend independent advice.): Where the lawyer has a continuing relationship with one of the proposed joint clients, rule 3.4-6 requires telling the other client about that relationship and recommending independent legal advice before the lawyer proceeds. Saying nothing to Dov withholds information he is entitled to, ending the older relationship is not what the rule asks for, and a blanket waiver of future duties is not the disclosure and advice the rule actually requires.
  4. The correct answer is B (Equitably as between Priya and Dov, since they never agreed otherwise.): Rule 3.6-4 requires a lawyer acting for two or more clients on the same matter to divide fees and disbursements equitably between them unless the clients have agreed on a different split. Assigning the whole account to Dov ignores that both clients were jointly retained, tying the split to drafting contributions is not the standard the rule sets, and leaving it to the bookkeeper's convenience is not an equitable division at all.
  5. The correct answer is C (Approval from a panel of the Tribunal's Hearing Division.): A lawyer formerly a judge of the Superior Court of Justice, and certain higher courts, must obtain express approval from a panel of the Law Society Tribunal's Hearing Division before appearing as counsel or advocate, granted only in exceptional circumstances. A reference letter from the court, an automatic one year clearance, and consent from opposing parties are not what the rule actually requires before that return to practice.
  6. The correct answer is A (Promptly notify the sender that the document was inadvertently received.): Rule 7.2-10 requires a lawyer who receives a document relating to the representation of the lawyer's client, and who knows or reasonably ought to know it was sent inadvertently, to promptly notify the sender. Forwarding it to the client, quietly deleting it, or simply using it treats the mistake as the lawyer's advantage instead of triggering the notification the rule requires.
  7. The correct answer is D (Make clear the lawyer acts exclusively in the interests of the employer client.): Rule 7.2-9 requires a lawyer dealing with an unrepresented person to take care that the person understands the lawyer is acting exclusively in the interests of the lawyer's own client. Leaving the mistaken impression uncorrected fails that duty, giving the employee legal advice crosses into representing her interests, and an outright refusal to communicate goes further than the rule requires.
  8. The correct answer is D (Yes, it bars harassment of a colleague, staff, client or anyone else.): Rule 6.3-3 states that a lawyer shall not sexually harass a colleague, a staff member, a client or any other person, so the prohibition plainly covers conduct toward a colleague at the same firm. Limiting the rule to clients, leaving the conduct mainly to internal policy, and requiring criminal conduct before the rule applies all understate what rule 6.3-3 actually covers.
  9. The correct answer is A (Ensuring no one is denied service on a protected ground like origin.): Rules 6.3.1-1 and 6.3.1-2 give a lawyer a special responsibility to honour human rights obligations and to ensure no one is denied services, or receives inferior service, on grounds including place of origin and ethnic origin. Confirming citizenship, charging a uniform flat fee, and requiring English at intake are not the obligation this scenario engages, since the problem here is the refusal based on origin itself.
  10. The correct answer is A (No, unless the lender is regulated or a related person.): Rule 3.4-28.1 bars a lawyer from borrowing from a client except from a regulated lender or a related person, and the fairness of the terms does not create an exception outside those two categories. A generous rate both sides accept, a written and signed agreement, and a promise to repay within a year all describe reasonable loan features but none of them turns this client into a regulated lender or a related person.
  11. The correct answer is B (Independent legal representation for the client on the loan.): For a loan to a client who is not a related person, rule 3.4-29 requires the lawyer to insist that the client obtain independent legal representation before the loan is made, rather than merely independent advice, which applies instead to a related person. A notarized waiver, prior Law Society approval, and a second lawyer's co-signature are not the safeguard the rule actually requires.
  12. The correct answer is B (A complete reporting letter and a signed trust declaration on the mortgage.): Rule 3.4-33.1 restricts a lawyer from holding a syndicated mortgage in trust for investor clients through an entity in which the lawyer has a financial interest unless each investor receives a complete reporting letter and a trust declaration signed by the person in whose name the mortgage is held, among the other listed protections. A verbal summary, a repayment guarantee, and treating the original loan paperwork as sufficient do not satisfy that requirement.
  13. The correct answer is C (Replenish the shortfall and report the misapplication to the Law Society.): The reporting duty applies to any misapplication of trust funds, regardless of whether the cause was a bookkeeping error or a returned cheque, so the lawyer must still replenish the shortfall and report the misapplication through LSO Connects. Correcting the ledger alone leaves the reportable event undisclosed, waiting for the annual filing delays a report that should be prompt, and telling only the affected client does not satisfy the duty to report to the Law Society.
  14. The correct answer is A (The Law Foundation of Ontario, rather than any individual client.): Section 57 of the Law Society Act deems interest accruing on money held for more than one client in a mixed trust account to be held in trust for The Law Foundation of Ontario, with the licensee filing reports and remitting that interest to the Foundation. Keeping the interest for the firm's own use, giving it to the largest depositor, or letting the bank retain it as compensation are all inconsistent with what section 57 actually directs.
  15. The correct answer is A (At least the six years immediately preceding the most recent fiscal year end.): By-Law 9, section 23(1) sets the general retention period for the records required under sections 18, 19 and 19.1 at least the six years immediately preceding the licensee's most recent fiscal year end, with a longer ten year period for a narrower set of specific paragraphs. Three years understates the requirement, twenty years overstates it, and tying retention only to a client file closing ignores the fiscal year end measure the by law actually uses.
  16. The correct answer is C (The coverage option needs approval before practising real estate law.): LAWPRO states that a lawyer who intends to practise real estate law in Ontario must be eligible for, apply for and be granted the real estate practice coverage option before being able to practise in that area, so starting beforehand leaves a real gap. Assuming the base coverage automatically extends to this work misstates the requirement, waiting until a claim arises comes far too late, and the requirement is not limited to only acting for a lender.
  17. The correct answer is B ($5,000 per claim, covering expenses, indemnity and repair costs.): LAWPRO's standard deductible under the base premium option is $5,000 per claim, applying to claim expenses, indemnity payments and costs of repairs together, while other deductible options range from nil up to $25,000. The $1,000 and $25,000 figures describe other points on that broader range rather than the standard base amount, and deductibles are not individually negotiated outside the listed options.
  18. The correct answer is A (Wait eight business days, or get written confirmation it is valid.): To rely on LAWPRO's enhanced coverage for a counterfeit certified cheque, the lawyer must either wait at least eight business days after depositing the instrument before disbursing, or obtain written confirmation from a financial institution that the instrument was verified. Disbursing right away treats certified cheques as risk free, which they are not, a full year is far longer than the coverage requires, and a client guarantee is not the safeguard LAWPRO's coverage depends on.
  19. The correct answer is A (No, that employment needs express approval from a panel of the Tribunal's Hearing Division.): Rule 7.6-1.1 places strict limits on employing, in any capacity having to do with the practice of law, a person whose licence has been revoked, requiring express approval from a panel of the Law Society Tribunal's Hearing Division before that employment is permitted. A promise of good conduct, treating revocation as limited to courtroom work, and ordinary supervision by another lawyer are not substitutes for that required Tribunal approval.
  20. The correct answer is C (Good cause for withdrawing, and reasonable notice given to the client.): Rule 3.7-1 provides that a lawyer shall not withdraw from representation except for good cause and on reasonable notice to the client, so a lawyer cannot simply step away because a file turned out to be less profitable than expected. Mere personal preference does not amount to good cause, opposing counsel's permission is not what the rule requires, and a court order is not needed for every withdrawal from a retainer.
  21. The correct answer is A (At least six years immediately preceding the licensee's most recent fiscal year end.): Where a licensee pays or receives a referral fee, the referral fee records, including a fully signed copy of the referral agreement, must be kept for at least six years immediately preceding the licensee's most recent fiscal year end, matching the general retention period that applies to many of the firm's other financial records. A one year period is far shorter than the six year minimum the guidance actually sets for these referral fee records. A three year period likewise understates the true retention requirement for this category of record. A twenty five year period substantially overstates what is required, even though some firms may choose to keep records for longer periods of their own accord.
  22. The correct answer is B (The general period is six years, while the listed paragraphs require ten years.): The retention periods for trust and general account records are six years immediately preceding the most recent fiscal year end for the records under sections 18, 19 and 19.1 generally, and ten years immediately preceding the most recent fiscal year end for the records under paragraphs 1, 2, 3, 8, 9, 10 and 11 of section 18 specifically, so the longer period applies only to the listed paragraphs rather than to every record. Reversing the two figures, with ten years as the general rule and six years for the listed paragraphs, gets the relationship backwards. The two periods are not identical at eight years each, since the by-law sets two genuinely different lengths for these two categories. The general period is not three years, nor is the listed paragraph period five years, since both figures understate what the by-law actually requires.
  23. The correct answer is B (A retention period of fifteen years from the date the client file is closed.): The Law Society's guide to closing, retaining and destroying client files states that generally, based on the provisions of the Limitations Act, 2002, a retention period of fifteen years from the date the client file is closed may be appropriate, treated as a general guideline requiring the lawyer's own judgment. A two year period falls far short of what the guidance actually suggests for an ordinary closed file. A one year period is even shorter and would leave the firm without records for most of the period during which a claim could still arise. Thirty years substantially overstates the general guideline figure, even though some file types may warrant longer retention for their own reasons.
  24. The correct answer is B (At least fifteen years after the estate has been finally distributed.): The Law Society's guidance recommends that lawyers retain an estate, trust or power of attorney file for at least fifteen years after the date the estate has been finally distributed, recognizing that a will does not take effect until the testator dies and that the file may later become relevant to testamentary capacity. Five years is shorter than what the guidance recommends for this particular category of file. One year is far too brief given how long a dispute over an estate can take to surface. Capping retention at no more than three years directly contradicts the fifteen year figure the guidance actually recommends for estate and trust files.
  25. The correct answer is C (Original testamentary documents such as wills and powers of attorney must not be destroyed.): The Law Society's guidance states that a lawyer must not destroy original client documents in the lawyer's possession, including original testamentary documents such as wills, powers of attorney, holograph wills and mutual will contracts, since these documents belong to the client rather than to the file generally. Destroying them along with the rest of the file after five years treats them the same as ordinary file contents, which the guidance specifically warns against. Inactivity of the underlying matter does not create an exception allowing destruction of these particular original documents. The end of the retainer likewise does not authorize destroying documents that remain the client's own property.
  26. The correct answer is A (Notify clients and manage any outstanding undertakings and trust conditions on their files.): The Law Society's guide to closing your practice lists active file steps that include reviewing the active client list, notifying clients, addressing Legal Aid files, managing undertakings and trust conditions, returning or transferring client property, and withdrawing from representation as appropriate. Shredding active files without contacting the clients ignores the client's own interest in the file and is not part of the orderly process the guide describes. Files cannot simply be transferred to whichever firm happens to be nearby without the client's own instructions about who will act. Relying on the client to discover the closure only through general public notices, rather than direct notification, falls short of the guide's own active file steps.
  27. The correct answer is C (Advise the clients the retainers cannot be completed and arrange for transfer of trust funds.): By-Law 7.1 defines winding up a licensee's professional business to include advising clients the retainers cannot be completed, arranging transfer or return of the files, and returning or transferring the trust funds as directed. Keeping trust funds in the account indefinitely while waiting for a buyer for the practice is inconsistent with the by-law's requirement to return or transfer the funds as the clients direct. Automatically transferring trust funds to the Law Society without any client instructions skips the step of following each client's own direction about the money. Simply closing the doors with no communication at all is the opposite of what the winding up definition in the by-law requires.
  28. The correct answer is B (The plan was reviewed in the preceding twelve months and the administrator consented to act.): Under the mandatory succession planning amendments to By-Law 7.1, a sole practitioner must confirm having created a compliant contingency plan, having reviewed it in the preceding twelve months, and having obtained the administrator's consent to act, all as part of the Annual Report filing. There is no requirement that a Law Society auditor review the plan within any particular window, since the review is the practitioner's own responsibility. The by-law does not require the named administrator to be a family member of the sole practitioner. Confirming that the plan has never been reviewed since it was created would actually be a failure to meet the requirement, not a valid confirmation of compliance.
  29. The correct answer is D (The call is confidential, but the licensee's identity is verified before questions are answered.): The Practice Management Helpline is described as a confidential telephone service for Ontario lawyers and paralegals, but it is confidential rather than anonymous, because the licensee's identity is verified before questions are answered. Describing the call as fully anonymous with no identity verification misstates how the confidential service actually operates. There is nothing in the description of the Helpline suggesting calls are recorded and automatically forwarded to the licensee's insurer. The Helpline answers questions about the Rules of Professional Conduct and practice management topics generally, not merely questions limited to billing software.
  30. The correct answer is C (A $1 million per claim limit, a $2 million aggregate limit, and a $5,000 deductible.): By-Law 6 and LAWPRO's own materials set the minimum base coverage at $1 million per claim and $2 million in the aggregate, with a standard deductible of $5,000 per claim applicable to claim expenses and indemnity payments together, which together describe the mandatory base program for a lawyer in private practice. A $500,000 per claim limit with a $1,000 deductible understates both the required policy limits and the standard deductible figure. A $2 million per claim limit with a $10,000 deductible overstates the mandatory minimum figures. A $1 million aggregate limit paired with a $2,500 deductible mismatches the aggregate figure, since the aggregate minimum is actually $2 million, not $1 million.
  31. The correct answer is D (The lawyer must be eligible for, apply for and be granted the coverage option first.): LAWPRO states that all lawyers who intend to practise real estate law in Ontario must be eligible for, apply for and be granted the real estate practice coverage option before being able to practise in that area of law, protecting members of the public and the Land Titles Assurance Fund against fraudulent instruments. Beginning to practise first and applying for the coverage option afterward reverses the required sequence LAWPRO actually sets out. The requirement does not depend on whether any particular client happens to ask about the coverage. Using title insurance on individual files does not eliminate the separate requirement to be granted this coverage option before practising real estate law at all.
  32. The correct answer is D (Give prompt notice of the circumstance to the insurer rather than waiting for a formal demand.): LAWPRO's guidance states that lawyers should provide notice of real or possible mistakes immediately, since early notice gives the best chance to put things right while late notice can jeopardise coverage, and rule 7.8-2 similarly requires prompt notice of any circumstance that may give rise to a claim. Waiting for a formal demand letter before notifying the insurer at all is exactly the kind of delay the guidance warns can jeopardise coverage. Waiting until a lawsuit has actually been commenced pushes notice even later than waiting for a demand letter, compounding the risk. Notifying the client's new lawyer does not substitute for the separate obligation to notify the lawyer's own professional liability insurer about the circumstance.
